How divorce filing works in Texas

Texas's official statewide self-help resource is TexasLawHelp (funded by the State Bar of Texas), with step-by-step divorce guides and free forms. Start there before anything else.

Statewide self-help

TexasLawHelp — Divorce Guides

The official starting point: divorce guides with and without minor children, free forms, and instructions.

texaslawhelp.org — divorce guides →

Spousal support and dividing retirement accounts (QDROs)

Two money questions decide more divorces than anything else: will one spouse pay support to the other, and who gets what share of the retirement savings? Here is how Texas answers both — plus the extra court order, the QDRO, that most people have never heard of until it is almost too late. One federal tax note first: for divorces finalized after December 31, 2018, spousal support is not tax-deductible for the payer and not taxable income for the receiver.

Spousal support

How Texas decides support — and how long it lasts

Texas calls court-ordered support spousal maintenance, and there is no formula — but eligibility is narrow and comes first. Under Family Code 8.051 a court may order maintenance only if the spouse asking will lack sufficient property to meet minimum reasonable needs AND meets a listed ground (family-violence conviction/deferred adjudication, incapacitating disability, a marriage of 10+ years plus inability to earn enough, or caring for a disabled child); if eligible, the judge weighs the factors in 8.052. Duration is capped by marriage length in Family Code 8.054: up to 5 years for under-10-year family-violence cases and for 10-to-20-year marriages, up to 7 years for 20-to-30-year marriages, and up to 10 years for 30+ year marriages, limited to the shortest reasonable period — except disability/disabled-child cases may continue while eligibility lasts. Amount is also capped at the lesser of $5,000 a month or 20% of the payer's average monthly gross income (8.055).

Dividing retirement

Retirement accounts and pensions in a Texas divorce

Texas is a community-property state and the Employees Retirement System of Texas (ERS) page opened explains the community share of an ERS benefit is calculated using service credit during the marriage divided by total service credit, with a QDRO splitting the benefit for the former spouse. For ERS, use the ERS QDRO model template (Chapter 804, Texas Government Code): ERS must receive a photocopy of the divorce decree and the original certified copy of the QDRO, and it will not pay the alternate payee until the member retires, takes a refund, or dies. The Teacher Retirement System of Texas (TRS) likewise requires its own model domestic relations order (DRO), per official TRS search results.

Federal rules — all 50 states

The QDRO: the separate order that actually splits a workplace plan

For most workplace plans — 401(k)s and private pensions covered by the federal ERISA law — the divorce decree by itself does not split the account. A separate court order called a QDRO (Qualified Domestic Relations Order) does that. The usual steps: (1) the settlement or judgment states exactly what share each spouse gets; (2) the QDRO is drafted — many plans publish a model form, so ask the plan administrator for theirs and have the plan pre-approve your draft before the judge signs; (3) the judge signs it; (4) a certified copy goes to the plan administrator, who approves it and sets up the former spouse’s separate share. Start before the divorce is final and finish right after: waiting can cost the benefit entirely if the plan member retires, dies, or remarries first. Government plans do not use QDROs — federal civilian and military retired pay, railroad retirement, and most state and local plans each have their own kind of court order and their own forms (the Texas card above covers the state plan where it applies). Tax note: money paid from a plan to a former spouse under a QDRO can be rolled into an IRA to defer the tax, and a QDRO payout is exempt from the usual 10% early-withdrawal penalty.

Official source

Texas — Family Code Chapter 8, Maintenance (official)

The official Texas source for divorce, spousal support, and retirement-division rules.

Official divorce & support information →

Prenuptial agreements: what they can and cannot do

A prenuptial agreement ("prenup") is a contract two people sign before marrying that sets their own rules for property and debts if the marriage ends in divorce or one of them dies — instead of the defaults in this section. It can say that a business, a house, or an inheritance stays one spouse's separate property, decide which debts belong to whom, and in many states limit or waive spousal support. What it can never do is decide child custody or child support: those belong to the court at the time, judged by the child's best interests, and a judge will not let a contract signed before the wedding override them. For a prenup to hold up, the pattern in most states is the same: it must be in writing and signed; both sides must give a full, honest picture of their money first (hidden assets are the classic way prenups die); and it must be signed willingly, with real time to think — an agreement presented the week of the wedding invites a coercion challenge. The single strongest protection is that each person has their own lawyer; one shared lawyer, or none, is the fact judges look at hardest when the poorer spouse later challenges the deal. Agreements signed after the wedding (postnuptial agreements) exist too, but courts in many states scrutinize them more closely. If you are the one being asked to sign, the consultation fee for your own lawyer to read it is the best money in the whole wedding budget.

Postnuptial agreements: the contract you sign after the wedding

A postnuptial agreement (“postnup”) is the same kind of contract as a prenup — it sets your own rules for property, debts, and sometimes spousal support if the marriage ends — except you sign it after the wedding, while you are married and planning to stay that way. (It is not a separation agreement: that is the paperwork for couples who are already splitting up.) Courts look harder at postnups than at prenups. Before the wedding, two people bargain at arm’s length; after it, they are spouses, and in many states the law says spouses owe each other a fiduciary duty — a duty of honesty and fair dealing like the one between business partners — so a judge checks a postnup for pressure, secrecy, and unfairness much more closely. Only two states, Colorado and North Dakota, have adopted the Uniform Law Commission’s model act covering these agreements (the UPMAA, 2012); everywhere else the rules come from each state’s own statutes or court decisions, and a few states barely enforce postnups at all. Almost everywhere, the basics are the same: the agreement must be in writing and signed by both spouses, entered into freely, based on a full and honest disclosure of each spouse’s money, and not grossly unfair. And like a prenup, it can never decide child custody or child support — those always belong to the court.

Texas: Texas lets married couples sign a written partition or exchange agreement that turns community property into separate property.

What Texas requires:

  • Writing signed by both parties
  • Enforceable without consideration
  • Voluntariness

Watch out: Subchapter B authorizes partition or exchange of community property and agreements about income from separate property; a partition or exchange provision is void as to a preexisting creditor whose rights it is intended to defraud.

Where this rule comes from: statute (Tex. Fam. Code § 4.102).

Official source: Tex. Fam. Code § 4.102 →

If you are thinking about a postnup: (1) each spouse should have their OWN lawyer — in some states that is required, everywhere it is the single best protection against the agreement being thrown out later; (2) swap complete financial disclosures and keep them with the agreement — hidden assets are the classic way these deals die; (3) sign when things are calm, not in the middle of a crisis or as a condition of staying together; (4) revisit the agreement after big life changes — a child, a business, an inheritance, a move to another state — because an agreement that was fair when signed can look very different years later; and (5) use the worksheet below to get your finances and decisions down on paper before you pay for lawyer time.

Postnuptial Agreement Worksheet — Texas

Use this worksheet to prepare — together or each on your own — before you talk to a lawyer. You can type right on the lines, or print it blank and fill it in by hand. Nothing you type here is saved or sent anywhere.

Preparation only — read this first. This worksheet is a planning tool, not the agreement itself. It is not a legal form, and filling it in does not create a postnuptial agreement or any other legal document. It is not legal advice. A postnuptial agreement should be drafted or reviewed by a lawyer — in most states, each spouse should have their own lawyer — and signed exactly the way Texas law requires. Rules checked November 2026; laws change, so confirm the current rules with your lawyer or the court.

Part 4 — Questions to bring to a lawyer

  • Does our agreement meet every Texas requirement to be enforced?
  • Do we each need our own lawyer — and is that required here, or just safer?
  • Is there anything on our list that Texas will not let an agreement decide?
  • Will this agreement still work if we move to another state?
  • Does it need to be notarized, witnessed, or recorded anywhere (for example, if it covers real estate)?
  • What are the most common reasons courts here throw these agreements out?
  • How do we change or cancel the agreement later if our lives change?
  • What will the whole process cost, start to finish?

Common-law marriage: married without a wedding — in a few states, if you meant to be

A common-law marriage is a real marriage formed without a license or ceremony: the couple agrees, now, to be married, lives together, and holds themselves out to the world as spouses. The popular version — "live together seven years and you're married" — is a myth; no state has a years rule, and simply living together, however long, creates no marriage anywhere. Only a few states still let couples form one: as of this writing, Colorado, Iowa, Kansas, Montana, Oklahoma, Rhode Island, and Texas (which calls it "informal marriage"), plus the District of Columbia — some by statute, Oklahoma and Rhode Island through their court decisions. Utah is narrower still: it does not have common-law marriage, but a court can declare an unsolemnized relationship a valid marriage if the couple petitions in time — during the relationship or within a year after it ends — and Utah closes that door to new petitions on May 5, 2027, so Utah couples should not wait. New Hampshire's rule operates only after a partner dies, for inheritance purposes. Several other states honor common-law marriages that were validly formed before they abolished the practice. And every state recognizes a common-law marriage that was validly formed in a state that allows it — which cuts both ways: move away and you are still married. That is why this card sits in the divorce section: a common-law marriage is ended the same way as any other, with a divorce, and splitting up without one leaves you legally married — to someone you may have to find years later when a house, a pension, or a new marriage depends on it. If you believe you may be in one, a family-law consultation is worth far more than the guesswork.

What to do next:

  1. Make sure you meet the residency requirement listed above before you file.
  2. Get the court’s free forms or online filing tool (linked above) and fill them out completely.
  3. File with the district court in the county where either spouse lives, and pay the filing fee.
  4. Know the cost. The filing fee is $350 ($365–$401 if you have minor children).
  5. Serve your spouse properly and follow the court’s steps from there — the self-help pages walk through each phase.

Can't afford the court fees in Texas? Ask for a fee waiver

  • What it's called: Statement of Inability to Afford Payment of Court Costs (or an Appeal Bond) — Texas Rule of Civil Procedure 145, 'Payment of Costs Not Required'.
  • Who qualifies: Any party who cannot afford payment of court costs files the sworn Statement (signed before a notary or made under penalty of perjury); on filing, the clerk must docket the case, issue citation, and provide ordinary services — no judge pre-approval is needed.
  • What it covers: Rule 145(a) defines 'costs' broadly: any fee charged by the court or an officer of the court, including filing fees, fees for issuance and service of process, fees for copies, fees for a court-appointed professional, and fees charged by the clerk or court reporter for preparation of the appellate record.

Apply: the official form or court page — file the request with your first papers, before any fee is due.

Official source: Texas fee-waiver law

Lawyer referral services in Texas

If you earn too much to qualify for free legal aid but can’t afford a private lawyer’s full fees, a lawyer referral service can bridge the gap: it matches you with a local lawyer for your issue, and many offer a low-cost first consultation so you can get advice before committing. Here’s the official route in Texas:

Initial consultation of up to 30 minutes for no more than $20 with a participating lawyer.

Fishing & Hunting Violations in Texas

A day outdoors can end with a citation — no license, one fish over the limit, the wrong season — and in every state that piece of paper is a real court case with real penalties. This section covers both sides in Texas: what the everyday violations cost, why poaching is punished so much harder, what to do if a game warden cites you, and how to report poaching when you see it.

If you’re cited

Everyday violations — and what they cost in Texas

Everyday offenses include hunting or fishing without a license, taking or possessing fish or wildlife over the bag or possession limit, out of season, or by an illegal method — TPWD says it is unlawful to take or possess wildlife in greater numbers, by other means, or at any time or place other than as allowed. Texas classifies these as Parks and Wildlife Code misdemeanors: Class C, $25-$500; Class B, $200-$2,000 and/or 6 months in jail; and Class A, $500-$4,000 and/or 1 year in jail.

Poaching costs more

When it stops being a ticket

Serious or repeat poaching can rise to a state jail felony, $1,500-$10,000 and/or up to 2 years in jail, on top of the criminal fine. TPWD will also seek civil restitution — the civil recovery value for the loss or damage to the wildlife resource, payable to TPWD — and a violator faces automatic suspension or revocation of licenses for up to five years and forfeiture of hunting gear, including firearms, used to commit the violation.

Official source

Texas Parks & Wildlife Department — Laws, Penalties & Restitution

The state agency’s own page for Texas — how to report a violation, the hotline, and program details.

Official page →

What to do:

  1. Treat a citation like a court case — because it is one. A game warden’s ticket carries a court date or a payment deadline. Ignoring it can add charges and cost you your license on top of the fine. If you believe the citation is wrong, your chance to fight it is in court, not at the truck.
  2. Write everything down the same day. Where you were, what the officer said, who was with you. Keep your license, tags, and the citation together — memories fade faster than court dates arrive.
  3. A conviction in Texas can follow you to other states. Most states honor each other’s hunting and fishing suspensions through the Interstate Wildlife Violator Compact, so losing your privileges here can mean losing them across most of the country.
  4. Serious charge? Talk to a lawyer before your court date. If the case involves big fines, possible jail, paying restitution for an animal, or years of license loss, a lawyer who handles fish and game cases can usually do more before the hearing than after it.
  5. Saw poaching instead? Report it. Report poaching to Operation Game Thief, Texas' wildlife crime-stoppers program, by calling 1-800-792-GAME (4263) — tips can be sent anonymously. If your tip leads to a conviction, you may be offered a reward of up to $1,000.

Seasons, bag limits, and legal methods change every year and differ by species and area — the current Texas hunting and fishing regulations are the final word, not last year’s booklet. Laws cited: Tex. Parks & Wild. Code §§ 12.301-12.305 (civil restitution / recovery of value), 12.405 (Class B penalty), 12.4061 (state jail felony), and 12.501-12.5015 (license suspension, revocation and automatic revocation). This is general information, not legal advice.

Intellectual Property in Texas

Intellectual property is the law of what you create — and it comes in four kinds that people mix up all the time. Copyright covers creative work: writing, music, photos, software. A trademark protects a business name, logo, or slogan. A patent protects an invention. A trade secret protects valuable information you keep secret. Most of the rules are federal, the same in every state — but Texas adds two things of its own: a cheaper state trademark registration and its own trade secret law, both covered below.

Copyright

Your work is protected the moment you create it

Copyright is automatic — you own your original work as soon as you write it, record it, or take the photo, and the © symbol is optional. Registering with the U.S. Copyright Office is what gives that protection its muscle: in most cases you cannot sue for infringement until you have registered, and registering early can make you eligible for statutory damages and attorney fees if someone copies you. Registration is online and cheap — $45 for a single work by one author, $65 for a standard application. A smaller dispute (up to $30,000) can go to the Copyright Claims Board instead of federal court — a process designed to work without a lawyer.

U.S. Copyright Office → Copyright Claims Board →

Official source

Register a trademark in Texas

Using a name or logo in business gives you some rights automatically, but registration makes them much stronger. A federal registration with the USPTO costs a $350 base fee per class and protects your mark nationwide. A state registration costs far less and protects your mark inside Texas — often the right first step for a business that only sells in-state. In Texas, trademark registration is handled by the Secretary of State. The filing fee is $50 per class, and the Texas registration lasts 5 years. Always search before you file: if someone is already using a similar name for similar goods, your application can be refused and the fee is gone.

Texas trademark registration →

Patents

Inventors: start with a provisional application

Patents are federal only — there is no state patent. The affordable first step is a provisional application: it locks in your filing date for 12 months while you test and improve the idea, and the USPTO filing fee is $65 for most individual inventors (micro entity), $130 for a small entity, or $325 at the standard rate. Miss the 12-month deadline and that filing date is gone for good. Texas inventors are served by the TALA Patent Pro Bono for Inventors (Texas Accountants and Lawyers for the Arts), part of the USPTO’s Patent Pro Bono Program — free help from volunteer patent attorneys for inventors who qualify. And be careful with “invention promotion” companies that promise to make you rich — federal law requires them to give you their success rates in writing before you sign, and very few of their customers ever make money.

USPTO Patent Pro Bono Program →

Trade secrets

No registration — protection lasts only while it stays secret

A recipe, a customer list, a way of doing the work: if it has value because it is secret, trade secret law can protect it — but only while you actually treat it as secret. Use written confidentiality agreements (NDAs), passwords, and need-to-know access; a secret you hand out freely stops being one. In Texas, trade secrets are covered by the Texas Uniform Trade Secrets Act, Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code ch. 134A (§§ 134A.001–134A.008). The federal Defend Trade Secrets Act also lets you sue in federal court in every state.

Scam warning

Official-looking trademark letters are usually not official

After a trademark filing becomes public, private companies mine the records and mail letters that look like government bills — hundreds of dollars to “register,” “renew,” or “monitor” your mark. Real USPTO fees are paid only through the USPTO itself, never by mailing a check to a company with a Washington-sounding name. Check any letter against your own application record at uspto.gov before paying anyone.

Report fraud to the FTC →

What to do:

  1. Match the problem to the right kind of protection. Copyright = creative work. Trademark = name, logo, slogan. Patent = invention. Trade secret = valuable secret. Filing the wrong kind wastes the fee.
  2. Search before you spend anything. Use the USPTO’s free trademark search for names and logos. For an invention, search existing patents first — Google Patents is free — because someone else may already own the idea.
  3. Register your copyright online. $45 to $65 at copyright.gov — and do it soon after you publish, while the strongest remedies are still on the table.
  4. Pick state or federal for your trademark. Only selling in Texas? The state filing above is cheaper. Selling — or planning to sell — across state lines? Go federal at the USPTO.
  5. Inventors: provisional first, deadline in ink. File the provisional application, write the 12-month deadline on the calendar, and ask the Patent Pro Bono Program for free help before paying any promoter.
  6. Keep secrets like secrets. NDAs in writing, passwords on files, customer lists shared on a need-to-know basis. The law protects the secrets you protect.
  7. Got an official-looking fee letter? Stop. Compare it against uspto.gov first. Most of those letters are private solicitations you can throw away.

Copyright, trademark, and patent rules are mostly federal: the Copyright Act (17 U.S.C.), the Lanham Act (15 U.S.C. §§ 1051–1127), the Patent Act (35 U.S.C.), and the Defend Trade Secrets Act (18 U.S.C. § 1836); Texas Uniform Trade Secrets Act, Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code ch. 134A (§§ 134A.001–134A.008) (Texas). Government fees above were checked against the official schedules in November 2026 and change over time — confirm the current fee on the official page before you file. This is general information, not legal advice.

Gender marker changes in Texas

The letter on your birth certificate or driver’s license — M, F, or X — is called a gender marker, and in most states you can change it so your ID matches who you are. But the rules are different in every state, and they have changed fast in 2025 and 2026. In 34 states you need neither a court order nor surgery. In 15 states a court order is required for at least one document. In 10 states (Florida, Idaho, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Oklahoma, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, and Wyoming) a gender-marker change is effectively blocked on both documents right now. And in Montana, the rules are set by a court injunction that could change. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

Birth certificates

Your birth certificate is the foundation document — a driver’s license change often depends on it, so start here. In the easiest states you change it with a sworn statement of your own, no doctor and no judge involved. In middle-ground states you need a letter from a doctor or other health care provider saying you have had appropriate clinical treatment, or a court order. In the strictest states you need a court order plus proof of surgery. And in 10 states there is currently no working route at all — the agency simply will not process the change, even with a court order. Where you were born matters more than where you live now: a birth certificate is always changed through the state where the birth happened.

Okay, so what do I do? Find the vital records office of the state where you were born — the agency linked in the card below — and check its current form before you file anything. Rules changed in several states during 2025 and 2026, and old guides online still describe routes that no longer work.

Driver’s licenses and state IDs

The license or state ID is the document you show every day, and its rules are separate from the birth certificate. In many states you can pick M, F, or X on a license by self-designation — just tell the DMV what marker you want, no paperwork beyond the usual ID application. In other states you need a doctor’s letter, a court order, or an amended birth certificate showing the new marker. In the 10 blocking states, the license marker cannot be changed to reflect gender identity at all right now — usually because the DMV now takes the marker from your birth certificate or passport and will not accept a gender-change request. A few states offer no X marker: only M or F.

Okay, so what do I do? Change the birth certificate first if your state’s DMV requires an amended birth certificate — that is the most common reason a license change gets stuck. If you only need the license changed, check whether your DMV accepts a doctor’s letter on its own; in several states that is the fastest route.

Court orders and surgery: when they are needed

A court order is the most common extra requirement: 15 states require one for at least one document, usually the birth certificate. Getting one means filing a petition in court — often the same court that handles name changes — and a judge signs an order directing the agency to amend the record. Surgery is required far less often than people think: only 10 states still require proof of surgery (or a doctor’s statement that the change happened) on the birth-certificate route — Alabama, Arkansas, Georgia, Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, Missouri, Nebraska, North Dakota, and Wisconsin. In the other 40 states, no surgery is required for either document. And a court order does not fix everything: in the blocking states, agencies are refusing to honor gender-marker court orders entirely.

Okay, so what do I do? If your state needs a court order, ask the legal aid organization in the card below — many help with these petitions for free. Do not assume you need surgery: check your state’s card below first, because in most states you do not.

Before you start: three things to know

First, a name change is a separate process. Changing your legal name goes through a court in the state where you live, and it is covered in the Name Changes section. You can often do the name and the marker together, but they are different filings. Second, federal documents have their own rules. A state change does not automatically change your U.S. passport or your Social Security record — those are federal, and you update them separately with the State Department and the Social Security Administration. Third, keep copies of everything. Save the court order, the doctor’s letter, and the old and new documents: if a rule changes again, you may need to prove what was already approved.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Decide which document to change first — usually the birth certificate, since the license often follows it. (2) Gather the proof your state asks for, using the card below. (3) File with the agency listed, and keep a copy of everything you send.

Gender marker changes in Texas

How it works here: Texas now bans gender-marker changes on both state documents for gender identity. Since August 20, 2024, the Texas Department of Public Safety (DPS) has stopped updating the sex marker on driver's licenses and state IDs, and since around August 30, 2024, the Department of State Health Services (DSHS) Vital Statistics Unit has stopped amending the sex marker on birth certificates. HB 229, effective September 1, 2025, defines male, female, man, and woman in state law, and most Texas judges have since stopped signing gender-marker orders. Only M and F markers are offered; no X marker is available.

Birth certificate: Not available for gender identity. DSHS Vital Statistics will issue a new birth certificate based on sex only where the sex recorded at birth is proved by satisfactory evidence, such as medical records, to have been incomplete or inaccurate at birth. The correction application is Form VS-170, with a $25 filing fee for a new birth certificate based on the child's sex, but DSHS will not process an order that changes both name and sex, so a name-only court order should be used for a name change. Court orders directing a gender-marker change are not accepted for this purpose.

Driver’s license or state ID: Not available except to correct a clerical error. DPS stopped processing gender-marker updates on driver's licenses and state IDs on August 20, 2024, even when presented with a court order, and will refuse a combined name-and-gender court order entirely, including for the name change in that order; a separate name-only court order is needed to update a name. First-time applicants are assigned the marker shown on their primary document, such as a birth certificate or passport. No X marker is available.

Court order required? No — a court order does not enable a gender-marker change in Texas; DPS and DSHS do not accept gender-marker court orders, and a court order is needed only for a legal name change, which must be in a separate name-only order.

Surgery required? No — no surgery or medical procedure can unlock a gender-marker change in Texas; the only exception is proving with medical records that the sex recorded at birth was incomplete or inaccurate, which is a correction of an error, not a change to reflect gender identity.

State agency: Texas Department of State Health Services, Vital Statistics Unit. Start here for the current forms and fees.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: Check the agency’s current form before you file — several states changed their rules in 2025 and 2026. If a court order is needed, get free help from the organization linked above before you file, and keep copies of everything you submit.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Work out which state issued your birth certificate — that state’s rules control the birth-certificate change. (2) Check the card above for what proof your state wants: a sworn statement, a doctor’s letter, a court order, or proof of surgery. (3) File with the agency linked above, then update your license, and separately update your passport and Social Security record. (4) If your state is one of the 10 blocking states, talk to the legal aid organization linked above before you spend money on a court filing — a court order may not help there right now.

Sources: the official state vital records and motor vehicle agencies, state statutes, and court decisions linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. In 34 states you need neither a court order nor surgery; 15 states require a court order for at least one document; 10 states require proof of surgery on the birth-certificate route; and 10 states effectively block gender-marker changes on both documents. Montana’s rules are set by a court injunction that could change. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Foster care & youth aging out in Texas

Every year, thousands of young people turn 18 while in foster care — and that birthday can mean losing the home, the caseworker, and the support they have had for years. But turning 18 does not have to mean being on your own. In 43 states a young person who turns 18 in foster care can choose to stay in care — usually until age 21, and to age 23 in Idaho and Maine. In 7 states (Delaware, Iowa, Kansas, Mississippi, Montana, Utah, and Wisconsin) there is no general extended foster care program, but each of those states offers other help instead, and the card below says what it is. And even after care ends, former foster youth can usually keep Medicaid until age 26 and get up to $5,000 a year in Education and Training Voucher money for college or job training. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

Aging out: what happens at 18

In most states, foster care ends at age 18 — the court case closes, the foster home placement ends, and the young person is legally an adult. Two states are different: in Nebraska the age of majority is 19, so youth age out at 19, and in Alabama no youth ages out before 19. Before the birthday, the agency is supposed to build a transition plan with the young person — housing, health care, school or work, and important documents like a birth certificate, Social Security card, state ID, and medical records. If that plan was never made, ask the caseworker for it now: it is much harder to get those papers later, after the case is closed.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are in care and near 18, ask your caseworker for your transition plan and for copies of your birth certificate, Social Security card, ID, and medical records. If you already left care and never got them, contact the agency linked in the card below — it can usually still help you get copies.

Staying in care longer: extended foster care

In 43 states, a young person who turns 18 in foster care can voluntarily stay in care — usually until age 21 — by signing a voluntary agreement with the agency. To stay, the young person generally has to be doing one of these: finishing high school or a GED, enrolled in college or job training, in a program that helps them find work, working at least 80 hours a month, or unable to do those things because of a medical condition. Many states also let a young person who left at 18 come back before 21 and re-enter care. Idaho and Maine go to 23, and Massachusetts goes to 22 — or 23 while finishing an associate’s degree or higher. The 7 states without a general program still offer help: aftercare services, housing support, stipends, or a narrower route to stay in placement, and the card below spells out each one.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you turn 18, tell your caseworker you want to stay in extended care and sign the voluntary agreement. If you already left and are still under 21 (under 23 in Idaho and Maine), contact the agency in the card below and ask about re-entering — most states allow it.

Help after you leave care

Leaving care does not cut off all help. A young person who was in foster care at 18 can usually keep Medicaid until age 26, with no income test. The federal Education and Training Voucher (ETV) program offers up to $5,000 a year for college or job training. The Chafee program — called the Independent Living Program in many states — helps with life skills, education, jobs, and housing, often up to age 23. Some states add their own help: tuition waivers at public colleges, Foster Youth to Independence (FYI) housing vouchers, and monthly stipends. The exact mix varies by state, and the card below lists what your state offers.

Okay, so what do I do? If you were in foster care at 18, apply for Medicaid in the former-foster-youth category — you should not need to prove low income. Ask the agency or your old caseworker about ETV money and Chafee services before you pay for any class or training out of pocket.

Relatives and kinship care

When a child has to leave home, every state tries relatives first — grandparents, aunts and uncles, older siblings, and other family, plus close family friends (often called fictive kin). Relatives can usually become licensed foster parents for the child in their family and receive the same foster care payments as any other foster parent; several states now have separate, simpler licensing standards for relatives. Relatives who become the child’s legal guardian can often get guardianship assistance, and some states pay a subsidy until the young person turns 21. If you are a relative raising a child, you do not have to figure this out alone — the agency and the legal aid organization in the card below can walk you through it.

Okay, so what do I do? If a child in your family is entering care, tell the caseworker right away that you are willing to take the child — relatives get priority, but only if the agency knows you exist. Ask about becoming a licensed kinship foster parent so the placement comes with financial support.

Foster care & youth aging out in Texas

How foster care works here: When a court finds a child cannot safely stay home, the Texas Department of Family and Protective Services (DFPS), Child Protective Services, takes managing conservatorship and places the child in a foster home, with kin, or in a residential setting. Foster parents provide a temporary family home and daily care while DFPS works toward reunification, guardianship, or adoption.

When youth age out: A youth ages out of foster care at 18. Texas Family Code sections 263.6015, 263.602, and 263.6021 govern extended foster care and trial independence after 18.

Staying in care longer: Yes — Texas offers Extended Foster Care to age 21, and to age 22 to finish high school, for youth who sign a voluntary foster care agreement. To stay in care the youth must be in school or vocational training, working at least 80 hours a month, in a program to remove barriers to employment, or unable to do those things because of a disability; youth who leave can return to extended care, and a Supervised Independent Living option lets them live on their own while still in care.

Help after leaving care: DFPS Transitional Living Services, including the Preparation for Adult Living (PAL) program, serve youth ages 14 to 23 with life-skills training, a Transitional Living Allowance, and Aftercare Room and Board help. Youth who were in foster care at 18 keep Former Foster Care Children's Medicaid until age 26, can get Education and Training Vouchers (ETV) of up to $5,000 a year until age 23, and can use the state College Tuition and Fee Waiver at Texas public colleges if they enroll before age 27.

Becoming a foster parent: Apply through the Texas Department of Family and Protective Services or a licensed child-placing agency that works with DFPS. Applicants must be at least 21, financially stable, pass criminal history and abuse/neglect background checks for household members age 14 and older, complete a home study and free training, and hold CPR and first aid certification.

Kinship care: Yes — kinship caregivers are the preferred placement in Texas, and relatives or close family friends can care for a child in DFPS conservatorship. Kinship caregivers can get a monthly Relative and Other Designated Caregiver (RODC) payment and one-time placement help, and they can become verified kinship foster homes under separate kinship standards for extra financial support; relatives who take permanent care may qualify for Permanency Care Assistance.

State agency: Texas Department of Family and Protective Services. Start here for foster care, extended care, and aftercare services.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: If you are in care and near 18, ask your caseworker about staying in extended care and get your transition plan and documents now. If you already left care, contact the agency above about re-entering, Medicaid to 26, and Education and Training Voucher money — and get free help from the organization linked above before you sign anything or give up on benefits you may be owed.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) If you are in care and near 18, ask your caseworker about extended foster care and sign the voluntary agreement before your birthday. (2) Get your birth certificate, Social Security card, ID, and medical records while the case is still open. (3) After care ends, apply for Medicaid as a former foster youth and ask about Education and Training Voucher money and Chafee services. (4) If you are a relative, tell the agency you will take the child and ask about licensed kinship care.

Sources: the official state child welfare agencies, state statutes, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. In 43 states a young person can stay in foster care past 18 — usually to 21, to 23 in Idaho and Maine; Delaware, Iowa, Kansas, Mississippi, Montana, Utah, and Wisconsin have no general extended foster care program to 21, but offer other help as described above. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Timeshare exit in Texas

A timeshare can feel like a trap: the maintenance fees keep rising, you cannot use the weeks you bought, and the resort will not take it back. Getting out is possible, but the route depends on when you bought it. If you just signed, most states give you a short window — usually 3 to 15 days — to cancel the purchase for a full refund, no reason needed. In 45 states that window exists by law; in 5 states (Illinois, Kansas, Montana, North Dakota, and Wyoming) there is no statutory cooling-off right at all, and your contract is all you have. After the window closes, the realistic exits are a deed-back to the resort, a resale or giveaway, or — as a last resort — stopping payments and facing foreclosure. And the exit industry is full of scams: any company that demands a large fee up front to sell or transfer your timeshare, especially one that claims a buyer is already waiting, is a red flag. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

The cooling-off window: your best exit, and it is short

Almost every state that regulates timeshares gives a new buyer a brief right to cancel — the rescission or cooling-off period. It runs from 72 hours (Indiana) to 15 days (Alaska), and most states land at 5, 7, or 10 days. The clock usually starts when you sign, or when you receive the disclosure documents, whichever is later. Cancel inside the window and you get a full refund. Miss it by a day and the right is gone — there is no second window for regret or rising fees. Five states have no statutory window at all, and a few more only give one in narrow cases (Ohio’s 3-business-day right, for example, applies only if the sale was a home solicitation sale). Check your contract too: it must disclose the cancellation right, and some contracts give you more time than the statute does.

Okay, so what do I do? Find the date you signed and the date you received the disclosure documents, then count the days against your state’s window in the card below. If you are still inside it, cancel today — do not wait for the resort to call you back.

How to cancel in time

Cancellation must be in writing, sent to the developer at the address in your contract, before the deadline runs out. In most states a mailed notice counts on the postmark date, so certified mail with return receipt requested is the safest way to prove you cancelled on time. Some states also allow hand delivery or email. Keep a copy of the notice and the mailing receipt, and expect your refund within 15 to 45 days depending on the state — the developer must return all the money you paid. The right to cancel cannot be waived in most states, so a contract clause that says otherwise does not bind you.

Okay, so what do I do? Write a short cancellation letter today, name the contract and the date you signed, and send it by certified mail to the address in your contract. Photograph the letter and the receipt, and keep both. If the refund does not arrive on time, file a complaint with the agency linked in the card below.

After the window closes: your real options

Once the cooling-off period ends, there is no legal right to cancel just because you changed your mind. The realistic exits, in order of safety, are: (1) a deed-back or surrender — ask the resort in writing to take the timeshare back, and get any release in writing before you stop paying; many resorts run such programs quietly. (2) Resale or giveaway — you can sell or give the timeshare away, but only after the loan is paid off and the fees are current, and resale prices are usually very low, sometimes near zero. (3) Stop paying — the resort can foreclose and report the missed payments, which can damage your credit for years, so talk to a lawyer before you do this. Paying off the loan first makes every other option easier.

Okay, so what do I do? Write to the resort and ask about a deed-back or surrender program — put it in writing and keep the reply. If they say no, ask again in a few months; programs change. Do not sign anything that promises an exit for a fee until you have checked the company with your attorney general.

Exit scams: the upfront-fee trap

The timeshare exit business is one of the most scam-ridden corners of consumer law. The classic pattern: a company cold-calls you, claims a buyer is already waiting for your timeshare at a high price, and demands a large fee up front — thousands of dollars — before any sale. Once you pay, the buyer never appears and the company disappears. A legitimate resale broker is paid a commission only after a sale actually closes. Several states ban resale brokers from charging advance fees at all. Other scams sell you a worthless “transfer” that never actually moves the deed out of your name, leaving you still on the hook for the fees.

Okay, so what do I do? Never pay a large fee in advance for a promised sale or transfer. Check any exit company with your attorney general or consumer protection office before you sign or pay, and get any promise in writing. If a company already took your money and did nothing, file a complaint with the agency in the card below and report it to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov.

Timeshare exit in Texas

Cooling-off window: Until midnight of the sixth day after the later of the day you sign and receive a copy of the contract or the day you receive the timeshare disclosure statement (Tex. Prop. Code § 221.041).

How to cancel: Cancel by hand-delivering written notice to the developer, or by sending it by prepaid U.S. mail or an overnight carrier to the developer or its agent for service of process, using the address in your contract. Keep proof of mailing or delivery, and expect a refund within 30 days of the developer receiving your notice.

After the window closes: After the rescission period, Texas law gives no second right to cancel just because of regret or rising fees, so ask the resort in writing about a deed-back or surrender program and get any release in writing. You can also try to resell or transfer the timeshare once the loan is paid off and fees are current, though resale prices are usually very low. If you stop paying, the lender can foreclose and unpaid fees can be sent to collections, which can hurt your credit.

Scam warning: Be wary of any company that charges a large upfront fee to sell or transfer your timeshare, especially one that claims a buyer is already waiting. A common scam takes the upfront fee and never sells anything, so never pay in advance for a promised sale.

State law: Texas Timeshare Act, Tex. Prop. Code Chapter 221

Where to complain: Texas Attorney General, Consumer Protection Division. File a complaint there if a resort or exit company misled you or took your money and did nothing.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: Check the dates against the cooling-off window above. If you are still inside it, cancel in writing today by certified mail. If the window has closed, ask the resort in writing about a deed-back, and get free help from the organization linked above before you stop paying or sign any exit contract.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Check the dates: are you still inside your state’s cooling-off window? If yes, cancel in writing today by certified mail. (2) If the window has closed, ask the resort in writing about a deed-back or surrender program. (3) Never pay a large fee in advance to any exit or resale company — check it with your attorney general first. (4) If a company took your money and did nothing, file a complaint with the agency above and report it to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov.

Sources: the official state timeshare statutes, state attorneys general and consumer protection agencies, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. In 45 states a new timeshare purchase can be cancelled within a short statutory window (72 hours to 15 days); Illinois, Kansas, Montana, North Dakota, and Wyoming have no statutory cooling-off right. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Life insurance claim denials in Texas

When a life insurance company denies a claim, the family that was counting on that money is left with a denial letter and a deadline. The denial is not always the last word: insurers deny claims for reasons the law does not allow, and every state gives you a way to push back. Three rules shape almost every case. First, the insurer has only two years — in all 50 states — to challenge the application for misstatements; after that, the policy is incontestable. Second, a missed premium does not end a policy right away: most states give a 30-day or one-month grace period, and California and Connecticut give at least 60 days. Third, 40 states let you sue your own insurer for bad faith if it denies a claim without a reasonable basis; the other 10 have no standalone bad-faith lawsuit, though most still ban unfair claim practices or offer a statutory remedy. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

Why life insurance claims get denied

The most common lawful reasons are a material misstatement on the application discovered within the two-year contestability period, the policy having lapsed for nonpayment after the grace period ran out, suicide within the policy’s exclusion period (usually the first one or two years — listed among the common reasons in 38 states), and a death that falls under a coverage exclusion in the policy. A death inside the contestability period alone is not a lawful reason to deny — the insurer needs actual proof the misstatement mattered to issuing the policy. And some denials are simply wrong: the insurer misread the policy, lost paperwork, or applied an exclusion that does not fit the facts. A denial letter that quotes no policy language, or that leans on a misstatement the insurer cannot show was material, is worth challenging.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask the insurer in writing for the exact reason for the denial and the exact policy language it relied on, and keep a copy of everything. Get the full policy — not just the summary — and check the denial against it yourself. Do not accept a phone-only explanation; a written denial with the policy language is what the regulator and a lawyer will want to see.

The two-year contestability period

In all 50 states, a life insurance policy becomes incontestable after it has been in force for two years during the insured’s lifetime. During those two years the insurer can void the policy if it finds a material misstatement on the application — something that mattered to its decision to issue the policy, like a hidden serious illness. After the two years run, the insurer cannot contest the policy over application statements at all, except for nonpayment of premiums — and in some states, fraud or violations of the policy’s military-service conditions. California is notable: it has no fraud exception once the two years have run. Each increase in the death benefit usually starts its own two-year period from the date of the increase.

Okay, so what do I do? Work out the dates: when was the policy issued, and when did the insured die? If more than two years passed, the insurer generally cannot deny over the application — say so in writing, and cite your state’s rule in the card below. If the death was inside the two years, the question is whether the misstatement was actually material; a small error that would not have changed the insurer’s decision is not enough.

The grace period: a missed premium does not always end the policy

Every state requires a grace period after a premium due date, during which the policy stays in full force — so if the insured dies during the grace period, the insurer still pays the benefit, minus the overdue premium. In most states the grace period is 30 days or one month; California and Connecticut give at least 60 days. California also requires the insurer to mail a lapse notice at least 30 days before the policy can end for nonpayment. The grace period only helps if the policy was still inside it when the insured died — once it runs out without payment, the policy lapses, and the insurer can deny on that ground.

Okay, so what do I do? Find the premium due date and the date of death, and count the days between them against your state’s grace period in the card below. Ask the insurer for its payment records — a payment the insurer received but did not post on time still counts. If the policy had an automatic premium loan or a nonforfeiture option, ask whether that kept the policy in force.

Can you sue the insurer for bad faith?

In 40 states, yes. If the insurer denied the claim without a reasonable basis — and knew it had no basis, or recklessly disregarded that fact — you can sue for bad faith and recover damages beyond the policy benefits, sometimes including emotional-distress damages and, for oppressive, fraudulent, or malicious conduct, punitive damages. In 10 states — Delaware, Kansas, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, New York, Oregon, Vermont, and Virginia — there is no standalone bad-faith lawsuit against your own insurer. That does not mean you have no remedy there: most of those states ban unfair claim settlement practices, which the insurance regulator enforces, and Minnesota has a statutory remedy that can add up to $250,000 in costs plus up to $100,000 in attorney’s fees. Everywhere, the ordinary remedy is a breach-of-contract suit for the policy benefits themselves.

Okay, so what do I do? File a complaint with your state insurance regulator first — it costs nothing, and the regulator can press the insurer for an explanation and sanction a company that broke the law. Keep a dated log of every call and letter, because a bad-faith case turns on what the insurer knew and when. And talk to a lawyer before any deadline in the policy runs out: policies often set their own time limit for suing, and it can be shorter than the state’s.

Life insurance claim denials in Texas

The two-year rule: Two years from the date of issue, while the insured is alive. After two years the policy is incontestable except for nonpayment of premiums (Tex. Ins. Code § 1101.006). A 2025 law (H.B. 3960, effective for policies delivered or renewed on or after January 1, 2026) clarifies that within the two years the insurer may rescind only for a material misrepresentation that affects the risk assumed, and must refund premiums.

Grace period: At least one month (31 days) for each premium after the first, during which the policy stays in force. If the insured dies during the grace period, the overdue premium is deducted from the payout (Tex. Ins. Code § 1101.005).

Common denial reasons: The insurer may deny for a material misstatement on the application during the first two years, for a lapsed policy after premiums went unpaid, or for a death excluded by the policy, such as suicide within the exclusion period. After two years, the insurer must pay unless premiums were not paid or the applicant committed fraud.

Bad faith: Yes. Texas allows a bad-faith lawsuit when an insurer denies without a reasonable basis, and the Insurance Code adds statutory remedies: up to three times actual damages for a knowing violation (Tex. Ins. Code § 541.152) and 18% annual interest on late-paid claims (Tex. Ins. Code § 542.058).

How to appeal: Ask the insurer in writing for the reason for the denial and the policy language behind it, and send any missing proof. Texas prompt-payment law requires the insurer to acknowledge your claim within 15 days, accept or deny it within 15 business days after receiving everything it needs, and pay within 5 business days of accepting (Tex. Ins. Code §§ 542.055–542.057). If the denial stands, you can sue — your policy cannot cut the time to sue to less than two years after the claim arose (Tex. Ins. Code § 1101.053).

How to complain: Talk to the insurance company first — many problems are fixed that way. If not, file a complaint with the Texas Department of Insurance (TDI) through its online complaint system, attaching the policy, denial letter, and supporting papers. TDI sends the complaint to the company, requires an answer, and investigates whether the law was broken, but it cannot force the company to pay your claim.

State insurance regulator: Texas Department of Insurance. File a complaint there if the insurer will not reconsider — it is free, and the regulator can investigate and sanction a company that broke the law.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: Get the denial in writing with the policy language it relies on. Check the dates against the two-year rule and the grace period above. File a complaint with the regulator linked above, and get free help from the organization linked above before any deadline in the policy runs out.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Get the denial in writing, with the exact policy language the insurer relied on. (2) Check the dates: was the death more than two years after the policy was issued, or inside the grace period after a missed premium? Either can defeat the denial. (3) Ask the insurer in writing to reconsider, with any supporting evidence. (4) File a complaint with your state insurance regulator — it is free. (5) If the insurer still will not pay, get free legal help before any deadline in the policy runs out.

Sources: the official state insurance statutes, state insurance departments, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. In all 50 states a life insurance policy becomes incontestable two years after issue; 40 states recognize a first-party bad-faith claim; California and Connecticut give at least a 60-day grace period. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Breaking a lease early, roommate disputes, and rent control in Texas

Leaving a lease early is usually a money question, not a permission question: you can almost always leave, but the question is how much rent you still owe. In 39 states the landlord has to make reasonable efforts to find a new tenant, so you usually owe rent only until someone else moves in. In 3 states — Arkansas, Pennsylvania, and Vermont — the landlord has no duty to re-rent at all. In 3 more the duty applies only sometimes, and in 5 the law is unsettled. A few situations let you leave without paying for the rest of the term at all: military orders, domestic violence, and a home that is unsafe to live in. And rent control is rare: only 3 states cap rent increases statewide — California, Oregon, and Washington — while 9 have local rent rules and 38 have none. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

Leaving early: what you actually owe

When you break a lease, the landlord’s claim is usually the rent for the rest of the term — minus whatever the landlord collects by re-renting, and minus any early-termination fee the lease sets instead. Some leases charge a flat fee, often one to two months’ rent, and that fee is usually enforceable if you signed it. What the landlord cannot do in most states is sit on an empty apartment and bill you for every month until the term ends: the duty to mitigate — to make reasonable efforts to re-rent — cuts the bill off when a new tenant moves in. The landlord does not have to show your unit before its other empty units, and it can charge a new tenant a different rent if the market changed.

Okay, so what do I do? Read your lease first — look for an early-termination clause and its fee. Then give written notice, keep a copy, and keep paying rent until the day you leave so the landlord cannot add late fees to the bill. Take dated photos of the empty, clean unit when you hand back the keys. If the landlord sues for the rest of the term, the re-renting question in your state’s card below is the first thing to raise.

Ways to leave without paying for the rest of the term

Military service. Active-duty service members can end a lease under the federal Servicemembers Civil Relief Act, 50 U.S.C. 3955, with deployment or permanent-change-of-station orders — the lease ends 30 days after the next rent due date, and no early-termination fee applies. Domestic violence. Nearly every state lets a victim of domestic violence, sexual assault, or stalking end a lease early with written notice and proof such as a protection order or police report; the exact deadline and paperwork vary by state. Unlivable home. If the landlord will not fix serious health or safety problems after written notice — no heat, no water, sewage, vermin, a broken lock — the tenant may be able to leave and claim constructive eviction, though the notice period and the proof required differ by state. Landlord harassment. Lockouts and utility shutoffs are illegal in most states and can support ending the lease. Some states add more exits, such as a senior moving into care or a job transfer — see your state’s card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Put everything in writing: the problem, the date, and what you asked the landlord to do, and keep copies of every notice and reply. For domestic violence, bring your proof to the landlord with the notice — do not wait for the next incident. For unlivable conditions, report the problem to the local housing or code inspector as well as the landlord, so there is an official record. Get free legal help from the organization in your state’s card below before you stop paying rent — stopping payment first can turn a strong exit into an eviction case.

Roommates: who owes what when someone leaves

When two or more people sign one lease, each of them is usually liable for the whole rent, not just their share — that is called joint and several liability, and it is the rule in nearly every state. If your roommate stops paying or moves out, the landlord can collect the full rent from you, and you would have to chase the roommate separately. One roommate leaving does not end the lease for the others, and adding or removing a name from the lease needs the landlord’s written agreement. A tenant cannot evict a roommate — only the landlord can end a tenancy, through court. If you are the one leaving, get the landlord’s written release, or you can still be on the hook for rent after you’re gone.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you move in together, write a simple roommate agreement: who pays what, what happens if someone leaves early, and who covers a missing share. If a roommate leaves, tell the landlord in writing right away and ask — in writing — for a new lease or a replacement roommate, and keep paying the full rent on time so the landlord has no reason to evict everyone. If a roommate will not pay, the free legal help in your state’s card below can tell you whether small claims court against the roommate is worth it.

Rent control: where it exists and where it does not

Only three states cap rent increases statewide: California (5% plus inflation, max 10% a year), Oregon (7% plus inflation, max 10%), and Washington (7% plus inflation, max 10%, since May 2025). Nine more states have local rent rules — for example New York City’s rent stabilization, Montgomery County in Maryland, Portland in Maine, St. Paul in Minnesota, and fair rent commissions in Connecticut. The other 38 states have no rent control at all, and most of them ban cities from creating it. Even where a cap exists, it usually does not cover new buildings, single-family homes, or small landlords, and the landlord can still raise the rent between tenants or when a lease renews within the cap.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your city’s rules, not just the state’s — the cap that matters is usually the local one. If an increase looks too big, ask the landlord in writing which cap it falls under and keep the notice. In fair-rent-commission states, the commission can hear a complaint that an increase is excessive. And remember: a landlord cannot raise the rent in the middle of a fixed-term lease unless the lease itself allows it.

Breaking a lease in Texas

Early-termination fee: No state cap — any early-termination fee must be in the lease. A landlord cannot charge both a fee and the full remaining rent without trying to re-rent the unit.

Must the landlord try to re-rent? Yes — the landlord must make reasonable efforts to re-rent the place, so you usually owe rent only until a new tenant moves in, not for the whole rest of the term. (Tex. Prop. Code § 91.006)

Legal ways out in this state:

  • military (SCRA) — active-duty service members can end the lease with written notice and orders under the SCRA and Tex. Prop. Code § 92.017; it ends 30 days after the next rent due date.
  • domestic violence — victims of family violence can end the lease with a protective order or provider documentation plus 30 days' written notice (no advance notice needed if the abuser is a co-tenant), and victims of sexual assault or stalking have a parallel right (Tex. Prop. Code §§ 92.016, 92.0161).
  • uninhabitable conditions — if the landlord fails to repair a condition that affects health or safety after proper written notice, the tenant may end the lease (Tex. Prop. Code §§ 92.052, 92.056).

Roommates: All tenants who sign are jointly responsible for the full rent, and one leaving does not end the others' duty to pay. A tenant may not sublet without the landlord's prior consent (Tex. Prop. Code § 91.005). Only the landlord can evict a roommate, through court, and changing names on the lease needs the landlord's agreement.

Rent control: No. No statewide cap; Tex. Local Gov't Code § 214.902 bars local rent control except a temporary disaster emergency approved by the governor.

State tenant-rights page: Tenant rights in Texas.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: Read your lease for an early-termination clause and fee before you give notice. Give notice in writing, keep a copy, keep paying rent until you leave, and photograph the clean, empty unit when you hand back the keys. If the landlord claims rent for the rest of the term, the re-renting rule above is the first thing to raise — and get free help from the organization linked above before you stop paying anything.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Read the lease for an early-termination clause and fee. (2) Give written notice and keep a copy. (3) If the home is unsafe or you are leaving for domestic violence or military orders, gather your proof and give notice with it. (4) Keep paying rent until you leave, photograph the empty unit, and hand back the keys in writing. (5) If the landlord bills you for the rest of the term, check the re-renting rule in your state’s card above and get free legal help before you pay or stop paying.

Sources: the official state landlord-tenant statutes, state attorneys general and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Thirty-nine states require the landlord to make reasonable efforts to re-rent; Arkansas, Pennsylvania, and Vermont do not; Kentucky, Missouri, and Tennessee apply the duty only sometimes; and Georgia, Massachusetts, Mississippi, New Hampshire, and South Dakota are unsettled. Only California, Oregon, and Washington cap rent increases statewide. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Digital assets after death in Texas

When someone dies, their email, photos, cloud files, social media, and online accounts do not just open up for the family. Federal privacy law and each company’s terms of service usually block access — even for the executor named in the will. Most states have fixed this with a law called the Revised Uniform Fiduciary Access to Digital Assets Act (RUFADAA): 46 states have adopted it in some form. Delaware and Louisiana have their own earlier laws instead, and Massachusetts and Oklahoma have no RUFADAA law at all. The law draws one line that matters more than any other: a family member can usually get a catalogue of an account — who emailed whom, when — but not the content of the messages unless the person consented while alive or a court orders it. And the person who plans ahead decides everything: what you set up in an online tool beats your will, and your will beats the company’s terms of service. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What the family can actually get: catalogue vs. content

Under RUFADAA, a fiduciary — the executor, trustee, agent under a power of attorney, or conservator — can get two kinds of things. First, the catalogue of electronic communications: who the person communicated with, when, and at what address — the envelope, not the letter. Second, other digital assets: photos, files, documents, and online accounts. The content of emails and private messages is different: it is disclosed only if the user consented while alive — through an online tool or in a will, trust, or power of attorney — or if a court orders it. That split comes from the federal Stored Communications Act, which bars a provider from handing over message content without the user’s consent or a court order.

To get anything, the fiduciary usually has to send the custodian — the company holding the account — a written request with a certified death certificate, proof of appointment as executor or administrator, the account identifier, and evidence linking the account to the person. The custodian then has to comply within 60 days in most states — 30 days in Arkansas, Delaware, and Louisiana — and may grant full access, partial access, or a copy of the assets, and may charge a reasonable administrative fee. Massachusetts and Oklahoma have no statutory deadline at all.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are the executor, start by making an inventory of the person’s accounts, devices, and passwords — including crypto wallets and seed phrases — then check whether they set up any online tool below before you write to a single company. A written request with the death certificate and your appointment papers is the key that opens the door; a phone call alone usually is not.

Who wins: the priority ladder

RUFADAA fixes a clear order for who decides what happens to an account. First, the user’s direction through the custodian’s online tool — a setting the user can change or delete at any time. Second, a direction in the user’s will, trust, or power of attorney. Third, the custodian’s terms of service. An online-tool direction beats a contrary direction in a will; a will beats a contrary term of service. If the user left no direction at all, the terms of service govern, and the fiduciary gets only what the law allows by default — the catalogue and other assets, not message content.

That order is exactly why the tools in the next card matter so much: they sit at the top of the ladder. A will that says “give my daughter my email” loses to a Google setting that says something else — so the setting and the will should agree with each other. In Delaware, Louisiana, Massachusetts, and Oklahoma there is no RUFADAA ladder at all, so the platform’s own tools and the estate documents do even more of the work — see your state’s card below.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are planning for yourself, set the online tools first and then write matching language into your will or power of attorney — do not rely on either one alone. If you are settling someone else’s estate, look for an online-tool direction before you assume the will controls.

The three tools to set up while you are alive

Google Inactive Account Manager lets you name trusted contacts and choose what happens after a period of inactivity — who gets which data, or whether the account is deleted. Apple Legacy Contact lets a named person request access to your Apple data with an access key and a death certificate. Meta legacy contact lets a Facebook user name someone to manage a memorialized account, and Instagram memorializes accounts on request with proof of death. These are the most reliable way to arrange access in advance — especially in the four states without RUFADAA, where they are often the only route that works without a court order.

No tool hands over your passwords, and no tool lets the named person log in as you or read your private messages without your consent. They are a controlled handover, not a spare key. Each platform has its own request form and proof requirements, and each will still ask for a death certificate.

Okay, so what do I do? Set all three up today if you use the services — it takes a few minutes each, and it is the single most useful thing on this page. Tell the person you named, and tell them where your access key or instructions are kept. Then put the same authority in your will or power of attorney so the documents agree.

If you are planning for yourself: do it now, on paper too

A password manager shared with a trusted person, a written inventory of accounts kept with your will, and express digital-asset authority in your will, trust, and durable power of attorney will do more than any law can. Some states require the power of attorney to expressly grant digital-asset authority — a general grant may not be enough — and Wisconsin even recognizes a separate written notarized consent instrument for message content. Crypto is the sharpest edge: if nobody has the seed phrase or private key, the coins are gone for good, and no court order can recover them.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Make the inventory — every account, device, password manager, and crypto wallet, with seed phrases stored somewhere safe at home. (2) Set up the three platform tools above. (3) Put express digital-asset authority in your will, trust, and power of attorney, naming who may access which accounts. (4) Tell that person where the inventory is. Do not put passwords in the will itself — a will becomes a public court record.

Digital assets in Texas

The law: Texas has adopted RUFADAA as the Texas Revised Uniform Fiduciary Access to Digital Assets Act in 2017 (Senate Bill 1193, 85th Legislature). It is codified at Texas Estates Code chapter 2001, sections 2001.001 et seq., effective September 1, 2017.

What a fiduciary can get: A fiduciary — personal representative, guardian, agent under a power of attorney, or trustee — can get the catalogue of electronic communications and other digital assets. The content of electronic communications is disclosed only if the user consented to it in an online tool or in a will, trust, or power of attorney, or if a court orders it. The custodian must comply within 60 days after receiving the required proof, may give full access, partial access, or a copy of the records, and may charge a reasonable administrative fee. It may require a written request, a death certificate, letters of appointment, a copy of the will, trust, or power of attorney, and an account identifier or evidence linking the account to the user.

Who decides: The user can direct disclosure through a custodian's online tool, naming a designated recipient, and can also allow or forbid disclosure in a will, trust, power of attorney, or other record. The priority order is: first the online tool, then the will, trust, or power of attorney, then the custodian's terms of service. Texas also added Estates Code section 752.1145, so a statutory durable power of attorney can expressly grant digital-asset authority.

Terms of service: The custodian's terms of service cannot override a direction the user gave through an online tool or in a will, trust, or power of attorney. If the user left no direction, the terms of service can limit or eliminate the fiduciary's access, and the fiduciary then gets only what the statute allows without the user's consent to content.

The statute: Texas Estates Code chapter 2001, sections 2001.001 et seq.; Texas Estates Code section 752.1145

Texas put its digital-assets law in its own title of the Estates Code, Title 4, Digital Assets, and in the same 2017 bill added express digital-asset authority to the statutory durable power of attorney form.

Who to contact: Texas Attorney General, Consumer Protection Division.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

What to do: First, make an inventory of accounts, devices, and crypto wallets and keys. Second, check whether the person set up an online tool such as Google Inactive Account Manager, Apple Legacy Contact, or a Meta legacy contact. Third, gather the will, trust, or power of attorney that names the fiduciary and gives digital-asset authority. Fourth, send each custodian a written request with a death certificate and proof of appointment. Fifth, if the custodian refuses or does not answer within 60 days, ask the probate court for an order directing disclosure.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) If you are the executor, inventory the accounts and devices first, then check for an online-tool or legacy-contact designation before writing to any company. (2) Send each custodian a written request with a certified death certificate and proof of appointment — and note the deadline: 60 days in most states, 30 days in Arkansas, Delaware, and Louisiana. (3) If a custodian refuses or the content of messages is needed, ask the court in your state’s card above for an order. (4) If you are planning for yourself, set up the three platform tools and put express digital-asset authority in your will and power of attorney today.

Sources: the official state digital-assets statutes, state attorneys general and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Forty-six states have adopted the Revised Uniform Fiduciary Access to Digital Assets Act; Delaware and Louisiana have their own earlier laws, and Massachusetts and Oklahoma have no RUFADAA law. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Service animals & disability access in Texas

A service animal is a dog trained to do work or perform tasks for a person with a disability — guiding, alerting, pulling a wheelchair, interrupting a PTSD episode, and more. Under the federal Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA), a business may ask you only two questions about it, may never demand papers, a vest, or a demonstration, and may never charge extra. An emotional support animal is not a service animal and has no right to enter public places — in any state. State law adds its own layers: 37 states punish faking a service animal (26 as a crime, 11 with a civil fine or forfeiture) and 13 have no specific penalty at all. 28 states also protect service animals still in training. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What counts as a service animal — and what doesn’t
