Under the ADA, a service animal is a dog individually trained to do work or perform tasks directly related to a person’s disability. The task is what matters, not the breed, the size, or a vest. Miniature horses can also qualify in limited cases, as a reasonable modification. Some states write a wider definition of their own — Illinois and Michigan, for example, name miniature horses in state law, and a few states cover other trained animals — but the ADA floor is dogs.

What does not count: an emotional support animal, a therapy animal, or a comfort animal. If the animal only provides comfort, companionship, or crime deterrence, it is not a service animal, no matter what a vest, a certificate, or a letter bought online says. There is no official service-animal registry, no required ID card, and no certification a business can ask to see.

Okay, so what do I do? If your dog is trained to do a specific task for your disability, that is what makes it a service animal — be ready to name the task, not to show a document. If your animal only comforts you, do not present it as a service animal in a store or restaurant; the housing rules below are different.

The two questions — the only ones a business may ask

When it is not obvious what service the animal provides, staff may ask only two questions: (1) Is this a service animal required because of a disability? and (2) What work or task has the animal been trained to perform? They may not ask about your disability, demand medical documentation, require a special ID or vest, ask the animal to demonstrate its task, or charge a fee or deposit. A service animal can be asked to leave only if it is out of control and the handler does not control it, it is not housebroken, or it poses a direct threat — and even then, the person must be allowed back without the animal. You are liable for any damage the animal causes.

Okay, so what do I do? If a business asks for papers or a vest, say plainly: “Under the ADA you may only ask whether this is a service animal and what task it performs. I am not required to show documentation.” Note the date, the place, and who refused you, and contact the agency in your state’s card below — in many states, denying access is itself a crime or a civil violation with a fine.

Emotional support animals: no public access — but housing is different

An emotional support animal (ESA) has no right to enter restaurants, stores, hotels, planes, or other public places, in any state. That is the single most misunderstood rule in this area, and it is the same everywhere: comfort and emotional support are not trained tasks.

Housing is the exception. Under the federal Fair Housing Act and most state fair-housing laws, an ESA can be a reasonable accommodation to a no-pets rule — a landlord must allow the animal if you need it because of a disability, with no pet deposit or extra fee, though you are liable for damage it causes. The landlord may ask for reliable documentation of the disability-related need when it is not obvious. Several states have cracked down on fake ESA letters: Louisiana’s 2024 law requires a real therapeutic relationship, and Kentucky and Indiana fine people who fake ESA documentation in housing.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not buy an online ESA letter or vest and expect it to get your animal into a restaurant — it will not, and in some states presenting one is itself penalized. For housing, ask your treating clinician for documentation of the need, keep it, and make the request to your landlord in writing.

Faking a service animal: the penalties, state by state

37 states punish passing a pet off as a service animal. In 26 it is a crime, usually a misdemeanor — Kansas allows up to a year in jail and a $2,500 fine, New Hampshire up to $2,000 and a year, and Michigan up to 90 days and $500. In 11 it is a civil fine or forfeiture — Wisconsin’s new 2025 law sets $200 for a first violation and $500 after that, and Maine fines up to $1,000 per occurrence. 13 states have no specific penalty at all — Alaska, Connecticut, Delaware, Illinois, Indiana, Maryland, Massachusetts, Mississippi, Nebraska, New Jersey, Ohio, Oregon, and Vermont — though general fraud laws can still apply. Several of these laws are brand new: Georgia’s “Mara Jade Act” and South Dakota’s law both took effect July 1, 2026, and Oklahoma’s took effect November 1, 2025. Montana is the outlier the other way: you can only be charged after a written warning.

Okay, so what do I do? If you see someone faking a service animal, the practical step is to tell the business — it is the business that can ask the animal to leave if it is disruptive. Do not confront the person yourself, and do not buy a fake vest or certificate: in most states that is the offense itself.

Service animals in Texas

What counts: Texas defines a service animal (Tex. Hum. Res. Code 121.002) as a canine specially trained or equipped to do work or perform tasks for a person with a disability and used by a person with a disability — dogs only, with no miniature horses in the state definition. The statute's disability definition is broad and expressly includes mental or physical disabilities, intellectual or developmental disabilities, hearing or speech impairments, visual impairments, deafness, post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and health conditions requiring special ambulatory devices. Animals in training have public access when with an approved trainer.

Going into public places: A person with a disability who has or obtains an assistance animal is entitled to full and equal access to all public facilities, including streets, transportation, aircraft, lodging, government buildings, retail establishments, educational facilities, and food service locations (Tex. Hum. Res. Code 121.002 and 121.003), with no extra charge. A business may ask only the two ADA questions when the need is not obvious — whether the animal is required because of a disability, and what work or task it has been trained to perform — and may not demand proof of certification or training. Texas goes beyond the ADA by providing both criminal penalties and civil remedies for access denial, including a $300 minimum damages presumption.

Emotional support animals: Emotional support animals are not service animals under the ADA and have no public-access rights in Texas. Texas law uses the broader term 'assistance animal,' which includes an animal that provides emotional support alleviating medically identified symptoms of a disability, and that broader term is what gives housing protection — not public-access rights.

Faking a service animal: Misdemeanor under Tex. Hum. Res. Code 121.006, as strengthened by HB 4164 (2023): intentionally or knowingly representing that an animal is an assistance or service animal when it is not specially trained is punishable by a fine of not more than $1,000 and 30 hours of community service for a governmental entity or nonprofit serving persons with disabilities, to be completed within one year. Older sources citing a $300 maximum fine reflect the pre-2023 law.

In housing: A person with a disability who has or obtains an assistance animal is entitled to full and equal access to all housing accommodations and may not be required to pay extra compensation or make a deposit for the animal, though the person is liable for damage the animal does beyond reasonable wear and tear (Tex. Hum. Res. Code 121.003(h)). A landlord may ask for documentation of the disability-related need for an assistance animal where the need is not apparent.

The law: Texas Human Resources Code Chapter 121 (Persons with Disabilities), especially Sections 121.002, 121.003, 121.004, and 121.006

Who to complain to: Texas Workforce Commission, Civil Rights Division.

Texas has one of the most detailed state frameworks: it expressly names PTSD as a qualifying disability, gives service dogs in training public access, and has tiered felony penalties for harming a service animal — up to a third-degree felony for killing one (Tex. Penal Code 42.091). Its 2023 increase of the fake-service-animal fine from $300 to $1,000 is recent, so older guides understate the penalty.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are denied entry, stay calm, state the two-question rule, and write down the date, place, and who refused you — then contact the agency above. If you are a business owner, train staff on the two questions and never ask for papers: that is where most violations happen.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Know the two questions — they are the whole test for a business. (2) Never pay for a service-animal certificate, registry, or vest: none is required, and using one to fake it is a crime in 26 states. (3) If you are denied access, write down the date, place, and who refused you, and contact the agency in your state’s card above. (4) For housing, make the request in writing with your clinician’s documentation. (5) If the animal is a legitimate service animal in training, check your state’s card — 28 states protect trainers too.

Sources: the official state service-animal statutes, state civil-rights agencies, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Several of these laws changed in 2025 and 2026, so the date decides which version applies. Two states — Alabama and Mississippi — have no state human-rights agency, so complaints there go to the U.S. Department of Justice. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Rideshare accidents (Uber & Lyft) in Texas

You were hurt in an Uber or a Lyft — as a passenger, as another driver, or as a pedestrian. Which insurance pays depends on what the rideshare driver was doing in the app when the crash happened: logged off, waiting for a ride, on the way to a pickup, or carrying a passenger. In 39 states the trip-period floor is $1,000,000, but in 11 states the state floor is different — higher in 4 (Minnesota, New Jersey, and Rhode Island at $1,500,000; New York at $1,250,000), lower in 4 (Pennsylvania at $500,000; Kentucky, Maryland, and New Hampshire lower still), split between the drive to the pickup and the ride itself in 2 (Arizona and Texas), and Oregon has no statewide tiered law at all. The deadline to file is also short in most states — 2 years in 25 states and 3 years in 17, and just 1 year in Tennessee. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

The three periods decide whose insurance pays

Every rideshare trip is split into periods, and the coverage changes with each one. App off: the driver is off duty, and only their personal auto policy applies. Period 1 — app on, waiting for a ride: the thinnest coverage, usually $50,000 per person / $100,000 per accident for injuries and $25,000 for property damage. Period 2 — ride accepted, driving to the pickup: the bigger policy switches on. Period 3 — passenger in the car: the same big policy, usually $1,000,000. Periods 2 and 3 are usually covered by one policy, so the moment that matters most is whether a ride had been accepted yet.

Okay, so what do I do? Write down the exact time of the crash and screenshot the ride in the app — the trip status, the driver’s name and photo, and the car’s plate. That screenshot is what proves which period you were in. Report the crash in the app the same day, get medical care even if you feel okay, and keep every bill and receipt.

When the state floor is NOT $1,000,000

Most people have heard “a million dollars” — and in 39 states that is the law once a ride is accepted. But the exceptions are big. Higher: Minnesota, New Jersey, and Rhode Island require $1,500,000 on a trip, and New York requires $1,250,000 plus another $1,250,000 in uninsured-motorist coverage. Lower: Pennsylvania’s trip minimum is only $500,000; New Hampshire’s is $300,000; Kentucky’s statutory floor for a ride is $100,000/$300,000/$50,000; and Maryland’s law never rises above $50,000/$100,000/$25,000 at all — the $1,000,000 you hear about there is the companies’ own policy, not the law. Split: in Arizona the drive to the pickup is $250,000 and only the passenger ride is $1,000,000, and in Texas the en-route period drops to $50,000/$100,000/$25,000 for policies renewed on or after January 1, 2026. Oregon has no statewide tiered law — the tiers come from city rules, with Portland as the model.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not assume the $1,000,000 figure. Check your state’s card below for the real floor — and remember the crash date decides which version of the law applies, because several of these limits changed in 2025 and 2026.

Your own policy probably won’t help — and that’s normal

Most personal auto policies exclude driving for hire. That means the rideshare driver’s own insurer can deny the claim outright once the app was on — and that is exactly what the rideshare policy is for. In nearly every state the rideshare coverage is primary (it pays first, not as a backup) and first-dollar (it pays from the first dollar if the driver’s own coverage lapsed or does not cover the crash). In most states it also does not wait for the personal insurer to deny the claim first.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are the passenger, file the claim with the rideshare company — Uber and Lyft both have in-app crash reporting — and do not let anyone tell you to wait for the driver’s personal insurer. If you are the other driver, get the rideshare driver’s insurance information at the scene, the same as any crash, and report it to your own insurer too.

Uninsured drivers and the deadline

Uninsured and underinsured motorist coverage — the coverage that pays when the driver who hit you has no insurance or not enough — varies more than the liability limits do. Delaware and Vermont require $1,000,000 of it during a ride. California cut theirs from $1,000,000 to $60,000 per person / $300,000 per incident on January 1, 2026. In Louisiana, Uber’s written waiver of that coverage has been upheld in court, so do not count on company UM coverage there. In Tennessee, a company can opt out of it in Period 1, leaving a real gap.

The filing deadline is just as uneven. Most states give you 2 or 3 years from the crash, but Tennessee gives only 1 year, Missouri gives 5, and Minnesota, Maine, and North Dakota give 6. Some states add shorter clocks on top: in New York a no-fault claim must be filed within 30 days of the crash, and claims against a city or other government body often need a written notice in 90 days or less.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not wait to see how you feel. Get checked the same day, keep the records, and find out your state’s deadline from the card below — in Tennessee and in no-fault states, the clock that matters may be weeks, not years.

Rideshare accidents in Texas

Waiting for a ride (Period 1): $50,000 per person / $100,000 per incident for bodily injury or death, $25,000 property damage

On the way to you / passenger in the car (Periods 2–3): $1,000,000 total aggregate liability for death, bodily injury, and property damage for each incident while a rider is in the vehicle (Period 3); under HB 3520, for policies delivered, issued, or renewed on or after January 1, 2026, the en-route period with no rider in the vehicle (Period 2) is covered at the $50,000/$100,000/$25,000 level instead of $1,000,000

Uninsured / underinsured motorist coverage: Uninsured/underinsured motorist coverage is required in both periods where required by Texas Insurance Code section 1952.101, and personal injury protection where required by section 1952.152; Texas UM/UIM is offered coverage that a named insured may reject in writing, so it is not automatic in every policy.

Who pays: The driver or the TNC on the driver's behalf must maintain primary automobile insurance that allows the personal vehicle to be used to transport riders for pay, and the required coverage may come from the driver, the TNC, or both. A personal auto policy may exclude commercial use, in which case the TNC's primary coverage responds rather than the driver's personal insurer. If the app is off, only the driver's personal policy applies.

The law: Texas Occupations Code chapter 2402, Transportation Network Companies (HB 100, 2017), and Texas Insurance Code chapter 1954, sections 1954.051 to 1954.053

Deadline to file: 2 years. Suit must be filed within two years after the cause of action accrues under Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code section 16.003, usually the crash date, with a narrow discovery rule and tolling for minors; a claim against a government entity needs written notice within six months under the Texas Tort Claims Act.

Who regulates rideshare: Texas Department of Licensing and Regulation.

Texas is the notable outlier on Period 2: HB 3520 (2025) moved the en-route, no-rider-in-car period down from $1,000,000 to the $50,000/$100,000/$25,000 level for policies renewed on or after January 1, 2026, so the $1,000,000 tier now applies only once a rider is in the vehicle; Texas also regulates TNCs statewide through the Department of Licensing and Regulation, preempting local rules.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Screenshot the ride and the driver’s details before you leave the scene, report the crash in the app the same day, get medical care, and check the deadline above — in some states the clock that matters is measured in days, not years.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Screenshot the ride, the driver, and the car before you leave the scene. (2) Report the crash in the app the same day. (3) Get medical care even if you feel fine, and keep every bill. (4) Find your state’s deadline in the card above and do not let it pass. (5) If the company or an insurer denies the claim, talk to the free legal aid linked above before you accept it.

Sources: the official state transportation network company statutes, state regulators, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Insurance laws change — several limits in this section changed in 2025 and 2026, so the crash date decides which version applies. Confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Adverse possession & easements in Texas

Someone has been using a piece of your land for years — mowing it, parking on it, driving across it to reach their own place. In every state, long enough use can change the legal picture: in 3 states the clock for taking ownership is only 5 years, in 16 states it is 10 years, and in 15 states it is 20 years. But using land is not the same as owning it — a driveway used for decades can become a permanent right to keep using it, without ever changing who owns the land. And if your own land has no road to it at all, every state has some path to get access. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What is adverse possession?

Adverse possession is how someone can gain ownership of land they do not hold the deed to, by using it as if it were theirs for a long time. The use has to meet five tests in almost every state: actual (they really use it), open and notorious (visible, not hidden), hostile (without the owner’s permission — “hostile” means without permission, not angry), exclusive (they treat it as their own), and continuous (unbroken for the whole period). Failing even one test defeats the claim.

Permission is the killer of these claims: if the owner ever said “go ahead,” the use was not hostile, and the clock never ran. Ownership also does not change by itself — the person claiming the land usually has to go to court and win a case called a quiet title action. And land owned by the government generally cannot be taken this way at all.

Okay, so what do I do? If someone is using your land without permission, do not wait: give them written permission (which stops the clock), put up a fence or a “no trespassing” sign, or talk to a lawyer before the years run out. If you are the one using the land, keep records — photos, tax receipts, dates — and talk to a lawyer before assuming the land is yours.

How long is the clock?

The standard period for taking ownership is 5 years in 3 states (California, Montana, and Nevada), 7 years in 3 (Arkansas, Florida, and Utah), 10 years in 16, 15 years in 8, 18 years in 1 (Colorado), 20 years in 15, 21 years in 2 (Ohio and Pennsylvania), and 30 years in 2 (Louisiana, under its civil-law acquisitive prescription, and New Jersey).

The headline number is rarely the whole story. In many states a flawed deed (called “color of title”) or paying the property taxes can shorten the clock — for example, Texas drops from 10 years to 3 with a deed, and Illinois from 20 to 7 with a deed plus taxes. In other states the taxes are mandatory: California, Montana, Nevada, Utah, New Mexico, Idaho, and Indiana all require the claimant to have paid the taxes, and a claim can fail on that alone. Colorado added a good-faith rule in 2008: a claimant who knew the land was not theirs cannot win, and may have to pay the former owner the land’s value.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your state’s card below for the exact number and the extras that apply — a deed, tax payments, or good faith can change everything. Do not count on the shortest number you have heard; it usually comes with a condition.

Prescriptive easements: use without ownership

A prescriptive easement is a permanent right to keep using someone else’s land for one purpose — a driveway, a path, a road — without owning any of it. The use has to be open, continuous, and without permission for the state’s period, which is 20 years in 20 states, the most common number. Unlike adverse possession, the use does not usually have to be exclusive: the owner can keep using the land too.

In 5 states the easement clock is longer than the ownership clock: Florida (7 years to own, 20 to use), Indiana (10 to own, 20 to use), South Carolina (10 to own, 20 to use), Utah (7 to own, 20 to use), and Virginia (15 to own, 20 to use). So a neighbor can win ownership of a strip faster than they can win a right to drive over it — the two clocks run separately.

Okay, so what do I do? If a neighbor has been crossing your land for years, a written permission letter stops the easement clock — permission can never ripen into a right. If you are the one crossing, keep a record of when the use started and whether anyone ever objected.

Landlocked? Easements by necessity

If your land has no road to it, you are not stuck forever. Every state recognizes some form of easement by necessity: when one owner splits a piece of land and leaves one part with no access, the law implies a right to cross the other part to reach a public road. The usual requirements are that the two parcels were once owned by the same person, and that the need for access existed when they were split.

The easement lasts only while the necessity lasts — if a new road reaches your land later, it can end. Some states also have a statutory route: a landlocked owner can ask a court or county board to lay out a private road, usually paying the neighbor for the strip taken. Louisiana reaches the same result through its civil law: the owner of an “enclosed estate” may claim a right of passage, but must pay the neighbor for it.

Okay, so what do I do? If you cannot reach your own land, start with your deed and the history of how the land was split — that history is what the court looks at. The legal aid or bar referral linked in your state’s card can tell you whether your state’s court route or statutory road process fits.

Adverse possession & easements in Texas

How long to claim ownership: 10 years. Texas has a ladder of periods, not one number. The standard period with no deed at all is 10 years of peaceable possession while cultivating, using, or enjoying the land (capped at 160 acres without a title document). It is 3 years with title or color of title, 5 years with a duly registered deed plus paying the property taxes, and 25 years in special cases (including claims against an owner under a legal disability). Possession must be actual and visible, hostile to the owner's claim, and continuous. Since 2021, the 5-year route does not apply to quitclaim deeds, forged deeds, or deeds signed under a forged power of attorney.

How long for a prescriptive easement: 10 years. A prescriptive easement is gained by at least 10 years of use that is open and notorious, continuous, exclusive, and adverse (without permission) — the same 10-year period as the general adverse possession statute. It gives only the right to keep using the land for that purpose (such as a driveway), not ownership. Texas courts disfavor these claims and scrutinize every element strictly.

Landlocked land: Yes — Texas recognizes easements by necessity, but being landlocked alone is not enough. The claimant must prove three things: the two parcels were once owned by the same person, the access is a true necessity (not a mere convenience), and the necessity existed when the parcels were split (Hamrick v. Ward, 2014). A landlocked owner can also ask the county commissioners court for a public access road under Texas Transportation Code § 251.053.

The law: Texas Civil Practice and Remedies Code, Chapter 16, Subchapter B (Limitations of Real Property Actions) — Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code §§ 16.021–16.034

Where to get help: State Bar of Texas.

Texas has three main regional legal aid programs: Lone Star Legal Aid (Houston and East Texas, 72 counties), Legal Aid of NorthWest Texas (114 counties in North and West Texas), and Texas RioGrande Legal Aid (South Texas). The State Bar's Lawyer Referral & Information Service offers a 30-minute consultation for $20.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? If someone is using your land, act before the clock runs out: written permission, a fence, or a lawyer’s letter. If you need access to your own land, gather your deed and the history of the split, then talk to the legal aid above before any deadline passes.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) If someone uses your land without permission, give written permission or stop the use — do not let years pass silently. (2) If you use a neighbor’s land, write down when it started and keep proof. (3) If your land has no road, pull your deed and the history of the split. (4) For a boundary fight or a claim of ownership, talk to a lawyer before the clock in your state runs out — these cases are won and lost on the details.

Sources: the official state adverse possession and easement statutes, state courts, state bars, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Property laws change — confirm the current law with a local attorney before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Data breach response in Texas

A company loses your personal information — your name with your Social Security number, your bank account, a password. Every state has a law that says how fast the company must tell you, whether it must also tell the state attorney general, and whether you can sue. The clock is short: in 6 states the company has 30 days, in 11 states 45 days, in 5 states 60 days — and in 27 states there is no fixed number of days, just “without unreasonable delay.” Only 5 states make the company pay for credit monitoring. And only 4 states clearly let you sue under the breach law itself. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

How fast must they tell you?

In 27 states the law says the company must notify you “without unreasonable delay” — no fixed number of days. In 6 states the deadline is 30 days (California, Colorado, Florida, Maine, New York, and Washington), in 11 states it is 45 days, and in 5 states it is 60 days (Connecticut, Delaware, Louisiana, South Dakota, and Texas). New Hampshire’s law uses its own words: “as soon as possible.”

In most states the clock starts when the company discovers the breach, not the day the breach happened. A company that finds a breach late can still be within the law — which is why the letter can arrive months after your information was taken. Some states let the company delay notice while law enforcement investigates. Check your state’s card below for the exact deadline and what starts the clock.

Okay, so what do I do? The day the letter arrives, start the clock on your own protection: freeze your credit (see the card below) and change the password on any account that used the same one.

Does the attorney general get told?

In 13 states the company must tell the attorney general about every breach. In 23 states it only has to tell the attorney general about bigger breaches — usually when 250, 500, or 1,000 residents are affected, depending on the state. In 14 states (Alaska, Georgia, Kansas, Kentucky, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Nevada, New Jersey, Ohio, Tennessee, West Virginia, Wisconsin, and Wyoming) the breach law does not require the company to tell the attorney general at all.

Attorney general notice does not make your letter arrive faster — it is the state’s enforcement hook. It is also how the attorney general learns about breaches and, in some states, posts them publicly. If you never got a letter but think your information was exposed, your attorney general’s consumer-protection office (linked in your state’s card below) is the place to report it.

Okay, so what do I do? If the company never told you and you learn about the breach another way, report it to your attorney general’s office. Keep the letter if you got one — it is your proof of when you were told.

Credit monitoring and freezes

Only 5 states make the company pay for credit monitoring when your information is exposed: California, Connecticut, Delaware, Massachusetts, and Pennsylvania. Rhode Island requires it only when a state or city agency is breached — private companies are not required to provide it. In the other 44 states the company may offer monitoring, but the law does not make it.

The protection that does not depend on the company is a credit freeze. In every state you can freeze your credit with each of the three credit bureaus for free — that right comes from federal law. A freeze stops anyone from opening a new account in your name. You can lift it temporarily when you need credit yourself. A fraud alert is the lighter option: it tells lenders to check your identity before opening an account, and it lasts one year (or seven years for an extended alert).

Okay, so what do I do? Freeze your credit at all three bureaus — it is free, and it is the single strongest step you can take. Your state’s card below has the details for your state.

Can you sue the company?

In only 4 states does the breach law clearly let you sue the company yourself: Hawaii, Louisiana, Tennessee, and Washington. In 12 states the right is limited — for example, California lets you sue only under its separate privacy law, and only for certain kinds of breaches. In 34 states there is no private right under the breach law at all: only the attorney general can enforce it.

No private right under the breach law does not always mean no lawsuit. You may still have a claim under a different law — a consumer-protection law, or a negligence claim — but that is harder to prove and depends on your state’s courts. If you lost money because of a breach, that is the question to take to a lawyer, not to guess at yourself.

Okay, so what do I do? If you lost money, save every document — the breach letter, bank statements showing the loss, anything you spent fixing it. Then talk to the legal aid linked in your state’s card, or a consumer lawyer, before any deadline passes.

Data breach response in Texas

How fast must they tell you: 60 days. The company must tell you without unreasonable delay and no later than 60 days after the date it determines the breach occurred. It may take extra time to figure out the scope of the breach and fix its systems, or if law enforcement asks for a delay.

Does the attorney general get told: It depends — only for larger breaches. The company must notify the Attorney General only if the breach involves at least 250 Texas residents. Since September 1, 2023 (SB 768), that AG notice is due as soon as practicable and within 30 days — faster than the 60-day deadline for telling you — and must be filed electronically through the AG's website.

Credit monitoring: Not required by the breach law.

Can you sue: No.

Freezing your credit: You can place a free security freeze on your credit file at Equifax, Experian, and TransUnion — federal law makes freezes free, and you must place one separately at each bureau. A freeze blocks new accounts from being opened in your name until you lift it.

The law: Identity Theft Enforcement and Protection Act — Tex. Bus. & Com. Code § 521.053

Where to report a breach: Texas Attorney General.

Texas posts breach reports publicly: the Attorney General must publish a listing of the breach notifications it receives on its website, so you can check whether a breach affecting you was reported.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Start with the attorney general’s office linked above if you were never told about a breach or the company is not answering. Freeze your credit, change reused passwords, and keep the breach letter. If you lost money, talk to the legal aid above before any deadline passes.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Freeze your credit at all three bureaus — it is free. (2) Change the password on any account that used the same password as the breached one. (3) Read the breach letter carefully for what was exposed and what the company is offering. (4) If the company never told you, report the breach to your attorney general’s office. (5) If you lost money, save every document and talk to legal aid before any deadline passes.

Sources: the official state breach-notification statutes, attorneys general, and legal aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Breach laws change — confirm the current law with your attorney general’s office before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Zoning, variances & land use in Texas

Zoning decides what you can build on your land and how you can use it — how close to the lot line, how tall, and whether a home, a shop, or a rental is allowed there at all. When the rules block what you need, a variance is the way to ask for an exception. When your use was legal before the rules changed, that is a nonconforming use, and it usually gets to stay — with limits. And when the board says no, you can usually appeal, but the deadline is short: 30 days in 28 states, and as little as 10 days in Mississippi and Texas. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What a variance is — and what it is not

A variance is permission to break one zoning rule for one lot — usually a dimensional rule, like a setback, a height limit, or a lot-size minimum. It is not a rezoning, and it is not a permit to use the land for something the zone forbids: a use variance — asking to run a business in a residential zone, for example — is barred outright in many states, including Minnesota, where the statute says a city may not permit by variance any use that is not allowed in the district.

The test you must meet depends on the state. In 25 states you must show unnecessary hardship — that the rule, applied to your lot, causes a hardship because of something unique about the property, not something you caused. In California and Minnesota the test is the looser practical difficulties standard. In the other 23 states there is no single statewide test: the county or city ordinance sets its own standard, so the exact words that decide your case are in your local ordinance, not in a state statute.

Okay, so what do I do? Read your state’s test in the card below, then get your local zoning ordinance and find the variance section — that is the document the board will actually apply to your lot.

How it usually works

You apply to the local board of adjustment (sometimes called the zoning board of appeals), which holds a public hearing and must make written findings — a reasoned decision, not just a vote. The board can attach conditions, like a fence or a limit on hours, to protect neighbors. A variance runs with the land, so it usually survives when the property is sold, but it can be lost if you do not use it or do not follow its conditions.

If the board denies you, appeal fast. The deadline to appeal a variance decision is 30 days in 28 states. It is 21 days in Oregon and Washington and 10 days in Mississippi and Texas. In 5 states (Alaska, Arkansas, Minnesota, New Mexico, and Oklahoma) no deadline is stated in the statute itself, so the deadline comes from the local ordinance or the court rules — check both before you assume you have time.

Okay, so what do I do? Mark the deadline the day the decision is issued, not the day you read it. Miss it and the court will not hear you, no matter how good your case is.

The use that was there first

If your home, shop, or building was legal when it was built and the zoning changed later, it is a nonconforming use — often called grandfathered. In 27 states that use is protected and may continue. In 4 states (California, Louisiana, Oklahoma, and South Carolina) protection is limited — for example, the use may have to end after a set time or may not be expanded. In the other 19 states it depends on local law.

Protection is not forever. In most places a nonconforming use ends if you abandon it, if you change it to a different use, or if the building is badly damaged or destroyed and you do not rebuild within the time the ordinance allows. Expanding a nonconforming use — a bigger building, more units, longer hours — usually needs its own approval.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you buy a property with a nonconforming use, or before you stop using it for a while, ask the zoning office in writing whether the use is protected and what would end that protection. Get the answer before you spend money.

Traps: do not get caught by these

Assuming a variance lets you use the land for a forbidden purpose. It almost never does — a variance bends a dimensional rule, not the allowed-use list. Assuming your neighbor’s variance means yours is automatic. Each lot is decided on its own facts, and a hardship you created yourself — building first, then asking — counts against you in most states. Assuming the city or county can zone everywhere. In Texas, a city cannot zone land in its extraterritorial jurisdiction, and Texas counties have no general zoning power at all. Assuming local zoning is the only approval. In Vermont, a large project also needs a separate state Act 250 land-use permit, judged against ten criteria. Assuming you have months to appeal. Ten days in Mississippi and Texas is less than two weeks.

Okay, so what do I do? Check three things before you apply or buy: the local ordinance’s variance test, the appeal deadline, and whether any separate state permit applies to your project.

Zoning, variances & land use in Texas

The variance test: Unnecessary hardship. A variance requires unnecessary hardship: due to special conditions, literal enforcement would cause unnecessary hardship, the variance is not against the public interest, and the spirit of the ordinance is observed. Since 2021 the board may treat very high compliance costs or a large loss of buildable area as grounds for hardship.

How to apply: Apply to the city's board of adjustment, which holds a public hearing and decides the request; a 75 percent concurring vote of the board is needed to grant a variance. The board cannot grant a variance that allows a land use the ordinance otherwise prohibits.

If you are denied — appeal: An aggrieved person, a taxpayer, or a municipal officer may file a verified petition in district court, county court, or county court at law, and the court may issue a writ of certiorari to review the board's decision. Deadline: 10 days

Nonconforming use: Varies — it depends on local law. Chapter 211 sets no single statewide rule for grandfathered uses, so protection depends on the city's ordinance; a lawful nonconforming use is commonly allowed to continue but not expand, and a city may phase some uses out over time. Separately, Chapter 245 protects vested rights in a pending permit application.

The law: Municipal Zoning Authority — Tex. Loc. Gov't Code § 211.009

Where to learn more: Texas Municipal League.

Cities may zone only inside their city limits: a city cannot zone land in its extraterritorial jurisdiction (ETJ), where it may regulate subdivision and platting but not the use, height, bulk, or density of buildings. Texas counties have no general zoning power, and Houston has no comprehensive zoning ordinance.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Start with the agency linked above for your local ordinance and application forms. If the board denies you, calendar the appeal deadline the same day. If the amount at stake is large — a home, a business, a development — talk to the legal aid above or a land-use lawyer before the hearing, not after.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Get your local zoning ordinance and find the variance section — that is the test the board will apply. (2) Apply to the board of adjustment with evidence of your lot’s unique conditions. (3) If denied, appeal before the deadline in your state’s card above. (4) Before buying a nonconforming property, confirm in writing that the use is protected. (5) Check whether a separate state permit applies to your project.

Sources: the official state statutes, planning agencies, and municipal leagues linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Zoning rules change — confirm the current ordinance with your city or county before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Firearm rights restoration in Texas

A felony conviction usually takes away the right to possess a firearm. In many states there is a way to get that right back — a pardon, a court petition, an expungement, or simply waiting out the ban. The route, the wait, and who qualifies are different in every state, and there is one warning that applies everywhere: getting your rights back under state law does not always lift the separate federal ban. Read the federal warning below first, then your state’s card. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

The federal warning first

Federal law (18 U.S.C. § 922(g)) makes it a federal crime for a person convicted of a felony punishable by more than a year in prison to possess a firearm. That ban is separate from your state’s ban, and a state pardon, court order, or expungement does not always lift it. Whether it does depends on the exact kind of relief you got and how federal law treats it — for example, a pardon that expressly restores firearm rights is treated differently from a pardon that says nothing about firearms, and an expungement that fully erases the conviction is treated differently from a record-sealing that leaves the conviction in place.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not buy, borrow, or handle a firearm until you are sure both the state ban and the federal ban are lifted for you. If you are not sure, ask a criminal-defense lawyer or the legal aid listed in your state’s card below before you touch one — possessing a firearm while still banned can mean a new felony charge, even if you believed your rights were restored.

The five routes back

Across the 50 states, restoration happens in five ways. Governor’s pardon (22 states): you apply to the governor (or a pardons board that recommends to the governor), and only a pardon that expressly restores firearm rights counts. Court petition (10 states): you ask a judge to lift the ban, usually by showing good cause and a clean record since the sentence ended. Varies (10 states): the route depends on the conviction — one kind of felony may be restored automatically, another only by pardon. Automatic (4 states — Alaska, Louisiana, North Dakota, South Dakota): the ban ends on its own when the waiting period runs out, with no application needed. Expungement (4 states — Indiana, Missouri, New Hampshire, New Jersey): clearing the conviction in court restores the right along with it.

In 14 states most people with a felony conviction have a real route back; in 36 states the route is limited — only some convictions qualify, or only a pardon will do. No state has a route for everyone: violent felonies, crimes with a firearm, and repeat felonies are excluded in most states.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s route in the card below. The route decides who you apply to — a governor, a board, or a judge — and the waiting period decides when you can apply.

Traps: do not get caught by these

Assuming getting your vote back restores your gun rights. It does not. In Florida, restoring voting rights under Amendment 4 does not restore firearm rights — that takes a separate clemency decision. In Kentucky, automatic restoration of voting rights does not restore firearm rights either. Assuming sealing or expunging the record is enough. In Arkansas, sealing a record alone does not restore firearm rights, and in California a dismissal under Penal Code 1203.4 does not either. Assuming time alone does it. In Oregon, the state Supreme Court has held that the 15-year provision does not lift the ban — you still need a court order, a set-aside, or a pardon. Assuming any pardon works. In Alabama, Arkansas, and Georgia, a pardon that does not expressly mention firearm rights does not restore them. Assuming the state ban is the only ban. The federal ban under 18 U.S.C. § 922(g) can still apply after a state restoration — see the warning at the top.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the exact words of whatever relief you get — a pardon, a court order, or an expungement — and make sure it says firearm rights are restored. If it does not say so, assume they are not.

Firearm rights restoration in Texas

Can rights be restored? Limited — only some people qualify. Full firearm rights in Texas can only come back through a full pardon from the Governor, followed by a separate firearm-rights restoration.

How it works: Governor’s pardon. The person first applies to the Texas Board of Pardons and Paroles for a full pardon, and after a pardon is granted applies again to the Board for restoration of firearm rights, which the Board only considers in extreme and unusual cases tied to earning a living.

Waiting period: 5 years after release from confinement or supervision for the limited home-only possession rule under state law; no fixed waiting period is stated in the law for a pardon

The law: Unlawful possession of firearm — Tex. Penal Code § 46.04

Where to apply: Texas Board of Pardons and Paroles, Clemency Section.

After 5 years from release, Texas state law only allows a person with a felony conviction to have a firearm at the home where they live, not anywhere else, and federal law still bans possession.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Start with the agency linked above — it has the application or the court forms. If your case is complicated, or you are not sure the federal ban is lifted too, talk to the legal aid above or a criminal-defense lawyer before you apply, and do not possess a firearm in the meantime.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Read the federal warning at the top — state restoration does not always lift the federal ban. (2) Find your state’s route and waiting period in the card above. (3) Apply to the agency linked there — a governor, a pardons board, or a court. (4) Make sure whatever you receive expressly restores firearm rights. (5) Do not possess a firearm until you are sure both bans are lifted.

Sources: the official state statutes, pardons boards, governors’ offices, and courts linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Restoration rules change — confirm the current law with your state’s agency before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Labor union rights in Texas

Can you join a union at your job? Can you be forced to pay union dues? If you work for the government, can you bargain over pay — or go on strike? The answers depend on two different sets of law: a federal law that covers private-sector workers in every state, and your state’s own rules, which decide what happens for public workers and whether dues can be required. This section explains both, and what to do in your state. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

The federal baseline: private-sector workers

Private-sector workers in every state are covered by the federal National Labor Relations Act (NLRA). It gives you the right to form or join a union, to bargain collectively over pay, hours, and working conditions, and to act together with coworkers to improve conditions — even if there is no union at your workplace yet. Your employer may not fire you, demote you, or punish you for exercising those rights, and the National Labor Relations Board (NLRB) is the agency that enforces them. Private-sector strikes are generally protected too, though there are rules about how and when you can strike, and some workers — such as supervisors and independent contractors — are not covered.

Public-sector workers — teachers, police, firefighters, state and city employees — are not covered by the NLRA. Their rights come from state law, and that is where the states differ sharply. Everything in the next two cards is about those state rules.

Okay, so what do I do? If you work for a private company, start with the NLRB: you can file a charge online or at a regional office if your employer retaliates against union activity. If you work for the government, read your state’s cards below — the rules are different.

Right-to-work: what it means

26 states have right-to-work laws; 24 do not. In a right-to-work state, no worker can be forced to join a union or pay union dues as a condition of keeping a job. That does not mean unions are banned there — a union can still form, bargain, and represent workers. It means paying is voluntary. And even in a right-to-work state, if a union represents your workplace, it must represent every worker in the unit fairly, whether or not they pay dues.

In a state without right-to-work, a union contract can require workers it covers to pay dues or fees for representation. But there is one big exception for public workers: the Supreme Court’s Janus decision (2018) says public employees cannot be forced to pay union fees at all, even in states without right-to-work laws. So in the public sector, paying is voluntary everywhere.

These laws change. Michigan repealed its right-to-work law in 2023, and the repeal took effect in March 2024 — the first state in nearly 60 years to do so. Illinois went the other way in 2022, when voters added a constitutional amendment banning any future right-to-work law in the state.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your state’s right-to-work line in the card below. If you are being told you must pay dues to keep your job in a right-to-work state, or you are being punished for not joining a union, that is worth a complaint — see the agency link in your state’s card.

Public-sector bargaining: the three buckets

Yes (27 states): public workers can form unions and bargain over pay, hours, and working conditions under a state law. Limited (17 states): only some public workers can bargain — teachers only, or firefighters only, or workers in some cities but not others. No (6 states): Arkansas, Georgia, Mississippi, North Carolina, South Carolina, and West Virginia have no law giving public workers bargaining rights at all. In North Carolina and South Carolina, the law goes further and declares public-sector bargaining contracts illegal.

“Limited” takes many shapes. Virginia banned public-sector bargaining for decades, then in 2021 let each city and county decide for itself whether to allow it. Colorado gave bargaining rights to state workers in 2020 and county workers in 2022, but most city and school workers still have none. Texas bans bargaining for most public workers, but police and firefighters in some cities can bargain under separate laws. Iowa narrowed bargaining in 2017 so most public workers can bargain only over base pay.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s bucket in the card below. If you are in a “limited” state, read the note carefully — the right may cover your job, or it may cover only a different group of public workers.

Can public workers strike?

No state gives public workers an unrestricted right to strike. In 39 states the answer is no — public-sector strikes are banned, and striking can mean being fired or fined. In 11 states the answer is “limited”: some public workers may strike, but only after finishing the full dispute process — mediation, fact-finding, an expired contract, and advance notice — and police, firefighters, and other safety workers are barred everywhere.

The 11 limited states are Alaska, California, Colorado, Hawaii, Illinois, Minnesota, Montana, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Vermont. Even there, the rules are strict: in Oregon, for example, most public workers may strike only after bargaining, mediation, a 30-day cooling-off period, and 10 days’ notice. In Minnesota, only workers who are not essential may strike. In Montana, the right comes from a 1974 state Supreme Court decision, not a statute.

Private-sector strikes are different: they are generally protected by federal law, though there are rules about timing and notice, and some strikes — such as a strike over a contract that is still in effect — are not protected.

Okay, so what do I do? If you work for the government, do not walk out without checking your state’s strike line below first. In most states a public-sector strike is illegal, and the consequences can include losing your job.

Recent changes worth knowing

Michigan (2023–2024): repealed its right-to-work law; the repeal took effect in March 2024. Utah (2025): the Legislature passed a ban on public-sector bargaining (HB 267), but voters collected over 250,000 signatures to put it on the ballot, and the Legislature repealed it in December 2025 before it ever took effect — so Utah public workers can still bargain. Wisconsin (Act 10): the 2011 law that limits most public workers to bargaining over base wages only, capped at inflation, is still in force — a court ruling against it in December 2024 was reversed on appeal in July 2026. Florida (2023): public-sector unions must now keep at least 60% of workers as dues-paying members or risk losing certification, and most public-employer dues deductions ended. Nevada (2019): state workers got bargaining rights for the first time. Illinois (2022): voters banned any future right-to-work law in the state constitution.

Okay, so what do I do? If a news story or a coworker tells you the rules changed, check the date — several of these changes are from the last few years, and older guides may still print the old rule.

Traps: do not get caught by these

Assuming the federal law covers you. The NLRA covers private-sector workers only. If you work for the government, your rights come from state law, and in six states there is no bargaining right at all. Assuming dues are optional everywhere. In states without right-to-work, a union contract can require dues — except for public workers, where Janus makes paying voluntary nationwide. Striking without checking. A public-sector strike is illegal in most states and can cost you your job. Assuming your union must take your case. A union must represent everyone in its unit fairly, but it has discretion about which grievances to take to arbitration. Trusting old guides. Michigan, Utah, Wisconsin, Illinois, Virginia, Florida, and Nevada have all changed their rules since 2019.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you act — join, refuse to pay, file a complaint, or walk out — confirm your state’s three lines below: right-to-work, bargaining, and strikes. Then use the agency link in your state’s card if you need help.

Labor union rights in Texas

Right-to-work? Yes — right-to-work state. Texas law, backed by the state constitution, says no one can be denied a job because they do or do not belong to a union.

Can public workers bargain? Limited. Texas generally bans collective bargaining for public workers, but police officers and firefighters in some cities can bargain or meet and confer under separate laws.

Can public workers strike? No. Public workers in Texas may not strike or take part in an organized work stoppage, and a striker loses civil service and reemployment rights.

Where to complain: Texas Workforce Commission.

Texas bans public-sector bargaining for most government workers, though police and firefighters in some cities are the exception.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the three lines above for your state. If your employer is retaliating against union activity, contact the agency linked above — and if you work for a private company, you can also file a charge with the NLRB.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find your state’s three answers above — right-to-work, public bargaining, and strikes. (2) If you work for a private company, remember the federal NLRA protects you in every state. (3) If you work for the government, your rights come from state law — check before you act. (4) If your employer punishes you for union activity, contact the agency in your state’s card. (5) Remember the rules change often — confirm the current law before you rely on it.

Sources: the official state statutes, labor agency, and labor-relations board pages linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Union and bargaining rules have changed recently in several states — confirm the current law with your state’s labor agency before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Felon voting rights in Texas

In most states, a felony conviction takes away the right to vote — but for how long, and how you get it back, depends entirely on your state. In two states you never lose the vote at all. In about half the states it comes back the day you walk out of prison. In others you must finish probation and parole first, or pay off fines and fees, or apply to a court or a governor. This section explains the rules, the traps, and what to do in your state. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What losing the vote means

Disenfranchisement means losing the right to vote because of a criminal conviction. It applies to felony convictions, not misdemeanors — with one exception: Indiana also takes the vote away while a person is incarcerated for a misdemeanor. Being arrested, charged, or held before trial does not take the vote away, and a conviction that is not final — for example, deferred adjudication in Texas, or a First Offender sentence in Georgia that has not been revoked — may not take it away either.

Losing the vote is separate from losing other rights. A felony conviction can also affect owning a firearm, serving on a jury, and holding public office, and those rights often come back on different schedules — in Utah, for example, voting comes back on probation or parole, while holding office takes ten years. Getting your vote back does not automatically restore the others.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s answer in the card below first. Then check your registration status with your local election office before you assume you can or cannot vote.

The six buckets: when the vote comes back

Never loses the vote (2 states): Maine and Vermont — a felony conviction never takes the vote away, even in prison; people vote by absentee ballot. Only while in prison (24 states): the vote comes back automatically on release, even while on parole or probation. After the sentence is complete (12 states): the vote returns once prison, parole, and probation are all finished. After the sentence plus fines/fees (3 states): Arizona, Arkansas, and South Dakota — unpaid fines, fees, or restitution can hold the vote back. Permanent for some offenses (8 states): Alabama, Delaware, Florida, Iowa, Kentucky, Mississippi, Tennessee, and Wyoming — most felonies restore after the sentence, but certain offenses (often murder and sexual offenses) are barred for life unless pardoned. Only by pardon (1 state): Virginia — as of November 2026 the governor must individually restore the right.

One state is about to change: Virginia voters approved a constitutional amendment on November 3, 2026 that automatically restores voting rights on release from incarceration, effective January 1, 2027. And Missouri changed its rule on August 28, 2026 (H.B. 1871): most people with felony convictions there can now vote while on probation or parole.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s bucket in the card below. If you are in a “permanent for some” state, read the note carefully — the bar usually applies only to specific offenses, not to every felony.

How restoration works

Automatic: in most states the right comes back by law — on release, or when the sentence ends. Nothing to file, no one to ask. But “automatic” does not mean “already registered”: in most states your old registration was cancelled, so you must re-register to vote. A few states make that easy — Michigan automatically registers people on release from prison, and North Dakota has no voter registration at all. By application: some states require a court petition or an application to a board — Tennessee, for example, requires a circuit-court order. By pardon/clemency: in Virginia (until January 1, 2027) and for the permanently barred offenses in several states, only the governor can restore the right.

Some states issue a paper that proves the right is back — a certificate of discharge, a certificate of restoration, or a court order. Keep it. An election office may ask for it when you re-register, and it is the simplest answer if anyone questions your eligibility.

Okay, so what do I do? Once your state’s trigger point has passed, re-register to vote — online, by mail, or in person at your local election office — and keep any discharge or restoration certificate they gave you.

Fines, fees, and restitution: the money question

In most states, unpaid fines, fees, or restitution do not block getting the vote back. But in a few states they do: Arizona, Arkansas, and South Dakota require the money to be paid (or the sentence, including its financial conditions, to be complete) before the vote returns. Florida’s rule is similar for most felonies — “completion of sentence” there includes full payment of fines, fees, and restitution.

Even where money is not a direct test, it can still delay things indirectly: if paying is a condition of probation or parole, and probation gets extended because the money was not paid, you are still on supervision — and in an after-sentence state, that means you still cannot vote. Georgia’s rule shows the other side: fines imposed as a condition of probation are automatically cancelled when probation ends, and the Secretary of State says a sentence counts as complete even with outstanding restitution or fees.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the money line in the state card below. If money is the only thing holding your sentence open, ask the sentencing court or your probation officer whether the obligation can be converted to community service, put on a payment plan, or ended — and get the answer in writing.

On probation or parole? In jail? Awaiting trial?

Whether you can vote while on probation or parole depends on your state’s bucket. In the 24 prison-only states, yes — release from prison is enough, and probation or parole does not matter. In after-sentence states, no — not until supervision ends. In Maine and Vermont, the question never arises.

People in jail before trial, and people serving a misdemeanor sentence, can vote in most states — usually by absentee ballot. Jails are supposed to help with that, but in practice many people in jail never get a ballot. If you or someone you know is in jail and eligible, ask the jail for an absentee ballot application early, and check the registration deadline — it is often weeks before Election Day.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are not serving a felony sentence, you can almost certainly vote — check your registration and request an absentee ballot if you are in jail. If you are on felony probation or parole, check your state’s bucket first.

Traps: do not get caught by these

Assuming you are still registered. Most states cancel a registration when a felony conviction is entered. Re-registering is part of getting the vote back, not an optional extra. Assuming the rules are the same everywhere. Moving to a new state does not move your old state’s rule with you — the new state’s law decides, and a federal or out-of-state conviction can be treated differently from an in-state one. Voting while ineligible. In some states, knowingly voting before your rights are restored is itself a crime — North Carolina made it a crime effective January 1, 2024. If you are unsure, check with your local election office before you cast a ballot, not after. Trusting old guides. These rules change often — Missouri, Minnesota, New Jersey, Nevada, and Virginia have all changed theirs since 2019, and several states have changes taking effect in 2026 and 2027.

Okay, so what do I do? Before an election, confirm three things: your state’s trigger point has passed, you are registered at your current address, and no money or supervision condition is still holding your sentence open.

Felon voting rights in Texas

When does the vote come back? After the sentence is complete. A final felony conviction takes the vote away for the whole sentence, including imprisonment, parole, community supervision and probation. The right returns automatically once the sentence is fully discharged, or on pardon.

How is it restored? Automatic. No application, court order or pardon is needed once the sentence is fully discharged; re-register to vote. Deferred adjudication is not a final conviction, so a person on deferred adjudication can vote.

Do fines, fees, or restitution block it? No. The statute lists only incarceration, parole, supervision and probation as the parts of a sentence that must be completed, and does not separately list fines, fees or restitution as a voting barrier. Case law treats a fine as part of the sentence itself, while restitution and court costs are not, so a fine imposed as part of the sentence or as a condition of supervision can delay discharge in a particular case; confirm the discharge status with the sentencing court if money is still owed.

The law: Tex. Const. art. VI, § 1; Tex. Elec. Code § 11.002 — Tex. Const. art. VI, § 1; Tex. Elec. Code § 11.002.

A person on federal supervised release has not fully discharged the sentence and cannot vote yet. Texas has three regional legal-aid programs in addition to the statewide Texas Legal Services Center.

Free legal help: Texas Legal Services Center.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the answers above for your state, re-register to vote once the trigger point has passed, and if you are unsure whether you are eligible, ask your local election office before Election Day — not after.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find your state’s answer above — when the vote comes back, and whether money blocks it. (2) Once the trigger point has passed, re-register to vote at your current address. (3) Keep any discharge or restoration certificate. (4) If you are on probation or parole, in jail, or unsure about fines, check with your local election office before you vote. (5) Remember the rules change often — confirm the current law before each election.

Sources: the official state constitutions, statutes, and Secretary of State / elections office pages linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Felon voting rules have changed recently in several states and more changes take effect in 2027 — confirm the current law with your local election office before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Applying for public benefits in Texas

Medicaid pays for health care and SNAP (Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, the food stamps program) helps buy groceries. Both are for people with low income, and both are applied for through your state — usually online, in one application that can cover both. This section explains who qualifies, where to apply, what happens after you apply, and what to do if you are denied. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

SSI is a different program. Supplemental Security Income (SSI) is cash help for people 65 or older, blind, or with a disability who have very low income and few assets. It is run by the federal Social Security Administration, not your state, and you apply for it separately — see Social Security Disability & SSI on this page.

What Medicaid and SNAP are

Medicaid is health coverage paid for jointly by the federal government and your state. It covers doctor visits, hospital care, prescriptions, and more, usually with little or no cost to you. Each state runs its own Medicaid program and gives it its own name — Medi-Cal in California, Apple Health in Washington, BadgerCare Plus in Wisconsin, and so on — but it is the same program underneath. SNAP is monthly money for groceries, loaded onto an EBT card you use like a debit card at grocery stores and farmers markets. SNAP is a federal program, but your state decides your application and the amount.

You can get one without the other, and applying for one does not hurt your chances for the other. Many states use a single application for both, plus cash help and child care, so one form can open several doors at once.

Okay, so what do I do? Start with the state card below — it names your state’s online portal and the agency that runs it. That portal is where you apply.

Who qualifies: the expansion split

Children, pregnant people, parents with very low income, seniors, and people with disabilities can qualify for Medicaid in every state. The big split is over other adults: in the 40 states that expanded Medicaid, most adults under 65 with income up to 138% of the federal poverty level qualify, whether or not they have children. In the 10 states that have not expanded — Alabama, Florida, Georgia, Kansas, Mississippi, South Carolina, Tennessee, Texas, Wisconsin, and Wyoming — most adults without children do not qualify for Medicaid at any income, and parents qualify only at very low incomes. Wisconsin is the partial exception: its BadgerCare Plus covers childless adults up to 100% of the poverty level under a federal waiver.

SNAP does not have that split: it is available in all 50 states for households with low income, based on household size, income, and some expenses like rent and child care. Citizens and many lawfully present immigrants can qualify; some immigration statuses are excluded, and the rules differ between Medicaid and SNAP, so check the program’s own page if that question applies to you.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your state’s answer in the card below. If your state has not expanded Medicaid and you do not qualify, check HealthCare.gov for a subsidized private plan — and still apply for SNAP, which has no expansion gap.

How to apply

Most people apply online through the portal in the state card below. You can also apply by phone, on paper (print the form or pick one up at a local office), or in person at your county or local benefits office. A few states split the application in two — one portal for food and cash help, another for health coverage — and the state card below says so when that is the case.

You do not need a complete application to start. For both SNAP and Medicaid, an application with just your name, address, and signature counts as filed that day — and the date matters, because SNAP benefits start on the day you apply. Send the rest of the information later; do not wait until everything is perfect.

Have ready if you can: ID for everyone applying, proof of income (pay stubs, benefit letters), proof of rent or mortgage, and proof of household size. If you do not have a document, apply anyway and tell the agency — it can often verify income directly or give you time to send it.

Okay, so what do I do? Go to the portal in the state card below and start the application with your name, address, and signature. That locks in your application date.

What happens after you apply

Most states will call you for a phone interview — usually for SNAP, sometimes for Medicaid too. Answer the call or call back promptly; a missed interview is one of the most common reasons applications stall. The agency may ask for documents to verify income, identity, or expenses, and will tell you how to send them (upload, fax, mail, or drop off).

Deadlines the agency must meet: SNAP decisions are due within 30 days of your application, and households with very little money or no income can get expedited SNAP within 7 days — ask for it when you apply if that is your situation. Medicaid decisions take longer and the timeline varies by state; if a disability decision is involved it can take up to 90 days.

You will get the decision in writing. If you are approved, the letter says what you get and when it starts. If you are denied, the letter must say why — keep it, because it is the key to appealing (see the next card).

Okay, so what do I do? Answer the interview call, send any documents they ask for right away, and keep copies of everything you send. If 30 days pass on a SNAP application with no decision, call the agency and ask where it stands.

If you are denied: appeal, and appeal fast

A denial is not the end of it. You have the right to a fair hearing — a review by someone who was not involved in the first decision — and many denials are overturned because of a missing document, a math error, or income counted the wrong way. For SNAP you usually have 90 days from the date of the notice to ask for a hearing, and Medicaid appeals work on a similar window — but do not wait: ask as soon as you get the letter.

If your benefits are being cut off or reduced, asking for a hearing quickly — usually within about 10 to 15 days of the notice, depending on the state — can keep your benefits going at the old level while the appeal is decided. The notice itself tells you the exact deadline and how to ask; read it the day it arrives.

You can bring someone to help you at the hearing, including a legal aid lawyer, and you can ask for an interpreter if you need one. Legal aid programs help with benefits appeals, often for free — the link in the state card below is a good place to start.

Okay, so what do I do? Read the denial letter the day it arrives, note the appeal deadline, and ask for a fair hearing in writing before that date. Keep a copy of your request.

Keeping your benefits: renewals and changes

Benefits are not forever — you have to renew them. Medicaid is usually renewed once a year; SNAP is usually recertified every 6 to 12 months. The agency will send you a renewal form — fill it out and return it by the deadline, or your benefits stop even if you still qualify. If you move, tell the agency your new address, or the renewal form will go to the wrong place.

Also report changes when they happen: a new job, a raise, someone moving in or out, a change in rent. Reporting late can mean an overpayment you have to pay back, so report promptly even if you think the change is small.

Watch for new work rules. Several states are adding work or community-engagement requirements for some adults on expanded Medicaid, starting January 1, 2027. Your state’s note below says if yours is one of them — and if it is, check what counts and what proof you need before the rules start.

Okay, so what do I do? Put the renewal deadline on your calendar the day the letter arrives, report changes as they happen, and keep your address current with the agency.

Applying for public benefits in Texas

Medicaid expanded? No — not expanded. Medicaid covers children, pregnant women, low-income parents, seniors, and people with disabilities, and most childless adults do not qualify at any income.

Where to apply: https://www.yourtexasbenefits.com — Your Texas Benefits is the single portal to apply for Medicaid, CHIP, SNAP, and TANF cash help.

Agency: Texas Health and Human Services Commission.

Texas has one of the strictest parent income limits in the country, so a parent working even part-time at minimum wage can earn too much for Medicaid yet too little for marketplace subsidies.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid — legal aid programs help with benefits applications and appeals, often for free.

Okay, so what do I do? Apply at the portal above — even an application with just your name, address, and signature starts the clock. Answer the interview call, send any documents they ask for, and if you are denied, ask for a fair hearing before the deadline on the letter.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Check whether your state expanded Medicaid in the card above. (2) Apply at your state’s portal — name, address, and signature is enough to start. (3) Answer the interview call and send any documents they ask for. (4) If you are denied, ask for a fair hearing before the deadline on the notice. (5) Renew on time and report changes as they happen. For SSI, see Social Security Disability & SSI on this page instead — that program runs through Social Security, not your state.

Sources: the official state benefits portals and agency pages linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Eligibility rules and work requirements are changing in several states in 2027 — confirm the current rules with your state agency before you rely on them. This is general information, not legal advice.

Non-compete agreements in Texas

A non-compete agreement is a promise not to work for a competitor, or start a competing business, after you leave a job. It can lock you out of your own profession for months or years, so whether it is enforceable matters a lot — and the answer depends almost entirely on your state. Five states ban employee non-competes outright, most others allow them only with limits, and a few still enforce them broadly. This section explains what a non-compete can and cannot do, the tests courts use, and what Texas says. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What a non-compete is

A non-compete usually says that after you leave, you cannot work for a competing business, or start one yourself, for a set time (often six months to two years) and within a set area (a city, a state, a radius around the office). It is often buried in an offer letter, an employment agreement, or a handbook you sign on your first day. It is different from a non-solicitation clause (do not poach the company’s customers or co-workers) and a confidentiality clause (do not share trade secrets) — those are usually enforceable even where non-competes are banned.

Non-competes are not enforceable just because you signed one.
