In most states a court will only enforce a non-compete that is reasonable in time, area, and scope, and that protects a real business interest like trade secrets or customer relationships — not one whose only purpose is to stop ordinary competition. Some courts will narrow an overbroad clause and enforce the trimmed version; others throw the whole clause out if any part is unreasonable.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you sign, read the clause and note the time, the area, and what work it covers. If you are asked to sign one after you already started the job, check the state card below — in some states your employer must give you something new in return, like a raise or a promotion, for it to count.

Banned, restricted, or allowed: the three buckets

Banned: California, Minnesota, North Dakota, Oklahoma, and Wyoming void employee non-competes by statute. In those states an employer generally cannot stop you from taking a competing job at all, though sale-of-business and trade-secret rules still apply. Restricted: most states allow non-competes only with limits — an earnings threshold below which the clause is void, a notice period, a time cap, or protection for certain professions like doctors. Allowed: the rest enforce non-competes under a court reasonableness test, with no statute setting a pay threshold.

The trend is one way: bans and thresholds are spreading, and several states have new laws that took effect in 2025 and 2026. A clause that was enforceable when you signed it may not be enforceable now, and the reverse is also true for older agreements in states that tightened the rules only for new contracts.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s bucket in the state card below first. If your state bans non-competes or you earn under its threshold, the clause may already be void.

Earnings thresholds: the pay line

Many restricted states draw a pay line: earn under it and a non-compete is void; earn over it and the clause can still bind you if it is reasonable. The lines vary widely — some are tied to the federal poverty level, some to the state minimum wage, some are fixed dollar amounts that rise on a schedule, and some adjust every year for inflation. A few states draw the line by hourly pay or by job type instead of salary.

Watch the dates: thresholds that adjust annually change every January, and a few states have new thresholds that only started in 2026. The figure in the state card below is the 2026 figure where one could be verified.

Okay, so what do I do? Compare your pay to your state’s threshold in the card below. If you are under it, the non-compete is likely void — but keep a copy of the agreement and your pay records anyway.

What makes a non-compete unenforceable

Even in states that allow non-competes, a clause can fail for several reasons: it lasts too long or covers too much territory; it bars work the employer has no legitimate interest in stopping; you were given no advance notice or no copy before you accepted the job; you received nothing new in exchange for signing mid-employment; or you were fired without cause or laid off, which in several states ends the employer’s right to enforce it. A clause that bans all work in your field, anywhere, is almost never reasonable.

Some states add teeth: an employer that presents or tries to enforce a void non-compete can owe the worker damages, a penalty per affected worker, and the worker’s attorney fees.

Okay, so what do I do? If any of those problems fits your situation, do not assume the clause binds you — but do not ignore it either. Get advice before you take the competing job, so you are not the one sued first.

Asked to sign one? Leaving a job with one?

Before you sign: ask for the full agreement in writing and time to read it. Negotiate the time and area down, ask what counts as a competitor, and ask what happens if you are laid off. In several states the employer must give you the agreement days or weeks before you start, and must tell you in writing to talk to a lawyer. After you leave: do not take customer lists, pricing, or trade secrets with you, even if the non-compete itself is void — those are separate obligations. Tell your new employer about the clause; a new employer that knowingly hires you into a breach can be sued too.

Okay, so what do I do? Keep a copy of everything you signed, note the dates, and get advice before you accept the competing job — not after the old employer’s lawyer writes to you.

What a non-compete ban does not cover

Even in states that ban non-competes, an employer can usually still enforce a confidentiality clause, a non-solicitation clause for customers and co-workers, a requirement to repay training costs, and a non-compete tied to selling a business. Trade-secret law applies in all 50 states regardless of what you signed. And a non-compete in a contract signed before a state’s ban took effect may still be judged under the old rules.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not treat a ban as a free pass to take the client list with you. The non-compete may be dead while the trade-secret and non-solicitation rules are very much alive.

Non-compete agreements in Texas

Enforceable? Allowed. Non-competes are generally enforceable when they are part of an otherwise enforceable agreement and reasonable in time, geographic area, and scope of activity.

Earnings threshold? No threshold. Texas has no earnings threshold for non-competes generally, so the reasonableness test applies at every pay level.

The law: Covenants Not to Compete Act — Tex. Bus. & Com. Code §§ 15.50–15.52.

An overbroad Texas non-compete is usually narrowed by the court rather than thrown out, but since September 1, 2025, physician, dentist, nurse, and physician assistant non-competes are capped at one year, five miles, and a buyout of no more than one year's pay.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the two answers above for your state, keep a copy of anything you signed, and if the clause looks void or overbroad, talk to an employment lawyer before you take the competing job — many offer a free first consultation.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find your state’s answer above — banned, restricted, or allowed — and its pay threshold. (2) Read the clause for its time, area, and scope, and keep a copy of everything you signed. (3) If you are under the threshold or your state bans non-competes, the clause may already be void. (4) Do not take customer lists or trade secrets with you, even if the non-compete is dead. (5) Get advice from an employment lawyer before you accept the competing job, not after your old employer writes to you.

Sources: the official state statutes and court sources linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Non-compete law is changing fast and thresholds adjust yearly — confirm the current law with a lawyer before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Product liability & defective products in Texas

When a product hurts you — a drug with a hidden side effect, a car part that fails, a tool that breaks, a children’s toy that chokes — the maker or seller may have to pay for the harm. Product liability is the area of law that covers that. This section explains the three kinds of defects, whether you have to prove the company was careless, the two deadlines that can end your claim, and what to do right after you are hurt. The picture here is as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

The three kinds of defects

A product-liability claim starts with showing the product was defective in one of three ways. A manufacturing defect means one unit went wrong on the line — your bottle had a crack the others did not, your airbag had a bad part. A design defect means the whole line is unsafe as designed — every unit has the same flaw. A failure to warn means the company did not give an adequate warning or instructions about a danger it knew about, or should have known about.

In every case you also have to show the defect made the product unreasonably dangerous and that it caused your injury. A product that simply did not work well, or that you used in a way it was never meant to be used, is usually not enough.

Okay, so what do I do? Write down what the product is, what went wrong, and how you were using it. That is the start of the defect question a lawyer will ask first.

Strict liability vs. negligence: do you have to prove carelessness?

In most states, strict liability applies: you do not have to prove the company was careless. You only have to prove the product was defective and unreasonably dangerous, and that the defect caused your injury. That matters, because a careful company can still sell a dangerous product. Check the state card below for whether Texas is a strict-liability state.

A few states never adopted strict liability for products — Delaware, Louisiana, North Carolina, and Virginia. There, you must prove the company was negligent or broke a warranty. Louisiana has its own statute that lists exactly four ways a product can be unreasonably dangerous. Even in strict-liability states, design-defect and failure-to-warn claims often turn on a risk-utility test: was the danger greater than the cost of preventing it?

Okay, so what do I do? Do not assume you have no case because the company “did its best.” In most states, doing its best is not a defense when the product was defective.

The two deadlines: limitations and repose

Product claims have two clocks, and missing either one ends the claim. The statute of limitations is the deadline to file after the injury — usually 1 to 6 years depending on the state, and in most states the clock starts when you discover, or reasonably should have discovered, the injury and that the product caused it. The statute of repose is an outer limit measured from when the product was first sold — 10 to 15 years in the states that have one. After the repose period runs out, you cannot sue even if you were only just hurt.

Some states have no repose period at all, and a few had one that their courts struck down as unconstitutional. A few states use a softer rule instead: after a certain number of years, the product is presumed to be past its useful safe life, but you can still win if you prove otherwise. The state card below gives both numbers for Texas.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not wait. The clock can start before you connect the injury to the product, and the repose clock runs from the sale date whether you knew about the defect or not. Talk to a lawyer as soon as you are hurt.

Right after you are hurt: what to keep

Get medical care first. Then protect the evidence. Keep the product. Do not throw it away, do not repair it, and do not let anyone alter it — the product itself is the most important piece of evidence in the case. Keep the packaging, the receipt, the instructions, and any warnings that came with it. Photograph the product, the injury, and where it happened. Write down what happened while it is fresh: the date, what you were doing, and who saw it.

Report the product to the U.S. Consumer Product Safety Commission at SaferProducts.gov — your report can trigger a recall that protects other people, and it creates a record of the problem. For a car or car part, report to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration; for a drug or medical device, to the Food and Drug Administration. Do not sign anything from the maker or its insurer, and do not give a recorded statement, before you talk to a lawyer.

Okay, so what do I do? Keep the product, keep the paperwork, take photos, write it down, and report it. Then call a lawyer — most product-liability lawyers offer a free first consultation.

Who you can sue

Usually you start with the manufacturer — the company that made the product or the defective part. But the whole chain of distribution can be liable in many states: the distributor, the wholesaler, and the store that sold it to you. In some states, a seller who did not make the product can step out of the case once the manufacturer is identified and can be sued. In others, the store stays on the hook. Online marketplaces are a newer question, and the rules vary by state and court.

It also matters where you sue. If the product was made in one state and sold in another, the other state’s deadlines can sometimes apply instead of yours — that is why a lawyer checks more than one state’s law before filing.

Okay, so what do I do? Save the receipt and the name of the store, and note the maker’s name from the product or packaging. A lawyer can work out who belongs in the case from there.

What you can recover

A successful claim can cover your medical bills, lost wages, pain and suffering, and damage to your property. If the company’s conduct was especially bad — it knew about the danger and hid it, for example — punitive damages may be available, though many states cap them. If the defective product kills someone, the family may have a wrongful-death claim.

Your own fault can matter. Most states reduce your recovery by your share of fault; a few still bar recovery entirely if you were even slightly at fault. The state card below notes the rule where it is unusual.

Okay, so what do I do? Keep every bill and every record of time you missed from work. Those papers are how damages are proved. If someone died, see the Wrongful Death section on this page.

Product liability & defective products in Texas

Deadline to file? 2 years. A personal-injury suit must be filed within two years after the cause of action accrues, with a discovery rule for harm that is not immediately apparent.

Outer limit from sale (statute of repose)? 15 years. A product-liability claim generally must be filed within 15 years after the date the defendant sold the product.

Strict liability? Yes. Texas adopted strict liability under Restatement (Second) of Torts section 402A, so a claimant need not prove the manufacturer was negligent.

The law: Texas Products Liability Act — Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code ch. 82.

A seller that did not manufacture the product is generally not liable unless one of the exceptions in Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code section 82.003 applies, such as the manufacturer being insolvent or outside the court's jurisdiction.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid. Note: free legal aid rarely takes injury cases. Product-liability lawyers usually work on contingency — no fee unless you win — and most offer a free first consultation, so that is the usual route for a defective-product claim.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the two deadlines above for your state, keep the product and all paperwork, report it to the Consumer Product Safety Commission, and talk to a product-liability lawyer before either deadline runs out.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Get medical care. (2) Keep the product — do not throw it away or repair it — and keep the packaging, receipt, and instructions. (3) Photograph everything and write down what happened. (4) Report the product at SaferProducts.gov. (5) Check your state’s two deadlines above — the filing deadline and the outer limit from the sale date. (6) Talk to a product-liability lawyer, usually free for a first consultation and on contingency, before you sign anything from the maker or its insurer. (7) If someone died, see the Wrongful Death section on this page.

Sources: the official state statutes and court sources linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Product liability law is fact-specific and deadlines are strict — confirm the current law with a lawyer before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Asylum, DACA & deportation defense in Texas

Every year, people come to the United States seeking safety from persecution, and others who have lived here for years find themselves facing removal. This section explains the three situations most people ask about — asylum, DACA, and deportation defense — plus your rights if immigration officers come to your door, and what happens if someone is detained. Immigration law changes fast and the deadlines are strict, so treat this as a starting point: the picture here is as of November 2026, and a lawyer should check anything before you file or sign it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Asylum: protection from persecution

Asylum protects people who are in the United States and were persecuted — or fear they will be — because of their race, religion, nationality, membership in a particular social group, or political opinion. You apply on Form I-589, Application for Asylum and for Withholding of Removal. There are two paths: affirmative asylum, where you apply to U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) and are interviewed by an asylum officer, and defensive asylum, where you ask an immigration judge for asylum as a defense in removal proceedings.

The deadline matters more than almost anything else: you must file Form I-589 within one year of arriving in the United States. There are narrow exceptions for changed circumstances and extraordinary circumstances, but they are hard to win — do not count on them. If you are granted asylum, you may stay in the United States, you are authorized to work right away, you can petition to bring your spouse and unmarried children under 21 (Form I-730, within two years of the grant), and you can apply for a green card one year after the grant.

While your asylum case is pending, you can apply for a work permit (Form I-765) 150 days after you file for asylum, but you cannot receive it until your case has been pending 180 days — the 180-day asylum clock. Delays that you cause, like asking to reschedule an interview, stop the clock.

Okay, so what do I do? If you fear persecution, file Form I-589 within a year of arrival and keep proof of the date you arrived. Get a lawyer before you file — a mistake on the application, or a detail left out, can hurt the case. Do not travel outside the United States while your case is pending unless a lawyer says it is safe: leaving can end your case.

DACA: where it stands

DACA (Deferred Action for Childhood Arrivals) is for people who were brought to the United States as children and meet specific guidelines: they came before their 16th birthday, were under 31 on June 15, 2012, have lived here continuously since June 15, 2007, are in school, graduated, have a GED, or are honorably discharged veterans, and have no felony, significant misdemeanor, or three or more other misdemeanors. DACA is not lawful status — it is a two-year pause on removal, plus permission to work.

As of November 2026, the courts have left DACA in a split state: USCIS is accepting and processing renewal requests, but it is accepting but not processing first-time requests, because of a 2021 Texas court order that was extended to the DACA rule and upheld on appeal in January 2025. Current DACA grants stay valid until they expire unless they are individually terminated. About 436,000 people held DACA as of mid-2026. Renewal waits have grown from weeks to months during 2026, so file early: USCIS says to renew 120 to 150 days before your DACA expires.

The court fight is not over, so the rules can change. Check the current status on the USCIS DACA page before you act, and do not pay anyone who promises they can get you DACA now — if you have never had it, USCIS is not processing new grants.

Okay, so what do I do? If you have DACA, renew 120 to 150 days before it expires and never let it lapse — a gap can mean losing your work authorization. If you have never had DACA, you can file a first-time request, but USCIS will not process it right now; talk to a lawyer about whether filing makes sense for you.

Deportation defense: removal proceedings

Removal proceedings usually start with a Notice to Appear (NTA) — a paper that says why the government believes you should be removed. Your first hearing is a master calendar hearing: it is usually short, the judge explains the charges, asks whether you have a lawyer, and sets dates. The later individual hearing is where you present your case and your evidence.

The defenses a judge can grant include asylum, withholding of removal (harder to win than asylum, and no path to a green card), protection under the Convention Against Torture for people likely to be tortured if returned, cancellation of removal for long-time residents with U.S. citizen or lawful permanent resident family who would suffer exceptional hardship, adjustment of status to a green card through a family member or employer if you qualify, and voluntary departure — leaving on your own instead of being ordered removed.

Never miss a hearing. If you do not show up, the judge can order you removed in your absence, without hearing your side. And keep your address updated with the immigration court and with USCIS every time you move — if a hearing notice goes to an old address, you can be ordered removed without ever knowing a hearing was scheduled. You have the right to a lawyer in immigration court, but the government does not provide one for free — you must find one yourself or through the free legal help in the state card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Read the Notice to Appear carefully and write down every court date. Get a lawyer before your first hearing if you possibly can. Never miss a date, and update your address with the court every time you move.

Know your rights with ICE

You have the right to remain silent. You do not have to answer questions about where you were born or your immigration status, and you can say, “I want to speak to a lawyer.” You do not have to open your door unless immigration officers show a warrant signed by a judge. An ICE administrative warrant (Form I-200 or I-205) does not authorize entry into your home — ask them to slide the paper under the door and look for a judge’s signature and your correct name and address.

Do not sign anything without a lawyer. Signing can mean giving up your right to a hearing or agreeing to leave the country. You do not have to consent to a search of your home, car, or phone. If you are stopped, stay calm, do not run, do not lie, and do not hand over false documents. You can ask, “Am I free to go?”

Memorize two things: your lawyer’s phone number and your “A-number” (alien number, on your immigration papers). Keep copies of your immigration papers with a trusted person, not only at home, and make a plan now — who to call, where your papers are, and who picks up your children — before anything happens.

Okay, so what do I do? Make the plan today: write down your lawyer’s number and your A-number, keep copies of your papers somewhere safe, and tell your family the plan. If officers come to the door, ask for a judge-signed warrant, say you want a lawyer, and do not sign anything.

Detention & bond

If immigration officers detain someone, you can find where they are held with the ICE Online Detainee Locator — you need their A-number and country of birth. Many detained people can ask an immigration judge for a bond hearing to be released while their case continues; the judge sets an amount, and family or a bond company can post it. Some people are subject to mandatory detention because of certain criminal convictions and cannot get bond.

Detention can last months. The first days matter most: getting a lawyer quickly, and asking for a bond hearing, can change the whole case. Do not sign voluntary departure papers in detention without a lawyer — signing can end the chance to fight the case.

Okay, so what do I do? If someone you love is detained, find them on the locator, call the free legal help in the state card below right away (several states have hotlines for detained people), and get a lawyer to request a bond hearing. Do not let them sign anything first.

What this topic does not cover

Asylum and DACA are not a path to citizenship by themselves — only a green card leads to citizenship, and for people granted asylum that means applying one year after the grant. Immigration court is civil, not criminal, so there is no free public defender. And a removal order is not the end of every case — appeals to the Board of Immigration Appeals and, in some situations, to a federal court exist, but they have short deadlines, sometimes 30 days.

Watch out for notario fraud. In many Latin American countries a “notario” is a lawyer. In the United States a notary public is not a lawyer and cannot give immigration legal advice. Only a licensed attorney or a Department of Justice–accredited representative may represent you in immigration court or before USCIS. Never pay someone who guarantees a result, says they have a special connection inside USCIS, or asks you to lie on a form.

Okay, so what do I do? Get help only from a licensed immigration lawyer or an accredited representative — the free legal help in the state card below can check credentials. Keep a copy of everything you file and every paper you receive.

Asylum, DACA & deportation defense in Texas

Immigration court: the Dallas Immigration Court in Dallas hears removal cases for Texas.

Free legal help: Refugee and Immigrant Center for Education and Legal Services (RAICES).

Texas has more immigration courts than any other state — Dallas, Houston (three courts), San Antonio, El Paso, Harlingen, Laredo and several detention-center courts — so always go by the court named on your hearing notice, not the nearest one.

Okay, so what do I do? Call the legal help above — most of these programs help with asylum, DACA renewals, and removal defense, and they can tell you whether you qualify for free help. If someone is detained, say so on the first call: detention cases move fastest. For the step after a green card, see the Becoming a U.S. citizen section on this page.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) If you fear persecution, file Form I-589 within one year of arriving — that deadline is strict. (2) If you have DACA, renew 120 to 150 days before it expires. (3) If you get a Notice to Appear, write down every court date, never miss one, and get a lawyer before the first hearing. (4) If immigration officers come to your door, ask for a judge-signed warrant, say you want a lawyer, and do not sign anything. (5) If someone is detained, find them on the ICE locator and call the legal help above right away. (6) Get help only from a licensed immigration lawyer or an accredited representative — never a notario. (7) If you are arrested, see the What to do if you’re arrested section on this page.

Sources: U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) asylum and DACA pages, the Executive Office for Immigration Review court list, and the legal-aid organizations linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Immigration law and court orders change quickly — confirm the current rules with a lawyer before you rely on them. This is general information, not legal advice.

Self-defense & use of force in Texas

Every state lets you defend yourself against an attack, but the rules about when you may use force, how much force, and whether you must retreat first are very different from state to state. This section explains the three rules that matter most — stand your ground, the castle doctrine, and the duty to retreat — and what Texas says about each one. This is the picture as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice. If you have used force, or think you may have to, talk to a criminal defense lawyer before you talk to police about what happened.

Stand your ground: what it means

In a stand-your-ground state, you have no duty to retreat before using force in self-defense when you are in a place you have a legal right to be, you did not start the fight, and you are not breaking the law. You may stand where you are and defend yourself, including with deadly force if the threat calls for it. In states without stand your ground, the law may require you to walk away if you safely can — that is the duty to retreat, covered below.

Stand your ground does not mean you can use force whenever you feel threatened. The force must still be reasonable and necessary against an imminent attack, and the protection is lost if you provoked the fight or were committing a crime. Some states have stand-your-ground protection only in limited situations — for example, only when the attacker has a weapon — so read your state’s card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the state card below for whether Texas is a stand-your-ground state. If it is not, know that walking away safely is not cowardice — it is what the law expects, and it keeps your self-defense claim intact.

The castle doctrine: your home

The castle doctrine says your home is your castle: inside it, you have no duty to retreat from an intruder. In most states the law also presumes that you reasonably feared death or serious injury when someone unlawfully and forcibly enters your home, which makes a self-defense claim much stronger. The doctrine usually covers your home and sometimes your occupied vehicle or workplace — not your yard, your porch, or a public place.

Two limits to know: the presumption usually does not apply against someone who also has a right to be there, like a lawful resident or a co-occupant, and it does not cover chasing someone who is already leaving. Force must still be reasonable for the threat in front of you.

Okay, so what do I do? If someone breaks in, call 911 as soon as you safely can, stay inside if you can, and do not go after a fleeing intruder. The castle doctrine protects you while you defend your home — not while you pursue someone outside it.

Duty to retreat: when you must walk away

In a duty-to-retreat state, you may not use deadly force in public if you know you can avoid it with complete safety by retreating — walking away, leaving the area, or giving up property. The duty usually does not apply inside your own home or workplace, and it never requires you to retreat into greater danger. Whether a safe escape existed is judged on the facts at the time, which is why these cases turn on details.

Some states sit in the middle: no statute says you must retreat, but a jury may still weigh whether you could have walked away when deciding if your force was reasonable. Those states are marked “It depends” in the state card below, with the reason in the note.

Okay, so what do I do? If you can safely walk away from a confrontation in public, walk away. It is the safest choice physically and legally, and in a duty-to-retreat state it is what the law requires.

Deadly force: the limits

Deadly force is justified only when you reasonably believe it is necessary to prevent imminent death, great bodily harm, or a serious violent felony against you or someone else. That is the rule in every state, including stand-your-ground states. Deadly force is never justified to protect property alone, against an insult, or against someone who is already fleeing.

If you were the initial aggressor — you started the fight — you usually lose the self-defense claim unless you clearly tried to stop and communicated that you wanted to stop. If you were committing a crime when you used force, the protection is usually lost too.

Okay, so what do I do? Use the least force that will keep you safe, and stop as soon as the threat stops. The moment the attacker is down, disarmed, or running away, the legal justification for force ends.

After you use force: what to do

Call 911. Report that you were attacked and that you defended yourself, and ask for medical help if anyone is hurt. Then say very little about the facts until you have a lawyer: give your name, say you were attacked, say you want a lawyer, and stop there. Anything you say to police can be used against you, even if you believe you were fully justified.

Do not leave the scene if it is safe to stay, do not move or clean up evidence, and do not talk about what happened on social media. Write down what happened as soon as you can — who, where, when, and what the other person did — and save any photos, video, or messages. If there were witnesses, get their names.

Okay, so what do I do? Call 911, ask for a lawyer, and say nothing about the facts without one. Then call the free legal help in the state card below — they can point you to a criminal defense lawyer.

What self-defense does not cover

Self-defense is not a free pass. It does not cover force used to get revenge after an attack is over, force against someone who is fleeing, force used because you were insulted or offended, or force to protect property alone. It does not cover a fight you started and then escalated, and it does not cover using more force than the threat required.

It also does not automatically protect you from a civil lawsuit: some states give you immunity from being sued when your force was justified, but many do not, and even in states that do, you may have to prove the force was justified first.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are unsure whether your situation counts as self-defense, treat it as uncertain and get a lawyer before you say or do anything that assumes you were justified.

Self-defense & use of force in Texas

Stand your ground? Yes. A person who has a right to be present, has not provoked the other person, and is not engaged in criminal activity is not required to retreat before using force or deadly force.

Castle doctrine? Yes. A person's belief that deadly force was immediately necessary is presumed reasonable if the other person unlawfully and forcibly entered or tried to enter their occupied habitation, vehicle, or place of business or employment, or was forcibly removing them from one.

Duty to retreat outside the home? No. Texas removed the general duty to retreat in 2007, and a jury may not consider a failure to retreat when deciding whether a qualifying person's belief that deadly force was necessary was reasonable.

The law: Self-Defense — Tex. Penal Code §§ 9.31–9.32.

Deadly force is still limited to stopping unlawful deadly force or preventing aggravated kidnapping, murder, sexual assault, aggravated sexual assault, robbery, or aggravated robbery, so do not use it over property or an insult alone.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the three answers above for your state, remember that deadly force is only for imminent death or serious injury, and if you ever use force, call 911 and a lawyer before you explain what happened.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Learn your state’s three answers above — stand your ground, castle doctrine, duty to retreat. (2) Remember the limits: no force for property alone, no force against a fleeing person, no force if you started it. (3) If you use force, call 911, say you were attacked, ask for a lawyer, and say nothing about the facts without one. (4) If you are charged, get a criminal defense lawyer — the free legal help above can point you to one. (5) If you carry a firearm, see the Carry permit reciprocity & travel and Pulled Over sections on this page.

Sources: the official state statutes and court sources linked in the research for this section, checked November 20, 2026. Self-defense law is fact-specific and changes — confirm the current law with a lawyer before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Social Security retirement & survivors benefits in Texas

Social Security pays a monthly benefit to retired workers and to the families of workers who have died. This section covers two kinds of benefits: retirement benefits from your own work record, and survivors benefits from the work record of a spouse, parent, or ex-spouse who has died. It also covers whether Texas taxes those benefits, and what to do if the Social Security Administration (SSA) says no. This is the picture as of November 2026. This is general information, not legal advice. The money is yours by law once you qualify — the hard part is usually knowing when to claim and what to do when something goes wrong.

Retirement benefits: when can you claim?

You qualify for retirement benefits once you have earned 40 work credits — roughly 10 years of work. Your full retirement age is 66 or 67, depending on the year you were born. You can start as early as age 62, but the check is smaller for life. You can also wait past full retirement age, up to age 70, and the check grows each month you wait. There is no one right answer: claiming early means more checks but smaller ones; waiting means fewer checks but bigger ones.

Apply online at ssa.gov, by phone at 1-800-772-1213, or at a local Social Security office. You can check your own estimated benefit any time by opening a my Social Security account at ssa.gov/myaccount — it shows your earnings record and what you would get at 62, at full retirement age, and at 70. Check that your earnings record is correct: a missing year of earnings lowers your check.

Okay, so what do I do? Open your my Social Security account and look at the three estimates. Then decide: do you need the money now, or can you afford to wait for a bigger check? If you are unsure, the free SHIP counselors in the state card below can walk through the trade-off with you.

Survivors benefits: who can get them

When a worker dies, Social Security can pay monthly benefits to their family from that worker’s record. A widow or widower can claim at 60 (or 50 if disabled), or at any age while caring for the worker’s child under 16. Unmarried children can claim under 18, or under 19 if still in high school. A dependent parent age 62 or older can claim. A divorced spouse can also claim on an ex-spouse’s record if the marriage lasted at least 10 years.

There is also a one-time lump-sum death payment of $255 to a surviving spouse, or to a child if there is no surviving spouse. Survivors usually have to apply — the benefits do not always start on their own. Apply as soon as you can: SSA can only backdate payments a few months, so a late application can cost you money. Call 1-800-772-1213 or visit a local office; survivors claims generally cannot be finished online.

Okay, so what do I do? If your spouse or parent has died, call SSA promptly and ask about survivors benefits on their record. Bring the death certificate, your marriage certificate or birth certificate, and the worker’s Social Security number.

How much will you get?

The amount is based on the worker’s earnings record — the longer and higher-paid the work, the bigger the check. Your my Social Security account shows your own estimate. A spouse can also claim a spousal benefit on their partner’s record, and a widow or widower can switch between their own benefit and the survivors benefit, taking whichever is bigger. You cannot collect two full benefits at once — SSA pays the higher of the two.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your estimate at ssa.gov/myaccount, and check your spouse’s too if you are married. If you are a widow or widower, ask SSA to compare your own benefit with the survivors benefit before you pick one.

Working while you collect

If you claim before full retirement age and keep working, there is a yearly earnings limit: earn over it and SSA withholds part of your benefits. Once you reach full retirement age there is no limit — you can earn as much as you want with no reduction. The withheld money is not lost: when you reach full retirement age, SSA recalculates and raises your monthly check to give it back over time.

Okay, so what do I do? If you claim early and keep working, tell SSA about your earnings so the withholding is right. If you can wait until full retirement age to claim, the earnings limit stops mattering.

Are the benefits taxed?

At the federal level, yes, for many people: depending on your total income, up to 85% of your benefits can count as taxable income. At the state level, most states do not tax Social Security benefits at all, and a few tax only part of them. Your state’s rule is in the state card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the state card below for your state’s rule. If your state taxes benefits only above an income limit, that limit is in the note — and a tax preparer or the free SHIP counselors can help you plan around it.

If SSA says no, or says you were overpaid

If SSA denies your claim, you have 60 days to appeal at every stage: first a reconsideration, then a hearing before a judge, then the Appeals Council, then federal court. Do not miss the 60 days — a late appeal can end the claim. If SSA says you were overpaid and wants the money back, you can ask for a waiver if the overpayment was not your fault and you cannot afford to repay it, or ask to repay it in small monthly amounts.

If the denial is about disability, see the Social Security Disability & SSI section on this page — it walks through the disability appeal steps. The legal-aid office in the state card below can help with any of these appeals, usually for free.

Okay, so what do I do? Read the denial letter for the deadline, file the appeal within 60 days, and call legal aid or the SHIP counselors below for help. Do not just re-apply — appeal instead, or you lose your place in line.

Social Security retirement & survivors benefits in Texas

Does Texas tax Social Security benefits? No. Texas has no state personal income tax, so Social Security benefits are not taxed by the state (federal tax on benefits may still apply).

Texas has three regional legal-aid providers; Lone Star Legal Aid serves 72 counties in East and Southeast Texas, while Legal Aid of NorthWest Texas and Texas RioGrande Legal Aid cover the rest of the state.

Free Medicare and benefits counseling: Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP) — free, one-on-one help with Medicare and Social Security questions from trained counselors who do not sell insurance.

Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP) phone: 800-252-9240.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your own benefit estimate at ssa.gov/myaccount, check whether your state taxes benefits above, and call the free counseling line or legal aid if SSA denies a claim or says you were overpaid.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Open your my Social Security account at ssa.gov/myaccount and check your earnings record and your estimates at 62, full retirement age, and 70. (2) If a spouse or parent has died, call SSA at 1-800-772-1213 promptly about survivors benefits — do not wait. (3) Check the state card above for whether your state taxes benefits. (4) If SSA denies a claim or claims an overpayment, appeal within 60 days and get free help from legal aid or the SHIP counselors above. (5) If the claim is about disability, see the Social Security Disability & SSI section on this page.

Sources: the Social Security Administration (ssa.gov), the official Texas revenue and SHIP sources linked above, checked November 20, 2026. Benefit amounts and tax thresholds change — confirm the current numbers with SSA or your state revenue department before you decide. This is general information, not legal advice.

Wrongful death in Texas

When someone dies because of another person’s or company’s negligence or wrongdoing — a careless driver, a dangerous product, a doctor’s mistake, an unsafe workplace — the surviving family may have a civil claim called a wrongful death claim. It is separate from any criminal case: the state can prosecute a killing whether or not the family sues, and the family can sue whether or not anyone is charged. The claim is about money — the income, support, and companionship the family lost — not about punishment. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — who can file, the deadline, what can be recovered, and what to do first. This is general information, not legal advice. The deadlines are short, so do not wait: in most states you have one to three years from the death to file.

What is a wrongful death claim?

A wrongful death claim says the death was caused by someone else’s fault — negligence, recklessness, or an intentional act — and that the survivors lost something real because of it: income the person would have earned, care and guidance for the children, companionship for a spouse, funeral and medical bills. The claim belongs to the survivors and the estate, not to the person who died.

It is not the same as a criminal case. A criminal case is brought by the state and can end in prison. A wrongful death case is brought by the family and can end in money damages. The two can run at the same time, and a family can win a civil case even if the criminal case fails — the burden of proof is lower in civil court.

Common causes: car and truck crashes, medical mistakes, dangerous drugs or products, workplace accidents, nursing-home neglect, and violence. If the person who died could have sued for their injuries had they lived, the family usually has a wrongful death claim.

Okay, so what do I do? The next cards are your checklist. The most important thing to know up front is that the clock is already running — see the deadline card below.

Who can file

Every state limits who may bring the claim. In most states the claim is filed by the personal representative of the estate (the executor named in a will, or an administrator appointed by the probate court) on behalf of the survivors. In some states the surviving spouse, children, or parents file directly. Who counts as a survivor, and how the money is divided among them, is set by state law — your state’s rule is in the state card below.

If there is no will, a family member can ask the probate court to be appointed as personal representative. That step is usually quick, and a probate lawyer or legal-aid office can walk you through it.

Okay, so what do I do? Find out who your state allows to file (state card below). If you are the spouse or a parent and no estate has been opened, ask the probate court about being appointed personal representative — that is usually the first legal step.

The deadline: the clock is short

Every state sets a statute of limitations for wrongful death — usually one to three years from the date of death. Miss it and the claim is gone, no matter how strong the facts are. A few states pause the clock for minor children, or start it when the cause of death is discovered rather than on the date of death. Your state’s deadline is in the state card below.

Two traps: (1) if a government agency is involved (a city bus, a public hospital, a state road), many states require a formal notice of claim within months — far shorter than the lawsuit deadline. (2) A criminal investigation does not pause the civil deadline. Do not wait for charges or a trial to start the claim.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s deadline in the card below and talk to a lawyer or legal-aid office well before it. Most wrongful-death lawyers work on contingency — no fee unless you win — and the first consultation is usually free.

What money can be recovered

Damages usually fall into a few buckets: economic losses — the income and benefits the person would have earned, medical bills before death, funeral and burial costs; loss of companionship and guidance — what a spouse, child, or parent lost in the relationship; and in some states, the person’s own pain and suffering before death (a separate “survival” claim). A few states allow punitive damages to punish especially reckless conduct; some cap non-economic damages. What your state allows is in the state card below.

There is no fixed formula. Lawyers and courts look at the person’s age, earnings, health, and the family’s dependence on them. Keep every bill and every record of income — pay stubs, tax returns, benefit statements.

Okay, so what do I do? Start a folder now: the death certificate, medical and funeral bills, pay stubs and tax returns, and any insurance letters. Do not throw anything away.

What to do right now

Preserve evidence. Photos of the scene or vehicle, medical records, the police or accident report, names of witnesses. Evidence disappears fast — vehicles get repaired, records get purged, memories fade. Ask a lawyer to send a preservation letter if a company or hospital holds records.

Do not give a recorded statement to an insurer. The other side’s insurance company may call quickly and ask for a statement or offer a fast settlement. You are not required to give a recorded statement, and an early offer is almost always less than the claim is worth. Talk to a lawyer first.

Do not sign anything. Releases, settlements, or waivers from an insurer, an employer, or a hospital can end the claim. Have a lawyer or legal-aid office read anything before you sign.

Get the personal representative appointed. If the claim must be filed by the estate, open probate and get appointed — the court, a probate lawyer, or legal aid can help, and it is usually straightforward.

Okay, so what do I do? Preserve the evidence, say no to recorded statements and quick settlements, get the estate opened, and call a wrongful-death lawyer or legal aid — the first call is usually free, and most take these cases on contingency.

What NOT to do

Do not wait out the deadline. Grief is real, and the legal clock does not pause for it. A lawyer can file the claim while the family grieves — that is the point of hiring one.

Do not post about the death or the accident on social media. Insurers and defense lawyers read it, and a post can be used against the claim.

Do not accept the first settlement offer. Early offers come before the full losses are known — future income, ongoing care for children, the full medical picture. Once you sign, it is over.

Do not assume a criminal case covers it. A conviction does not pay the family, and an acquittal does not end the civil claim. They are separate tracks.

Wrongful death in Texas

Who can file: Only the surviving spouse, children (including adopted children), and parents of the person who died may bring the claim; any one of them may file for the benefit of all. If none of them files within 3 months of the death, the executor or administrator of the estate must file instead. Siblings, grandparents, and unmarried partners cannot file. (Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 71.004)

The law: Texas Wrongful Death Act (Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 71.002).

Deadline in Texas: 2 years from the date of death. (Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 16.003(b)) The discovery rule does not extend this deadline. The clock can be paused (tolled) if a beneficiary is a minor or of unsound mind, or if the defendant fraudulently concealed the cause of action. Claims against a government entity generally require written notice within 6 months.

What can be recovered: Survivors can recover lost financial support and earnings, lost household services, loss of companionship and society, mental anguish, loss of inheritance, and funeral and burial costs. There is generally no cap on these compensatory damages. Exemplary (punitive) damages are allowed only for a willful act or omission or gross negligence, proved by clear and convincing evidence, and are capped at the greater of $200,000 or two times the economic damages plus non-economic damages up to $750,000. (Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code §§ 71.009, 71.010, 41.008)

Texas also allows a separate 'survival' claim for the estate, covering what the person who died could have recovered had they lived (such as medical bills and pre-death pain and suffering). Note this legal-aid program serves 72 counties in East and Southeast Texas; Legal Aid of NorthWest Texas and Texas RioGrande Legal Aid serve the rest of the state.

Legal aid phone: 1-800-733-8394.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? Get the estate opened if one is needed, preserve the evidence, and call a wrongful-death lawyer or the legal-aid office above well before the deadline. The first consultation is usually free, and most of these cases are taken on contingency.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Get the death certificate and start a folder with bills, pay stubs, and tax returns. (2) Preserve evidence: photos, reports, witness names, medical records. (3) Do not give a recorded statement or sign anything from an insurer. (4) Get a personal representative appointed if the estate must file. (5) Call a wrongful-death lawyer or legal aid well before your state’s deadline — the first call is usually free. (6) Do not post about the case on social media.

Sources: the official Texas statute, court, and legal-aid sources linked above, checked November 20, 2026. Deadlines and damage rules change — confirm the current deadline with a lawyer or the court before you file. This is general information, not legal advice.

Sexual harassment at work in Texas

Sexual harassment at work is illegal. Under federal law (Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964), your employer may not allow sexual harassment that affects your job — and most states have their own law that covers smaller employers the federal law does not reach. You do not have to prove you were fired or demoted: harassment that creates a hostile work environment is enough. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — what counts, what to do right now, the deadlines, and where to file a complaint. This is general information, not legal advice. The deadlines are short, so do not wait: in most states you have months, not years, to file. If you are in immediate danger, call 911. To file a federal complaint, call the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC) at 1-800-669-4000.

What counts as sexual harassment

Quid pro quo (“this for that”): a boss or supervisor conditions a job benefit — a hire, a promotion, a raise, keeping your job — on sexual favors, or punishes you for refusing. One incident can be enough.

Hostile work environment: unwelcome sexual conduct so severe or repeated that it makes your workplace abusive — sexual comments or jokes, leering, unwanted touching, sexual pictures or messages, repeated unwanted invitations, or sexual rumors about you. It does not have to come from your boss: a coworker, a client, or a customer can create it, and your employer is responsible if it knew or should have known and did nothing.

It counts even if the harasser is the same sex as you, even if it happens outside the office (a work trip, a work party, a text message after hours), and even if nobody else saw it. What matters is that the conduct was unwelcome and based on sex. A single very serious incident (like an assault) can qualify on its own; lesser conduct usually has to be repeated.

Okay, so what do I do? If it happened to you, the next card is your checklist. If you are not sure it “counts,” write down what happened anyway — investigators and lawyers decide that question, and they need the facts.

What to do right now

Write it down, today. For each incident: the date, time, place, exactly what was said or done, who was there, and how it affected your work. Keep adding to the list. Memory fades; a written log is evidence.

Save everything. Texts, emails, messages, photos, schedules, performance reviews, pay stubs. Screenshot messages before they can be deleted, and keep copies outside of work — not only on a work computer or phone you could lose access to.

Tell the harasser to stop, if you safely can. A clear “stop, that is not okay” helps show the conduct was unwelcome. If saying it in person is unsafe or could cost you your job, skip this step — it is not required.

Report it in writing. Tell your supervisor, HR, or whoever your employer’s policy names — in writing (email counts), keeping a copy for yourself. Say what happened, when, and what you want: for it to stop. If the harasser is your supervisor, report to HR or to their boss. Your employer’s duty to act usually starts when it knows.

Okay, so what do I do? Start the written log today, save copies off your work devices, and put the complaint in writing. Then watch the deadlines in the next card — reporting to your employer does not pause the filing clock.

The deadlines: the clock is short

Federal law gives you 180 days from the harassment to file a charge with the EEOC — 300 days if your state has its own fair-employment agency. For ongoing harassment, the clock runs from the last incident, but earlier incidents still count as evidence. State deadlines are all over the map: some are 180 days, some are a year, a few are two or three years. Your state’s deadline is in the state card below.

Two traps: (1) trying to resolve it inside the company does not pause the clock — the EEOC says so plainly. (2) The state deadline and the federal deadline are different clocks. File with whichever agency has the shorter deadline first, and ask about dual-filing — in many states one filing counts for both.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your state’s deadline in the card below, and file well before it. If you are anywhere near the limit, call the agency or the EEOC (1-800-669-4000) today and ask how much time you have left.

How to file a complaint

Step 1: pick the agency. If your state has a fair-employment agency (most do — see the state card below), file there; it usually dual-files with the EEOC, so one filing protects both claims. If your state has no agency, or your employer is big enough for federal law (15 or more employees), file with the EEOC directly — online, by phone at 1-800-669-4000, or at a field office.

Step 2: the charge. You describe what happened, when, and who did it. The agency may interview you, ask for your log and saved messages, and contact your employer. Filing is free, and you do not need a lawyer to file.

Step 3: investigation, then a decision. The agency investigates and either finds cause (and tries to settle it or sues on your behalf) or closes the case. If it closes, you get a “right-to-sue” letter — usually 90 days from that letter to file a lawsuit in court. You can also ask for a right-to-sue letter earlier in many states.

Okay, so what do I do? File with the agency in the state card below (or the EEOC if there is none). Bring your written log and your saved messages. Do not sign anything from your employer about the harassment without reading the next card first.

Retaliation is illegal too

It is illegal for your employer to punish you for complaining about harassment, filing a charge, or helping a coworker’s complaint — firing you, demoting you, cutting your hours, giving you worse shifts, or suddenly writing you up. This is called retaliation, and it is a separate violation even if the harassment claim itself is hard to prove.

Write down any change in how you are treated after you complain, with dates. If you are fired or punished after complaining, tell the agency right away — a retaliation claim has its own deadline, and it usually starts on the day of the retaliatory act.

Okay, so what do I do? Keep logging. If anything about your job gets worse after you report, that goes in the log too, and you tell the agency.

What NOT to do

Do not delete evidence. Do not delete texts, emails, or photos, and do not “clean up” your side of a message thread.

Do not sign a severance, settlement, or release without reading it closely. These papers often include a clause giving up your right to sue or to talk about what happened. Ask a lawyer or a legal-aid office to read it first — many will do that for free.

Do not wait out the deadline. Hoping HR fixes it, waiting for a transfer, or waiting until you find a new job can all run out the filing clock. You can keep working with HR and file on time.

Do not quit in anger without a plan, if you can avoid it. Quitting can make a claim harder (and can affect unemployment benefits), though staying in an abusive workplace is not required either. If you are thinking of quitting, talk to a lawyer or legal aid first — it is a free call in most states.

Do not confront the harasser alone if it feels unsafe. Report through HR, the agency, or the police if there was an assault. Your safety comes before any procedure.

Where to file in Texas

State agency: Texas Workforce Commission, Civil Rights Division (TWC CRD).

The law: Texas Labor Code Chapter 21 (Texas Commission on Human Rights Act) (Tex. Lab. Code § 21.202).

Deadline in Texas: For sexual harassment, file with the TWC Civil Rights Division within 300 days of the harassment; most other discrimination claims have a 180-day state deadline. The EEOC deadline in Texas is 300 days.

Texas sexual-harassment protections apply to employers with just 1 or more employees, unlike the 15-employee threshold for most other discrimination claims under Chapter 21. TRLA is the largest legal-aid provider in Texas and handles labor and employment cases.

Free legal help: Texas RioGrande Legal Aid (TRLA).

Okay, so what do I do? Start your written log today, report the harassment in writing at work, and file with the agency above (or the EEOC at 1-800-669-4000) well before the deadline. Bring your log and your saved messages.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Start a written log today: dates, exact words, witnesses. (2) Save texts, emails, and photos off your work devices. (3) Report it in writing at work and keep a copy. (4) File with your state agency — or the EEOC at 1-800-669-4000 — well before the deadline in the card above. (5) If you are punished for complaining, log that too and tell the agency. (6) Do not sign a release or severance without a free legal-aid review first.

Sources: U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (Title VII; time limits for filing a charge; 1-800-669-4000), and the official Texas agency, statute, and legal-aid sources linked above, checked November 20, 2026. Deadlines and agency procedures change — confirm the current deadline with the agency before you file. This is general information, not legal advice.

What to do if you’re arrested in Texas

Getting arrested is frightening, and the first hour matters more than most people realize. What you say — and what you don’t say — in those first minutes can follow your case all the way to trial. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — your rights in the first minutes, booking, bail, arraignment, pleas, diversion, and how to get a public defender. This is general information, not legal advice. If you are arrested right now, the short version is: stay silent, ask for a lawyer, and do not consent to searches.

The first minutes: stay silent, ask for a lawyer

You have the right to remain silent (Miranda v. Arizona, 1966). Anything you say can and will be used against you in court. You do not have to explain yourself, apologize, or talk your way out of it — and trying usually makes things worse.

Say this, out loud and clearly: “I am exercising my right to remain silent. I want a lawyer.” Then stop talking — to the police, to other people in the cell, and on the jail phone, which is usually recorded. Repeat the request if you are questioned again.

Do not consent to searches. Police can search you and the area within your reach when they arrest you, and they can search with a warrant or in a genuine emergency. For anything else, they need your permission or a warrant. “I do not consent to a search” is a sentence worth knowing. Say it calmly, and do not physically resist if they search anyway — your lawyer can challenge an illegal search in court later.

Do not resist, run, or fight. Even if the arrest is wrong, resisting adds charges and can get you hurt. Stay calm, keep your hands visible, and remember names, badge numbers, and what happened — write it down as soon as you can.

Okay, so what do I do? Say the two sentences above, then say nothing else about the case until a lawyer is with you.

Booking and the phone call

After arrest, police take you to be booked: photo, fingerprints, a record of your belongings, and a check for warrants. You will be held until your first court appearance, until bail is posted, or until you are released on your own recognizance (a signed promise to come back to court).

The phone call. You generally get to make calls to reach a lawyer or family — but jail calls are usually recorded, so do not discuss the facts of your case on the phone. Use the call to get a lawyer, tell someone where you are, and ask them to bring your ID and any medicine you need.

Okay, so what do I do? Call a lawyer first if you have one. If not, call a family member and ask them to find one — or wait for the public defender at your first court appearance (next card). Do not talk about the case on a recorded line.

Bail, arraignment, and your plea

Bail is money or a promise that gets you out of jail while your case is pending. A judge sets it — or releases you without money — based on the charge, your ties to the community, your record, and whether you are likely to come back to court. How bail works in your state is in the state card below. More detail is in Bail & bail bondsmen on this page.

Arraignment is your first court appearance. The judge reads the charges, makes sure you have a lawyer, and asks for your plea. You will usually be arraigned within a day or two of arrest.

Your plea options: not guilty (the usual first plea — it keeps every option open and does not mean you are lying); guilty (you admit the charge and accept the sentence); or no contest (you do not admit guilt, but you accept the punishment — not available everywhere). Never plead guilty at arraignment just to get it over with. Plead not guilty, get a lawyer, and decide with them.

Okay, so what do I do? At arraignment, plead not guilty and ask for a lawyer if you do not have one. Write down your next court date before you leave — missing it can mean a warrant for your arrest.

Getting a public defender

If you cannot afford a lawyer, the court must appoint one for you (Gideon v. Wainwright, 1963, decided unanimously by the U.S. Supreme Court). This is not a favor — it is a constitutional right, and it applies whether the charge is a felony or a misdemeanor that could put you in jail.

How to get one: at your first court appearance, tell the judge you cannot afford a lawyer and ask for appointed counsel. The court will ask about your income, job, and expenses to decide if you qualify. Be honest — lying about your finances can be a separate problem. If you qualify, a public defender is assigned, usually that same day.

Who qualifies: anyone who cannot afford a private lawyer. The exact income line varies by state and county. If you are turned down and you still cannot afford a lawyer, say so and ask what the appeal or review process is — and check the free legal help in the state card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask the judge at your first appearance. Do not wait until trial to ask — the earlier a lawyer is on your case, the more they can do for you.

Diversion: a way to avoid a conviction

Diversion (also called deferred prosecution or pretrial intervention) lets some people — usually first-time, non-violent offenders — complete conditions like community service, counseling, classes, or restitution instead of going to trial. Finish the conditions and the charge is dismissed, usually with no conviction on your record.

Every state runs it differently: some have a statewide program, some leave it to each county’s prosecutor, and some only offer it for certain charges (often drug possession or first-time misdemeanors). Whether it is available for your charge is in the state card below.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask your lawyer — or the prosecutor, if you do not have a lawyer yet — whether diversion is available for your charge, and ask early: some programs have short application deadlines. Do not admit anything to get into a program without a lawyer’s advice.

What NOT to do

Do not talk to the police about the case without a lawyer — not to explain, not to apologize, not to “clear things up.”

Do not post about it on social media. Posts, photos, messages, and even deleted posts can be used as evidence.

Do not contact the alleged victim or witnesses — a text meant as an apology can become a new charge (witness tampering or violating a no-contact order).

Do not miss court. A missed date usually means a bench warrant and possibly losing your bail. If you cannot make a date, call the court and your lawyer before the date, not after.

Do not leave the state, skip town, or lie to your lawyer. Your lawyer can only help you with the truth, and they cannot tell the police what you tell them.

Related guides on this page: Pulled over (traffic stops), DUI / DWI, and Probation.

Public defender, bail, and diversion in Texas

Public defender: Texas Indigent Defense Commission — no office website link could be verified; ask the judge for appointed counsel at your first court appearance.

Texas has no statewide public defender office. Each county appoints counsel under the Fair Defense Act, overseen by the Texas Indigent Defense Commission. Ask the judge for appointed counsel at your first appearance.

Bail in Texas: Texas has no statewide public defender office; each county appoints counsel under the Fair Defense Act, overseen by the Texas Indigent Defense Commission. Under Senate Bill 9 (2025), a person charged with a felony must appear before a magistrate who considers a public safety report before bail is set, and personal (no-cash) bonds are barred for a longer list of offenses, including unlawful possession of a firearm and violation of a family-violence protective order.

Diversion in Texas: Texas prosecutors operate pretrial intervention programs, and Senate Bill 9 (2025) requires the state to enter the conditions of a defendant's pretrial intervention program into the statewide law-enforcement database within 10 business days of the defendant entering the program.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are arrested, say you want a lawyer and stay silent. At your first court appearance, ask the judge for a public defender if you cannot afford a lawyer, plead not guilty, and ask about diversion before any deadlines pass.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Say “I am exercising my right to remain silent. I want a lawyer.” Then stop talking about the case. (2) Do not consent to searches and do not resist. (3) At your first court appearance, ask for a public defender if you cannot afford a lawyer, and plead not guilty. (4) Ask about diversion early. (5) Go to every court date, and do not post about the case or contact victims or witnesses.

Sources: U.S. Supreme Court (Miranda v. Arizona, 1966; Gideon v. Wainwright, 1963), and the official Texas statutes, court rules, and agencies linked above, checked November 20, 2026. Bail and diversion rules change — confirm the current rules with your lawyer or the court before you decide. This is general information, not legal advice.

Social Security Disability & SSI in Texas

If a health problem keeps you from working, Social Security has two programs that may pay you monthly benefits: SSDI and SSI. They have different rules, and you can apply for both at the same time. Most people are turned down the first time — that is normal, and it is not the end of the road. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — which program fits you, the five-step test Social Security uses, how to apply, what to do if you are denied, and where to get free help. This is general information, not legal advice.

SSDI vs SSI: which one fits you?

SSDI (Social Security Disability Insurance) is for people who worked and paid Social Security taxes. You need enough work credits — in 2026, one credit is $1,890 of earnings, and you can earn at most 4 credits a year. Most adults need 40 credits, with 20 of them earned in the last 10 years. Your monthly amount depends on your earnings record. There is a five-month waiting period after Social Security says you are disabled before payments start, and Medicare starts 24 months after that.

SSI (Supplemental Security Income) is for people with limited income and few assets — no work history needed. In 2026 the limits are $2,000 in countable assets for one person, $3,000 for a couple (your home and one car usually do not count). The most SSI pays in 2026 is $994 a month for one person, $1,491 for a couple — your state may add a small supplement on top. SSI usually comes with Medicaid right away.

You can get both at the same time if you qualify for both. Apply for both — Social Security will sort out which ones you qualify for.

Okay, so what do I do? If you worked for years and then had to stop, start with SSDI. If you have little income and few savings, start with SSI. If you are not sure, apply for both — the application covers both programs.

The five-step test Social Security uses

Every claim goes through the same five questions, in order. A “no” at step 5 means you are approved.

Step 1 — Are you working? If you earn more than $1,690 a month in 2026 ($2,830 if you are blind), Social Security says you are doing “substantial gainful activity” and stops there.

Step 2 — Is your condition severe? It must significantly limit basic work activities — walking, standing, lifting, remembering, concentrating, getting along with people — and be expected to last at least 12 months or result in death.

Step 3 — Does it meet a listing? Social Security has a book of conditions (“the listings”). If yours matches one, you are approved. Most conditions do not match a listing exactly, so most claims move on.

Step 4 — Can you do your past work? If you can still do any job you held in the last 15 years, you are denied. If not, on to step 5.

Step 5 — Can you do any other work? Social Security looks at your age, education, work history, and what you can still do, and asks whether any job exists in the national economy that you could do. If the answer is no, you are approved. Older workers with limited education and a long work history have the best odds at this step.

Okay, so what do I do? When you describe your condition, describe what you cannot do — not just the diagnosis. “I have a bad back” is weak. “I cannot sit for more than 20 minutes, I cannot lift a gallon of milk, and I have to lie down twice a day” is what the examiner needs.

How to apply, and what evidence to gather

Three ways to apply: online at SSA’s online disability application, by phone at 1-800-772-1213 (weekdays, 8 a.m. to 7 p.m. local time), or in person at your local Social Security office (find it at SSA’s office locator). You apply to Social Security first. Social Security checks the non-medical rules (work credits for SSDI, income and assets for SSI), then sends your file to your state’s Disability Determination Services office, which makes the medical decision.

Gather this before you apply: the names, addresses, and phone numbers of every doctor, clinic, and hospital that treated you; the dates you were seen; a list of your medicines; your work history for the last 15 years; and your most recent W-2 or tax return. Social Security will ask for your medical records directly, but it is faster if you bring copies of the most important ones yourself.

Two things that help a lot: a short written statement from your doctor describing your limits (not just your diagnosis), and your own notes about a typical day — what hurts, what you cannot finish, how often you have to rest. If Social Security sends you to one of its own doctors for an exam, go — missing it can get your claim denied.

Can I try working? Yes. In 2026 you can earn up to $1,210 a month for up to 9 months (the “trial work period”) without losing SSDI. Tell Social Security before you start.

Okay, so what do I do? Apply as soon as you stop working — do not wait until your savings run out, because the process takes months and the five-month SSDI waiting period only starts once you are found disabled.

If you are denied: appeal, don’t give up

Most first applications are denied. That is the normal path, not a final answer. There are four appeal stages, and you have 60 days to appeal at every stage. Miss the deadline and you have to start over.

1. Reconsideration. A different examiner looks at your file fresh. Send in any new medical records with it.

2. Hearing before an administrative law judge (ALJ). This is where most people win. You can testify, bring witnesses, and have a representative. Hearings are usually by phone or video now. It can take a year or more to get a hearing date — appeal anyway, and keep seeing your doctors while you wait.

3. Appeals Council. It reviews the judge’s decision for legal mistakes. It can deny review, send the case back, or decide it itself.

4. Federal court. The last step — a federal judge reviews whether Social Security followed the law. You will almost certainly want a lawyer for this stage.

Okay, so what do I do? The day a denial letter arrives, mark the 60-day deadline on your calendar and appeal in writing. Do not file a brand-new application instead of appealing — you lose your back-pay start date if you do.

Free help: legal aid and disability lawyers

Legal aid (free, in the state card below) helps low-income people apply and appeal. Many legal aid offices handle Social Security cases.

Disability lawyers work on contingency — you pay nothing up front and nothing at all unless you win. If you win, the fee comes out of your back pay: 25%, capped at $9,200. The fee has to be approved by Social Security. A lawyer is most useful at the hearing stage, but many will take a case earlier.

Social Security itself answers questions for free at 1-800-772-1213 — including TTY at 1-800-325-0778.

Okay, so what do I do? If you were denied, call the legal aid office below or a disability lawyer before your 60 days run out. The first consultation is free — ask exactly that before you give anyone your information.

Disability decisions and free help in Texas

Your claim starts with Social Security, but the medical decision is made by your state’s Disability Determination Services (DDS) office — the state agency that decides whether you meet Social Security’s medical rules.

Disability Determination Services: Texas Disability Determination Services (Division for Disability Determination Services, Texas Health and Human Services Commission) — no standalone DDS webpage could be verified, so apply through Social Security (1-800-772-1213 or the online application above) and Social Security will send your file to the DDS office.

Free legal help: Lone Star Legal Aid.

About Texas: Texas DDS operates as a division of the Texas Health and Human Services Commission, with its central office in Austin; in Texas, SSI approval also brings Medicaid enrollment.

Okay, so what do I do? Apply through Social Security first — 1-800-772-1213 or the online application — then watch for mail from the DDS office. Answer every DDS letter and form quickly: the DDS decides your case on the records it has, and silence looks like you got better.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Apply for both SSDI and SSI through Social Security as soon as you stop working. (2) Gather your doctors’ names and your medical records, and describe what you cannot do — not just your diagnosis. (3) If you are denied, appeal within 60 days at every stage; do not file a new application instead. (4) Get free help before the deadline — legal aid or a disability lawyer who only gets paid if you win.

Sources: Social Security Administration (2026 work credit, substantial gainful activity, trial work period, SSI federal benefit rate and asset limits, 60-day appeal deadlines, DDS directory), Federal Register, and the official Texas agencies linked above, checked November 20, 2026. Benefit amounts and limits change each year — confirm the current figures with Social Security before you decide. This is general information, not legal advice.

Pensions & retirement benefits in Texas
