Your pension is money you already earned. If your employer is sold, merges, or goes out of business, the benefits you have already earned — and vested in — generally cannot be taken away from you. Federal law (ERISA, the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974) protects them. What can change is the future: a new owner can freeze the plan so nobody earns more benefits going forward. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — what vesting means, what a sale can and can’t change, what the federal pension insurer covers, what to do with a 401(k), and where to complain. This is general information, not legal advice.

Your pension after a sale or merger

When a company is sold, the buyer usually takes over the pension plan along with the business. Your earned benefits stay yours — a sale does not erase them.

What can change: the new owner can freeze the plan, so nobody earns more benefits after the freeze date. Benefits already earned stay earned. If the plan ends entirely, the federal PBGC (below) may take over and pay benefits up to its limits.

Okay, so what do I do? Get your Summary Plan Description (SPD) and your latest benefit statement in writing — from HR or the plan administrator — and keep copies. Write down the plan’s name and the administrator’s contact information. Do not sign anything about your pension until you have read it — and if it is a buyout or a waiver, talk it through with someone you trust first (see the buyout card below).

Vesting: when the pension is really yours

Vesting is the point where the employer’s contributions to your pension become yours to keep, even if you leave the job.

Traditional pension (defined benefit): either a “cliff” — nothing until 5 years, then 100% vested — or “graded” — 20% after 3 years, rising to 100% after 7 years. Cash-balance plans use a 3-year cliff.

401(k) and other defined-contribution plans: money you put in yourself is always 100% yours. Employer matching money vests on a 3-year cliff or graded over 2 to 6 years.

Once you are vested, those benefits generally cannot be taken away — even if the company is sold.

Okay, so what do I do? Check your benefit statement or SPD for your vesting date. If you are close to vesting, know the date before you make any job decision.

Is your pension insured? The PBGC

The Pension Benefit Guaranty Corporation (PBGC), a federal agency, insures most private-sector traditional (defined-benefit) pensions. It does not insure 401(k)s, profit-sharing plans, IRAs, church plans, or government pensions.

If a covered plan ends without enough money, the PBGC takes over and pays benefits up to a legal maximum. For plans ending in 2026, the maximum is $7,789.77 a month for a 65-year-old taking a straight-life annuity (about $93,477 a year) — less if you retire younger or choose a survivor benefit. Only benefits you were vested in are covered, and amounts above the cap can be lost.

Okay, so what do I do? If your plan ends, the PBGC will contact you — but keep your address updated with the plan and with the PBGC. PBGC: pbgc.gov, 1-800-400-7242.

Your 401(k) after a job loss or a sale

A 401(k) is yours — the money sits in your account, not the company’s. A sale does not take it.

Your options: leave it where it is, roll it into your new employer’s plan, or roll it into an IRA. A direct rollover (plan to plan, or plan to IRA) has no tax and no withholding.

Cashing out is the expensive option: if you are under 59½, you pay income tax on the money plus a 10% additional tax, and the plan must withhold 20% for taxes up front. A $10,000 cash-out can leave you with roughly $6,000 to $7,000 after taxes and the penalty, depending on your tax bracket. Exception: if you leave your job in or after the year you turn 55, the 10% penalty usually does not apply (the “rule of 55”). If you do get a check, you have 60 days to roll it into an IRA or another plan to avoid the tax hit.

Okay, so what do I do? Do not cash out in a panic. A direct rollover keeps the full amount working for you and costs nothing in tax.

Don’t sign a buyout or waiver in a hurry

After a sale, some employers offer a lump-sum buyout (“take $X now instead of $Y a month for life”) or ask you to sign a waiver giving up benefits.

A lump sum can look big and still be worth less than the monthly checks over your lifetime. And once you take it, PBGC insurance no longer applies to that money.

If you are married, your pension’s default payout is a joint-and-survivor annuity — it keeps paying your spouse after you die. Waiving it usually requires your spouse’s written, notarized consent.

Okay, so what do I do? Get any offer in writing, ask for the numbers behind it (what monthly benefit it replaces), and do not sign on the spot. EBSA’s free benefits advisors (below) can walk through an offer with you before you decide.

If the plan says no: appealing a denial

If the plan denies your claim or says you get less than you expected, ask for the denial in writing — it must explain why and how to appeal.

Federal deadlines (ERISA, 29 CFR 2560.503-1): the plan has 90 days to decide a pension claim (it can extend once, by up to 90 more days, if it tells you in writing). You then have at least 60 days to appeal. The plan has 60 days to decide the appeal (again, one extension of up to 60 days).

Miss the appeal deadline and you can lose the right to fight it — mark the date the day the letter arrives.

Okay, so what do I do? Appeal in writing, keep copies of everything, and call EBSA’s free benefits advisors — 1-866-444-3272 — if the plan will not answer or the deadline is near. You can also sue under ERISA after the appeal, but that is the last step, not the first.

Where to complain in Texas

Federal help, free: EBSA benefits advisors help with plan problems — call 1-866-444-3272 or visit dol.gov/agencies/ebsa. For a failed pension plan, the PBGC: pbgc.gov, 1-800-400-7242.

State labor office: Texas Workforce Commission, Wage and Hour Department.

State labor office phone: 800-832-9243.

State attorney general: Texas Attorney General, Consumer Protection Division.

Public-sector pensions in Texas: Texas state employees get a lifetime monthly pension through the Employees Retirement System of Texas (ERS), based on years of service and highest average salary.

Okay, so what do I do? For a private pension, start with EBSA — 1-866-444-3272, free. For a public-employee pension, start with the retirement system named above.

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Get your Summary Plan Description and latest benefit statement in writing and keep copies. (2) Check your vesting date before you make any job decision. (3) Do not sign a buyout or waiver on the spot, and do not cash out a 401(k) in a panic — a direct rollover costs nothing in tax. (4) If the plan denies you, appeal in writing before the deadline, and call EBSA’s free benefits advisors at 1-866-444-3272 if you need help.

Sources: U.S. Department of Labor (ERISA vesting and claims rules), Pension Benefit Guaranty Corporation (2026 maximum guarantee table), IRS Topic 558, and the official Texas agencies linked above, checked November 17, 2026. Pension maximums and plan rules change — confirm the current figures with the plan administrator or EBSA before you decide. This is general information, not legal advice.

Carry permit reciprocity & travel in Texas

Carrying a handgun across state lines is where the law gets complicated: your permit may be good in one state and worthless in the next, and the rules change without much notice. This section is the Texas picture as of November 2026 — which states honor a Texas permit, which states’ permits Texas honors, whether you can carry here without a permit, and where you still can’t carry even with one. The lists below are dated, because reciprocity lists change: check the attorney general’s current list for every state on your route before you travel. This is general information, not legal advice.

Which states honor your permit

No permit needed. Texas lets anyone 21 or older who can legally possess a firearm carry a concealed handgun here — resident or visitor, no permit required.

Honored in 16 states: Colorado, Delaware, Idaho, Kansas, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Mexico, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Wisconsin.

Lists above assume a permit issued by your home state — some states honor only resident permits, and if you move, your new state will expect you to get its permit.

No permit needed in 29 states: Alabama, Alaska, Arizona, Arkansas, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Mississippi, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, New Hampshire, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Vermont, West Virginia, Wyoming — these states let any eligible adult carry concealed without a permit. Age and eligibility rules differ from state to state, so check the state before you go.

These states honor no out-of-state permits: California, Connecticut, Hawaii, Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Jersey, New York, Oregon, Rhode Island. Leave the gun home, or use the locked-transport rules below.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you travel, check the attorney general’s current list for every state on your route — the lists on this page are dated November 2026, and a state can drop recognition with little notice.

Permitless carry in Texas

Yes — no permit needed. Anyone 21 or older who can legally possess a firearm may carry a concealed handgun in Texas without a permit, resident or visitor.

Okay, so what do I do? You still have to follow Texas’s rules on where you can’t carry (the cards below) — permitless does not mean carry-anywhere. And a permit is still worth having: it is what other states recognize when you travel, and it exempts you from the federal Gun-Free School Zones Act near schools.

Traveling with a firearm

Passing through a state where you can’t carry? Federal law (18 U.S.C. § 926A) protects transporting a firearm through a state where you can’t carry — if it is unloaded, locked in a container, with ammunition kept separate and not within ready reach, and you may legally possess it where your trip starts and where it ends. Treat it as pass-through protection only: keep stops to what is necessary, because a few states enforce transport rules strictly.

A handgun in your vehicle: Yes - the handgun must not be in plain view (or must be in a holster).

Telling an officer — only if asked: If you carry on a License to Carry, you must display it when an officer demands your ID. There is no equivalent display duty for permitless carriers.

Watch out: Some locations open to LTC holders remain off-limits to permitless carriers, and 30.06/30.07 signs can bar license holders from private property.

For the full rules on a traffic stop — what to say, what to show, and your rights — see Pulled Over: Your Rights in Texas on this page.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are stopped in another state, tell the officer you are carrying if that state’s law requires it (check that state’s page before you go), keep your hands visible, and do not reach for the firearm unless the officer tells you to.

Where you cannot carry even with a permit

Even a valid permit does not open every door in Texas. The list below is the state-law list; federal law adds its own (federal buildings, post offices, secure airport areas, military installations).

  • Schools and school buses
  • Courts and court offices
  • Polling places on election day and during early voting
  • Racetracks
  • Secured airport areas
  • 51% establishments — businesses that derive 51% or more of income from on-premises alcohol sales (look for the red “51%” sign)
  • High school and collegiate sporting events; correctional facilities
  • Hospitals, nursing homes, amusement parks, and government meetings with proper notice (Penal Code §§ 46.03, 46.035)
  • Colleges: license holders may carry on campus; permitless carriers may not carry in college buildings
  • Private property owners can bar carry with 30.06 (concealed) and 30.07 (open) signs

Okay, so what do I do? Check the door before you walk in — if a place is posted or on the list above, leave the firearm locked in the vehicle (where the law allows that) or leave it home. When in doubt, ask the property owner or the staff.

Getting a non-resident permit

Yes. Apply to the Department of Public Safety (Gov’t Code § 411.173).

If your home state’s permit doesn’t travel well, a non-resident permit from Utah, Arizona, or Florida is the usual workaround — all three issue to non-residents and are honored in dozens of states. Utah’s permit is honored in about 36 states. Check each state’s official reciprocity page before you rely on it.

  • Utah’s permit-recognition page
  • Florida’s permit-recognition page
  • Arizona’s permit law (A.R.S. § 13-3112)

Okay, so what do I do? If you live in a state whose permit travels poorly, get a non-resident Utah, Arizona, or Florida permit — but check first that the states you actually visit honor it.

Official links in Texas

  • Official source: Texas's permit-recognition page &rarr;

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Check the attorney general’s current list for every state on your route before you travel — the lists here are dated November 2026. (2) If you carry without a permit, make sure every state on your route allows it. (3) In the car, follow the vehicle rule above, and for pass-through states use the federal locked-transport rule. (4) Know where you can’t carry before you walk in.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above, checked November 14, 2026. Reciprocity lists change — the lists on this page are dated November 2026. Where an official source publishes no figure or list, this page says so instead of guessing. Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you travel with a firearm. This is general information, not legal advice.

Appealing a civil judgment in Texas

Lost a civil case in Texas? You may be able to appeal — ask a higher court to look at the judgment. But the deadline is the whole ballgame: miss it and the judgment stands, however strong your argument is. The deadlines below are the ones the court rules and statutes publish for Texas, checked November 14, 2026. Small-claims appeals and general civil appeals run on different clocks in most states — do not use one for the other. This is general information, not legal advice.

What appealing means

An appeal asks a higher court to look at what the court below did. In a regular civil case, the higher court reads the record of what already happened and looks for legal errors — a wrong rule applied, evidence admitted that should not have been, a decision no reasonable judge could reach on that record. New evidence and new arguments you never made below are generally not allowed. The big exception is small claims and the lowest courts: in many states those appeals are a genuine fresh start — a trial de novo — where the case is heard all over again as if the first one never happened. The cards below say which kind your appeal is in Texas.

Who can appeal: the side that lost. In a few states the plaintiff gives up appeal rights by choosing small claims in the first place — the Texas card below says if that applies here.

Okay, so what do I do? Read the two deadline cards below first. Everything else in an appeal can be figured out after you file; the deadline cannot.

Small claims: the deadline in Texas

Deadline: 21 days. New trial (de novo): the county court hears the case all over again — new evidence, new witnesses, as if the first hearing never happened.

You have 21 days after the judgment is signed — or after a motion to reinstate, set aside, or for a new trial is denied — to appeal from the justice court to the county court, which holds a trial de novo. You can appeal only if the judgment or the amount in dispute is more than $250, not counting court costs. Perfect the appeal with an appeal bond, a cash deposit, or a Statement of Inability to Afford Payment of Court Costs, and pay the county court filing fee; once perfected, the justice court judgment is no longer valid or enforceable. The bond is $500 for a plaintiff and twice the judgment for a defendant. (Tex. R. Civ. P. 506.1.)

Okay, so what do I do? File the notice of appeal first, then sort out the bond and the transcript. A late notice ends the appeal; a late transcript usually does not.

Other civil cases: the deadline in Texas

Deadline: 30 days (90 with a timely post-judgment motion). Appeal to the Texas Court of Appeals.

In other civil cases, you have 30 days after the judgment is signed to file a notice of appeal to the Texas Court of Appeals — 90 days if a timely motion for a new trial or another listed post-judgment filing was made. The court reviews the record for legal errors. (Tex. R. App. P. 26.1.)

Okay, so what do I do? Calendar this deadline separately from the small-claims one above — they are different clocks, and using the wrong one is how appeals are lost.

What to file and what it costs

You start an appeal by filing a notice of appeal with the clerk of the court that decided your case (in some states, with the clerk of the court you are appealing to — the Texas cards above say which). The notice is usually a short form: who you are, what judgment you are appealing, and which court you want to hear it.

What it costs: a filing fee, the cost of the transcript or record (often more than the fee), and sometimes an appeal bond. A bond — also called a supersedeas bond — guarantees the winner gets paid if you lose again, and in many states it is the only thing that stops the winner from collecting while the appeal is pending. Ask the clerk for the current fee and bond amounts on day one; the Texas cards give the figures the official sources publish, and where no figure is published this page says so rather than guessing.

Okay, so what do I do? File the notice first, then sort out the bond and the transcript. A late notice ends the appeal; a late transcript usually does not.

If you won and they appeal

Filing an appeal does not automatically stop collection. In most states the winner can keep enforcing the judgment — garnishing wages, levying accounts — unless the loser gets a stay, usually by posting an appeal bond. In a few states the appeal pauses enforcement automatically. The Texas card above says what happens here.

Watch for the notice of appeal — it must be served on you — and calendar the deadline to respond. Do not spend the money yet: if the higher court reverses, you may have to give it back. Keep the evidence and the judgment paperwork together; if the appeal is a trial de novo, you will present your case again.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask the clerk whether the appeal in your case pauses collection, and check whether a bond was posted. If no bond was posted and collection is not stayed, you can keep enforcing — but talk to the clerk or a lawyer before spending what you collect.

If you lost and cannot afford the appeal

Most courts will waive or reduce appeal fees for people who cannot afford them — usually by filing a short form (often called in forma pauperis, a fee-waiver application, or a poverty affidavit) and showing your income. A waiver covers the filing fee; a bond is harder to waive, and in some states it cannot be waived at all. Legal aid and lawyer-referral programs (in the Free and Low-Cost Legal Help section on this page) can help with the forms, and a one-hour consultation with an appellate lawyer before the deadline is the cheapest reality check in law.

In Texas, a Statement of Inability to Afford Payment of Court Costs can take the place of an appeal bond or cash deposit.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask the clerk for the fee-waiver form the same day you learn the deadline — the waiver does not extend the deadline, so file the notice first and the waiver with it.

Official links in Texas

  • Appealing a Small Claims Case | Texas State Law Library
  • Texas Rules of Appellate Procedure | Texas Judicial Branch

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find your deadline in the two cards above and calendar it with days to spare. (2) File the notice of appeal with the right clerk before that date — the notice is what keeps the appeal alive. (3) Ask the clerk the same day about the filing fee, the bond, and whether filing pauses collection. (4) If you cannot afford the fee, file the fee-waiver form with the notice.

Sources: the official Texas sources linked above, checked November 14, 2026. Where an official source publishes no figure for a fee, a court, or a deadline, this page says so instead of guessing. Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Misclassification in Texas

You were hired as a “contractor” and paid on a 1099, but the boss sets your hours, hands you the tools, and tells you exactly how to do the job. That is misclassification — being called an independent contractor while being treated like an employee — and the label does not decide. What decides is how the work actually works, and the tests, the costs, and the ways to challenge it are in this section. A shorter version of this lived inside Workplace Rights on this page; this is the complete section. Checked November 14, 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What misclassification is and what it costs you

Whether you are an employee or an independent contractor turns on the real working relationship — who controls how, when, and where the work is done, how central the work is to the business, how you are paid, and how permanent the arrangement is. A 1099 form, a job title, or a signed “independent contractor agreement” does not settle it, and neither does the fact that you agreed to it.

Classified as a contractor, you usually get no unemployment benefits when the work ends, no workers’ compensation if you are hurt on the job, no minimum-wage or overtime protection, and no employer share of payroll taxes — you owe self-employment tax on both halves of Social Security and Medicare. If the label is wrong, those protections were yours all along.

Okay, so what do I do? Start with the job as it really works, not the paperwork: who sets your schedule, who provides the tools, whether you can work for anyone else, and how you are paid. Write it down now, while it is fresh — that list is the evidence every route below asks for.

The tests: which one applies depends on the law

There is no single national test. Three frameworks cover most cases, and the one that matters depends on which law you are claiming under — wage law, tax law, unemployment, or workers’ compensation — and the same state can use different tests for different programs.

1. The FLSA economic-reality test (federal wage law). Under the Fair Labor Standards Act, the question is whether you are economically dependent on the employer or in business for yourself. The Labor Department’s Fact Sheet 13 lists six factors: (1) opportunity for profit or loss depending on managerial skill; (2) investments by the worker and the employer; (3) permanence of the work relationship; (4) nature and degree of control; (5) whether the work is integral to the employer’s business; and (6) skill and initiative. No single factor decides, and the totality of the relationship is what counts. The rule is at 29 CFR Part 795 (effective March 11, 2024). Note: the Labor Department says the 2024 rule’s legality is the subject of current litigation and points to its Field Assistance Bulletin 2025-1 for its current enforcement position — the six factors above are the test as the fact sheet states it.

2. The IRS common-law test (federal tax law). The IRS weighs all the facts about control and independence in three categories: behavioral control (does the company control what you do and how you do it), financial control (who controls the business side — how you are paid, whether expenses are reimbursed, who provides tools and supplies), and the type of relationship (written contracts, employee-type benefits, whether the work continues and is a key aspect of the business). There is no set number of factors that makes someone an employee.

3. The ABC test (many states). Under the ABC test, you are an employee unless the employer proves all three: (A) you are free from the employer’s control and direction, (B) the work is outside the employer’s usual course of business (or outside all its places of business), and (C) you are customarily engaged in an independently established trade, occupation, or business of the same nature. Fail any one part and you are an employee. Fourteen states use the ABC test for at least one program, and a few more use ABC-style two- or three-part tests — the Texas card below says which one applies here.

One state, different tests. Oregon applies the economic-reality test to wage-and-hour claims and a four-factor right-to-control test to civil rights disputes. Maine uses one common standard across workers’ compensation, unemployment insurance, and wage and hour. Vermont uses the ABC test for unemployment compensation and Health Care Fund contributions, while withholding uses the same definitions as the IRS. West Virginia uses one statutory test across unemployment, workers’ compensation, human rights, and wage payment laws. Always check which test your claim falls under — winning under one does not automatically mean winning under another.

Okay, so what do I do? Figure out what you lost before you pick a test: unpaid overtime points to the FLSA test, a tax bill points to the IRS test, and denied unemployment or workers’ comp points to your state’s test in the card below.

How Texas decides it

The Texas Workforce Commission says the Texas Unemployment Compensation Act uses a direction-or-control, or common-law, test — employment unless service is shown free from control or direction — and TWC has adapted the old IRS twenty-factor test to apply it.

In short: Texas uses the Common-law / direction-or-control (adapted IRS 20-factor) for Unemployment, as the official source linked below describes it.

Okay, so what do I do? Open the official Texas source linked below and read the test in its own words before you file anything — the wording is what the agency will hold you to.

What you can get back — and what it costs the employer

If you were really an employee, the rights you lost were yours all along. What you can recover depends on the route: back pay for minimum wage and overtime you should have been paid under the FLSA; the employer’s share of Social Security and Medicare taxes it should have paid; unemployment benefits and workers’ compensation coverage for the period you were misclassified; and in some cases, benefits you were denied, such as paid leave or health coverage the employer offered its employees.

For the employer, the bill runs the other way: back wages and overtime, back payroll taxes, and unemployment and workers’ compensation premiums it should have paid. The IRS says an employer that treats an employee as an independent contractor with no reasonable basis for doing so may be held liable for employment taxes for that worker. State agencies can also assess back taxes plus penalties and interest.

Okay, so what do I do? Add up what the label cost you before you file: unpaid overtime hours, the extra tax you paid, and any medical bill or lost week of pay from an injury or layoff. That total is what your claim is actually worth, and it tells you which agency to start with.

How to challenge it, step by step

(1) Gather the evidence. Who sets your schedule and hours? Who provides the tools, materials, and workplace? How are you paid — by the hour, by the job, or by salary? Can you work for anyone else, set your own rates, or hire help? How long has the arrangement lasted, and is the work central to the business? Keep pay stubs, schedules, messages, and the contractor agreement in one folder, somewhere your employer cannot delete them.

(2) File a wage claim if you lost pay. If the label cost you minimum wage or overtime, file a wage claim with the U.S. Department of Labor’s Wage and Hour Division (free help at 1-866-487-9243) or with your state labor agency — the Texas source linked below is the place to start. State unemployment agencies also audit classification because it decides who pays unemployment tax — a report there can trigger a review of your whole workplace.

(3) Ask the IRS to rule: Form SS-8. A worker or a firm can file Form SS-8 and ask the IRS to rule on the worker’s status for federal tax purposes. It is free, though the IRS says it can take at least six months. File it before you file Form 8919, or at the same time.

(4) Fix the tax bill: Form 8919. If you were paid as a contractor but believe you were an employee, Form 8919 lets you report the wages and pay only the employee share of Social Security and Medicare tax instead of the full self-employment tax. Attach it to your tax return.

Okay, so what do I do? Pick the route that matches what you lost — wage claim for lost pay, SS-8 for a federal ruling, Form 8919 for the tax bill, your state unemployment agency if benefits were denied — and file before any deadline runs. You can use more than one route; they do not cancel each other out.

Punished for raising it?

It is illegal for an employer to fire, demote, or cut the hours of a worker for filing a wage claim, reporting misclassification, or asking about unpaid wages. If that happens, tell the agency you filed with right away — retaliation is its own violation, with its own deadline, and it does not depend on whether your original claim succeeds.

Okay, so what do I do? Write down exactly what happened, when, and who was there, the same day it happens. Keep any message, schedule change, or write-up that shows the timing — retaliation cases often turn on how soon the punishment followed the complaint.

Official links in Texas

  • Misclassification of Employees as Independent Contractors Under the Fair Labor Standards Act | U.S. Department of Labor
  • Independent contractor (self-employed) or employee? | IRS
  • Completing Form SS-8 | IRS
  • TWC — Independent Contractors / Contract Labor

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Write down how the job actually works — who sets your schedule, who provides the tools, whether you can work for anyone else, and how you are paid. (2) Add up what the label cost you: unpaid overtime, extra tax, lost benefits. (3) Pick your route: a wage claim with the Labor Department or your state agency for lost pay, Form SS-8 for a federal ruling, Form 8919 for the tax bill. (4) If you are fired or punished for raising it, tell the agency right away — retaliation is its own violation.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above (where one is listed), the U.S. Department of Labor pages linked above, and the IRS pages linked above, checked November 14, 2026, and the misclassification cards in the Workplace Rights section on this page, which were built from official state sources. Federal law cited: the Fair Labor Standards Act, 29 U.S.C. 201 et seq. (minimum wage and overtime; economic-reality test at 29 CFR Part 795); Internal Revenue Code section 3509 (employer liability for employment taxes). Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Eviction records in Texas

An eviction case can follow you for years — even one you won, and even one that was dismissed. This section is about the record itself: the court file, the tenant-screening report a landlord buys, and your credit report — what each one shows, your federal rights to fix bad reports, whether Texas lets you seal or expunge the court record, and the cleanup step by step. The eviction process itself — notices, court, and defenses — is in the Tenant Rights section on this page. Clearing a criminal record is a different process; see the record-clearing section on this page. A shorter version of this lived inside Tenant Rights on this page; this is the complete section. Checked November 14, 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What an eviction record is and where it shows up

An eviction record is not one document. It is three things that can each hurt you separately. First, the court file: the landlord’s filing, every paper in the case, and the judgment if one was entered — a judgment for possession, and a money judgment if rent or costs were awarded. Second, the tenant-screening report: a consumer report a screening company sells to landlords, built largely by collecting court filings in bulk. Third, your credit report: a money judgment can reach it if the unpaid rent or judgment debt is sent to collections.

What a landlord actually sees. Most landlords never read the court file. They see the screening report’s summary — often just “eviction filed” with a date — and sometimes a score or a recommendation. The report may not say you won, the case was dismissed, or you paid. That is why the outcome paperwork matters more than the filing: a dismissal order or a satisfaction of judgment is the proof that turns a bare filing into a finished story.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you apply anywhere, get your own screening report (the federal-rights card below explains how, free) and read it the way a landlord would: what does the first line say about you? If it only shows a filing, gather the court’s final disposition before your next application.

A filing is not a judgment

When a landlord files an eviction case, that filing exists as a public court record from day one — before any judge hears anything, and even if the case is later dismissed, settled, or decided in your favor. Screening companies collect those filings in bulk, so a case that went nowhere can still show up as an “eviction” on a report and cost you an apartment. That is why cleaning up the report (below) and the court record itself (the Texas card) are two separate jobs — most people need both.

Okay, so what do I do? When you talk to a landlord, say the outcome in one sentence: “The case was filed on [date], and it was dismissed [or: I won, or: we settled and I paid]” — and hand over the court paper that proves it. A filing with a proven good outcome is a very different story from an unexplained filing.

Your federal rights when a landlord screens you

A tenant-screening report is a consumer report under the federal Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA). If a landlord turns you down — or charges you more — based on one, the landlord must give you an adverse-action notice naming the screening company that made the report. You then have the right to a free copy of that report from the company if you ask within 60 days, and to dispute anything wrong or incomplete: the company generally must investigate within 30 days, fix what it cannot verify, and tell you the result. If a dispute does not resolve it, you may add a short written statement of dispute that must go out with future reports.

The company generally has 30 days to investigate your dispute; in some cases it has 45 days. An adverse action is not only a denial — it can also mean the landlord requires a co-signer, a larger deposit, or higher rent because of the report. If you believe your rights were violated, you may be able to sue under the FCRA, and you can submit a complaint to the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (see the official link below).

Okay, so what do I do? Keep every adverse-action notice — it names the company to dispute with. Ask that company for your free report within 60 days of the notice, and dispute in writing, with the court’s final disposition attached.

The 7-year rule — and what it does not do

Under the FCRA, screening companies must stop reporting most negative information after 7 years — civil judgments included. But the rule only limits what a report may contain. The underlying court record does not disappear, and a record a landlord finds by searching court files directly is not the screening company’s report. Closing the court file itself — sealing, expungement, or limited access — is a matter of state law, and states differ sharply, as the Texas card below shows.

Okay, so what do I do? If a report still lists an eviction older than 7 years, dispute it as outdated — that is one of the easiest disputes to win. But do not assume the court file is gone: check the Texas card below for whether the record itself can be sealed or expunged.

Eviction records in Texas: sealing and access

Texas does not have a process to remove or seal an eviction from your record, and that is mostly true even if the eviction case was dismissed or you won your case. Eviction court records are public under Rule 76a of the Texas Rules of Civil Procedure; the only exceptions the State Law Library lists are certain Rule 76a circumstances and cases dismissed under the Texas Eviction Diversion Program, which no longer exists and whose records the court must keep sealed.

Okay, so what do I do? If the card above gives Texas a route — automatic, by petition, or by motion — start at the court clerk where your case was heard and ask for the exact form and any filing fee before you file anything. If it says Texas has no such route, the FCRA steps above are your cleanup: they reach every screening report even when the court file stays public.

Applying in Texas: screening and fee rules

When an applicant is given a rental application, the landlord must make available printed notice of the landlord's tenant selection criteria and the grounds for denial, including criminal history, previous rental history, current income, credit history, or failure to provide accurate or complete information. If the landlord rejects an applicant without making that notice available, the landlord must return the application fee and any application deposit. Tex. Prop. Code section 92.3515.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you pay any application or screening fee, ask the landlord to put the fee, the screening criteria, and the screening company in writing — then check it against the rule above. Keep the receipt and a copy of any report you are given.

The cleanup, step by step

(1) Get the report. Use the company named in your adverse-action notice first (free within 60 days); before you apply anywhere else, also order your file from the big tenant-screening companies — you have FCRA rights against each of them. (2) Dispute every error in writing, with a copy of the court’s final disposition (dismissal, judgment in your favor, satisfaction) attached, and keep proof of mailing. (3) Send court orders. If your case was sealed or expunged, send the order to every screening company listing the case — a sealed record must not be reported. (4) Explain what remains. For anything accurate that is still reportable, attach a short written explanation and bring your disposition paperwork when you apply, so a landlord sees the outcome, not just the filing.

Okay, so what do I do? Work the steps in order — report first, then disputes, then the sealing petition, then the explanation packet. Disputing before you have the disposition paperwork in hand wastes the 30-day clock.

If you were evicted

If the court entered a judgment for the landlord, the record shows two things: the judgment for possession, and a money judgment if rent or costs were awarded. Both can appear on a screening report. How long it matters depends on which record you mean: a screening company generally may not report most negative information more than 7 years old (see the 7-year card above), but the court file itself stays public unless your state seals or expunges it (see the Texas card above) — and a landlord who searches court records directly can find an old case even after it ages off a report.

What helps. Proof first: the judgment, proof you paid or satisfied it, and any settlement. Then a short written explanation — one page: what happened, what changed, and what your rental history since then shows. References from landlords you have rented from since the eviction carry real weight. If a landlord asks, a co-signer or a larger deposit can help where your state’s law allows it — the CFPB lists both as things a landlord may require after an adverse action. And keep building on-time rent history: every clean rental is new evidence for the next application.

Watch out for cleanup scams. No one can erase an accurate, still-reportable eviction from a screening report before its time, no matter what they charge. The CFPB says you generally cannot remove negative information unless you can show it is incorrect or outdated. If a “credit repair” company promises to delete an accurate eviction, walk away — the dispute and sealing steps above are free, and they are the only routes that work.

Okay, so what do I do? Put together a one-page packet before your next application: the judgment, proof of payment or satisfaction, your one-page explanation, and a reference from your most recent landlord. Bring it to every showing — it is easier to hand a landlord the outcome than to explain a bare filing after the screening report lands.

Official links in Texas

  • What should I do if my rental application is denied because of a tenant screening report? | Consumer Financial Protection Bureau
  • Texas State Law Library — Can I remove an eviction from my record?

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find the adverse-action notice from your most recent denial — it names the screening company. Order that report today; it is free if you ask within 60 days of the notice. (2) Circle every error — wrong outcome, wrong person, a case that was sealed, anything older than 7 years — and dispute each one in writing with the court disposition attached. (3) Work the Texas route in the card above to seal, expunge, or limit the court record itself; where Texas has no such route, the FCRA steps are your cleanup — they reach every screening report even when the court file stays public. (4) Keep the final disposition and any sealing order with your rental papers, and bring copies when you apply.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau page linked above, checked November 14, 2026, and the eviction-records cards in the Tenant Rights section on this page, which were built from official state sources. Federal law cited: the Fair Credit Reporting Act, 15 U.S.C. 1681 et seq. (adverse-action notice, free report within 60 days, 30-day dispute investigation, 7-year reporting limit under section 605). Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Probation in Texas

Probation is supervision in the community instead of — or after — jail or prison. The judge suspends the sentence and lets you live at home, work, and see your family, as long as you follow the conditions in your probation order. Break a condition and the judge can send you back to serve the sentence that was hanging over the case. This section is the full picture for Texas: what probation is, the conditions, what happens if you violate, getting off early, moving to another state, the fees, your rights, and how probation ends. A shorter version of this lived inside Wrongly Accused on this page; this is the complete section. Checked November 14, 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What probation is

When a judge grants probation, the judge usually imposes a sentence and then suspends it — the sentence is hanging over the case, not being served, as long as you stay in compliance. You are assigned a probation officer (sometimes called a probation agent or community supervision officer) who checks that you are following your conditions, reports to the court, and can ask the court to act if you are not. Probation is not the same as parole: parole follows release from prison, while probation is a sentence the court imposes instead of sending you to jail or prison in the first place. How long probation lasts depends on the charge and the judge — it can run from months to years.

Okay, so what do I do? Read your probation order the day you get it — every condition, every date, every dollar. Keep it somewhere you can find it, and ask your officer about anything you do not understand before you guess.

Standard vs. special conditions

Almost every probation order has the same standard conditions: report to your probation officer as directed and keep every appointment, obey all laws, tell your officer before you change your address or job, and get permission before leaving the state (often the county). Your order can add special conditions for your case: drug or alcohol testing, treatment or counseling, community service, a no-contact order, staying away from certain places or people, and payments such as fines, fees, and restitution. Your own probation order controls — what it says is what you must do.

What your officer can and cannot do. Your officer can set your reporting schedule, visit your home or workplace, order drug tests your conditions require, require treatment or classes, and file a violation report with the court. Your officer cannot change your sentence, revoke your probation, or waive a condition the court ordered — only the judge can do those things. If a condition seems impossible or unfair — a treatment program you cannot reach, a curfew that conflicts with your work shift, a fee you cannot pay — do not just break it. Tell your officer first, in writing, explain the problem, and ask the court to change the condition. Judges change conditions far more often than people expect, but only when someone asks before the violation happens.

Okay, so what do I do? If a condition clashes with your job, your housing, or your ability to pay, write to your officer the same day: what the condition is, why you cannot meet it as written, and what would work instead. Keep a copy. If the officer cannot fix it, ask the court — through your officer or a lawyer — to modify the condition.

If you violate: the violation hearing

A violation can be a new arrest or new charge, or a technical violation: a missed appointment, a failed drug test, a missed payment, leaving the state without permission, or breaking a no-contact order. Either kind can land you in front of the judge. The usual pattern: your officer files a violation report or petition with the sentencing court, the court issues a summons or a warrant, and you get a hearing before the judge. Under the U.S. Supreme Court’s decision in Gagnon v. Scarpelli, 411 U.S. 778 (1973), you are entitled to notice of the alleged violation and a hearing where you can present your side and the evidence that helps you. You can have a lawyer — ask the court about a public defender if you cannot afford one.

The standard of proof is lower than at trial. The state does not have to prove a violation beyond a reasonable doubt. In most states it only has to show by a preponderance of the evidence — that it is more likely than not that you violated a condition. That is why a violation hearing can go against you even when a new criminal charge would not. Hearsay and other evidence that would not be allowed at trial is often allowed at a violation hearing.

What the judge can do. The judge can warn you and continue probation as-is, add or change conditions, extend the probation term, order a short jail stay as a sanction, or revoke probation and impose the sentence that was suspended. Bring proof to the hearing: completion certificates, payment receipts, pay stubs, treatment letters, and anyone who can speak for you.

No Texas-specific violation process is printed in this section — the official sources checked do not lay out this state’s hearing steps in plain terms. The general pattern above still applies: your officer files a report with the sentencing court, the court holds a hearing, and the judge decides. Ask your officer or the court clerk for the local violation-hearing procedure before your hearing date.

Okay, so what do I do? If you miss an appointment, fail a test, or anything goes wrong, call your officer before it turns into a violation report, not after. If you get a summons or a warrant, do not ignore it — get a lawyer, gather your proof, and show up to the hearing.

Getting off probation early

Courts in many states can end probation early for people who have finished their conditions and stayed in compliance. The request goes to the sentencing court, and your officer’s compliance report usually carries the most weight. Judges typically look at how much of the term you have served, whether every condition is complete, whether fines and restitution are paid, and whether you have stayed out of trouble. Some states also let you earn time off the term for compliance, which can end supervision early without a separate court request.

In Texas: Texas law (Code of Criminal Procedure art. 42A.701) builds in early-ending reviews: after a person has satisfactorily completed one-third of the original supervision period or two years, whichever is less, the judge may reduce or end supervision early; and at one-half of the period or two years, whichever is more, the judge must review the record and consider ending it, unless the person is behind on restitution they have the ability to pay or has not finished court-ordered treatment. Some offenses are excluded, including DWI offenses and offenses requiring sex-offender registration.

Okay, so what do I do? Finish treatment, community service, and restitution first — open conditions are the most common reason requests stall. Then ask your officer whether they will recommend early termination, and ask the court through your officer or a lawyer. Keep proof of everything you have completed in one folder.

Moving to another state: the Interstate Compact

All 50 states — plus the District of Columbia, Puerto Rico, and the U.S. Virgin Islands — belong to the Interstate Compact for Adult Offender Supervision (ICAOS). If you need to move, you apply through your probation officer: the state that sentenced you has the final say, the state you want to move to must accept you, and there is no right to a transfer. The receiving state will supervise you under its own rules once you are accepted, but the sentencing state keeps authority over your case. Do not move first and ask later — with limited exceptions you cannot travel to the new state while the application is pending, and moving without approval can itself be a violation.

Fees. Under ICAOS Rule 4.107, the sending state may charge a transfer application fee and the receiving state may charge a supervision fee. The amounts differ by state: for example, Oregon charges a $50 application fee, Louisiana charges $150, and Wisconsin charges $150. Ask your officer what Texas charges before you apply, and keep the receipt.

Okay, so what do I do? Tell your officer as soon as you know you may need to move — transfers take time, and the application goes through your officer, not around them. Have a real address, a job or family support, and a treatment plan (if one applies) lined up in the new state before you apply; the receiving state weighs all of that.

Fees and costs

Probation is rarely free. Most people on probation are charged a monthly supervision fee, plus the cost of drug tests, treatment or classes the court orders, and any fines and restitution from the case. The amounts are set by state law or by the court, and they add up over a long probation term — so get the full list in writing and keep every receipt.

No Texas-specific fee amount is printed in this section — the official sources checked do not state this state’s supervision-fee amount. Ask your officer for the exact monthly amount, what it covers, and where to pay it, and get it in writing. The Bearden rule below still protects you if you genuinely cannot pay.

If you cannot pay. A court cannot revoke your probation solely because you cannot pay a fine or restitution when you have genuinely tried. In Bearden v. Georgia, 461 U.S. 660 (1983), the U.S. Supreme Court held that the court must first ask why you did not pay — if you made bona fide efforts and still could not pay through no fault of your own, the court must consider alternatives to jail, such as a payment plan, a lower amount, or community service. But a court can revoke if you willfully refused to pay when you had the money, or never tried. Tell the court early, in writing, that you cannot pay and why, and bring proof: pay stubs, bills, and a record of the jobs you applied for.

Okay, so what do I do? If money is tight, do not go silent and miss payments — ask your officer or the court for a payment plan, a reduced fee, or a waiver before you fall behind, and keep proof of every payment you make.

Your rights on probation

Probation takes away some privacy, but not all of it. Your probation order itself is a search condition in many states: your officer may be allowed to search your home, car, or person without a warrant. The U.S. Supreme Court has upheld that, but with limits — in Griffin v. Wisconsin, 483 U.S. 868 (1987), a warrantless probation search was allowed because the officer had “reasonable grounds” to believe contraband was present, and in United States v. Knights, 534 U.S. 112 (2001), the Court required reasonable suspicion for a search of a probationer. An officer still cannot search for no reason at all, and police who are not your probation officer do not get a free pass from your probation status alone.

If your rights are violated. Write down what happened the same day — who, when, where, what was said, and any witnesses — and keep any paperwork. Tell a lawyer right away: a lawyer can raise an illegal search at your violation hearing or in court, and can ask the court to change a condition that is being enforced unfairly. Do not argue with the officer at the door or refuse in a way that becomes a new violation; say plainly, “I do not consent to this search,” and let the lawyer fight it in court.

Getting a lawyer. You can have a lawyer at your violation hearing and when asking the court to change a condition or end probation early. If you cannot afford one, ask the court about a public defender and see the Free Legal Help section on this page for legal aid in Texas.

Okay, so what do I do? Know your conditions word for word — they set the limits on searches and on your officer. If a search or a demand goes beyond them, say you do not consent, write it down, and call a lawyer the same day.

How probation ends

Probation ends when you complete the full term and the court discharges you, when the court ends it early, or when you earn it off through a compliance-credit program where your state has one. Get the discharge in writing — a discharge order or letter from the court or your officer — and keep it with your probation order. It is the proof that supervision is over.

Your record after probation. Finishing probation does not erase the conviction. The case stays on your record unless you get it sealed or expunged under your state’s record-clearing law, and not every case qualifies. See the record-clearing (expungement and sealing) section of this page for what can be cleared in Texas and how to ask. If the conviction is still showing up on background checks after a discharge or an expungement order, the court clerk can tell you which agencies hold the record.

Okay, so what do I do? When your term ends, ask your officer for written proof of discharge, confirm with the court clerk that the case is closed, and then — separately — look into record clearing on this page.

Probation in Texas

In Texas, adult probation — officially called community supervision — is run day to day by local Community Supervision and Corrections Departments (CSCDs), which serve the courts of one or more counties. The Texas Department of Criminal Justice's Community Justice Assistance Division provides funding, standards, and oversight but does not supervise cases itself.

Okay, so what do I do? Find out which agency supervises your county — the official source below lists it — and confirm your officer’s name, office, and reporting instructions in writing.

Official links in Texas

  • Texas Dept. of Criminal Justice — Community Justice Assistance Division (CSCDs)

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Read your probation order today and write down every condition, date, and dollar. (2) If a condition clashes with work, housing, or your ability to pay, tell your officer in writing and ask the court to change it — do not just break it. (3) If anything goes wrong, call your officer before it becomes a violation report. (4) If you need to move, apply through your officer before you move. (5) Keep proof of everything — completion certificates, payment receipts, pay stubs, treatment letters — in one folder, and get your discharge in writing when probation ends.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above, checked November 14, 2026, and the probation cards in the Wrongly Accused section on this page, which were built from official state sources. U.S. Supreme Court cases cited: Gagnon v. Scarpelli, 411 U.S. 778 (1973); Griffin v. Wisconsin, 483 U.S. 868 (1987); United States v. Knights, 534 U.S. 112 (2001); Bearden v. Georgia, 461 U.S. 660 (1983). Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Payday / Title Loans in Texas

A payday loan is a small loan due in full on your next payday — usually a few hundred dollars, at a fee that looks small and isn’t. A title loan borrows against your car. The rules are completely different from state to state: some states cap the cost at 36% APR or ban these loans outright, and others let lenders charge what they want. This section is the full picture for Texas: what the loan really costs, where Texas stands, title loans and your car, payment-plan rights, how to get out of a loan you already have, and the scams to watch for. For the wider debt picture — collection, default judgments, and negotiating other debts — see Credit Card & Debt on this page. Checked November 14, 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

What a payday loan really costs

The standard pitch is about $15 for every $100 borrowed, due in about two weeks. That sounds like 15%. Stated the way other loans are priced, it is roughly 391% APR — the math is $15 ÷ $100 = 15% for two weeks, and 15% × (365 ÷ 14) ≈ 391% a year. A $300 loan at that price costs $45 for two weeks, and the real damage comes from repeat borrowing: if you cannot pay the whole amount plus the fee on payday, a new loan (or a rollover, where allowed) starts the fee clock again, and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau has found most payday loans go to borrowers who take out loan after loan. Price any payday or title loan by its APR and its total payback amount — never by the fee alone.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you sign, ask the lender for the APR and the total amount you will pay back, in writing. If the APR is in triple digits, treat the loan as a last resort and price the safer exits below first.

Your bank account: the federal two-strike rule

Since March 30, 2025, a federal CFPB rule has limited how payday, title, and similar high-cost lenders can pull payments from your bank account: after two consecutive failed withdrawal attempts, the lender must get your new written authorization before trying again, and it must send you payment notices. Know the limits of that rule, too: the CFPB announced in March 2025 that it is not prioritizing enforcement of these payment provisions, and the rule never capped prices — a planned federal requirement that lenders check you can afford the loan was repealed in 2020. The Texas law below is your main protection.

Okay, so what do I do? If the lender keeps debiting your account, revoke the debit authorization: tell the lender in writing, tell your bank, and keep copies — the two-strike rule above also limits repeat failed attempts.

In the military? Federal law caps the cost at 36%

The federal Military Lending Act caps active-duty servicemembers and their dependents at a 36% military annual percentage rate on payday loans, title loans, and most other consumer credit — and a loan made over that cap to a covered borrower is void. Lenders must check military status before lending. If a lender charged you more, complain to the CFPB and your installation legal assistance office.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are covered and were charged over 36%, do not keep paying the overcharge: complain to the CFPB and your installation legal assistance office, and keep the loan papers.

Where Texas stands

In Texas, payday and title loans run through the credit services organization (CSO) model — there is no state cap on the CSO fee, though many cities add local limits.

In Texas
StatusLegal and licensed — fee and term limits apply
Rate capNo cap on the credit services organization (CSO) fee or the rate
Biggest loanNot stated in the sources checked.
Longest termNot stated in the sources checked.
LicenseCSO registration under Finance Code ch. 393; many cities add local limits

Payday lending in Texas operates through the Credit Services Organization (CSO) model rather than a direct payday-loan license: the Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner (OCCC) states that payday businesses register as credit services organizations under Texas Finance Code ch. 393 and arrange loans made by separate third-party lenders under the Finance Code's authorized interest frameworks, and Texas law does not cap the CSO fee or the rate on these transactions in the way a payday statute would. OCCC registers CSOs and licenses the underlying lenders; municipal ordinances in many Texas cities add amount and repayment limits locally. Characterized as OCCC describes the model.

Across the country: 12 states ban payday lending outright, 11 more cap it at 36% APR, Maine and Ohio reformed it into installment lending, and 25 states still license fee-based payday loans. Wherever you live, the federal protections in the cards above apply.

Okay, so what do I do? Check the row that matters to you in the table above — the rate cap first, then the biggest loan and the license. If a lender offers you terms that break the cap or the limits, do not sign: complain to the official source below.

Title loans in Texas

A title loan is not like a payday loan with a different name — you hand the lender a claim on your car’s title, and if you miss payments the lender can repossess and sell the car, often without going to court first. Losing the car usually means losing the ride to work, which makes the debt harder, not easier. If a lender has taken or threatens to take your vehicle, read the Car Repossession section of this page for your rights and the steps to get the car back.

Texas title loans run through the same CSO model and, for direct lenders, the pawn and Finance Code frameworks: title loans arranged by CSOs are registered under ch. 393, and motor vehicle title loans made directly are governed by Texas Finance Code ch. 342's provisions as OCCC summarizes them — the car is the collateral either way.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you pledge a car title, ask what happens on the first missed payment — how many days before repossession, and what fees get added. If the car is how you get to work, a title loan risks the job that pays the loan: try the safer exits below first.

Can you get more time? Payment plans in Texas

An extended payment plan turns one balloon payment into several smaller installments timed to your paydays — usually at no extra interest or fees. It is not a rollover: a rollover pushes the due date two weeks in exchange for another full fee, while a payment plan is designed to pay the loan off. In most states that require one, you must ask before the due date — miss that window and the lender can legally refuse.

No extended-payment-plan right is printed for Texas in this section — in the sources checked, no verified state-law right to demand an installment plan on a payday loan was found for Texas. That does not leave you without options: ask the lender in writing for an installment plan before the due date anyway — some lenders, including members of the CFSA trade group, offer one voluntarily — and if the lender refuses, complain to the official source below.

Okay, so what do I do? The day you realize you cannot pay in full, call or visit the lender and use the exact words: “I am requesting the Extended Payment Plan under state law.” Do not say “extend” or “renew” — those words usually mean a rollover.

If you already have one: how to get out

(1) Ask for the payment plan above, in writing, before the due date. (2) Do not roll it over or take a second loan to pay the first — that is how a $300 loan becomes a $1,000 problem. (3) Stop the automatic withdrawals if the lender keeps debiting your account: revoke the authorization in writing to the lender and your bank, and keep copies. (4) Get free help from a nonprofit credit counselor (look for NFCC members): they review your budget for free or nearly free and can negotiate with creditors for you. (5) Complain to the official source below and to the CFPB if the lender broke the caps, refused a payment plan the law gives you, or is unlicensed — in several states an illegal loan is void and uncollectible. (6) If a collector threatens you, know this: you cannot be jailed for not paying a payday loan — it is a civil debt, not a crime, and a collector who threatens arrest is breaking the law.

Okay, so what do I do? Today: ask for the payment plan in writing, revoke any automatic withdrawal you do not want, and call a nonprofit credit counselor. This week: complain to the regulator if the lender broke the rules.

Safer exits to try first

(1) A credit union Payday Alternative Loan (PAL). Many federal credit unions offer PALs under National Credit Union Administration rules: PAL I loans run $200 to $1,000 over 1 to 6 months (you must have been a member for at least a month), PAL II loans go up to $2,000 over 1 to 12 months with no waiting period. Both cap APR at 28%, cap the application fee at $20, and forbid rollovers — ask any federal credit union if it offers them. (2) Ask your employer for an advance or early pay. Many employers will advance a paycheck or let you draw earned wages early for no fee or a small one — far cheaper than a payday loan. (3) Negotiate the original bill. The utility, landlord, or medical office you actually owe will often take a payment plan for far less than a payday loan costs — ask before you borrow. (4) Local emergency assistance. Community action agencies, churches, and local charities often have one-time emergency help for rent, utilities, or car repairs — dial 211 or search your county’s community action agency. (5) Be wary of “debt settlement” pitches that charge fees to negotiate your debts down; nonprofit credit counselors (look for NFCC members) review your budget for free or nearly free.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you borrow, price the PAL and the original creditor’s payment plan first — both are almost always cheaper than a payday or title loan.

Scams and traps

Rollovers. Each rollover charges a new fee without reducing what you owe — that is the trap that turns a two-week loan into months of fees. Illegal online lenders. A website offering payday loans to Texas residents must hold a Texas license to lend here legally; check the lender on the regulator’s license lookup (the official source below) using the company’s exact legal name, not its website name. A loan from an unlicensed lender may be void — and the lender may still try to collect it. Tribal-lending claims. Some online lenders say tribal law exempts them from your state’s law. State regulators and courts have repeatedly treated loans made to state residents as subject to state law anyway — if a lender says state law does not apply to it, treat that as a red flag and complain to the regulator. Upfront fees and threats. A real lender never asks you to pay a fee before you get the money, and no collector can have you arrested for an unpaid payday loan.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you borrow from any lender — storefront or online — check the license on the official source below. If the lender is not licensed in Texas, walk away and report it.

Help in Texas

If a lender breaks the rules above: (1) complain in writing to the lender, keep a copy, and keep every notice the lender sends you. (2) Take the paper trail to the official source linked below — that is where you check whether a lender is licensed and file a complaint. (3) File a complaint with the CFPB as well; it takes complaints about payday and title lenders online. (4) Get free help from legal aid — see the Free Legal Help section on this page — especially if a lender is threatening your car or your bank account.

Okay, so what do I do? One call solves most problems: the lender in writing first, then the official source below, then legal aid if the lender will not fix it.

Official links in Texas

  • Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner - Credit Access Businesses

Okay, so what do I do? (1) If you are thinking about one of these loans, price the PAL and the original creditor’s payment plan first — both are almost always cheaper. (2) If you already have a payday loan, do not roll it over or take a second one to pay the first; ask the lender, in writing, for the payment plan described above if Texas gives you one, and keep the reply. (3) If the lender keeps debiting your account, revoke the debit authorization: tell the lender in writing, tell your bank, and keep copies. (4) If a lender here is unlicensed or broke the caps above, complain to the official source on this page — in several states an illegal loan is void and uncollectible. (5) If your car is on the line, act before the due date, not after: repossession is faster and cheaper for the lender than it is for you to undo.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above, checked November 14, 2026, and the payday and title loan cards in the Credit Card & Debt section on this page, which were built from official state sources. Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Manufactured-Home Parks in Texas

If you own a manufactured or mobile home but rent the lot under it, you live under two rulebooks at once — and the park rulebook is the one that decides whether you can stay. Your home is expensive to move — often $5,000 to $15,000 or more, and some homes cannot survive a move at all — so losing the lot can mean losing the home. This section is the park rulebook for Texas: the state park act, lot-rent increases, park rules and evictions, what happens if the park is sold or closes, and what to do if the park retaliates for a complaint. The general rules for renters — deposits, repairs, and the eviction process for a rented home — are in the Tenant Rights section on this page; this section is about the lot under a home you own. Checked November 14, 2026. This is general information, not legal advice.

Own the home, rent the lot

Your home is expensive to move — often $5,000 to $15,000 or more, and some homes cannot survive a move at all — so losing the lot can mean losing the home. That is why your lot lease and the park rules are the most important papers you own after the title. Read them before you buy a home in a park, get every promise about rent and rules in writing, and keep copies of every notice the park sends you. The protections in the Texas cards below come from state law; where your lease promises more, the lease promise counts too.

Before you buy a home in a park, ask for the lot lease, the park rules, and the last two rent-increase notices, and check them against the Texas cards below — especially the rent notice period, the eviction grounds, and the closure rules. If the seller or the park will not show you the papers, walk away: the lot, not the home, is what you are really buying into.

Okay, so what do I do? Find your lot lease and park rules today and keep them with your title. If you are buying, get the lease, the rules, and the recent rent history in writing before you sign anything.

The four fights that matter

(1) Lot-rent increases — most park laws require long written notice, and a few states now cap the amount. (2) Eviction — in most park acts the owner may evict only for a short list of reasons (“good cause”), not for any reason or no reason. (3) Closure or conversion — closing a park usually requires many months of notice, and some states make the owner help pay to move your home. (4) Selling — you generally may sell your home in place, and in a growing number of states residents get a chance to buy the park itself when it is sold. Each fight has its own Texas card below.

Lot-rent increases in Texas

Chapter 94 sets no standalone rent-increase notice period; a rent change comes with renewal. Under section 94.055 the landlord must give a notice to vacate or an offer of renewal, stating the proposed rent, not later than the 60th day before the lease expires (or, for a month-to-month lease, the 60th day before the intended termination), and the tenant must reject the new terms not later than the 30th day before expiration or the lease renews on the modified terms. A lease provision raising rent or fees during the term is void unless the tenant initialed it, under section 94.053(d).

Where no cap applies, the amount of an increase is set by your lease and the local market — which makes the notice period the thing to watch. An increase that arrives with too little notice, or only by word of mouth, is not valid under the notice rules above: say so in writing, keep a copy, and keep paying the old rent while you dispute it. A rent increase that lands right after you complained may be retaliation instead — see the retaliation card below.

Okay, so what do I do? When a rent-increase notice arrives, check the date on it against the notice period in the card above. If the notice was short, late, or only verbal, object in writing the same week and keep a copy.

Park rules and evictions in Texas

Park rules must be in writing — in your rental agreement or given to you in writing — and they must be reasonable and applied the same way to everyone in the park. A rule that contradicts Texas's park law is usually unenforceable, and the park cannot use a rule to take away a right the act gives you, such as selling your home in place. What a park commonly may require: keeping your home and lot maintained, following reasonable appearance and safety rules, and paying the charges your agreement lists. What it commonly may not do: change the rules to punish one resident, charge a fee the act forbids, or require you to buy goods or services from the park or its chosen seller.

Chapter 94 sets its own eviction grounds in Subchapter E. A landlord may terminate and evict for a violation of a lease provision, including a community rule incorporated in the lease (section 94.205), and for nonpayment where the amounts due equal at least one month's rent, the landlord gives written notice the payment is delinquent, and the tenant has not paid in full before the 10th day after receiving the notice (section 94.206).

Okay, so what do I do? If you get a rule-violation or termination notice, read it against the grounds above and check whether the park gave you the notice and the chance to fix it that the law requires. Get legal aid involved before the deadline in the notice runs out — eviction deadlines are short, and missing one is hard to undo.

If the park is sold or closing in Texas

A landlord may choose not to renew a lease to change the community's land use only on notice given not later than the 180th day before the date the land use will change, under section 94.204; the disclosure required by section 94.051 likewise states at least 180 days' notice for a land-use change, versus 60 days for ordinary nonrenewal. Chapter 94 states no general relocation-assistance payment for a closure, although a landlord who asks a tenant to leave before the section 94.055 notice period ends must compensate the tenant in advance for relocation expenses, including moving and installing the home.

The owner of a manufactured home may sell a home located on the leased premises if the purchaser is approved in writing by the landlord and the purchaser signs a lease agreement, under section 94.252. Unless the owner has agreed in writing, the landlord may not require the owner to contract with the landlord as agent or broker in selling the home, or require the owner to pay a commission or fee from the sale.

Resident purchase windows are measured in days, not months — commonly 45 to 120 days from the owner’s notice — and relocation help usually has to be claimed, not waited for. If a sale or closure notice arrives: (1) note the date you received it and keep the envelope; (2) contact your homeowners’ association the same week — purchase rights usually run through an association, and if none exists, the notice itself often explains how to form one; (3) call the official source below and your state Attorney General’s office and ask what deadlines apply to you; (4) do not sign a buyout, waiver, or new lease at the door without advice — legal aid (see the Free Legal Help section) reviews these free.

Okay, so what do I do? Calendar every deadline in any sale or closure notice the day it arrives — in park law, missing a short window (a purchase offer, a relocation claim, an objection) usually cannot be undone.

Retaliation in Texas

Retaliation is when the park punishes you for standing up for your rights: raising your lot rent, cutting services, changing the rules against you, refusing to renew, or starting an eviction — right after you complained about the park, reported a code violation, joined a residents’ association, or went to court. The timing is the evidence: the closer the park’s move follows your complaint, the harder it is for the park to call it a coincidence.

No park-specific retaliation rule is printed for Texas in this section. That does not mean the park can punish you for complaining — Texas’s general landlord-tenant law may still protect you, and in many states retaliation is a defense to eviction. If a rent increase, service cut, or eviction notice follows a complaint, write down the date of the complaint and the date of the park’s action, keep copies of both, and ask legal aid (see the Free Legal Help section on this page) whether Texas’s general retaliation rule covers park tenancies.

Okay, so what do I do? Complain in writing, not just in person, and keep a dated copy of every complaint. If the park moves against you within weeks of a complaint, write down both dates, keep the notice, and take the paper trail to legal aid before you answer the notice.

The law that governs your lot in Texas

Manufactured Home Tenancies, Texas Property Code Chapter 94. Under section 94.002 it applies only to a landlord who leases property in a manufactured home community (a parcel on which four or more lots are offered for lease for manufactured homes, section 94.001) to a tenant for situating a manufactured home on it; it does not apply where the tenant rents the manufactured home itself from the landlord.

Okay, so what do I do? Read the official source linked below before you act on a deadline — statutes change, and the official text is what a court will read.
