Help in Texas

If the park breaks the rules above: (1) complain in writing to the park owner or manager, keep a copy, and keep every notice the park sends you. (2) Take the paper trail to the official source linked below — park complaints in many states go to the attorney general’s office, the housing agency, or the courts, and the official page is where to start. (3) Get free help from legal aid — see the Free Legal Help section on this page; legal aid reviews sale and closure notices free, and the clocks on those are short. (4) Do not sign a buyout, waiver, or new lease at the door without advice. No state manufactured-housing association is named here because none was verified against an official source for Texas in this build — the official source below is the verified starting point.

Okay, so what do I do? One call solves most problems: the park owner in writing first, then the official source below, then legal aid if the park will not fix it.

Official links in Texas

  • Texas Property Code Chapter 94, Manufactured Home Tenancies - statute text hosted by Cooke County, Texas

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Find your lot lease and park rules and check them against the Texas cards above — a rule that contradicts state law is usually unenforceable. (2) Join your park’s homeowners’ association, or keep its contact information with your lease; it is the vehicle for purchase rights and the fastest warning system for sales and closures. (3) Calendar every deadline in any notice you receive the day it arrives — in park law, missing a short window (a purchase offer, a relocation claim, an objection) usually cannot be undone. (4) If the park breaks the rules above, complain in writing to the official source on this page and keep a copy; a paper trail is what enforcement runs on.

Sources: the official Texas source linked above, checked November 14, 2026, and the manufactured-home park cards in the Tenant Rights section on this page, which were built from official state sources. Laws change — confirm the current rule in the official source before you rely on a deadline. This is general information, not legal advice.

Student Loans in Texas

Student loans split into two worlds. Federal loans come with repayment plans based on your income, official ways out of default, and free help from the U.S. Department of Education. Private loans are just a contract with a lender — the federal options do not apply. The federal rules changed on July 1, 2026 under the 2025 law, and this section follows what the Department of Education itself publishes now, checked November 14, 2026. This area is still moving, so confirm your own status in your StudentAid.gov account before you act. Bankruptcy and debt settlement are covered in the Credit Card & Debt section on this page — this section is about the loans themselves: repayment, forgiveness, default, your state's servicer rules, and state programs. This is general information, not legal advice.

Federal or private? How to tell

Log in at StudentAid.gov: every federal loan you owe is listed there, with its type, balance, servicer, and repayment plan. A loan that is not listed is private — contact that lender directly about your options. Everything below about repayment plans, forgiveness, default, and getting out of default applies to federal loans only. For a private loan, your rights are the contract you signed, plus Texas law (see the Texas rules card below) — private lenders generally do not have to offer income-based payments, deferment, or forgiveness.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you call anyone or pay anyone, log in at StudentAid.gov and write down each loan, whether it is federal or private, who services it, and whether any loan is in default — a red warning box shows on the dashboard for defaulted loans.

Can't afford the payment? Income-driven plans

Ask about an income-driven plan before you miss payments. The Department's current plans are Income-Based Repayment (IBR) and the new Repayment Assistance Plan (RAP), which started July 1, 2026. The older PAYE and ICR plans are being phased out under the 2025 law; borrowers in them with loans from before July 1, 2026 have until July 1, 2028 to choose RAP, the new Tiered Standard plan, or IBR. Under RAP, payments run between 1% and 10% of your adjusted gross income depending on what you earn, reduced by $50 a month for each dependent; on-time payments get leftover monthly interest waived plus a matching principal payment of up to $50, and a remaining balance can be discharged after 360 qualifying payments (30 years). Loans first taken out on or after July 1, 2026 use RAP or the Tiered Standard plan (fixed terms of 10, 15, 20, or 25 years depending on the amount borrowed) — and if you never choose, the servicer will place a new loan on Tiered Standard. Applying takes about 10 minutes in your StudentAid.gov account. The SAVE plan is gone — wound down after a court ruling in March 2026; if you were in SAVE, check your account for your loans' current status. One thing to check before you count on forgiveness at the end of an income-driven plan: under the 2025 law, a balance forgiven that way counts as taxable income for discharges after 2025 — PSLF and Teacher Loan Forgiveness stay tax-free.

Okay, so what do I do? Behind but not yet in default? Apply for IBR or RAP now — getting onto a plan beats every cure for default.

Public Service Loan Forgiveness — and Teacher Loan Forgiveness

Public Service Loan Forgiveness (PSLF) cancels the remaining balance on your Direct Loans after 120 qualifying monthly payments made under a qualifying repayment plan while you work full-time for a government or not-for-profit employer. The 120 payments do not need to be consecutive, and RAP counts as a qualifying plan. FFEL and Perkins loans count only if consolidated into a Direct Consolidation Loan first, and private loans never qualify. Use the PSLF Help Tool on StudentAid.gov to check your employer and submit the form — do not rely on your servicer's say-so alone — and certify your employment every year and whenever you change jobs, so errors get caught while they are still fixable.

Teacher Loan Forgiveness is the faster, smaller route for teachers: teach full-time for five complete and consecutive academic years in a low-income school or educational service agency, and up to $17,500 can be forgiven if you teach math, science, or special education (up to $5,000 for other teachers). You cannot count the same years toward both Teacher Loan Forgiveness and PSLF — pick the one that pays more for your situation.

Okay, so what do I do? If you work for government or a nonprofit, open the PSLF Help Tool today and check whether your employer qualifies and how many payments you already have. If you teach, check whether your school is on the federal low-income schools list.

What default means — and why to avoid it

A federal Direct or FFEL loan goes into default after 270 days without a scheduled payment. The consequences the Department lists: the entire unpaid balance becomes due at once; tax refunds and federal benefit payments can be taken through Treasury offset; wages can be garnished; you lose deferment, forbearance, and your choice of repayment plan; you lose eligibility for more federal student aid; and the default is reported to the credit bureaus. Even before default, a delinquency of 90 days or more is reported. One warning: “Fresh Start,” the temporary program you may have heard about, ended on October 2, 2024 — anyone offering to enroll you in it now is offering a program that no longer exists.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are behind, act before day 270: an income-driven plan, a deferment, or a forbearance arranged now is far easier than getting out of default later.

In default? The two ways out

The Department names two main exits (paying in full is a third, impractical for most people). (1) Rehabilitation — usually the better deal. You agree in writing to make nine voluntary, reasonable, and affordable payments — they can be as low as $5 — during a period of 10 consecutive months. Finish, and the default status is removed, garnishment and offset stop, and the record of default is removed from your credit history (late payments from before the default stay). Rehabilitation is a one-time opportunity per loan today; starting July 1, 2027, you will be able to rehabilitate the same loan twice. (2) Consolidation — the faster exit. Roll the defaulted loan into a new Direct Consolidation Loan after three consecutive, voluntary, on-time, full payments, or by applying with an income-driven plan. The catches: unpaid interest is added to your principal, and the record of default stays on your credit history. Compare both at StudentAid.gov/default-support — the Defaulted Loans Support Center, open since September 30, 2026 — or call the Default Resolution Group at 1-800-621-3115.

Okay, so what do I do? Already in default? Start at StudentAid.gov/default-support or call the Default Resolution Group (1-800-621-3115), and choose rehabilitation unless you need consolidation's faster exit.

Wage garnishment: no court order needed — and a current pause

On a defaulted federal loan, the Department can order your employer to withhold up to 15% of your disposable pay — this is called administrative wage garnishment, and it does not require taking you to court. You have rights first: request a hearing in writing, postmarked within 30 days of the garnishment notice, and the garnishment pauses until after the hearing; Treasury offset likewise comes with a written notice and a 65-day window to dispute it. Current status: in January 2026 the Department announced it was delaying involuntary collections — wage garnishment and Treasury offset — while the new repayment plans roll out, and no restart date has been announced as of November 14, 2026. News reports in October 2026 said a restart could come soon; a Department spokesperson denied those reports. Defaults are still reported to the credit bureaus during the delay. Check StudentAid.gov for the current status, and do not count on the pause lasting.

Okay, so what do I do? If a garnishment or offset notice arrives, do not ignore it: the 30-day and 65-day windows run from the notice, and asking for a hearing in writing is what pauses the garnishment.

Private loans: fewer protections

A private student loan is a contract with a bank, credit union, or other lender. There is no income-driven plan, no PSLF, no federal rehabilitation, and no 270-day rule: your contract sets when the loan defaults, and it is usually much sooner than a federal loan. The lender can sue you for the balance, and a court judgment can lead to garnishment under state law — unlike a federal loan, the lender cannot garnish your wages without going to court first. Student loans are also very hard to wipe out in bankruptcy: most survive unless you prove undue hardship, a high bar — see the bankruptcy card in the Credit Card & Debt section on this page.

Okay, so what do I do? If you cannot pay a private loan, ask the lender in writing what hardship options exist — a temporary lower payment, interest-only payments, or a modified term — and keep a copy of everything. If the lender breaks Texas rules, use the Texas office linked below.

Your state's rules in Texas

No statewide student-loan servicer licensing law is printed for Texas in this section — federal servicing rules still apply to your federal loans, and the official Texas page linked below is where to start if a servicer breaks its promises.

Okay, so what do I do? If your servicer misapplies a payment, gives you wrong information about repayment options, or will not fix an error, complain to the servicer in writing first and keep a copy — then take that letter to the Texas office linked below.

State loan repayment and forgiveness programs

For health professionals: the federal Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA) State Loan Repayment Program funds state-run programs that repay loans for doctors, nurses, dentists, and other clinicians who work in shortage areas. In the 2026 funding cycle the program in Texas is run by the Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board — the HRSA contact list linked below has the details. Eligibility, the length of service required, and the amount repaid vary by state.

Teachers have the federal Teacher Loan Forgiveness program in the card above. Beyond that, states run their own repayment programs for particular jobs — teachers, nurses, public defenders, rural doctors — and they open, close, and change amounts often. No other state program is named here because none was verified against an official source for Texas in this build: check the official Texas page linked below and your state's higher-education agency before you apply, and never pay anyone to apply for you.

Okay, so what do I do? If you work in health care, teaching, or another public-service job, ask your employer and your state's higher-education agency which repayment programs you qualify for — and apply through the official program, not through a company that found you online.

Scams: never pay for free federal help

Help with your federal loans is free. A company charging “enrollment,” “subscription,” or “maintenance” fees to get you out of default, into a repayment plan, or onto forgiveness is running a scam — walk away. Red flags: they ask for your FSA ID or password (your servicer and the Department will never ask for it by phone or email), they promise instant forgiveness or a specific payment before seeing your loans, they tell you to stop paying your servicer and pay them instead, or they pressure you to act today. The same companies often call themselves “student loan rescue” or “debt relief” services — everything they offer is something you can do yourself, free, at StudentAid.gov.

Okay, so what do I do? If a company took your money or your FSA ID, change your StudentAid.gov password at once, report it through the FSA Feedback Center on StudentAid.gov, report it to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov, and tell your state attorney general — start with the official Texas page linked below.

Help

Your servicer is the company that bills you. Find yours by logging in at StudentAid.gov, or call the Federal Student Aid Information Center at 1-800-433-3243. If the servicer will not fix a problem, submit it through the FSA Feedback Center on StudentAid.gov — and if the answer looks wrong or incomplete, you can add information to your case and ask for escalation to the Office of the Ombudsman.

Okay, so what do I do? One call solves most problems: your servicer first, then the FSA Feedback Center if the servicer will not fix it, then the Texas office linked below.

Official links in Texas

  • Federal Student Aid — Top FAQs About Income-Driven Repayment Plans (studentaid.gov)
  • HRSA — State Loan Repayment Program contacts (find your state's program office)
  • Texas Attorney General — Beware of Student Loan Scams

Okay, so what do I do? (1) Log in at StudentAid.gov and confirm what you owe, who services it, and whether any loan is in default. (2) Behind but not yet in default? Ask about IBR or RAP now. (3) Already in default? Start at StudentAid.gov/default-support or call the Default Resolution Group (1-800-621-3115), and choose rehabilitation unless you need consolidation's faster exit. (4) Work for government or a nonprofit? Open the PSLF Help Tool and certify your employment. (5) Never pay a company to do any of this — every step above is free. (6) If your loan is private, read your contract, ask the lender in writing what hardship options exist, and use the Texas office linked above if the lender breaks state rules.

Sources: Federal Student Aid (studentaid.gov) income-driven repayment FAQs and default, rehabilitation, and forgiveness guidance, checked November 14, 2026; the U.S. Department of Education's January 16, 2026 announcement delaying involuntary collections; HRSA / National Health Service Corps State Loan Repayment Program contacts; and the official Texas source linked above. Federal figures change — confirm the current rules in your StudentAid.gov account before you rely on them. This is general information, not legal advice.

Mental-Health Crisis Holds in Texas

A crisis hold is a short emergency detention for evaluation when someone, because of a mental-health crisis, appears to be a danger to themselves, a danger to others, or so unable to care for themselves that they cannot meet basic needs. It is an evaluation, not a punishment and not a commitment: the facility has a short window to examine the person, and then must release them, let them stay voluntarily, or go to court for longer treatment. In Texas this is known as the emergency detention order. The law that sets the rules in Texas is Tex. Health & Safety Code § 573.021. This section covers what a hold is, who can start one, how long it lasts here, the person's rights at a hearing, and what a hold does not do. The Guardianship section on this page covers the slower, longer-term court process for someone who cannot make decisions over months and years — a hold is not a guardianship. This is general information, not legal advice.

What a crisis hold is

A hold happens in hours, not weeks. Someone in crisis is taken to a hospital or crisis facility and kept there while a doctor or other qualified professional examines them and decides whether they meet the legal test: a mental illness that makes them a danger to themselves, a danger to others, or gravely disabled — unable to provide for their own food, clothing, shelter, or safety. Being sad, strange, or difficult is not enough, and a diagnosis alone is not enough: the law requires recent behavior that shows the danger. The hold buys time for an evaluation. It does not decide treatment, and it does not last indefinitely.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are with someone in crisis right now, skip the law and go to the “Help right now” card below — call 911 if anyone is in immediate danger, or call or text 988.

Who can start one in Texas — and how

A peace officer can take the person into custody without a warrant when there is a substantial risk of serious harm, or with a warrant issued by a magistrate. Any adult can also file a written application for emergency detention.

How it usually starts: call 911 and say plainly that it is a mental-health crisis, where the person is, and what they have said or done most recently — dates, exact words, any threats, any weapons, any medicines or treatment stopped. Ask for officers trained in crisis response if your area has them. Or call or text 988 and ask for the nearest crisis team, or go to an emergency room and tell the staff the same facts. What helps most is specific, recent behavior: evaluators and judges act on specifics, not general worry. Bring a list of the person's medicines, their doctor or clinic, and any advance directive or crisis plan they have.

Okay, so what do I do? Write down the specific recent behavior with dates before you call — that note is what the officer, the screener, and the judge will all ask for.

How long the hold lasts in Texas

Up to 48 hours, not counting weekends and holidays.

A preliminary examination must happen within that time, and the person must be released unless a court orders continued detention.

What happens next: during the hold, a professional examines the person and decides. Three outcomes are possible: the person is released because they no longer meet the test; the person agrees to stay for treatment voluntarily; or the facility asks a court to order longer treatment, and a judge decides at a hearing. The facility cannot simply keep holding the person past the limit while it waits — when the clock runs out without a court order, the person must be released.

Okay, so what do I do? Ask the facility, in writing, when the hold started and when it expires — and ask what hearing, if any, has been scheduled.

Your rights at the hearing

In Texas: If a judge signs an order of protective custody, a probable cause hearing must be held within 72 hours, and the full hearing on court-ordered treatment must be held within 14 days of the application being filed.

At any hearing, the person has the right to be there, to have a lawyer (appointed free if they cannot pay, in commitment proceedings), to present evidence and call witnesses, to question the other side's evidence, and to argue for a less restrictive option than the hospital. The state must prove its case by clear and convincing evidence — a higher bar than ordinary civil cases. The person also has the right to be told why they are being held, to be examined promptly, and to communicate with family and a lawyer. Nobody can be kept indefinitely on an emergency hold — the state must go to court, and the judge decides.

Okay, so what do I do? If you are the person held, ask for a lawyer at once and say you want a hearing. If you are family, find out which court and which date — and show up with the specific facts, not just your worry.

What a hold does NOT do

A hold is not a criminal charge. The person has not been arrested, has not been charged with a crime, and a hold by itself is not a criminal record. Police may be the ones who bring the person in, but the process is civil, not criminal.

A hold is not a guardianship. It does not appoint anyone to make decisions for the person, and it does not decide who controls their money, their home, or their medical care. Guardianship is a separate, slower court case — see the Guardianship section on this page.

A hold does not by itself take away rights. Being held for evaluation does not by itself end the person's right to vote, to make a will, to refuse treatment outside the emergency, or to make their own decisions once the hold ends. Longer treatment, and any loss of rights, requires a separate court order — and the person can contest it.

Okay, so what do I do? If anyone tells you the hold means the person is now a criminal, or now has a guardian, or has now lost their rights — that is not what the law says. Ask them to show you the court order that says so.

Help right now

Call or text 988 — the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline — free, confidential, and answered around the clock. Counselors can talk with the person in crisis, or with you, and can connect you to the nearest local crisis team. If someone is in immediate danger or has already hurt themselves, call 911 first.

Okay, so what do I do? In immediate danger: 911. In crisis but not immediate danger: call or text 988 now, and stay with the person until help arrives.

Official links in Texas

  • 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline — call or text 988, free and 24/7
  • Office of the Texas Governor — Mental Health Protections, Detaining a Mental Health Patien

Okay, so what do I do? If someone is in crisis right now, call 911 if there is immediate danger, or call or text 988. Write down the specific recent behavior with dates, and take that note to the crisis team or the emergency room. If the person is already on a hold, ask the facility in writing when it started, when it expires, and when the hearing is — and make sure the person has a lawyer. A hold is an emergency evaluation, not a commitment and not a guardianship: longer treatment needs a judge.

Sources: the emergency-hold statute cited above (Tex. Health & Safety Code § 573.021); North Dakota Legislative Council memorandum 17.9251.01000 (March 2016) and Hedman et al., “State Laws on Emergency Holds for Mental Health Stabilization,” Psychiatric Services 2016; the Treatment Advocacy Center emergency-hospitalization chart; and your state’s official source linked above. Hold lengths and hearing deadlines change — confirm the current law with the facility or the court before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Signing Up for Medicare in Texas

Medicare is the federal health insurance program for people 65 and older (and some younger people with disabilities). The part that trips people up is not the coverage — it is the calendar. Sign up in the wrong window, or late, and you can wait months for coverage and pay a higher premium for the rest of your life. This section covers when to sign up, what each part covers and costs in 2026, the late penalties, Medigap versus Medicare Advantage, free help in Texas, and how to enroll. If Medicare denies a claim or cuts a benefit you already have, that is an appeal — see the Public Benefits Appeals section on this page. This is general information, not legal advice.

When to sign up — three windows

Your first window (Initial Enrollment Period): 7 months — the 3 months before the month you turn 65, that month, and the 3 months after. Sign up in the 3 months before your birthday month and coverage can start the first day of your birthday month; sign up during or after it and coverage usually starts the first day of the next month.

If you miss it (General Enrollment Period): January 1 to March 31 every year. Coverage starts the first day of the month after you sign up, and a late penalty may apply (below).

Still working at 65 (Special Enrollment Period): if you or your spouse are still working with health coverage from that job, you can usually sign up later without a penalty — but the clock is strict: the special window ends 8 months after the job or the job coverage ends, whichever comes first. Two traps: COBRA does not count as job coverage and does not extend the 8 months, and retiree coverage ending does not start a new window. If the employer has fewer than 20 workers, Medicare is expected to pay first — ask your state's free counselors below before you delay Part B.

Already on Medicare and want to change plans: the Annual Enrollment Period runs October 15 to December 7 each year, with changes taking effect January 1.

Okay, so what do I do? Mark your 7-month window on a calendar the day you turn 64 and a half, and sign up in the first 3 months of it if you can.

What each part covers — and what it costs in 2026

Part A (hospital insurance): inpatient hospital stays, skilled nursing care, hospice, and some home health care. Most people pay no Part A premium — you qualify if you or your spouse paid Medicare taxes for 40 quarters (10 years). With fewer than 30 quarters the premium is $565 a month in 2026; with 30 to 39 quarters it is lower — check the exact amount at medicare.gov or with your SHIP counselor. The Part A deductible is $1,736 for each benefit period in 2026 (a benefit period is not the same as a calendar year).

Part B (medical insurance): doctors, outpatient care, medical equipment, and many preventive services. The standard premium is $202.90 a month in 2026, and the yearly deductible is $283. After the deductible you usually pay 20% of the Medicare-approved amount for most services, and Original Medicare has no yearly cap on what you can owe. Higher earners pay an extra amount (called IRMAA) on top of the Part B and Part D premiums, based on a tax return from 2 years ago.

Part D (prescription drugs): sold by private companies Medicare approves. Premiums vary by plan. In 2026 a plan can charge a deductible of no more than $615 a year, and your out-of-pocket spending on covered drugs is capped at $2,100 for the year — after that you pay nothing more for covered drugs until January.

Part C (Medicare Advantage): a private plan Medicare approves that bundles Parts A and B, usually with drug coverage too — see the Medigap card below before you choose.

Lower income? Medicare Savings Programs, run through your state Medicaid office, can help pay Part A and B costs like premiums — and getting one also gives you Extra Help with drug costs automatically.

Okay, so what do I do? Budget for the 2026 Part B premium ($202.90 a month) plus the $283 deductible, and compare drug plans on their total yearly cost for your prescriptions — not just the monthly premium.

Late sign-up penalties — they last

Part B: your premium goes up an extra 10% for each full 12-month period you could have had Part B but did not — and you usually pay that higher premium for as long as you have Part B. Two full years late means 20% more, every month. (On the 2026 standard premium of $202.90, one 12-month delay adds about $20.30 a month.)

Part D: if you go 63 days or more without a drug plan or other creditable drug coverage, you pay an extra 1% for each month you went without it — also for as long as you have drug coverage. The 1% is figured on the national base premium ($38.99 a month in 2026).

Part A (if you pay a premium for it): signing up late can raise your premium by 10% for twice as many years as you waited.

Okay, so what do I do? If your first window has already passed, do not wait for January — check today whether a Special Enrollment Period applies to you, because every full 12 months of delay adds another 10% to Part B.

Original Medicare + Medigap, or a Medicare Advantage plan?

Path one: Original Medicare plus a Medigap policy. Medigap is extra private insurance that helps pay your share of Original Medicare costs, and you can see any doctor who takes Medicare. Your best time to buy is the 6-month window that starts the month you have Part B and are 65 or older — during it, insurers cannot turn you down for health problems; later, they usually can. In most states Medigap plans are sold as standardized lettered plans (A, B, D, G, K, L, M, N — Plans C and F are closed to anyone who became eligible for Medicare on or after January 1, 2020); the same letter means the same benefits no matter which company sells it, so compare on price and service.

Path two: a Medicare Advantage plan. One private plan instead of Original Medicare, usually with a doctor network, approvals for some care, a yearly cap on out-of-pocket costs, and extras like dental, vision, or hearing. You cannot use a Medigap policy with an Advantage plan. Neither path is right for everyone — that is what the free counselors below are for.

Okay, so what do I do? Decide the path before you buy anything: if you want Medigap, buy inside your 6-month window at 65 — after it closes, your health history can shut the door in most states.

Free Medicare counseling in Texas

Texas's free Medicare counseling program is Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP). Call 800-252-9240. SHIP counselors do not sell insurance and do not get paid by any plan, so their only job is to help you choose. HICAP counseling is reached through the statewide Area Agency on Aging line — ask for HICAP when you call.

Anywhere in the country, you can also find your local SHIP through the national SHIP line at 1-877-839-2675 (say “Medicare” when prompted), or call 1-800-MEDICARE (1-800-633-4227), 24 hours a day.

Okay, so what do I do? Before you pick any plan, call your state's free counseling program above — a 20-minute call can save you a lifetime penalty or a Medigap door closing.

How to enroll, step by step

If you are already getting Social Security benefits at least 4 months before you turn 65, Parts A and B start automatically and your Medicare card comes in the mail — check that it arrived and that the start date is right. Everyone else signs up through Social Security: online at ssa.gov, by phone, or at a local Social Security office. Have your Social Security number, date of birth, and proof of age and citizenship or lawful residence ready, plus your work history if you are claiming premium-free Part A through a spouse. You can sign up for Part D drug coverage, or a Medicare Advantage plan, at medicare.gov or by calling 1-800-MEDICARE.

Watch out for scams: real Medicare and Social Security will not call you out of the blue asking for money, gift cards, or your bank details to enroll you. Enroll only through the official sites and numbers above.

Okay, so what do I do? Sign up through Social Security in the first 3 months of your 7-month window, then check your Medicare card the day it arrives and confirm the coverage start date.

Official links in Texas

  • Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP) (Texas)
  • Medicare.gov — the official Medicare site (or call 1-800-MEDICARE, 1-800-633-4227)

Okay, so what do I do? Turning 65 in the next few months? Mark your 7-month window on a calendar today, sign up in the first 3 months of it, and before you pick any plan, call your state's free counseling program above — SHIP counselors do not sell insurance, so their only job is to help you choose. If a benefit is denied or cut, the Public Benefits Appeals section on this page covers how to appeal.

Sources: CMS 2026 Medicare Parts A & B premiums and deductibles (announced November 2025) and the CMS CY2026 Part D benefit parameters; medicare.gov enrollment and penalty pages; your state's SHIP program page linked above. All dollar figures are for 2026 and change every year — confirm the current numbers at medicare.gov before you enroll. This is general information, not legal advice.

Appealing Your Property Tax Assessment in Texas

The value on your assessment notice decides your tax bill, and you have the right to challenge it if you think it is too high. Appeals are local: they start with the assessor or a local board of review or equalization, with a further appeal to a state board or tax court if you still disagree. This section gives you Texas's deadline, where to file, what to bring, what it costs, and what happens if the board says no. The Foreclosure Defense section on this page has a shorter version of this process. This is general information, not legal advice.

The deadline — read it the day the notice arrives

File a protest with your county appraisal district by May 15, or within 30 days after the district mails your Notice of Appraised Value, whichever is later.

Appeal windows are short and they are enforced. Miss the window and you usually wait until next year, paying the higher bill in the meantime. Read the date on your notice the day it arrives and calendar it.

Where to file

County appraisal district for an informal conference, then the Appraisal Review Board (ARB).

How to file, step by step

File a written notice of protest (Comptroller Form 50-132) with the appraisal district; you may request an informal conference first, and if that fails the ARB holds a formal hearing.

What evidence wins

Comparable sales, an appraisal, photos of condition or damage, repair estimates, and errors in the district's property records; you can also protest on unequal appraisal - that similar homes are valued lower than yours.

Get your property record card from the assessor and check every line for errors. Bring copies to the hearing and keep your originals.

What it costs

We could not confirm a filing fee from an official source for Texas. Ask the board or your assessor before you file — do not assume it is free, and do not assume a fee quoted by a third-party site is current.

If the board says no

Within 60 days of receiving the ARB's written order, appeal to district court or choose binding arbitration; an appeal to the State Office of Administrative Hearings is available for qualifying property over $1 million within 30 days.

Keep paying your property taxes on time while you appeal. An appeal does not pause the bill, and unpaid taxes can draw penalties or a lien even while your case is pending.

Official form and help in Texas

Your county assessor's office has the appeal form for your county — ask for it by name and confirm the deadline in writing.

Okay, so what do I do? Find the appeal deadline on your assessment notice and calendar it today. Then pull your property record card, check it line by line for errors, gather recent sales of comparable homes, and file the appeal this section describes — in writing, and keep proof you filed on time.

Sources: official state and county assessment-appeal pages listed above. Checked against official sources in November 2026. Deadlines and fees change — confirm the date on your own assessment notice before you file. This is general information, not legal advice.

Becoming a U.S. Citizen in Texas

Naturalization is how a green-card holder becomes a U.S. citizen. This section is the full step-by-step: who can apply, the form and the fee, the English and civics tests, what happens after you are approved, and free help in Texas. The Immigration Help section on this page has a shorter version of this process. This is general information, not legal advice.

Who can apply in Texas

You must be at least 18 when you file and have been a lawful permanent resident (green card holder) for 5 years under INA 316, or 3 years if you are married to and living with a U.S. citizen spouse under INA 319(a). You must have lived continuously in the United States for that same 5 or 3 years, been physically present for at least 30 months out of the last 5 years (or 18 months out of the last 3 years for spouses), and lived at least 3 months in the state or USCIS district where you file. You must also show good moral character for the required period up to the oath (INA 316(a)(3)), pass English and civics tests (INA 312), and be willing to take the Oath of Allegiance and support the Constitution. You may file up to 90 days before you complete the continuous-residence requirement (INA 334).

The steps, in order

  1. Confirm you meet the eligibility rules above and gather your green card, tax returns, and travel history. 2) File Form N-400 online through a USCIS online account ($710) or by paper ($760); if you are asking for a fee waiver or reduced fee, you must file by paper. 3) Attend a biometrics appointment (fingerprints and photo) if USCIS schedules one. 4) Study for the English and civics tests, then attend your naturalization interview at a USCIS field office, where an officer reviews your application and tests you. 5) If approved, receive Form N-445, attend your oath ceremony, return your green card, take the Oath of Allegiance, and receive your Certificate of Naturalization.

The form and the fee

You apply on Form N-400, online or on paper. The fee is $710 filed online or $760 on paper, with biometrics included. Fees change: confirm the current amount on the USCIS N-400 page before you file, because a wrong fee means a rejected application.

You can ask USCIS to waive the N-400 fee entirely by filing Form I-912 (there is no fee to file I-912) with a paper N-400; you cannot file online if you request a waiver. You qualify if you receive a means-tested benefit, your household income is at or below 150% of the Federal Poverty Guidelines (for example, $21,870 for a household of 1 in the contiguous United States under the guidelines effective January 13, 2026), or you can show financial hardship. The N-400 fee remains waiver-eligible even though H.R.-1 (Pub. L. 119-21, July 4, 2025) made some other USCIS fees non-waivable.

If your household income is more than 150% but not more than 400% of the Federal Poverty Guidelines, you may qualify for a reduced N-400 fee of $380 by filing Form I-942, which is now Part 10 of Form N-400. Like a fee waiver, a reduced-fee request must be filed by paper, not online. See the USCIS reduced-fee page and the I-942 page for the current income tables.

The two tests — and who is excused from them

The English test has three parts: reading (read one of three sentences aloud), writing (write one of three sentences correctly), and speaking and understanding, which the officer assesses throughout the interview. The civics test depends on when you filed: if you filed BEFORE October 20, 2025, you take the 2008 test, drawn from 100 questions, with the officer asking up to 10 and 6 correct answers needed to pass (5 wrong answers is a fail). If you filed ON OR AFTER October 20, 2025, you take the 2025 test, drawn from 128 questions, with the officer asking 20 and 12 correct answers needed to pass (9 wrong answers is a fail). Applicants age 65 or older with 20 years as a resident (65/20) answer 10 questions from a special bank of 20 under either version. If you fail any part at your first interview, USCIS schedules a re-examination 60 to 90 days later covering only the part you failed; failing a second time means your application is denied, though you may apply again later with a new fee.

If you are 50 or older and have been a lawful permanent resident for 20 years (50/20), or 55 or older with 15 years as a resident (55/15), you are exempt from the English test but must still take the civics test, which you may take in your own language with an interpreter. If you are 65 or older with 20 years as a resident (65/20), you also get a shorter civics test drawn from a special list of 20 questions. If a medically determinable physical, developmental, or mental disability lasting or expected to last at least 12 months prevents you from meeting the English and/or civics requirement, a licensed medical professional can certify the exception on Form N-648, explaining how the disability prevents you from meeting the requirement.

Free study materials — including the civics questions and practice tests — are on the USCIS study page.

How long it takes

USCIS publishes processing-time estimates by field office in its Case Processing Times tool, updated monthly, showing the time in which it completed 80% of adjudicated cases over the prior six months, from receipt to decision. There is no single national figure, so check your own field office in the tool. As of the USCIS data checked November 14, 2026, the national median was about 7.4 months for fiscal year 2026 through August 30, 2026, with published office ranges roughly 8 to 17.5 months for 80% of cases and the slowest offices over 20 months; do not treat any single number as a guarantee for your case.

Check your own field office in the USCIS Case Processing Times tool.

After you are approved

You are not a U.S. citizen until you take the Oath of Allegiance at a naturalization ceremony (run by USCIS or by a court). USCIS sends Form N-445 telling you when and where to appear; at the ceremony you check in, return your Permanent Resident Card (green card), take the oath, and receive your Certificate of Naturalization, which you should check for errors before you leave. After the ceremony, update your Social Security record with the Social Security Administration (generally wait at least 10 days, or ask USCIS to update it when you file the N-400), apply for a U.S. passport, and register to vote where you are eligible; a voter registration application is often provided at the ceremony.

Free and low-cost help in Texas

  • American Gateways
  • BakerRipley Immigration and Citizenship Program
  • Catholic Charities Dallas - Immigration and Legal Services

USCIS field offices in Texas where interviews and oath ceremonies happen: Houston, Dallas, San Antonio, El Paso, Harlingen. Field offices do not take walk-ins — go only with an appointment on your notice. Find your assigned office in the USCIS office locator.

Questions for USCIS: the USCIS Contact Center at 1-800-375-5283 (TTY 1-800-767-1833). Beware of immigration-services scams: only a licensed attorney or a DOJ-accredited representative may give you legal advice about your case.

Sources: USCIS — Application for Naturalization (Form N-400). Checked against official sources in November 2026. Fees and processing times change — confirm the current fee on the USCIS fee schedule before you file. This is general information, not legal advice.

Stalking in Texas

Stalking is a pattern of unwanted attention that makes you afraid — being followed, watched, threatened, or contacted over and over after you have made it clear you want it to stop. It can come from a stranger, a neighbor, a coworker, an ex, or someone you barely know. This section covers what to do right now, how to document it, how to report it, the protective order in Texas, when stalking is a crime, and online stalking. This is general information, not legal advice.

What to do right now in Texas

If you are in danger right now, call 911. Then: tell someone you trust what is happening and who is doing it. Vary your routines and routes where you can. Lock your doors and windows, and tell your workplace or school security if the person shows up there. Do not agree to meet the person to talk it out, and do not reply to their messages — silence is safer than an argument, and every reply can encourage more contact.

If someone may be watching what you do on this phone or computer, use a safer device if you can — a friend’s phone, a work or library computer. The Quick exit button at the bottom-right of this page leaves this site fast, but it cannot erase your browsing history.

For free confidential help anytime, call the National Domestic Violence Hotline at 1-800-799-7233 or text START to 88788.

How to document stalking in Texas

Start a log now: dates, times, places, what happened, and any witnesses. Save texts, messages, voicemails, photos, and emails — do not delete anything, even if it upsets you to keep it. Screenshots with visible dates help. Back the log up somewhere the other person cannot reach, such as a trusted friend’s email or a cloud account they do not know about. A police report helps too, even if no arrest is made — bring copies of everything to the hearing.

Reporting stalking to the police in Texas

Report it to your local police department or sheriff’s office, in person if you can. Bring your log, your saved messages and photos, and the names of any witnesses. Tell them the full pattern, not just the latest incident — stalking is about repeated conduct, and a single incident rarely tells the story. Ask for the report number and keep it with your log. A report does not force you to press charges, but it creates a record, and it is often what a judge wants to see before granting a protective order.

The protective order in Texas

  • Protective Order for Victims of Stalking (Chapter 7B): Tex. Code Crim. Proc. arts. 7B.001-7B.007
  • Where to file: File an application for a protective order under Chapter 7B in the court in the county where you live; no relationship to the offender is required.
  • Cost: Ask the clerk what it costs to file and whether a fee waiver is available — the fee was not published on an official source we could verify.
  • How long it lasts: For the duration of the lives of the offender and victim, or for a shorter period stated in the order; if no period is stated, the order lasts until the second anniversary of the date it was issued.

The Domestic Abuse section on this page has more detail on how protective orders work — a judge can often issue a temporary order the same day you file, with a hearing within a few weeks where both sides are heard before a longer order is granted. After the judge signs, law enforcement serves the order on the other person — you do not deliver it yourself.

Stalking as a crime in Texas

Tex. Penal Code § 42.072. Stalking is knowingly engaging, on more than one occasion and as part of the same scheme or course of conduct directed at a specific person, in conduct that constitutes harassment or involves threats of bodily injury, death, or property damage, which the actor knows or reasonably should know the other person will regard as threatening.

Penalty: Third-degree felony (2 to 10 years in prison, fine up to $10,000); a second or subsequent stalking conviction is a second-degree felony (2 to 20 years, fine up to $10,000).

Online stalking (cyberstalking) in Texas

Texas has no separate cyberstalking statute. Texas has no separate cyberstalking statute; online conduct is prosecuted under the stalking statute, Tex. Penal Code § 42.072, which covers electronic conduct.

If someone is tracking you, check your phone for unknown apps and location sharing, change your passwords from a safe device, and turn off location sharing on apps and photos. Do not delete the messages — they are evidence.

Where to get help in Texas

  • Texas Council on Family Violence — shelters, legal help, and safety planning across Texas.
  • National Domestic Violence Hotline: 1-800-799-7233 (24/7) or text START to 88788.

Texas source: official Texas page. Checked against official sources in November 2026. Stalking laws change — confirm the current law on the official source before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

End of Life & Death with Dignity in Texas

This section covers end-of-life law in Texas: medical aid in dying, advance directives, naming someone to make health care decisions for you, POLST medical orders, and refusing treatment or choosing hospice care. The law differs sharply from state to state on these questions, so what follows is what Texas law says, in plain language. This is general information, not legal advice.

Medical aid in dying in Texas

Medical aid in dying is not authorized in Texas. Tex. Penal Code § 22.08 makes it an offense, with intent to promote or assist suicide by another, to aid or attempt to aid the other to commit or attempt to commit suicide.

Advance directives in Texas

  • Form: Directive to Physicians and Family or Surrogates, and Medical Power of Attorney
  • Get the official form: official Texas form
  • Signing rules: Sign the directive in front of two adult witnesses, at least one of whom must be disinterested, or have it notarized, under the Texas Advance Directives Act, Tex. Health & Safety Code ch. 166.
  • See also: Wills & Probate in Texas on this page covers wills and estates; this section covers health care decisions.

Naming someone to decide for you in Texas

  • Medical Power of Attorney agent: Name an agent in a Medical Power of Attorney. The agent makes health care decisions, including decisions about life-prolonging care, when your doctor determines you can no longer make them.

MOST (Medical Orders for Scope of Treatment) in Texas

Texas has no officially recognized statewide POLST form. A MOST is completed with a doctor and does not replace an advance directive, and Texas also has an Out-of-Hospital Do-Not-Resuscitate order under Tex. Health & Safety Code ch. 166.

Refusing treatment and hospice care in Texas

You may direct withholding or withdrawal of life-sustaining treatment when you have a terminal or irreversible condition. Comfort care and pain relief must still be provided, and hospice is comfort-focused care.

Where to get help in Texas

  • Find a lawyer or legal help in Texas
  • Phone: (800) 252-9690

Texas source: official Texas page. Checked against official sources in November 2026. End-of-life laws change — confirm the current law on the official source before you rely on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Daycare & Childcare in Texas

If you run a daycare or you are choosing one for your child, Texas has one licensing agency and one set of rules. This page covers both sides — what providers must do to stay licensed, and how parents can check a provider, see its record, and complain if something goes wrong. If you suspect a child is being abused or neglected, report it right away — the Childhelp National Child Abuse Hotline is 1-800-422-4453, also listed under National resources at the bottom of this page. This section is about licensing, inspections, and complaints about the program itself.

Licensing in Texas — at a glance

  • Licensing agency: Texas Health and Human Services Commission, Child Care Regulation
  • License types: Licensed Child Care Center, Registered Child Care Home, and Listed Family Home.
  • How to get licensed: Apply for a permit from HHSC Child Care Regulation, pass inspections, and meet the Minimum Standards for the permit type.

Staff-to-child ratios in Texas

These are the state’s minimum ratios — the most children one adult may care for at a time:

  • 0–11 months: 1 adult to 4 children
  • 12–17 months: 1 adult to 5 children
  • 18–23 months: 1 adult to 9 children
  • 2 years: 1 adult to 11 children
  • 3 years: 1 adult to 15 children
  • 4 years: 1 adult to 18 children
  • 5 years: 1 adult to 22 children
  • 6 years and older: 1 adult to 26 children

Source: official Texas ratio rules

For parents: check a provider before you enroll

  • Check the license: look up this provider with the licensing agency before you enroll.
  • See inspection history: the same lookup shows inspection and violation records for licensed providers.
  • What the provider must tell you: Parents have the right to file a complaint, review the home's publicly accessible records, receive HHSC inspection reports, and be given contact information for Child Care Regulation and instructions on accessing the compliance history online.
  • Always ask: ask for the provider’s license number and the name of the agency that issued it, then check both with the agency above.

If something goes wrong with your child

Start with the provider — tell the director what happened, in writing, and keep a copy. If that does not fix it, or the problem is about safety or licensing rules:

  • Child Care Regulation inspects licensed and registered operations and investigates reports and complaints of possible violations, including unpermitted operation; suspected abuse or neglect should be reported to the Texas Abuse Hotline.
  • Complaint line: 1-800-252-5400
  • File a complaint: complaint page
  • If a child is in immediate danger, call 911. If you suspect abuse or neglect, report it right away — the Childhelp hotline is 1-800-422-4453.

Contracts, deposits, and withdrawing your child

Read the contract before you sign it. Daycare terms vary by provider, so get these in writing: the weekly rate and what it covers, the deposit and when it is refunded, how much notice you must give to withdraw, what happens if you pull your child out mid-month, and late pickup fees. Keep a signed copy. If the provider changes the terms, ask for the change in writing too.

Texas source: official Texas page. Checked against official sources in November 2026. Child care rules change — confirm the current requirements on the agency’s page before you rely on them. This is general information, not legal advice.

Pulled Over: Your Rights in Texas

Getting pulled over is stressful even when nothing is wrong. Most of a traffic stop works the same in every state — the steps and rights below are federal. The card at the top covers the few things your state does differently.

At a traffic stop in Texas

  • Carrying a firearm: If you are carrying a firearm, you must disclose it when the officer asks — including showing your permit on demand.
  • Refusing a DUI test: License suspended 180 days (2 years with an alcohol- or drug-related contact in the prior 10 years).
  • Passenger ID: No duty to identify merely as a detained passenger — refusing to give your name is an offense only after a lawful arrest; giving a false name while detained is a crime.
  • Signing the ticket: You are taken immediately before a magistrate; signing the written notice as a promise to appear is what secures your release.
  • Official source: Official Texas traffic-stop source

Worth knowing in Texas

The duty to display your handgun license with your ID is still in the law, but since 2009 there is no penalty or license suspension for failing to display it.

Step by step: what to do at the stop

  1. Pull over safely. Slow down, signal, and stop somewhere safe and lit if you can. Hazards on.
  2. Set the stage. Engine off, window down, interior light on at night, hands on the wheel where they can be seen.
  3. Announce your movements. "My registration is in the glove box" before you reach. Sudden reaching is what makes stops dangerous.
  4. Hand over license, registration, and insurance. The driver must provide these when asked — that is the law everywhere.
  5. You don't have to chat. Beyond identifying yourself, you may politely decline questions: "I'd rather not answer questions." A routine stop does not require Miranda warnings — silence is still your right.
  6. Don't consent to a search. If asked, say clearly: "I don't consent to a search." Consent makes a search legal even when the officer had no right to search without it. Never physically resist a search — say no, and fight it in court.
  7. Orders are different from requests. An officer may order you — and your passengers — out of the car. Comply with orders; ask about the rest.
  8. Ask before you assume it's over: "Am I free to go?" If yes, leave calmly. If you are being arrested, say you want a lawyer and then stay silent.
  9. Note the details right after: the officer's name and badge number, the agency, time, place, and what was said. Write it down while it is fresh.

Searches, dogs, and how long a stop can last

  • A stop needs a lawful reason to start — an officer may not pull you over on a hunch (Delaware v. Prouse).
  • Without probable cause, an officer needs your permission to search — and permission is yours to withhold. Under Rodriguez v. United States, a stop that is finished — or should be — cannot be stretched out to wait for a drug dog without reasonable suspicion.
  • An officer can pat down your outer clothing if they reasonably believe you are armed (Terry v. Ohio) — that is not the same as a full search, and it does not need your consent.

If it's a DUI stop

  • Roadside field sobriety tests are voluntary in most states — but refusing the chemical test after arrest is different. Every state has an implied-consent law, and refusal carries the consequence in your state's card above; in a few states, refusal is itself a crime.
  • Police generally need a warrant for a blood draw (Birchfield v. North Dakota) — breath tests are treated differently.
  • If you are arrested, further questions require Miranda warnings. Say you want a lawyer, clearly, and then stop talking.

If you believe you were profiled

Being stopped because of your race is unconstitutional — but be clear-eyed about how these cases work:

  • Under Whren v. United States, if the officer genuinely observed a traffic violation, courts usually uphold the stop even when the driver believes race was the real reason. That makes the pattern and the paper trail what matters.
  • Write down everything while it's fresh: badge number, agency, time, place, the reason given, and anything said about race. Ask whether dashcam or bodycam video exists — complaints and lawsuits can obtain it.
  • File the complaint anyway: with the agency itself, your state attorney general or civil-rights agency, and the U.S. Department of Justice Civil Rights Division. This page's Civil Rights section has the links and the deadlines — many run out in 180 days to a year.
  • Some states ban racial profiling by statute and require officers to record data at every stop; where those laws exist, your complaint becomes part of an official record that can prove a pattern.
  • If you were ticketed or arrested at a stop you believe was profiling, tell your lawyer about it at the first meeting — it can matter to the case even when the stop itself is hard to challenge.

Texas source: official Texas page. Checked against official sources in November 2026. Traffic-stop law is mostly federal; the state-specific rules above change with new legislation, so confirm on the official page. This is general information, not legal advice.

Online Gambling Laws in Texas

Neither online sports betting nor online casino gambling is legal in Texas — any site offering them to people here is unlicensed.

There is no federal gambling license and no single national law. Since the Supreme Court struck down the federal sports-betting ban in 2018, each state decides for itself — and most states allow some kinds of online gambling while banning others. An ad on national TV does not mean the app is licensed in your state.

Online gambling in Texas at a glance

  • Sports betting: Not legal — no licensed sports betting, online or in person.
  • Online casino: Not offered — no licensed online casino operates.
  • Online poker: Not offered — no licensed online poker operates.
  • Sweepstakes casinos: No ban in force — sweeps sites still operate here, in a legal gray area.
  • Official source: Official Texas gambling source

Worth knowing in Texas

Placing a bet is itself a Class C misdemeanor for the bettor under Penal Code §47.02 — not just for the bookmaker.

Licensed vs. offshore — the distinction that matters

The single most important distinction in online gambling is not what you play — it is who holds the license.

  • Licensed sites are approved by the state regulator, must verify your age and location, must pay winnings, must offer self-exclusion and deposit limits, and answer to the state when something goes wrong.
  • Offshore sites (licensed in Curaçao, or nowhere) operate outside U.S. law. If one refuses to pay you, there is no regulator to call and usually no way to get your money. Running them is illegal in the U.S., and in a few states using them is an offense for the player too.
  • The 30-second check: a licensed operator appears on your state regulator's official licensee list, and its app will refuse your bet when you are physically outside the state. If a site takes your bet from anywhere and never checks where you are, it is not a U.S.-licensed site.

Sweepstakes casinos and prediction markets

  • Sweepstakes casinos — the "free" coin sites that let you cash out winnings — claimed they were not gambling at all. States have been shutting that door since 2025; check your state's line above. Where they are banned, operating one is usually the crime, not playing — but your balance can still vanish when a site is forced out of a state.
  • Prediction markets sell "event contracts" on sports and claim federal regulation lets them operate even where sports betting is illegal. State regulators disagree, lawsuits are flying, and New Jersey's case against one operator has reached the U.S. Supreme Court. Until the courts settle it, treat prediction-market sports contracts as legally unsettled: money deposited there sits outside your state's gambling protections.

Taxes — and if gambling stops being fun

  • Gambling winnings are taxable income in every state, licensed or not — and the IRS does not care whether the bet was legal. Big wins generate a W-2G form.
  • You may deduct losses only up to your winnings, and you must itemize. Starting with tax year 2026, only 90% of losses count — so a year where you broke even can still leave you owing tax on "profit" you never saw. Keep records of wins and losses as you go.
  • Every legal state runs a self-exclusion program: one signup bars you from every licensed site and sportsbook in the state, and the sites must stop marketing to you. Licensed apps also have to offer deposit and time limits.
  • Free, confidential help is available 24/7 at 1-800-522-4700 (National Problem Gambling Helpline) — call or text. Using it does not go on any record.

Texas source: official Texas gambling page. Checked against official sources in November 2026 — gambling law is changing state by state every year, so confirm the current status on the regulator's page before relying on it. This is general information, not legal advice.

Cannabis Laws in Texas

Marijuana law in America is two laws at once — your state’s law and federal law — and they do not agree. In Texas, there is no legal marijuana — only a narrow exception for low-THC CBD oil. Everything else is still against state law.

Official source

Texas at a glance.
Status: CBD / low-THC oil only — no legal marijuana.
Growing at home: not allowed in Texas.
Where it is sold: Licensed medical dispensaries only.
Small amounts: still a crime — Texas has no decriminalization law, so possession can bring a criminal charge.
Official source: Texas Department of Public Safety — Compassionate Use Program.

Heads up

Worth knowing in Texas. Texas calls it a medical program, but DPS defines 'low-THC cannabis' as no more than 1.0% THC by weight — HB 46 (2025) added conditions and licenses but kept the 1% cap.

Federal law

The federal catch — read this before you travel, apply for a job, or buy a gun. Washington changed the rules in April 2026 — but only partway. A Justice Department order that took effect April 28, 2026 moved FDA-approved marijuana medicines and state-licensed medical marijuana to Schedule III, the less-strict federal drug category. Recreational marijuana was left out on purpose: it is still a Schedule I drug — illegal under federal law in every state, no matter what your state says. A separate case that could move all marijuana is still being decided. Until then:

Guns. Federal law bars anyone who uses a controlled substance unlawfully from buying or owning a gun — and recreational use is still unlawful federally, even where your state allows it. The federal firearm purchase form asks about marijuana use; answering falsely is itself a federal crime.

Jobs and drug tests. Federal employees, truck drivers, and anyone in a federally regulated drug-testing job can still lose the job — or the offer — over marijuana that was legal at home. A state medical card is not a shield.

Housing. Federally assisted housing can deny an application, or evict, over marijuana use — including state-legal medical use.

Federal land. National parks, national forests, and other federal land follow federal law. Possession there is a federal offense, even inside a legal state.

Travel. Never take marijuana across a state line — not even between two legal states — and never bring it to an airport or onto a plane.

Driving. In every state, driving after using marijuana is a DUI — a legal purchase is no defense. Keep any marijuana sealed and out of the driver’s reach, ideally in the trunk.

Deadline

The gas-station loophole closes this winter. The delta-8, THCA flower, and THC seltzers sold at gas stations and smoke shops under the farm-bill “hemp” loophole are losing their federal legal cover in two steps: lab-made cannabinoids the hemp plant cannot produce naturally on November 12, 2026, and — from December 11, 2026 — any finished hemp product with more than 0.4 mg of total THC in the container counts as marijuana under federal law. Products from licensed state dispensaries are not affected.

Charged with a marijuana offense in Texas?

  1. Stay calm and be polite — arguing on the roadside never helps and often hurts.
  2. You may refuse a search of your car or home: “I do not consent to a search.” Say it clearly, then stop talking about it.
  3. Do not explain where you got it, how much you have, or who else is involved. Answering questions is optional — use that.
  4. Write down everything while it is fresh: where, when, what was said, what was taken.
  5. Do not miss your first court date — missing it turns one charge into two.
  6. Ask about diversion, expungement, or record-sealing — many states now clear old marijuana convictions automatically or on request (see the Record Clearing section on this page).
  7. Talk to a criminal-defense lawyer before pleading to anything: a marijuana conviction can affect jobs, housing, student aid, and — for non-citizens — immigration status.

Texas sources: Texas Department of Public Safety — Compassionate Use Program. Federal sources: the Justice Department’s April 2026 rescheduling order (published in the Federal Register on April 28, 2026) and the hemp changes in Public Law 119-37, as delayed by Public Law 119-103. Checked against official sources in November 2026 — cannabis laws change fast, so confirm the current rules on the official page before relying on them. This is general information, not legal advice.

Personal Injury in Texas

Personal injury is the law that applies when someone else’s carelessness hurts you: a fall on a wet floor or a broken step, a dog bite, a dangerous condition on someone’s property, a product that injures you. Car crashes, medical malpractice, and injuries at work have their own sections on this page — but the three Texas rules below decide most injury cases of every kind: how long you have to sue, what happens if you were partly at fault, and the special fast track for claims against the government.

Official source

Your deadline to sue in Texas: 2 years

Texas gives you 2 years from the date of the injury to file a personal-injury lawsuit (Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 16.003(a)). Miss that deadline and the court will almost always throw the case out — it does not matter how strong it is or how badly you were hurt. The clock usually starts the day of the injury; injured children and some hidden injuries are treated differently, which is one more reason to ask a lawyer early instead of guessing. Read Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 16.003(a) →

Fault rules

If you were partly at fault

Texas follows modified comparative fault: you can recover as long as you were not more than 50% at fault, and your award is reduced by your share of the blame. Once your share passes 50%, you recover nothing. Read Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 33.001 →

Government claims

Hurt on government property, or by a government vehicle?

Claims against a city, county, or the state play by different rules, with a first step that usually comes long before the lawsuit deadline above. In Texas: written notice of claim to the government unit within 6 months after the incident. Miss that step and the claim is usually over before it starts — so if a public sidewalk, a city bus, a public building, or a government driver was involved, treat it as urgent. Read Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 101.101(a) →

Dog bites

Bitten by a dog in Texas?

Texas follows the older “one-bite” / negligence rule: there is no automatic liability. To win, you generally have to show the owner knew — or should have known — the dog was dangerous (for example, it had bitten or lunged at someone before), or that the owner was careless, like letting it run loose in violation of a leash law.

Fees

How injury lawyers get paid: nothing up front

Most personal-injury lawyers work on contingency: you pay nothing up front and nothing out of pocket, and the lawyer takes a percentage of what you recover — commonly a third (33⅓%), sometimes up to 40% if the case has to be tried — plus the case’s costs. If you lose, you generally owe no attorney fee. First consultations are almost always free. Get the fee agreement in writing before you sign, and ask exactly which costs come out of your share. And never hire a lawyer who shows up uninvited at a hospital or demands cash up front — that is a classic runner scam.

Report fraud to the FTC →

Watch out in Texas: City charters can impose notice deadlines much shorter than the state's 6 months (§ 101.101(b)) — check the city's charter.

What to do:

  1. Get medical care first. Your health comes first — and the medical records become the backbone of any claim. Gaps in treatment, or skipping follow-up visits, get used against you later.
  2. Report it, and get the report. A store, landlord, or property owner should make an incident report; ask for a copy or the report number. If nobody will take a report, write down the date, time, and who you told.
  3. Photograph everything before it changes. The hazard that hurt you, the whole scene, and your injuries as they heal. Stores record over their camera footage fast — ask in writing, right away, for the video to be saved.
  4. Keep the paper trail. Medical bills, prescriptions, pay stubs for missed work, and receipts for anything the injury cost you. Get witnesses’ names and phone numbers while memories are fresh.
  5. Be careful with the other side’s insurance adjuster. You do not have to give a recorded statement, sign a release, or accept a quick offer on their timetable. Early offers often come before anyone knows how serious the injury is — and signing a release ends the claim for good. Do not post about the accident, or your activities, on social media.
  6. Mind both clocks. Texas’s lawsuit deadline is 2 years — and a claim involving the government can have a notice deadline measured in days. Talk to a lawyer early, while the evidence still exists; the first consultation is usually free.
  7. Small injury, small dollars? If the bills are modest and fault is clear, you can send a written demand yourself — and if they won’t pay fairly, small claims court handles cases without a lawyer. For a car crash specifically, see Car Accident Injuries on this page.

Texas sources: filing deadline Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 16.003(a); fault Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 33.001; government claims Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 101.101(a). Checked against official sources in November 2026 — deadlines change, and the clock rules have exceptions, so confirm the current law on the official page or with a lawyer before relying on a date. This is general information, not legal advice.

Jury Duty in Texas

A jury summons is a court order, not an invitation — but millions of people serve every year without a lawyer and without trouble. This section explains how jury service works in Texas: what it pays, what your employer can and cannot do, how to ask for an excuse or a different date, and what happens if you ignore the envelope.

Jury duty

How jury service works in Texas

Each county receives a list of potential jurors from the Secretary of State made up of county residents who are registered to vote or hold a Texas driver's license or identification card; citizens are randomly selected and mailed a summons. The jury selection process may take only a day or a fraction of a day, and jury duty generally lasts about one week for those selected. Jurors are paid by the county not less than $20.00 for the first day or fraction of the first day served and not less than $58.00 for each day or fraction of a day served after the first day. A county may also provide mileage, transportation, parking, or other reimbursement.

Work, excuses & moving your date

Your job, asking to be excused, and postponing

An employer is not required to pay an employee for time missed from work due to jury service. An employer cannot fire, threaten to fire, penalize, or threaten to penalize an employee for performing jury duty, and one who does is subject to sanctions for contempt of court and payment of damages to the employee. A person may choose to be excused if, among other listed exemptions, they are over 75 years of age, have legal custody of a child younger than 12 who would be left without adequate supervision, are a student, or are the primary caretaker of a person unable to care for themselves; a judge may also permanently or temporarily exempt a person with a physical or mental impairment.

Official source

Texas Judicial Branch — Jury Service in Texas

The courts' own juror information for Texas — reporting instructions, excuse and postponement requests, and county jury offices.

Official juror information →

What to do:

  1. Answer the summons. Follow the instructions on it — most courts let you respond, confirm your date, or check reporting status online or by phone, and many require a response before you appear.
  2. Can’t make that date? Ask — don’t skip. Use the jury office contact on your summons to request a postponement or an excuse before the date arrives. Courts move dates routinely; silence is what gets people in trouble.
  3. Tell your employer right away. Show them the summons and keep a copy for yourself — the work rules for Texas are in the card above.
  4. Skipping has real consequences in Texas: A person who fails to answer a jury summons as directed is subject to a contempt action punishable by a fine of not less than $100 nor more than $1,000 (Gov't Code 62.0141). A juror lawfully notified who fails to attend court without a reasonable excuse, or files a false exemption claim, is subject to a fine of not less than $100 nor more than $500 (Gov't Code 62.111).
  5. Beware the jury-duty phone scam. Courts and sheriffs never call demanding gift cards, wire transfers, or payment for “missed jury duty,” and they never threaten arrest over the phone. If you get that call, hang up — then call the court yourself at the number on its official website.

Jury service is run by county courts, so reporting times, room locations, and some pay details vary by county — your summons and your county jury office have the final word. This is general information, not legal advice.

Trespassing in Texas

Trespassing cuts both ways, so this section does too: what to do about the person who will not stay off your property, and what it means when you are the one told to leave or banned from a place. Most of it turns on notice — being told, a sign, a fence, or paint — though some offenses need no warning at all. Here is how Texas draws the line, and what the basic offense costs.

Your property

Someone on your property who won’t leave?

Texas notice (§ 30.05(b)(2)) means oral or written communication by the owner or a person with apparent authority to act for the owner; fencing or another enclosure obviously designed to exclude intruders; a sign or signs posted on the property or at the entrance to a building, reasonably likely to come to the attention of intruders, indicating entry is forbidden; growing crops or crops being harvested; or purple paint marks on trees or posts: each mark a vertical line at least 8 inches long and 1 inch wide, the bottom not less than 3 feet nor more than 5 feet from the ground, placed at readily visible locations no more than 100 feet apart on forest land or 1,000 feet apart on other land. There is no statewide trespass-warning form or registry — the owner's notice itself is what makes a later entry, or a refusal to leave, arrestable.

Accused

Told you’re trespassing — or banned from a place?

Criminal trespass is a Class B misdemeanor (§ 30.05(d)(1)); it is a Class C misdemeanor on agricultural land within 100 feet of the boundary or residential land within 100 feet of a protected freshwater area, and a Class A misdemeanor in a habitation or shelter center, on a Superfund site, or when the person carries a deadly weapon. Whatever the merits, leave when you are told to: arguing your case on the spot can turn a warning into an arrest. Write down who told you, when, and where, and do not go back until it is resolved. If you believe the warning was wrong — you had permission, the place was public, or nobody ever actually told you — that is a defense to raise afterward, in writing or in court, not in the doorway.

Official source

Texas trespass law — Tex. Penal Code § 30.05

The Texas rules above are based on this official source — the statute itself. Penalties climb for dwellings, repeat offenses, and property damage, so if any of those are in play, the statute’s higher tiers are the part to read, alongside any warning or citation you received.

Read the official source →

What to do:

  1. Owners: say it clearly, then put it in writing. A dated note or letter saying the person is not allowed on the property, with a copy kept, is what most trespass charges stand on — in most states, the warning comes first.
  2. Posting land? Follow the exact rules in the first card. Sign wording, paint color, and spacing are the first things challenged when a posting is sloppy.
  3. If they won’t leave, or they keep coming back: call the police non-emergency line — 911 if anyone is threatened or it is happening right now. Don’t physically remove them yourself.
  4. Document every incident. Dates, times, photos or video, witnesses. That record backs up criminal charges and a civil claim for any damage they did — the small claims section covers suing without a lawyer, and if the trespasser is a neighbor, the neighbor disputes section covers that fight.
