  5. If you were the one warned or banned: leave immediately, write down who warned you, when, and where, and stay away until it is sorted out. Returning after a warning is usually the crime itself — challenge it afterward, in writing or in court.

This is general information, not legal advice. Trespass law turns on the exact facts — who owns the place, what notice was given, and whether anyone was told to leave — and local ordinances can add rules on top of the state statute.

Car booted or towed in Texas

A boot — a clamp locked over your wheel — means your car is not moving until money changes hands. Cars get booted two ways: a city clamps the cars of people who pile up unpaid parking tickets, and a private parking-lot operator clamps a car it says parked without permission or payment. Who put it there decides which rules protect you, and in Texas the two rulebooks are very different. Start with the notice left on your car: it names who booted you, what they say you owe, and the number to call.

City boot

Booted for unpaid parking tickets?

Municipal, not state — Texas cities run their own scofflaw booting for unpaid parking tickets under city ordinances; ch. 2308 regulates private booting companies. Move today: many city programs tow a booted car that is not settled promptly — sometimes within 24 to 72 hours — and daily storage charges pile up after that. When you call, ask two questions: is there a payment plan, and how do I contest the tickets?

Private lot

Booted in a parking lot?

Texas licenses booting companies and individual boot operators through the Department of Licensing and Regulation (Occupations Code ch. 2308). Parking facilities where booting is enforced must post signs, a conspicuous notice on the booted vehicle must tell the owner how to complain, and cities and counties set the maximum boot-removal fee (§ 2308.2085) — so there is no single statewide dollar cap. An owner who believes a boot was unlawful or the fee too high may request a tow hearing in justice court and file a complaint with TDLR. If the company broke these rules — no required sign, a charge over the cap, no release after you paid — you may not owe the fee at all. If you need the car now, pay and keep the receipt: paying gets your car back, it does not end the fight. Then complain to the regulator named in the official source below and ask for your money back.

Official source

Texas TDLR — towing and booting consumer information

The Texas rules above are based on this official source. Booting rules are mostly local, so read it alongside the notice on your car, the city’s parking rules, and any terms posted at the lot.

Read the official source →

What to do:

  1. Photograph everything first. The boot, the notice on the windshield, any signs at the lot entrance, and your parking receipt or payment-app screen if you had paid. Photos taken now are your evidence later.
  2. Read the notice — it tells you who booted you. A city boot means unpaid tickets; a lot boot means a private operator. The notice carries the amount and the phone number, and the two problems have different rulebooks, so do not pay the wrong office.
  3. Do not drive on the boot, and do not pry or cut it off. You can wreck your wheel and suspension, and damaging or removing a boot adds fines or criminal charges in many places. Some modern boots are self-releasing once you pay electronically — the notice will say if yours is.
  4. Get it released today. City boot: pay the tickets plus the boot fee, or get on a payment plan if the city offers one — waiting risks a tow and storage fees. Lot boot: pay what is demanded if you need the car, and keep the receipt.
  5. Then dispute it, in writing. Wrong tickets, a meter you had paid, no posted sign, a charge over the legal cap — each has a route: the hearing or appeal named on the notice, a complaint through the Consumer protection section of this page, and small claims court for the fee if it comes to that.

This is general information, not legal advice. Booting rules are mostly local and change often — the notice on your windshield, the city parking office, and the lot’s posted terms control the exact amounts and deadlines in your case.

If your car was impounded in Texas

Impoundment is the step after a tow: your car sits in a lot, storage charges pile up daily, and if you wait too long the lot can sell it. Here is how to find it, what it costs, how to fight the tow, and what happens if you leave it there.

Find your car

Where is my car, and how do I get it back?

The Texas Department of Licensing and Regulation (TDLR) maintains a statewide towed-vehicle locator at tdlr.texas.gov/towing. Enter the license plate number or vehicle identification number to find where the vehicle was taken. To reclaim the vehicle, the owner must present proof of ownership (title or registration), a valid driver's license, and proof of insurance to the towing or storage company, and pay the applicable towing and storage fees. The towing company must release the vehicle within 1 hour of a request made during normal business hours.

Fees

What will it cost?

State law (Texas Occupations Code 2308.2065) caps the maximum amount a towing company may charge for a nonconsent tow of a vehicle of 10,000 pounds or less at $255 for a tow from private property, plus $20 per day for storage. A towing company may also charge a $50 gate fee for a release after hours. These are statewide maximums; a company may charge less.

Contest the tow

How do I contest the tow or impoundment?

You may request a hearing from the justice court in the county where the vehicle was towed. The hearing request must be filed within 14 days of the date the vehicle was towed. If the court finds the tow was not authorized, the towing company must return the vehicle and refund the towing and storage fees.

If you don't claim it

What happens if I don't claim my car?

A vehicle that is not claimed within 30 days after the notice of tow is mailed may be sold at public auction to satisfy the lien for towing and storage charges. The towing or storage company must publish a notice of sale in a newspaper of general circulation once a week for two consecutive weeks. The owner may still be liable for any deficiency if the sale does not cover the towing and storage charges.

Police vs. private tow

Police-ordered tow vs. private-property tow

A police-ordered tow is authorized when a vehicle is abandoned, illegally parked, or involved in a traffic violation. A private-property tow is authorized under Texas Occupations Code chapter 2308 when a vehicle is parked on private property without authorization; the property owner must have signs posted and a contract with the towing company, and the towing company must notify the owner within 24 hours. Both kinds of tow are subject to the same 30-day claim window before the vehicle may be sold at public auction. A nonconsent tow from private property is capped at $255 plus $20 per day for storage under section 2308.2065.

Official sources

Where these rules come from

The rules above come from these official sources. Tow and impound rules are often local, so read them alongside the notice on your car and the lot's posted terms.
tdlr.texas.gov →
tdlr.texas.gov →
TxDMV.gov →

Contractor disputes in Texas

You paid for the work, and the work isn’t done — the contractor stopped showing up, walked off with your deposit, or left you with a job done badly. This is one of the most common consumer problems there is, and you have more moves than most people realize. Work them in order: paper trail first, written demand second, then the state — licensing board, recovery fund, attorney general — and court if it comes to that. And do not hand over more money to lure a contractor back to the job. That is how people lose twice.

Licensing

Texas does not license home contractors at the state level

Texas does not license general or residential contractors at the state level — trades like electricians and plumbers are licensed by the Texas Department of Licensing and Regulation, but a general contractor is not. Because there is no state license to complain about, your leverage is the written demand, the attorney general’s consumer complaint (next card), and court — and checking any license or registration a contractor claims before you hire, through your city or county.

Consumer complaint

Also complain to the Attorney General

Licensed or not, file a consumer complaint with Texas’s attorney general / consumer protection office — it is the same office linked in the Consumer protection section of this page. They cannot act as your private lawyer, but complaints build the pattern that triggers investigations, and some offices mediate. Attach the same paper trail.

File a consumer complaint in Texas →

What to do:

  1. Document everything, starting today. Save the contract and any change orders, cancelled checks and receipts, photos and video of the work as it stands, and every text and email. Write down the dates the contractor showed up — and the dates they didn’t.
  2. Stop paying. Do not pay the balance or any more draws until this is resolved, and do not pay extra to get the contractor back on the job. If part of the work was done right, keep notes on what it was worth — you may still owe for that part, and a lawyer can advise you if the amounts are large.
  3. Send a written demand with a deadline. Say what was agreed, what you paid, what is unfinished or wrong, and what you want — the work finished by a date certain, or your money back for the work not done. There is a free fill-in template: the contractor demand letter. Send it certified mail with return receipt requested, and keep your copy — boards, funds, and courts all ask for it.
  4. Complain to the state — the licensing authority and the attorney general, using the cards above. Both are free, and neither stops you from also going to court.
  5. If the money fits small claims, sue there. Most contractor cases are about a dollar amount you can prove — what you paid minus the value of work actually done, plus what it costs to finish or fix. See the Small claims section on this page for Texas’s limit, fees, and forms.

This is general information, not legal advice. Contractor licensing and complaint rules differ by state and change, and the exact wording of your contract controls a lot — for large amounts, talk to a lawyer in Texas.

Car repossession in Texas

If you're behind on car payments, know your rights before the tow truck shows up — and after.

Your rights

What they can't do

No breaking into a locked garage, no physical force, no threats — that's "breach of the peace" and it's illegal everywhere. They must return your personal belongings from the car.

Get it back

Cure and redemption

No state cure right — only what your loan contract allows. Pay the full balance plus costs any time before the car is sold.

Money owed

Deficiency balance

Allowed; 4-year time limit to sue.

Notice

What they must tell you

No advance warning required — the lender can repossess immediately upon default. Reasonable notice before the sale required.

What to do next:

  1. If your car was taken, call the lender immediately and ask for the exact payoff or cure amount in writing.
  2. Remove valuables from your car now — don't wait for repossession.
  3. If the repo agent used force, threats, or broke into a locked space, document everything and contact free legal help in Texas.

Learn more about repossession in Texas →

Consumer protection in Texas

Ripped off by a business? Hit with hidden fees, a scam, or a contractor who vanished? Every state has a consumer protection office that takes complaints and goes after deceptive practices.

Take action

File a consumer complaint

Submit a complaint online with Consumer Protection Division (Office of the Attorney General). They investigate patterns of fraud and can mediate disputes — they don't act as your private lawyer, but complaints trigger real enforcement.

File a complaint in Texas →
Official resource

Consumer Protection Division (Office of the Attorney General)

Your state's consumer protection office — guides on scams, deceptive business practices, and your rights as a buyer.

Visit Texas consumer protection →
Federal

Report fraud nationally

For scams and identity theft, file with the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov. For financial products (banks, lenders, debt collectors), complain to the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau at consumerfinance.gov.

What to do next:

  1. Gather everything: receipts, contracts, screenshots, names, dates — a paper trail makes your complaint stronger.
  2. File a complaint with your state consumer protection office (link above) — it's free.
  3. Also report scams to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov so it's on the national radar.

Health insurance & medical bills in Texas

A denied claim or a shocking hospital bill is not the end of the story. Health insurance denials can be appealed twice — first to the insurer itself, then to an independent reviewer whose decision the insurer must accept — and federal law now bans most surprise out-of-network bills outright. Separately, if you cannot afford a hospital bill, nonprofit hospitals are required to have a financial assistance program, and many people who qualify never apply. This section is about getting a denial or a bill fixed. It is not about suing a doctor or hospital for harm from care itself — for that, see the Medical malpractice section on this page.

Step 1

Internal appeal: 180 days from the denial

When your plan denies a claim or refuses to pay for care, you have 180 days from the date you got the denial notice to file an internal appeal — ask the insurer to review its own decision with a different reviewer. The plan must decide within 30 days if the service has not happened yet, or 60 days if it already has; urgent cases must be decided within 72 hours. Your strongest move is a letter from your doctor explaining, in plain terms, why the care is medically necessary — attach it, keep a copy of everything, and write down the date you filed and every name you spoke to.

Step 2

External review: an independent reviewer, and the decision binds the insurer

If the internal appeal says no, you can ask for external review within 4 months of the final internal denial. An independent reviewer with no ties to your insurer looks at the medical evidence and decides — and the insurer is legally required to accept that decision. Standard reviews are decided within 45 days; urgent ones within 72 hours. If your plan uses the federal process, it is free: call 1-888-866-6205 to request a form, or use the HealthCare.gov external review page, which also lists state programs.

HealthCare.gov: external review →
Surprise bills

Surprise bill? The No Surprises Act may make it illegal

Since January 1, 2022, federal law bans balance billing in three situations: emergency care, non-emergency care from an out-of-network provider at an in-network hospital or surgery center (the anesthesiologist you never chose, for example), and air ambulance rides. In those cases you generally owe only your normal in-network cost-sharing — the provider and the plan fight over the rest, not you. Ground ambulance rides are not covered by the federal law, though some state laws cover them. If you get a bill that looks like a banned surprise bill, do not just pay it: dispute it in writing with the provider and the plan, and complain to the federal No Surprises Help Desk at 1-800-985-3059.

File a No Surprises complaint with CMS →
No insurance

Uninsured or paying yourself: get the estimate, then dispute the gap

If you have no insurance or are paying out of pocket, you have the right to a good faith estimate before scheduled care. If the final bill is at least $400 more than that estimate, you may dispute it through the federal patient-provider dispute process within 120 days of the first bill. Keep the estimate and the bill side by side — the gap is the whole dispute.

Charity care

Hospital financial assistance (charity care): ask before you pay

Nonprofit hospitals — most community hospitals — are required by federal tax law to have a written financial assistance policy, publish it, and cap what qualifying patients are charged at the amounts generally billed to insured patients. Each hospital sets its own income limits in its policy, so read that policy, not a rumor about it. Call the hospital’s billing or financial counseling office and ask, by name, for the financial assistance application. Nonprofit hospitals must accept applications for at least 240 days from your first bill after discharge, and a complete application in that window pauses collection efforts while it is decided. If you are denied, ask for the reason in writing.

Official resource

In Texas: your state insurance department and your denial letter

Your denial letter or Explanation of Benefits must tell you how to appeal and, in many states, names the organization that handles your external review. Your state insurance department also takes complaints about state-regulated plans and can point you to the right review program. This page does not list one Texas phone number or form because the right office depends on your plan — a plan you bought yourself, a plan through a small employer, and a large employer’s self-funded plan follow different tracks — so start with the contact information on your denial letter and the HealthCare.gov external review page linked above, which lists state programs.

What to do:

  1. Read the denial or bill the day it arrives. Circle the reason given, the amount, and the appeal deadline — 180 days for an internal appeal, 4 months after that for external review.
  2. Ask for an itemized bill and compare it line by line with your Explanation of Benefits. Wrong codes, duplicate lines, and services you never got are common — and a billing-office correction is faster than an appeal.
  3. File the internal appeal in writing, with your doctor’s letter attached, and keep copies plus proof you sent it.
  4. If the internal appeal fails, file for external review — do not stop there. Independent reviewers overturn denials often enough that skipping this step leaves money on the table.
  5. For a hospital bill you cannot afford, ask for the financial assistance application before you agree to a payment plan, and get any payment-plan or hold-on-collections promise in writing.
  6. Never ignore a bill while you dispute it: tell the provider and any collector in writing that you dispute it, and ask them to pause collections while the dispute is pending.

This page does not list one Texas insurance-department phone number or external-review form because the right office depends on your plan type — your denial letter and the HealthCare.gov page above point to the right one. This is general information, not legal advice. Plan rules, state surprise-billing laws, and hospital policies change, and your plan documents and your state’s current rules control.

Medical malpractice in Texas

A bad outcome is not always malpractice. To have a case, you usually must show the doctor or hospital fell below the accepted standard of care — what a careful provider would have done — and that the mistake caused your injury. These cases turn on strict deadlines and expert review, so the clock matters here more than almost anywhere else.

Take action

Complain to the Texas Medical Board

Filing a complaint with the state medical board is free. The board can investigate and discipline a doctor, but it cannot award you money — that is what a lawsuit is for. Filing a complaint does not pause your lawsuit deadline.

File a complaint or look up a doctor →
Deadline

Texas's deadline to sue

You generally have 2 years from the breach or from when the treatment at issue ended — Texas has no general discovery rule, and never more than 10 years from the act. Children under 12 have until their 14th birthday. Miss the deadline and your case is usually over, no matter how strong it is — talk to a lawyer well before it runs out.

Free help

Talk to a malpractice lawyer

Most malpractice lawyers give a free first consultation and work on contingency — you pay nothing up front, and they take a percentage only if you win. Many states also require an expert to certify the case before you can file; a lawyer handles that.

Your records

Get your medical records

You have the right to copies of your own medical records. Ask every provider involved, in writing, and keep the copies somewhere safe — records are the backbone of any malpractice case.

What to do next:

  1. Request complete copies of your medical records from every provider involved.
  2. Write down your timeline — dates, symptoms, what you were told — while it is fresh.
  3. Do not sign anything from the hospital or its insurer, and do not accept a quick settlement, before a lawyer looks at it.
  4. Get a free consultation with a malpractice attorney well before Texas's deadline above.
  5. If you want the doctor investigated, file the free board complaint too — just remember it does not stop your lawsuit clock.

Wrongly accused in Texas

Being accused of a crime you did not commit is frightening — but you have rights from the first minute. You are presumed innocent, you do not have to talk to police, and if you cannot afford a lawyer, the court must appoint one to defend you for free. What you do in the first days matters most.

Free help

Get a free defense lawyer

Texas has no state public defender office — counties provide defense lawyers. Ask the court at your first appearance to appoint one; the Texas Indigent Defense Commission is the state's official indigent-defense body.

Texas Indigent Defense Commission →
Take action

Wrongfully convicted? Ask Innocence Project of Texas for a case review

The Innocence Project of Texas reviews cases of people convicted of crimes they did not commit and fights to free the innocent. If you or a loved one was wrongfully convicted in Texas, you can ask them to review the case — most take applications by mail or online.

Learn how to ask for a case review →
Know your rights

Your first court appearance

At your first appearance (arraignment), the judge reads the charges and asks how you plead. Pleading not guilty keeps every option open — it does not lock you into a trial. This is also where you ask the court to appoint a public defender if you cannot afford a lawyer. Do not plead guilty just to get it over with.

On probation? How supervision, violations, and early endings work

The conditions almost everyone gets

Almost every probation order has the same core conditions: report to your probation officer as directed and keep every appointment, obey all laws, tell your officer before you change your address or job, and get permission before leaving the state (often the county). Your own probation order controls — it can add treatment, drug testing, community service, no-contact rules, and payments. When in doubt, ask your officer first and get the answer in writing.

If your officer says you violated

The pattern in most states: your officer files a violation report or petition with the sentencing court; the court issues a summons or a warrant; and you get a hearing before the judge. At the hearing you can have a lawyer and present your side. The judge can continue your probation as-is, add or change conditions, extend the term, or revoke probation and impose the sentence that was hanging over the case. Where your state's card below describes its process, that description comes from the state's own official sources.

Moving to another state

All 50 states belong to the Interstate Compact for Adult Offender Supervision (ICAOS). If you need to move, you apply through your probation officer: the state that sentenced you has the final say, the state you want to move to must accept you, and there is no right to a transfer. Do not move first and ask later — with limited exceptions you cannot travel to the new state while the application is pending, the sending state may charge a transfer application fee, and moving without approval can itself be a violation.

Want it over sooner? Early termination

Courts in many states can end probation early for people who have finished their conditions and stayed in compliance — the request goes to the sentencing court, and your officer's compliance report usually carries the most weight. Where your state's card below describes an official early-termination or earned-discharge process, follow it. If it does not, ask your officer how early-termination requests are made in your court — and finish treatment, community service, and restitution first, because open conditions are the most common reason requests stall.

Probation in Texas

In Texas, adult probation — officially called community supervision — is run day to day by local Community Supervision and Corrections Departments (CSCDs), which serve the courts of one or more counties. The Texas Department of Criminal Justice's Community Justice Assistance Division provides funding, standards, and oversight but does not supervise cases itself.

Ending early: Texas law (Code of Criminal Procedure art. 42A.701) builds in early-ending reviews: after a person has satisfactorily completed one-third of the original supervision period or two years, whichever is less, the judge may reduce or end supervision early; and at one-half of the period or two years, whichever is more, the judge must review the record and consider ending it, unless the person is behind on restitution they have the ability to pay or has not finished court-ordered treatment. Some offenses are excluded, including DWI offenses and offenses requiring sex-offender registration.

Official source

Texas Dept. of Criminal Justice — Community Justice Assistance Division (CSCDs)

Probation is not the same as clearing your record. If a criminal record is holding you back, see the record-clearing (expungement and sealing) section of this page for what can be cleared and how.

Do this now: Keep proof of everything — completion certificates, payment receipts, pay stubs, treatment letters — in one folder. If you are going to miss a report date or something goes wrong, call your officer before it turns into a violation report, not after. And if you want probation to end early or need to move, ask first: both requests exist, and both go through your officer and your sentencing court.

What to do next:

  1. Do not talk to police without a lawyer. Say: “I am using my right to remain silent, and I want a lawyer” — then stop talking.
  2. Do not consent to searches of your home, car, or phone. Saying “I do not consent to a search” protects you later, even if police search anyway.
  3. Write down your timeline and where you were, plus the names of anyone who can confirm it, while it is fresh.
  4. Save everything — texts, emails, receipts, photos — and do not delete anything, even if it looks bad.
  5. Do not contact the person who accused you, and do not post about the case online.
  6. Ask the court for a public defender at your first appearance if you cannot afford a lawyer.

Immigration help in Texas

Immigration law is mostly federal — the same rules apply in every state — but the help is local. The organizations below give free or low-cost immigration legal help in Texas, and they are recognized to do this work. If money is tight, start there instead of paying someone who is not a lawyer.

Free help

Immigration legal help: Texas RioGrande Legal Aid

Texas RioGrande Legal Aid helps people in Texas with immigration cases — green cards, citizenship, asylum, deportation defense, and work permits. Call before your situation becomes an emergency; these offices are busy and waitlists are common.

Get immigration legal help →
Know your rights

If immigration officers stop you or come to your home

You have the right to remain silent, and you do not have to answer questions about where you were born. You do not have to open your door unless officers show a warrant signed by a judge — an ICE administrative warrant is not the same thing. Do not sign anything, and do not show false documents, before talking to a lawyer. Say: “I want to remain silent and I want to speak to a lawyer.”

Driver’s licenses

Can you get a license in Texas?

Texas requires proof of legal presence for a driver’s license, so undocumented residents cannot get one. Driving without a license risks tickets and arrest — which is often how immigration cases start — so talk to the legal help organization above about your options before you drive.

State resources

Texas Office for Refugees

Texas’s own office or program for newcomers and refugees — services, benefits, and local programs in your area.

State resources for immigrants →

Becoming a U.S. citizen: naturalization, step by step

Who can apply — the basics

Naturalization is how a green-card holder becomes a U.S. citizen. In general you must be at least 18, have been a permanent resident for 5 years (3 years if you are married to and living with a U.S. citizen), have lived in the U.S. continuously and been physically present for at least half that time, have lived at least 3 months in the state where you file, and be a person of good moral character. You can file up to 90 days before you reach the 3- or 5-year mark. USCIS's eligibility worksheet (Form M-477, linked from the N-400 page below) walks through every requirement.

The form and the fee

You apply on Form N-400, online or on paper. As of the current USCIS fee schedule (Form G-1055), the fee is $710 filed online or $760 on paper, with biometrics included. If your household income is at or below 400% of the federal poverty guidelines, a reduced fee of $380 is available on a paper filing (requested in Part 10 of the N-400). If you receive a means-tested benefit, your income is at or below 150% of the guidelines, or you have a financial hardship, you may qualify for a full fee waiver with Form I-912 — the fee becomes $0. Fees change: confirm the current amount on the USCIS fee schedule before you file, because a wrong fee means a rejected application.

The two tests — and who is excused from them

At your interview an officer tests your English (speaking, reading, writing) and your knowledge of U.S. history and government (civics). Which civics test you take depends on your filing date: applications filed on or after October 20, 2025 use the 2025 test (study 128 questions; the officer asks 20 and you must answer 12 correctly); earlier applications use the 2008 test (100 questions; 10 asked, 6 to pass). Free study materials are on the USCIS site. Age-and-residence exemptions excuse the English test only: at 50 with 20 years as a permanent resident (50/20), or 55 with 15 years (55/15), you may take civics in your own language with your own interpreter. At 65 with 20 years (65/20), the civics test shrinks to 20 designated questions. A disability that prevents you from meeting the test requirements can excuse English, civics, or both — that takes Form N-648, completed by a medical professional.

Interview, decision, and the oath

After you file, USCIS schedules fingerprinting (biometrics), then the interview, where the officer reviews your application and administers the tests. Bring your green card, ID, and the documents your appointment notice lists; be ready to explain trips abroad and anything in your record — arrests must be disclosed even if charges were dropped. If approved, you take the Oath of Allegiance at a ceremony (sometimes the same day) and receive your Certificate of Naturalization. You are not a citizen until the oath — then register to vote and update your Social Security record.

Get help before you file if anything is complicated

A clean case is usually a do-it-yourself case. But if you have any arrest or immigration violation in your past, long trips abroad, tax or child-support problems, or a prior denial, have an accredited representative or immigration lawyer review your case first — the legal-aid organizations earlier in this section include immigration help, and a consultation costs far less than a denied application. Never pay a "notario" for immigration legal advice; in the U.S. that word does not mean lawyer.

Official sources

USCIS — Form N-400, Application for Naturalization (fees, eligibility, study materials)

Do this now: run the eligibility worksheet on the USCIS N-400 page, then check the current fee on the fee schedule — and whether the $380 reduced fee or an I-912 waiver fits your household. Gather your green card, travel dates for the last 5 years, and tax records before you start the form.

What to do next:

  1. Call the legal help organization above before any deadline or court date — immigration deadlines are strict and missing one can end a case.
  2. Beware of “notarios” and immigration consultants who are not lawyers or accredited representatives. In the U.S., a notario is not a lawyer, and bad paperwork help can get you deported.
  3. Gather your documents — passport, any papers you have ever received from immigration, court notices — and keep copies somewhere safe.
  4. Never sign a document you do not understand, and never pay in cash without a written agreement and a receipt.
  5. Write down important dates: entries, filings, hearings. Immigration cases turn on dates.

Elder Abuse & Adult Protective Services in Texas

If an older adult — or any adult who cannot protect themselves — is being hurt, ignored, or taken advantage of, there is a state agency whose job is to check on them: Adult Protective Services (APS). You do not need proof to make a report. A reasonable concern is enough, and most APS programs would rather check and find nothing wrong than never hear about a real problem.

Start here

Adult Protective Services in Texas

In Texas, Adult Protective Services is part of the Texas Department of Family and Protective Services (DFPS). APS investigates abuse, neglect, and financial exploitation of people age 65 and older and adults 18-64 with a disability who live in their own homes or other community settings. Texas law defines this group as 'elderly persons' and 'persons with a disability' who cannot protect themselves; investigations inside state-regulated facilities such as nursing homes and mental health facilities are handled by the Texas Health and Human Services Commission (HHSC) instead. Call the Texas Abuse Hotline at 1-800-252-5400, run by DFPS — the same hotline takes child and adult reports — or report online at www.txabusehotline.org, which DFPS says is for non-urgent situations. The hotline is staffed 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. If someone is in immediate danger, call 911 first rather than using the website.

Texas DFPS — Adult Protective Services (APS)
Know the difference

Three doors — picking the right one speeds up help

APS handles abuse, neglect, and exploitation of adults living at home or in the community. Problems inside a nursing home or assisted living facility go to the state's Long-Term Care Ombudsman and the agency that licenses the facility. And if a crime is happening or someone is in danger right now, call 911 — APS is a social-services agency, not an emergency service.

Warning signs

Signs an adult may need help

Unexplained bruises or injuries. Sudden changes in bank accounts, property, or who controls the money. Bills going unpaid despite enough income. A caregiver who isolates the person, answers every question for them, or seems to frighten them. Missed medications, poor hygiene, or unsafe living conditions. One sign can have an innocent explanation — a pattern is a reason to call.

Finding help anywhere

Not sure where to call? The Eldercare Locator can route you

The Eldercare Locator is a free national service of the U.S. Administration for Community Living that connects you with local aging and protective-services agencies: call 1-800-677-1116 on weekdays, or search at eldercare.acl.gov. It is the reliable fallback if the state route above does not get you to the right office.

What to do

  • If someone is in danger right now, call 911 first.
  • Write down what you noticed: dates, what happened, who was involved. Specific facts help the caseworker act.
  • Make the report to Texas APS using the number or link in the first card. Reports can usually be made anonymously; in most states your name is kept confidential and people who report in good faith are protected by law.
  • Know what APS does: a caseworker visits, talks with the adult, and works on safety and services — it is a helping agency, not the police. An adult who can make their own decisions can refuse services.
  • If money is being taken, also alert the adult's bank — banks can often pause suspicious activity — and see the Financial Scams & Fraud section on this page.

Guardianship, conservatorship & long-term care in Texas

When an adult can no longer make safe decisions about their own health, home, or money — after dementia, a brain injury, or a serious disability — someone has to be legally able to decide for them. Guardianship and conservatorship are the court’s answer, but they are also the most restrictive answer: a court order takes away an adult’s right to decide. That is why courts are supposed to try the least restrictive option first, and why this section starts with the alternatives. The Wills & probate section on this page covers what happens after death; this section covers what happens while the person is still alive and needs help deciding.

What it means

Guardianship vs. conservatorship: who decides what

A guardian is a person a court appoints to make personal decisions for an adult who cannot safely make them — where they live, what medical care they get, and day-to-day care. A conservator is a person a court appoints to handle money and property — paying bills, managing accounts, and protecting assets. In many states those are two separate jobs; in others, one appointment covers both, or the state uses only one of the words. The exact titles and powers in Texas come from Texas law and from the judge’s order itself, so read the order, not just the label: a good order is narrow, naming only the decisions the person truly cannot make, and leaving the rest with the person.

Step 1

Who can petition, and how the court process works

Usually a family member, close friend, or concerned professional files a petition in the probate court of the county where the adult lives, explaining what decisions the person can no longer make safely and what evidence supports that. The adult must be given notice and a chance to be heard, and in most states the court orders an independent evaluation — often by a doctor or a court visitor — and appoints a lawyer for the adult if they do not have one. At a hearing, the judge decides whether the person lacks capacity for specific decisions and, if so, who should serve and how broad the powers should be. Bring medical records, a list of the person’s existing helpers (family, home aides, a power of attorney), and any written wishes the person expressed while they could still decide.

Contested?

If family members disagree, or the adult objects

Guardianships are contested more often than people expect — siblings disagree about who should serve, or the adult themselves says they do not need a guardian. Anyone with an interest can object, present their own evidence, and ask the judge to choose a different person or a narrower order. If you object, say so in writing by the court’s deadline and show up: an unopposed petition is usually granted. After appointment, the guardian or conservator must report to the court — typically an annual report on the person’s condition and, for money, an accounting of every dollar in and out. If a guardian misuses money, ignores the person’s wishes, or is no longer needed, anyone can ask the court to review, limit, replace, or end the guardianship.

Try first

Less-restrictive alternatives: try these before court

Courts are supposed to prefer the least restrictive option that keeps the person safe, and these often avoid guardianship entirely: a durable power of attorney lets a trusted person handle money and sign documents (durable means it stays valid if the person later loses capacity); a health care directive or proxy names who makes medical decisions; supported decision-making — now recognized in a growing number of states — is a written agreement where the person keeps their legal rights and names supporters who help them understand options and communicate choices, rather than deciding for them; and practical tools like joint accounts, automatic bill pay, and a Social Security representative payee can solve a single problem without a court case. If the person can still understand and communicate with support, an alternative usually fits better than a guardianship — and it is far cheaper and faster.

Nursing home

Nursing-home resident rights and discharge protections

A nursing-home resident keeps their rights: to be treated with dignity, to take part in their own care plan, to have visitors, to manage their own money or choose someone who will, and to complain — to the facility, to the state, or to the ombudsman — without retaliation. A facility generally cannot just throw a resident out: federal rules require written notice of a discharge or transfer, usually 30 days in advance, stating the reason and the right to appeal, with shorter notice only in emergencies such as a medical crisis or an immediate safety risk. If you get a discharge notice, do not move the resident out quietly and do not miss the appeal deadline on the notice — call the ombudsman the same day (below). Before choosing a facility, compare inspection results, staffing, and star ratings on Medicare’s Care Compare.

Free help

Every state has a long-term-care ombudsman — free and independent

The Long-Term Care Ombudsman Program exists in every state to help residents of nursing homes, assisted living, and similar facilities, and their families. Ombudsmen investigate complaints, explain resident rights, help with discharge appeals, and visit facilities regularly. The service is free, and you do not need a lawyer to use it. Use the federal program page below to find your state’s ombudsman office, or call the Eldercare Locator at 1-800-677-1116 and ask for your local ombudsman program.

Find your state’s long-term-care ombudsman → Compare nursing homes on Medicare Care Compare →
Official resource

In Texas: start with the probate court

Guardianship and conservatorship cases are filed in the probate court in Texas. The court’s probate pages are the place to find this state’s forms, filing steps, and hearing information. This page links the court’s own probate resources, already listed in the Wills & probate section above.

Probate court resources in Texas →

Power of attorney & a health care directive in Texas — set them up before you need them

  • Power of attorney (money & property): The state publishes its own form — the Statutory Durable Power of Attorney (Texas Estates Code section 752.051). Signing: Notary required, no witnesses required for a financial durable power of attorney: Texas Estates Code section 751.0021 requires a writing signed by the adult principal (or by another adult in the principal's conscious presence at the principal's direction).
  • Health care decisions: Texas calls it the Directive to Physicians and Family or Surrogates (living will) and Medical Power of Attorney — Texas Health and Safety Code chapter 166, Advance Directives Act. Signing: Sign the directive in the presence of two qualified witnesses, at least one of them a disinterested witness who is not a relative, an heir, or involved in your care, and the witnesses sign too (Tex. Health & Safety Code sections 166.003, 166.032).
  • Getting it done: Choose a trusted agent and complete the Statutory Durable Power of Attorney in Estates Code section 752.051, initialing only the powers you want to grant — it covers finances/property only, not health care. Sign it and have your signature acknowledged before a notary.

The paperwork: Power of attorney: the state's official form. Health care directive: the state's form or official information. We only link the state's own forms — we never host copies.

Official source: Texas power-of-attorney law

Mental-health crisis holds and involuntary commitment

If someone may be a danger to themselves or others

Every state allows a short emergency hold for evaluation when a person, because of a mental-health crisis, appears to be a danger to themselves or others, or is gravely disabled. How long the hold lasts and who can start it — police, a doctor, an examiner, or a family member petitioning a court — is set by state law (see the state card below). An emergency hold is an evaluation, not a commitment: holding someone longer requires a court order.

In a crisis right now

Call or text 988 — the Suicide and Crisis Lifeline — free, confidential, and answered around the clock. If someone is in immediate danger or has already hurt themselves, call 911 and ask for officers trained in crisis response if your area has them.

The person's rights

A person on a hold keeps important rights: to be told why they are being held, to be examined promptly, to communicate with family and a lawyer, to a court hearing before any longer commitment, to have a lawyer (appointed free if they cannot pay, in commitment proceedings), and to regular review of whether the hold still applies. Nobody can be kept indefinitely on an emergency hold — the state must go to court, and the judge decides.

For families: how to ask for an evaluation

Start with your county or local crisis team, or call 988 for the nearest one. Police can check on the person and, in most states, take them for an emergency evaluation. In many states a family member can also file a petition with the court or county asking for an evaluation or commitment hearing. What helps most: specific, recent facts — dates, what the person said or did, any weapons, medicines, or treatment stopped. Evaluators and judges act on specifics, not general worry.

What Texas's official sources say

A peace officer, without a warrant, may take a person into custody if the officer has reason to believe the person has a mental illness and, because of that illness, there is a substantial risk of serious harm to the person or others, severe emotional distress and deterioration, or an inability to recognize symptoms or appreciate the risks and benefits of treatment. A physician shall examine the person as soon as possible within 12 hours after the person is brought to a facility, and the person has the right to be advised of the location and reasons for detention, to communicate with and retain an attorney, and to communicate with a relative or other responsible person.

Official sources

988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline — call or text 988, free and 24/7

Office of the Texas Governor — Mental Health Protections, Detaining a Mental Health Patient

Do this now: if anyone is in immediate danger, call 911. For crisis support and the nearest crisis team, call or text 988. Otherwise, write down the specific recent behavior that worries you — with dates — and take it to the county crisis team or the court your state's source above points to.

What to do:

  1. Start with the least restrictive option: a durable power of attorney, a health care directive, or a supported decision-making agreement may solve the problem without a court case.
  2. If court is needed, file in the probate court where the adult lives, with medical evidence and a list of the person’s existing helpers and wishes.
  3. If you disagree with a petition, object in writing by the court’s deadline and attend the hearing — unopposed petitions are usually granted.
  4. After appointment, keep the order narrow, meet every court reporting and accounting deadline, and ask the court to limit or end the guardianship if the person regains capacity.
  5. Nursing-home problem or discharge notice? Call your state’s long-term-care ombudsman the same day (1-800-677-1116) and do not miss the appeal deadline printed on the notice.
  6. Choosing a facility: check Medicare Care Compare for inspections, staffing, and star ratings before you sign anything.

This page does not print one national guardianship filing fee, deadline, or ombudsman address because all three vary by state — the probate court link and the federal ombudsman locator above route you to Texas’s exact rules. This is general information, not legal advice. Guardianship law and nursing-home rules change, and Texas law controls.

Wills & probate in Texas

When someone dies, their property does not just move on its own. Probate is the court process that proves the will, pays the debts, and transfers what is left to the family. Every state also has a shortcut for small estates — and if you are making your own will, Texas’s signing rules decide whether it counts. Here is where to start in Texas.

Where probate happens

Texas Judicial Branch — Forms (Probate & Guardianship)

This is the official court starting point for wills and estates in Texas — forms, filing steps, and the court that handles the estate.

Start with the probate court →
Small estates

Texas’s small-estate shortcut

Estates of $75,000 or less can use a small-estate affidavit — intestate estates only, not counting the homestead and exempt property, and a judge must approve it (Tex. Est. Code §205.001).

Making a will

Are handwritten wills valid in Texas?

Yes. Texas recognizes handwritten (holographic) wills (Tex. Est. Code §251.052). The rules are exact — key parts usually must be in the maker’s own handwriting and signed — and a small mistake can void the will. A witnessed will is always safer.

First steps

When someone dies: the first week

Order several certified copies of the death certificate — banks, insurers, and agencies each want one. Find the original will (check the home, a safe deposit box, and the court or lawyer who prepared it). Secure the home and vehicles. Do not pay the person’s debts from your own money — debts are paid from the estate, in an order the law sets. Notify Social Security and the person’s employer and insurers.

Avoiding probate: trusts, TOD deeds, and beneficiary names

Probate is not always a disaster — the small-estate shortcuts elsewhere in this section may be all your family needs. But three tools can move your biggest assets outside probate entirely, and they are cheaper than most people expect. What they cannot do is replace a will: a will is still the safety net for everything the tools miss.

Tool 1 — every state

A living trust only works if it is funded

A revocable living trust lets a successor trustee hand your property to your family without probate — but only for assets you actually transferred into the trust while you were alive. Signing the trust paper and leaving the house, accounts, and titles in your own name accomplishes nothing; lawyers call the transfer step “funding,” and it is the step people forget. A pour-over will catches leftovers at death and pours them into the trust — but anything that arrives that way still passes through probate first. A trust costs more to set up than a will; what it buys is privacy and skipping the court process for the assets inside it.

Tool 2 — every state

Beneficiary designations beat your will

Retirement accounts, life insurance, payable-on-death (POD) bank accounts, and transfer-on-death (TOD) brokerage accounts go to whoever is named on the account — not to whoever your will names. If the two disagree, the account paperwork wins. That makes designations the cheapest probate-avoidance there is, and the easiest to get wrong: review them after every marriage, divorce, birth, or death in the family, and name a backup (contingent) beneficiary too.

Tool 3 — in Texas

Transfer-on-death deeds in Texas

Texas does authorize a transfer-on-death deed for real estate. The Texas Real Property Transfer on Death Act (Texas Estates Code ch. 114, §§ 114.001–114.105) calls the instrument a 'transfer-on-death deed.' It has no effect during the owner's life, can be revoked, and must be recorded in the deed records of the county where the property is located before the owner's death to be effective. The statute includes an optional statutory form (Estates Code § 114.054), though owners are not required to use it.

Tool 4 — with a catch

Joint ownership: easy now, tradeoffs later

Property owned jointly with right of survivorship passes to the surviving owner automatically, with no probate. The honest tradeoff: while you are alive, the property is exposed to the other owner’s debts, lawsuits, and divorce, you generally cannot sell or change the arrangement without their signature, and for property that has grown in value the survivor may lose part of the tax basis step-up a probate transfer would have given. It is a real tool — just not a free one.

Official source

Texas Legislature Online — Texas Estates Code ch. 114 (Transfer-on-Death Deeds, §§ 114.001–114.105)

The official Texas source behind the deed rules above — read it before recording anything.

Official probate-avoidance information →

Step by step: (1) List your assets and how each one is titled today — that list, not your will, decides what goes through probate. (2) Check and update the beneficiary names on every account and policy; it is free and it is where most probate is quietly avoided. (3) For real estate, use the Texas card above: if your state offers a TOD or beneficiary deed, record it with the county while you are alive — an unrecorded deed in a drawer does nothing. If your state does not offer one, weigh a trust against joint ownership with the tradeoff in view. (4) If you set up a trust, fund it the same week. (5) Keep a will anyway, and tell your family where the paperwork lives.

Quitclaim deeds: fast and common — but they promise nothing

What a quitclaim deed actually does

It transfers whatever interest the signer actually has in the property — if any. That is all. Unlike a warranty deed, a quitclaim makes no promise that the signer owns the property, that the title is clean, or that nobody else has a claim. If a problem turns up later, the person who received the property usually has no claim against the person who signed it over.

Where it is commonly used

Between people who trust each other and know the property: transfers between family members, adding or removing a spouse's name in a divorce, moving a home into a living trust, or clearing a possible claim from someone who may not own anything at all. Buying a home from a stranger with only a quitclaim is risky — you take every hidden title problem as your own.

The traps to check first

The mortgage does not move with the deed. If there is a loan, the borrower still owes it — and many mortgages let the lender demand full payment when ownership changes (federal law protects some family, divorce, and trust transfers; ask the lender in writing before you sign). Title insurance usually does not carry over to the new owner. And a gift transfer keeps the giver's original tax basis — no step-up — and can count against Medicaid eligibility if care is needed within the look-back window. For an expensive property, a short consult with a local real estate lawyer is cheap insurance.

How to do it right

Copy the names and the full legal description exactly from the current deed (get it from the county recorder if you do not have it). Follow your state's signing rules — many states print a statutory form (see the state card) and most deeds must be notarized. Then record the signed deed with the county recorder or clerk promptly: an unrecorded deed can lose to a later recorded claim. Keep a copy with your estate papers and tell your homeowner's insurer about the change.

In Texas

The Texas State Law Library says there are no standard, one-size-fits-all forms to change the name on a property deed. Its page explains that a quitclaim merely "quits" any claim by the grantor to any right, title, and interest the grantor may have in a certain property, if any such interest exists, and does not "grant, sell, and convey" as a deed does.

Official sources

Texas State Law Library — What form do I need to change the name on the deed of my property?

Do this now: before anyone signs, pull the current deed from the county recorder, check whether a mortgage is on the property, and — if there is one — get the lender's position in writing. Then sign, notarize, and record the new deed with the county the same week.

What to do next:

  1. Get certified death certificates and locate the original will.
  2. Check the estate’s size against Texas’s small-estate shortcut above — it can save months and court fees.
  3. If the estate is larger, or includes a house or a dispute, open probate at the court above — and ask legal aid or an estate lawyer about a free first consultation.
  4. Do not distribute property, sell the car, or clean out accounts until the court says who has authority — handing things out early can make you personally responsible.
  5. Making your own will? Follow Texas’s signing and witness rules exactly, tell your executor where the original is, and review it after marriages, divorces, births, and deaths.

Bankruptcy in Texas

Bankruptcy is a legal way to wipe out or reorganize debts you cannot pay. Chapter 7 erases most unsecured debts like credit cards and medical bills, usually in a few months. Chapter 13 is a 3-to-5-year payment plan that can stop a foreclosure and let you catch up. The day you file, an “automatic stay” stops most collections, lawsuits, wage garnishments, and shutoffs. Some debts survive — most student loans, recent taxes, and child support among them.

Your court

The bankruptcy court for Texas

Bankruptcy is a federal case — you file in the U.S. Bankruptcy Court, not a state court. Texas is served by the courts below; the court site has the forms, filing fees, courthouse locations, and local rules.

U.S. Bankruptcy Court — Northern District of Texas → U.S. Bankruptcy Court — Southern District of Texas → U.S. Bankruptcy Court — Eastern District of Texas → U.S. Bankruptcy Court — Western District of Texas →
Keeping your home

How much home equity does Texas protect?

Texas has no dollar cap — protection is limited by size: 10 acres in a city or town; in the country, 200 acres for a family or 100 for a single adult (Tex. Prop. Code §§41.001–41.002).

Your choice: Texas lets you pick either the state exemption list or the federal exemption list — whichever protects more of what you own. The federal list protects about $31,575 of home equity per filer (adjusted for inflation), plus a wildcard amount that can cover more. A bankruptcy lawyer or legal aid can run both lists against your situation before you file.

Free help

Free bankruptcy guidance: Texas Legal Services Center

Texas Legal Services Center helps people in Texas understand whether bankruptcy is the right move, and what the alternatives are. Many bankruptcy lawyers also offer a free first consultation — ask what the full cost will be before you hire anyone.

Get free bankruptcy guidance →
Before you file

Credit counseling is required

Within 180 days before filing, you must complete a short credit counseling course from an approved agency and file the certificate with the court — without it, your case can be dismissed. The U.S. Trustee Program keeps the official list of approved agencies.

Find an approved credit counseling agency →

What to do next:

  1. List everything: every debt, every asset, your income, and your monthly expenses. A lawyer or counselor can only help with the full picture.
  2. Get the free guidance above first — bankruptcy is powerful, but it stays on your credit report for up to 10 years, and sometimes a payment plan or negotiation works better.
  3. Stop using credit cards once you decide to file, and do not transfer or give away property to friends or family — the court can undo transfers and it can sink your case.
  4. Complete the required credit counseling and keep the certificate.
  5. Ask the court about the filing fee: if you cannot afford it, you can ask to pay in installments, or to have the Chapter 7 fee waived.

Workers’ compensation in Texas

If you are hurt at work or made sick by your job, workers’ compensation is the system that pays for your medical care and replaces part of your lost wages — and you do not have to prove your employer did anything wrong to use it. But the system runs on deadlines and paperwork, and the first steps matter more than people expect: report the injury in writing right away, get medical care and tell the provider it is a work injury, and keep a copy of everything. This section walks through the claim from the day of the injury to a denial or a return to work. It is the dedicated guide; the Workplace rights section on this page only mentions workers’ compensation in passing.

Step 1

Report the injury in writing, the same day if you can

Tell your supervisor or employer about the injury or illness as soon as possible, in writing — an email or a dated note counts, and keep a copy for yourself. Say what happened, when, where, and what part of your body was hurt. Deadlines for reporting vary by state, and in some states they are short (days or weeks), so do not wait to see if it gets better on its own. A late report is one of the most common reasons claims get denied. If your injury or illness developed over time — a bad back, a breathing problem, a repetitive-strain injury — report it as soon as a doctor connects it to your work.

Step 2

Get medical care, and say it is a work injury

Get care promptly, and tell every provider the injury or illness is work-related so the visit is billed to workers’ compensation, not to you or your health plan. In some states your employer or its insurer picks the first doctor; in others you pick. Follow the treatment plan and go to every appointment — missed care is used against claims. Keep every bill, work note, and restriction slip (for example, “no lifting over 10 pounds”), and give copies to your employer.

Step 3

Your employer reports it, the insurer decides

Your employer must report the injury to its workers’ compensation insurer, which opens a claim and decides whether to accept or deny it. Ask your employer for the insurer’s name, the claim number, and the adjuster’s contact information, and write them down. If your employer will not report it, or has no insurance, contact the Texas agency below directly — that is what it is there for. Never pay for work-injury care out of pocket without telling the agency and the insurer first.

Benefits

What workers’ compensation pays: medical care and part of your wages

An accepted claim generally pays for reasonable and necessary medical treatment for the work injury, with no copay or deductible to you. If the injury keeps you from working, wage-replacement benefits replace part of your pay — in many states about two-thirds of your average weekly wage, up to a state maximum, after a short waiting period (a few days, which are often paid back if you are out longer). Exact rates, waiting periods, and how long benefits last are set by Texas law, so check the agency page below rather than trusting a single national number. Permanent injury may also bring a separate scheduled payment.

Denied?

If the claim is denied, appeal — and watch the deadline

A denial letter must explain why, and it starts a clock: you can challenge it by filing an appeal or a claim petition with the Texas agency, which leads to a hearing before a workers’ compensation judge or board. Appeal deadlines vary by state and are strict — read the letter the day it arrives, file even if your evidence is not complete yet, and bring your medical records, your written injury report, and any witness names to the hearing. Many workers’ compensation lawyers take these cases on contingency (paid only if you win), and the agency below can explain the process for free. If you are fired, demoted, or punished after filing, tell the agency promptly and get legal help — many states prohibit retaliation for filing a claim.

Return to work

Returning to work: light duty, restrictions, and honest communication

When your doctor clears you with restrictions, your employer may offer light-duty work that fits them. Accepting suitable light duty usually keeps benefits flowing and protects your job; refusing work that fits your restrictions can cut off wage benefits, so talk to your doctor and the agency before refusing anything. If no suitable work exists, say so in writing and keep certifying your restrictions. Never work outside your restrictions to “prove” you are fine — re-injury hurts your health and your claim.

Official resource

In Texas: Texas Department of Insurance, Division of Workers' Compensation

This is the state agency that runs the workers’ compensation system in Texas — its site has the claim forms, the exact reporting and appeal deadlines for this state, and free help for injured workers. This page does not print one national deadline or benefit rate because both are set by Texas law and differ from state to state.

Texas Department of Insurance, Division of Workers' Compensation →

What to do:

  1. Report in writing the day you are hurt — what, when, where, what body part — and keep a copy.
  2. Get medical care and say it is a work injury at every visit; keep every bill, work note, and restriction slip.
  3. Get the insurer’s name, claim number, and adjuster from your employer; if the employer will not report it, call the Texas agency below.
  4. Read every letter the day it arrives. A denial starts an appeal clock that varies by state — file first, gather evidence second.
  5. Follow your restrictions exactly at work and at home, and get any light-duty offer and any refusal in writing.
  6. Never sign a settlement or a resignation tied to your injury without understanding what medical care and benefits you are giving up — ask the agency or a workers’ compensation lawyer first.

This page does not state one national reporting deadline or wage-replacement rate because both vary by state — the Texas agency page linked above has the exact numbers for this state. This is general information, not legal advice. Workers’ compensation rules, deadlines, and benefit rates change, and Texas law controls.

Veterans & military legal help in Texas

Veterans and active-duty service members have three separate legal toolkits that most people never hear about in one place: a way to challenge a VA benefits decision, a way to upgrade a less-than-honorable discharge, and a federal law that pauses or caps civilian debts and lawsuits while you serve. All three are federal, so they work the same in every state — what changes by state is only where you find free local help. The one veteran resource already on this page, the VA homelessness hotline, is not this guide: this section is about benefits decisions, discharge upgrades, and service-member protections.

VA decision

VA denied your claim? Pick the lane that matches what is wrong

You have three decision-review options, and the right one depends on your evidence. Higher-Level Review: a senior reviewer looks at the exact same file for an error — you cannot add new evidence, but you can request a one-time informal conference to point out the mistake. Supplemental Claim: you bring new and relevant evidence the VA did not have before, such as a new medical opinion or a buddy statement. Board Appeal: a Veterans Law Judge decides — on the existing record (Direct Review), with new evidence added within 90 days (Evidence Submission), or after a hearing where you testify (Hearing docket). You can choose a different option for each issue, but you cannot use two options for the same issue at the same time, and you cannot request a second Higher-Level Review or a second Board Appeal in a row on the same issue.

VA: decision reviews and appeals →
Deadline

The one-year VA clock — counted from the date on the letter

For most benefits, you have 1 year from the date printed on your VA decision letter — not the date you received it — to request a Higher-Level Review or a Board Appeal. A Supplemental Claim can be filed at any time, but filing it within that same year keeps your claim continuously pursued, which protects your original effective date and your back pay; filed later, the effective date generally moves to the new filing date. Exception: in a contested claim, where two people claim a benefit only one can receive, a Board Appeal is due within 60 days. Some benefit types have shorter limits stated on the letter itself, so read your letter first. After a Board decision you disagree with, you can file a Supplemental Claim with new and relevant evidence, or appeal to the U.S. Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims within 120 days. VA’s published average goals are about 125 days for Higher-Level Reviews and Supplemental Claims, and 365 days (Direct Review), 550 days (Evidence Submission), or 730 days (Hearing) for Board appeals — goals, not guarantees.

VA: choosing a decision review option →
Discharge

Discharge upgrade: DD Form 293 within 15 years, DD Form 149 after that

A discharge below Honorable can block VA benefits, jobs, and education aid, and it can be challenged. If you were discharged within the last 15 years, apply to your branch’s Discharge Review Board with DD Form 293 — the board can upgrade the characterization and change the reason, and it cannot make your discharge worse, but it cannot review a general court-martial discharge. If you are past 15 years, apply to your branch’s Board for Correction of Military Records with DD Form 149; its usual 3-year filing limit is often waived in the interest of justice. A denial from the review board can still go to the correction board. Include your DD 214, a short statement of why the discharge was unjust or in error, medical records, and proof of your life since service — and if PTSD, traumatic brain injury, or sexual trauma during service played a role, say so plainly: the boards must give that evidence liberal consideration.

SCRA

On active duty? The SCRA caps interest at 6% and pauses lawsuits

The Servicemembers Civil Relief Act (federal law, 50 U.S.C. §§ 3901–4043) protects active-duty members, reservists and Guard members on federal orders, and commissioned Public Health Service and NOAA officers, plus many protections for their dependents. The three most-used protections: (1) 6% interest cap on debts from before you entered service — credit cards, car loans, mortgages — but you must request it in writing from each creditor with a copy of your orders; the interest above 6% is forgiven, not deferred. (2) Lease termination without penalty when you receive orders for a permanent change of station, a deployment of 90 days or more, or separation/retirement — give written notice with your orders. (3) Default-judgment protection: a court cannot enter a default judgment against you while service keeps you from responding; it must appoint an attorney for you and can stay the case. Landlords also generally cannot evict you without a court order, and a pre-service mortgage cannot be foreclosed without a court order. None of this is automatic — you have to ask, in writing, with your orders attached.

Justice Department: SCRA “Know Your Rights” guide →
Free help

In Texas: free help, and how to find it

Start free, not paid. A VA-accredited Veterans Service Organization (VSO) representative — from groups such as the VFW, American Legion, or DAV — will help you file and appeal a VA claim at no cost, whether or not you are a member, and an accredited attorney or claims agent may charge only a regulated fee on past-due benefits, never for an initial claim. Texas also has its own department of veterans affairs and county veterans service officers who file claims and connect you to state benefits: search for “Texas department of veterans affairs” and your county veterans service officer, or use the VA directory below. For VA benefits questions call 800-827-1000. If you are in crisis, call or text 988 and press 1 for the Veterans Crisis Line.

VA directory of Veterans Service Organizations →

What to do:

  1. VA decision you disagree with: circle the date on the decision letter, count forward 1 year, and pick your lane — new evidence means Supplemental Claim, a mistake on the existing file means Higher-Level Review, a judge means Board Appeal.
  2. Before you file anything with VA, talk to a free accredited VSO representative — they file these every day and it costs you nothing.
  3. Discharge upgrade: within 15 years, file DD Form 293 with your branch’s review board; past 15 years, file DD Form 149 with the correction board and ask for the time limit to be waived.
  4. On active duty with pre-service debts or a lease: send each creditor and landlord a written SCRA request with a copy of your orders, and keep proof you sent it — do not wait for them to offer it.
  5. Sued while serving, or facing eviction or foreclosure: tell the court and your military legal assistance office at once — default judgments and out-of-court foreclosures are restricted while you serve.
  6. Never pay anyone upfront for an initial VA claim. Free accredited help exists; a fee is lawful only within VA’s rules, and only after a claim is already filed.

This section does not list one Texas veterans-agency phone number or office because the 50 state agency sites were not individually re-verified for this guide — use the VA directory above and search for Texas’s department of veterans affairs and your county veterans service officer. VA and SCRA rules change, and your decision letter and orders control your deadlines. This is general information, not legal advice.

Unemployment benefits in Texas

Losing a job is expensive enough without losing benefits to a missed week or a missed deadline. Unemployment benefits replace part of your pay while you look for new work, but they are not automatic: you must file promptly, prove you are able to work and looking for work, and certify every week to keep the money coming. This is the dedicated guide — the Workplace rights section on this page only gives unemployment one paragraph. The rules below are the ones that hold in most states; where a number varies by state, this section says so and sends you to the Texas agency for the exact figure, because trusting a national number is how people miss a deadline.

Step 1

File in your first week — benefits generally are not retroactive

File your claim in the first week you are out of work, even if you are still waiting on a final paycheck or severance paperwork. In most states you will not be paid for weeks before the week you filed, no matter how good the reason you waited. You will need your Social Security number, your employer’s name and address, your dates of employment, and your recent earnings. File in the state where you worked, not necessarily the state where you live now. If you were laid off, fired, or quit, answer honestly about why — the agency checks with your employer, and a mismatch delays everything.

Step 2

Certify every week, and keep looking for work

Once your claim is open, you must certify — usually online or by phone, every week or every two weeks depending on the state — that you were able to work, available for work, and looking for work, and you must report any earnings. Miss a certification and that week is not paid, even if you were eligible. Keep a written log of every job you apply for, with dates and employer names: the agency can ask for it, and a thin log is a common reason benefits stop. If you are sick, in school, or unavailable for a week, report it rather than certifying something untrue.

Denied?

If you are denied, appeal the day you get the notice

A denial (often called a determination) can be appealed to a hearing, usually before an administrative law judge, where you can present evidence and witnesses. The deadline varies by state — commonly about 10 to 30 days — and the clock usually runs from the mailing date printed on the notice, not the date you opened it, so read every notice the day it arrives and file your appeal that same day even if your evidence is not ready yet; you can add it before the hearing. If you miss the deadline, you may still file late with a good-cause reason, such as serious illness or never receiving the notice, but “I did not understand” is rarely enough. Keep certifying while your appeal is pending — if you win, you can only be paid for weeks you certified.

Overpayment

Overpayment notice? Do not ignore it — appeal it or ask about a waiver

An overpayment notice says you were paid benefits you were not entitled to, and it states an amount and a reason. In most cases overpayments must be repaid even if you already spent the money, and the agency can take it from future benefits or tax refunds if you do nothing. But you have options, and both have deadlines: appeal if the decision is wrong (for example, earnings you did report), and ask about a waiver if you were not at fault and repayment would cause real hardship — many states waive non-fault overpayments on hardship grounds, but the rules and the form are state-specific, so use the agency link below and do not assume a waiver is automatic. Never ignore the notice while you decide: an unanswered overpayment grows penalties and collection action.

Part-time work

Part-time work: report every dollar, and partial benefits may still be possible

Taking part-time or temporary work does not automatically end your benefits — but you must report all earnings for the week you earned them, not the week you were paid, and failing to report earnings is the fastest route to an overpayment and a fraud penalty. In most states part of your earnings is disregarded and the rest reduces your weekly benefit dollar-for-dollar or by a formula; earn too much in a week and that week pays nothing, but the claim itself usually stays open. The exact disregard and formula are set by Texas law, so check the agency page below before you turn down a shift out of fear of losing everything, or accept one without reporting it.

Official resource

In Texas: Texas Workforce Commission

This is the agency that runs unemployment benefits in Texas. Its site is where you file, certify, appeal, and find this state’s exact deadlines, benefit amounts, and overpayment-waiver rules. This page does not print one national appeal deadline or benefit formula because both vary by state — your determination letter and this agency’s site control.

File for unemployment benefits in Texas →

What to do:

  1. File in your first week out of work — do not wait for severance or a final paycheck to clear.
  2. Certify on time, every period, and keep a written log of your job search with dates and employer names.
  3. Report every dollar you earn, in the week you earned it, including part-time, temporary, and gig work.
  4. Read every agency letter the day it arrives. Appeal deadlines run from the mailing date and are commonly only 10 to 30 days — appeal the same day, even with incomplete evidence.
  5. Keep certifying during any appeal. Weeks you skip cannot be paid later, even if you win.
  6. Overpayment notice: appeal if it is wrong, ask about a waiver if you were not at fault and cannot afford repayment, and never let it sit unanswered.
