This page does not state one national appeal deadline, benefit amount, or part-time earnings formula because all three vary by state — the Texas agency page linked above and your determination letter have the exact numbers for this state. This is general information, not legal advice. Unemployment rules and deadlines change, and Texas law controls.

Workplace rights in Texas

Two problems hit workers hardest: being treated unfairly on the job, and losing the job altogether. Both have agencies, both have deadlines, and both are free to use. Here is where each one goes in Texas.

Discrimination

Filing a discrimination charge: Texas Workforce Commission — Civil Rights Division

If an employer treats you unfairly because of your race, sex, age (40+), disability, religion, national origin, or another protected trait, you file a charge with the Texas Workforce Commission — Civil Rights Division. The deadline in Texas is 180 days, 300 days for sexual harassment (Tex. Lab. Code § 21.202(a). Missing it can end your claim before it starts — file first, gather documents second.

File a discrimination charge →
Unemployment

Lost your job? File for unemployment in Texas

Unemployment benefits are run by the Texas Workforce Commission. File in your first week out of work — benefits do not backdate just because you waited, and most states make you certify every week to keep them coming. You will need your Social Security number, your employer’s details, and your recent work history.

File for unemployment benefits →
Pay & hours

Minimum wage, overtime, and unpaid pay

The federal floor is a $7.25 minimum wage and time-and-a-half after 40 hours in a week for most workers — and Texas’s own wage law may promise more. If your pay itself is short, see the unpaid wages section on this page, and know that the U.S. Department of Labor’s Wage and Hour Division also takes complaints for free.

U.S. Department of Labor — Wage and Hour Division →
Retaliation

Punished for speaking up?

It is illegal for an employer to fire, demote, or cut the hours of a worker for filing a discrimination charge, reporting a safety problem, or asking about unpaid wages. If that happens, tell the agency you filed with right away — retaliation is its own violation, with its own deadline.

Family and medical leave — FMLA and state paid leave

Need time off for a baby, your own serious illness or surgery, or to care for a family member? Two layers of law can protect your job — and in a growing number of states, part of your pay while you are out: the federal FMLA, and Texas’s own leave laws. Leave under these laws generally runs at the same time as (not on top of) other leave, and most require you to ask in advance when the need is foreseeable — tell your employer as early as you can, and keep copies of everything.

Federal law — FMLA

The federal floor: 12 weeks of job-protected leave

The Family and Medical Leave Act (FMLA) gives eligible workers up to 12 weeks of unpaid, job-protected leave a year — for a new child (birth, adoption, or foster placement), your own serious health condition, or to care for a spouse, child, or parent with a serious health condition — with your health coverage continued and your job (or an equivalent one) waiting when you return. You are eligible if your employer has 50 or more employees within 75 miles of your worksite (all public agencies and schools are covered regardless of size) and you have worked there at least 12 months and at least 1,250 hours in the last year. Leave can often be taken intermittently — a few hours or days at a time — and your employer can require a medical certification form, so return it by its deadline. If your employer denies leave you qualify for, punishes you for taking it, or refuses to restore your job, you can complain to the U.S. Department of Labor’s Wage and Hour Division at 1-866-487-9243.

U.S. Department of Labor — FMLA overview →

Federal law — pregnancy

Pregnant workers: accommodation rights (PWFA)

Separate from leave, the federal Pregnant Workers Fairness Act (in force since June 2023) requires employers with 15 or more employees to provide reasonable accommodations for pregnancy, childbirth, and related medical conditions — things like extra breaks, a stool to sit on, light duty, limits on heavy lifting, or schedule changes — unless the accommodation would cause the employer undue hardship. You do not have to use legal words to ask; telling your supervisor what you need and why is enough to start the conversation. The EEOC enforces this law.

Official source

Texas Workforce Commission - Family and Medical Leave Act (official)

On the official Texas pages we checked, Texas has no separate state family-leave law and no state paid-leave insurance program beyond the federal FMLA — so for most workers here, the federal rules in the first card plus your employer’s own paid time off are what apply. Programs change, so confirm with the agency.

Official leave information →

Severance packages and releases: read before you sign

When a job ends, some employers offer a severance package: money (and sometimes continued benefits) paid after you leave. Almost always, the money comes with a paper to sign — a release giving up your right to sue the employer. That signature is the real transaction, and it is worth understanding before you trade. Here is the national picture, then exactly where Texas stands.

Every state

Severance is almost never owed to you — it is a trade

No federal law requires an employer to pay severance. The U.S. Department of Labor states that severance pay is a matter of agreement between an employer and an employee (or the employee’s representative) — it comes from a company policy, a union contract, your employment contract, or negotiation, not from a general legal duty. The rare exceptions are narrow state rules for big plant closings and mass layoffs, covered in the Texas card below. Because the money is voluntary, the employer is buying something with it: your signature on a release. That is also why the amount and terms can often be negotiated — before you sign, not after.

Every state

What the release takes — and what to ask for

A typical agreement asks you to give up claims for wrongful termination, discrimination, unpaid wages and overtime, and sometimes more — and may add confidentiality, a promise not to disparage the company, and limits on working for competitors. Before signing, read for: what claims you release, any non-compete or non-solicit clause, what happens to your unused vacation and final pay, the last-day and pay-through dates, and when the money is actually paid. Common things employees ask for in exchange: more weeks of pay, a later separation date, a neutral reference or agreed reference letter, help with health-coverage costs, and removal of a non-compete. A one-hour review by an employment lawyer is cheap insurance on a document that ends your claims.

Every state

Age 40 or older? Federal law gives you time

If you are 40 or older and the agreement asks you to waive age-discrimination claims, the federal Older Workers Benefit Protection Act (part of the ADEA) sets strict conditions: the waiver must be written in understandable language, must specifically mention the Age Discrimination in Employment Act, cannot waive claims that arise after you sign, must give you something beyond what you were already owed, and must advise you in writing to consult a lawyer. You must get at least 21 days to consider it — 45 days if it is part of a group layoff or exit-incentive program — and after signing you get 7 days to revoke. In a group program the employer must also give you written information about who was selected and who was not, by job title and age. An agreement that skips these steps may not hold up as a waiver of your age claims at all.

Every state

A big layoff or shutdown? The 60-day federal notice rule

The federal WARN Act requires employers with 100 or more employees to give at least 60 calendar days’ written notice before a plant closing or mass layoff affecting 50 or more workers at a single site. It is a notice law, not a severance law — but an employer that skips the notice can owe back pay and benefits for the violation period, up to 60 days, and some states add their own notice laws (and a couple add real severance). The Texas card below tells you which rules apply where you work.

In Texas

Layoff notice and severance in Texas

Only the federal WARN Act applies in Texas — there is no separate state layoff-notice law. Under the Texas Payday Law, an employer must pay severance pay only if its written policy or agreement provides for it (Texas Workforce Commission).

In Texas

Severance pay and unemployment benefits in Texas

Report severance when you apply: TWC states that under Texas law you cannot receive benefits while you are receiving certain types of severance pay, and it will mail you a decision on whether your severance affects your benefits.

Official source

Texas Workforce Commission — Worker Adjustment & Retraining Notification Notices (WARN)

The official Texas source for layoff notice, severance, and unemployment rules — confirm the current rule there before you rely on it, and keep a copy of everything your employer gives you.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Do not sign on the spot. Use every day of the consideration period you are given — if you are 40 or older, the 21/45-day clock and 7-day revocation right above are yours by law. (2) Make any counter-offer before you sign: weeks of pay, separation date, references, and health-coverage help are all negotiable. (3) File for unemployment right away, even if severance is coming — taking severance usually delays or reduces benefits rather than canceling them (the Texas rule is in the card above), but filing late costs you weeks you cannot get back, and report the severance honestly when you file. (4) Keep the agreement, your last pay stub, and the company policy in one folder. Your final paycheck itself runs on its own deadline — the final-pay rules are covered earlier in this Workplace Rights section.

Lost the job? COBRA lets you keep the health plan — at a price

What COBRA is

COBRA is a federal law that lets you keep your employer's group health plan after the job ends or your hours are cut (termination for gross misconduct is the exception). It generally covers employers with 20 or more employees. You keep the same plan and the same doctors — what changes is who pays the premium.

The clock: 60 days to decide, and coverage backdates

You get at least 60 days from the date your election notice is provided to say yes. If you elect and pay, coverage is retroactive — it backdates to the day your group coverage ended, so there is no gap — and you get at least 45 days after electing to make the first payment. Let the election window close and the right is gone for good.

The real cost: up to 102% of the premium

While you worked there, your employer probably paid most of the premium. On COBRA, the plan can charge you the entire premium — both shares — plus up to 2% for administration: up to 102% of the plan's cost. The sticker shock is normal, and it is why the Marketplace comparison below matters.

How long it lasts

For job loss or reduced hours, federal COBRA lasts up to 18 months. For some family events — the employee's death, divorce, or becoming entitled to Medicare — a spouse or child can continue for up to 36 months. A disability determination can extend the 18 months by 11 more (at up to 150% of the premium).

Compare before you pay: the Marketplace

Losing job-based coverage also opens a 60-day Special Enrollment Period at HealthCare.gov. With income down, premium tax credits often make a Marketplace plan far cheaper than COBRA — price both before your COBRA window closes. One caution: dropping COBRA early, before it runs out, usually does not open a new Marketplace window. Questions about COBRA itself? The Labor Department's benefits advisors answer free at 1-866-444-3272.

What Texas adds

Texas's state continuation law, under the Small Employer Health Insurance Availability Act, requires continuation rights for employees of companies with two to 50 employees, lasting up to nine months following a qualifying event if federal COBRA did not cover them.

Official sources

U.S. Department of Labor — changing jobs and job loss (COBRA basics)

HealthCare.gov — Marketplace plans and special enrollment

Texas Workforce Commission — COBRA and Texas COBRA law

Do this now: when the COBRA election notice arrives, calendar day 60. Before it runs out, price your coverage on HealthCare.gov — then elect COBRA or enroll in the Marketplace, but do not let the window close with neither done.

Called a contractor, treated like an employee? The label does not decide

The label does not decide — the work does

Whether you are an employee or an independent contractor turns on the real working relationship — who controls how, when, and where the work is done, how central the work is to the business, how you are paid, and how permanent the arrangement is. A 1099 form, a job title, or a signed "independent contractor agreement" does not settle it, and neither does the fact that you agreed to it.

What a wrong label costs you

Classified as a contractor, you usually get no unemployment benefits when the work ends, no workers' compensation if you are hurt on the job, no minimum-wage or overtime protection, and no employer share of payroll taxes — you owe self-employment tax on both halves of Social Security and Medicare. If the label is wrong, those protections were yours all along.

Get an official ruling: IRS Form SS-8

A worker or a firm can file Form SS-8 and ask the IRS to rule on the worker's status for federal tax purposes — it is free, though it can take months. If you were paid as a contractor but believe you were an employee, Form 8919 lets you report wages and pay only the employee share of Social Security and Medicare tax instead of full self-employment tax.

Unpaid wages or overtime? File a claim

If the label cost you minimum wage or overtime, you can file a wage claim with the U.S. Department of Labor's Wage and Hour Division (free help at 1-866-487-9243) or with your state labor agency. State unemployment agencies also audit classification because it decides who pays unemployment tax — a report there can trigger a review of your whole workplace.

How Texas decides it

The Texas Workforce Commission says the Texas Unemployment Compensation Act uses a direction-or-control, or common-law, test — employment unless service is shown free from control or direction — and TWC has adapted the old IRS twenty-factor test to apply it.

Official sources

U.S. Department of Labor — employee or independent contractor (misclassification)

IRS — independent contractor or employee?

TWC — Independent Contractors / Contract Labor

Do this now: write down how the job actually works — who sets your schedule, who provides the tools, whether you can work for anyone else, and how you are paid. That list is your evidence. Then pick a route: Form SS-8 for a federal ruling, or a wage claim with the Labor Department or your state agency.

If your professional license is threatened

Nurses, teachers, contractors, real-estate agents, cosmetologists, counselors, and dozens of other workers hold a state-issued license — and once granted, that license is a legal right the licensing board cannot take without process. If you get a letter saying a complaint was filed or you are under investigation, treat it like a lawsuit with a deadline, because it is one: the letter states what you must do and by when, and ignoring it is the fastest route to losing by default. The usual sequence, in general terms, is complaint, investigation, a formal notice of the charges, a hearing before the board or an administrative law judge where you can present your side, a written decision, and then an appeal to court. One warning above all: do not surrender your license "voluntarily" to make an investigation go away without legal advice first. A surrender is usually recorded and reported like a revocation — it can follow you to other states through licensing compacts and background checks, and it can end careers in other fields that ask whether you have ever had a license disciplined. Respond in writing, on time, briefly and professionally; keep a copy of everything; and do not discuss the matter with investigators beyond what the process requires until you have talked to a lawyer who handles license defense — this is administrative law, and many bar lawyer-referral programs in this section can point you to one. Discipline can also reach your job (many employers must be told), other licenses you hold, and future applications, which is exactly why the cheap move is advice at the letter stage, not after the hearing.

What to do next:

  1. Write down what happened, with dates, names, and witnesses, while it is fresh — and keep pay stubs, schedules, and messages somewhere your employer cannot delete them.
  2. Check the deadline above and file your charge before it runs out, even if you are still gathering documents.
  3. If you lost the job, file for unemployment the same week — then keep up the weekly certifications.
  4. Do not sign a severance agreement or a “resignation” letter under pressure. You can ask for time to have a lawyer or legal aid look at it first.
  5. If money is tight, ask legal aid about employment cases — many discrimination lawyers also offer a free first consultation.

Adoption, paternity & parentage in Texas

Two different questions live in this section, and both come down to one thing: who counts, legally, as a child’s parent. Adoption creates a legal parent-child relationship where none existed before. Parentage (often called paternity when the father is the parent being established) proves that relationship when a child is born to unmarried parents. Parentage matters beyond the birth certificate: until parentage is legally established, a court generally cannot order custody, visitation, or child support. The Child custody and Child support sections on this page cover what happens after parentage exists; this section covers how you get there, and how adoption works from the other side.

Adoption types

The four paths to adoption

Agency adoption goes through a licensed public or private agency, which matches children with families and completes the required home study. Private (independent) adoption is arranged directly between birth parents and adoptive parents, usually with lawyers on both sides and a court-approved home study. Stepparent adoption is when a spouse adopts their partner’s child — often the simplest path, but it still requires the other birth parent’s rights to be terminated or surrendered, unless that parent is deceased or their rights were already terminated. Foster-care adoption is adopting a child already in state care, usually after parental rights have been terminated; children adopted from foster care who meet the state’s definition of special needs may qualify for federal or state adoption assistance to help with costs. Every path ends the same way: a court hearing that finalizes the adoption and issues a new birth certificate naming the adoptive parents.

Adoption steps

How adoption works, step by step

First, the birth parents’ legal rights must end — by voluntary surrender or consent, or by a court order terminating rights after a hearing. Second, the adoptive family completes a home study: interviews, background checks, a home visit, and references, done by a licensed agency or social worker. Third, the child is placed with the family for a supervision period set by state law. Fourth, the court holds a finalization hearing and signs the adoption decree. Two warnings: never pay for a child or promise payment in exchange for consent — baby-selling is illegal in every state, and legitimate adoption costs are fees for agencies, lawyers, and the home study, not payments to birth parents — and never skip the home study or the court hearing, because an adoption that is not finalized by a court is not an adoption.

Parentage

Establishing paternity and parentage: three ways

When parents are married, the spouse is usually presumed to be the legal parent automatically. When they are not, parentage must be established one of three ways. Voluntary acknowledgment: both parents sign a Voluntary Acknowledgment of Paternity (VAP) — hospitals are required to offer this form at birth, and it can also be signed later. Signing is a legal act with real consequences, including child support, so do not sign it casually or under pressure; most states give a short window to rescind it. Court order: either parent, or the child-support agency, can file a parentage case and ask a judge to decide. Genetic testing: the court can order DNA testing, and the child-support agency can usually arrange testing without a full court case. If the test excludes a man, he is not the legal father; if it matches, the court declares parentage.

Why it matters

Why parentage comes first: no parentage, no custody or support order

Parentage is the key that unlocks everything else. Without it, a father generally cannot get a court order for custody or visitation, and a mother generally cannot get a court order for child support from him — the court has no legal parent to order. Parentage also affects the child’s rights: inheritance, health-insurance coverage through a parent, Social Security benefits on a parent’s record, and access to family medical history. If you are unsure whether parentage is established for your child, check whether a VAP was signed or a court order exists; a name on the birth certificate alone does not always settle the legal question, and the rules vary by state.

Federal help

Free national guidance: Child Welfare Information Gateway

Child Welfare Information Gateway is a service of the Children’s Bureau in the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. It publishes plain-language guides to every type of adoption, to parentage, and to post-adoption help, plus state-by-state information on adoption assistance. It is free, and it is the best first stop before you pay anyone for adoption advice.

Child Welfare Information Gateway: adoption & parentage guides →
Official resource

In Texas: start with the family court

Adoption and parentage cases are filed in the family court in Texas. The court’s family-law pages are the place to find this state’s forms, filing steps, and hearing information. This page links the court’s own family resources, already listed in the Child custody section above.

Family court resources in Texas →

What to do:

  1. Adopting? Pick your path (agency, private, stepparent, or foster care), then arrange the home study through a licensed agency or social worker — it is required in almost every adoption.
  2. Stepparent adoption: confirm how the other birth parent’s rights will end (consent, surrender, or court order) before you file — that is usually the longest step.
  3. Establishing parentage: if both parents agree, sign a Voluntary Acknowledgment of Paternity — but read it first, and know your state’s short window to change your mind.
  4. If you disagree about parentage, file a parentage case or contact the child-support agency about genetic testing; do not rely on an informal agreement.
  5. After parentage is established, use the Child custody and Child support sections on this page to get orders for custody, visitation, and support.
  6. Never pay anyone for consent to adopt, and never skip the court finalization — an adoption is not legal until a judge signs it.

This page does not print one national adoption fee, waiting period, or home-study rule because all three vary by state — the family-court link and Child Welfare Information Gateway above route you to Texas’s exact rules. This is general information, not legal advice. Adoption and parentage law change, and Texas law controls.

Child custody & visitation in Texas

When parents live apart, a court order is what turns an arrangement into something enforceable — who the child lives with, when each parent has them, and who decides about school and medical care. You do not need a lawyer to start, and in Texas the courts publish the path. Here it is.

Where to start

Self-Represented Litigants

This is the official court starting point for custody cases in Texas — the process, the forms, and the terms the court uses.

Start with the court →
The words matter

What Texas calls it

The exact words and forms differ by county in Texas — ask the court clerk which term and packet your county uses, because using the court’s own wording speeds everything up.

Forms & parenting plans

Free forms and the parenting plan

The Texas courts publish no custody forms packet of their own — the judiciary’s self-help page directs parents to TexasLawHelp.org for forms and guides.

Safety

If a child is in danger

If a child is in immediate danger, call 911. Courts can issue emergency and temporary custody orders quickly when a child’s safety is at risk — ask the clerk how to request one. Many states also require or offer mediation before a custody hearing; it is cheaper and faster than a courtroom fight, and it does not require you to give up your rights.

When CPS investigates your family

The cards above are about reporting harm and about custody between parents. This part is for the other side of the knock on the door: what happens when child protective services — CPS — comes to investigate your family. An investigation is not a criminal charge and it is not a removal, and most end without a court case. Protecting children and knowing your own rights are both real — this block covers the process and your rights, in Texas and everywhere.

Every state

What an investigation is — and what a caseworker can and cannot do

Someone reported a concern; the agency screened the report and sent a caseworker to look into it. The worker will want to see the child, see the home, and ask questions — of you, the child, and people who know your family. In general, a caseworker cannot come into your home without your permission, a court order, or a genuine emergency, and workers can often interview a child at school without asking first. What you say can be written down and used later, including in court. A safety plan — a written agreement about who cares for the child and what changes at home — is voluntary: it is not a court order. Read it before you sign, ask for time to think, and know you can ask a lawyer first; also know that refusing can lead the agency to ask a court to step in. If the agency believes a child is in immediate danger, it can involve police or a court to remove the child temporarily — and a judge must review that quickly.

In Texas

How an investigation runs in Texas

Texas Child Protective Investigations (CPI) is part of the Texas Department of Family and Protective Services (DFPS), the state agency. Reports go to the Texas Abuse Hotline and are screened and given a priority: Priority 1 reports are investigated within 24 hours, while lower-priority reports get more time, and investigators aim to complete most investigations in about 60 days. The investigator interviews the child, parents, and others and decides each allegation as 'reason to believe' (confirmed), 'ruled out,' or 'unable to determine.' The case may close, open for family-based safety services, or DFPS may ask a court to order services or remove the child.

Your rights

Your rights during and after an investigation in Texas

DFPS publishes a 'Parent's Guide to Child Protective Services' explaining the process and your rights. It covers that you will be told the reported concerns and the findings, and that a Parental Child Safety Placement — where your child stays with someone you choose during the investigation — is a voluntary agreement, not a court order. If a finding of 'reason to believe' is made against you, your name is entered in the DFPS central registry, and DFPS offers an Administrative Review of Investigation Findings (ARIF) process to challenge it. If DFPS files a court case, parents who cannot afford a lawyer have the right to a court-appointed attorney.

Official source

Texas DFPS — A Parent's Guide to Child Protective Services

The official Texas source for parents in an investigation — start there, and with any letter the agency sends you.

Official CPS information for parents →

Do this now: write down the caseworker’s name, phone number, and office, and the date and time of every visit and call. Keep copies of everything you sign and every letter you receive. If a letter mentions an appeal or a deadline, the date in your letter controls — act on it right away, because those windows are often short. If relatives might care for your child if it ever becomes necessary, tell the worker their names early — agencies are generally required to consider family first. And if the case heads to court, ask about a court-appointed lawyer the same day; in most states a parent who cannot afford one gets one in these cases.

Grandparents and kin: visitation rights and raising a relative’s child

The cards above are about custody and visitation between parents. This part is for grandparents and other kin. Every state has a law that lets a grandparent ask a court for visitation — but the doorway is narrow, and it is different in every state. It is also a separate door from kinship care, where a relative steps in to raise the child. Both are covered here for Texas.

Every state

Fit parents get the benefit of the doubt — in every state

In Troxel v. Granville (2000), the U.S. Supreme Court held that fit parents have a constitutional right to decide who spends time with their children, and that a court must give a fit parent’s decision special weight. That is why the laws below ask for more than a close bond: most states require a trigger — a parent’s death, a divorce, the child having lived with you — and proof that being cut off would harm the child, that a parent is unfit, or both.

Every state

Agreement and mediation come before court

A court fight is the last resort. Ask in writing, keep the answer, and try to agree on a schedule — many states let the court order mediation in grandparent cases, and in some the person asking has to pay for it. If you do reach an agreement, put it in writing; if a custody case between the parents is already open, ask whether your time can be written into that order.

In Texas

Who can ask for visitation in Texas — and when

Under Texas Family Code Sec. 153.432, a biological or adoptive grandparent may request possession of or access to a grandchild by an original suit or a suit for modification, and must attach an affidavit alleging, with supporting facts, that denial of possession or access would significantly impair the child's physical health or emotional well-being; the court must dismiss unless the affidavit facts, if true, would support relief under Sec. 153.433. Under Sec. 153.433, access may be ordered only if at least one biological or adoptive parent has not had parental rights terminated and the petitioning grandparent is the parent of a parent of the child who has been incarcerated during the three months before filing, has been found incompetent, is dead, or does not have actual or court-ordered possession of or access to the child; Sec. 153.434 further limits when a grandparent may request access, including after adoption.

In Texas

What you must prove in Texas

The grandparent must overcome the presumption that a parent acts in the best interest of the child by proving by a preponderance of the evidence that denial of possession or access would significantly impair the child's physical health or emotional well-being (Family Code Sec. 153.433(a)(2)). No mediation-first rule specific to grandparent access appears in Secs. 153.432-153.434.

Kinship care

Raising a relative’s child in Texas

Texas DFPS states it must have the family help identify grandparents and other adult relatives for placement when a child is in state conservatorship, and it publishes kinship help including the Relative and Other Designated Caregiver (RODC) monthly payment for eligible kinship caregivers, one-time kinship placement assistance, and Permanency Care Assistance for a relative or fictive kin who was the child's verified foster parent for at least six months, signs a permanency care agreement, and is granted permanent managing conservatorship. Separately, Family Code Chapter 34 lets a parent sign an Authorization Agreement for a nonparent relative or voluntary caregiver to consent to medical care, enroll the child in school, and make other listed decisions without a court order.

Official source

Texas Family Code, Chapter 153 (Texas Legislative Council)

The official Texas source for grandparent visitation and kinship care — start there, and confirm your local court’s forms and filing steps before you file.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Find your doorway in the Texas cards above — if none of the listed circumstances fits, a petition is unlikely to be heard, and a lawyer can tell you whether another route, like intervening in a pending custody case, exists. (2) Write down your history with the child: dates you lived together, caregiving, visits, and support — the proof standards run on exactly those facts. (3) Ask the parent for time in writing and keep the reply. (4) If a child is living with you now, use the official source above to ask about kinship help — placement preference, monthly payments, and school and medical consent paperwork may be available even without a court case. (5) Watch the refiling limits: several states bar a second petition for months or years after a denial.

What to do next:

  1. Gather the basics: the child’s birth certificate, any existing court orders, and school and medical information.
  2. Write down the schedule you are asking for — school weeks, weekends, holidays, summers — and how it serves the child. Judges decide on the child’s best interests, not on which parent argues best.
  3. File in the court for the county where the child lives, using the court’s own forms and terms above.
  4. Do not withhold the child, move away, or skip a court date out of frustration — each one damages your case.
  5. If you cannot afford a lawyer, ask the court about legal aid and filing-fee waivers — custody help is one of the most common things legal aid offices handle.

Voting rights in Texas

Voting rules are set by each state, and the two that trip people up are ID and registration deadlines. Here is exactly what Texas’s own elections officials say — plus what to do if something goes wrong at the polls.

At the polls

What ID do you need in Texas?

Photo ID required if you have one of the seven acceptable forms of photo ID. Voters who do not possess one and cannot reasonably obtain one may fill out a Reasonable Impediment Declaration at the polls and present an alternative form of ID — a utility bill, bank statement, government check, or voter registration certificate.

Deadlines

Registration deadlines in Texas

Texas law requires eligible voters to register by the 30th day before Election Day — October 5, 2026 for the Nov. 3, 2026 election. No same-day registration.

Problems at the polls

If you are turned away

Do not just leave. If officials say you are not on the list or your ID is not accepted, ask for a provisional (or affidavit) ballot — federal law gives you that right, and your vote counts once your registration is confirmed. Then call your county elections office or the official voter page above and report what happened. If you believe your rights were violated, you can also call the national, nonpartisan Election Protection hotline at 866-OUR-VOTE (866-687-8683).

What to do next:

  1. Check your registration on the official page above now — not the week of the election — and fix any problem while there is time.
  2. Check the ID rule for Texas above and get the document in hand early; several states give free voter ID cards to people who ask.
  3. Find your polling place and its hours, or vote early or by mail if your state offers it and your schedule is tight.
  4. If anything goes wrong on the day, ask for a provisional ballot, write down the names of the officials involved, and report it.

Utility shutoffs in Texas

A shutoff notice is not the end of the story. Most states restrict when a utility can turn off your heat or power — especially in winter — and there is a federally funded program in every state that helps pay the bill. Here is Texas’s rule, the help, and where to complain.

Winter protection

Texas’s shutoff rule

Temperature-based, no winter dates: an electric utility or retail provider cannot disconnect on a day when the previous day’s highest temperature did not exceed 32°F and the temperature is predicted to remain at or below that level for the next 24 hours, per the nearest National Weather Service reports — and disconnection is also barred while an NWS heat advisory is in effect (PUCT Rules §25.29(i), §25.483(j)).

Help paying

Energy assistance: Comprehensive Energy Assistance Program (CEAP) — Texas's LIHEAP, Texas Dept. of Housing and Community Affairs (TDHCA) via local subrecipients

The federal energy assistance program (LIHEAP, under its Texas name above) helps low-income households pay heating and cooling bills and can cover crisis reconnections. Apply as early as you can — in many states, being approved for assistance is itself what switches the winter protection on, and the money runs out each season.

Apply for energy assistance →
Complaints

If the utility will not work with you: Public Utility Commission of Texas

The Public Utility Commission of Texas regulates utilities in Texas and takes consumer complaints. Call them at 1-888-782-8477. Filing a complaint often pauses a shutoff while the regulator reviews it — file before the shutoff date, not after.

File a utility complaint →
Right now

Got a shutoff notice?

Call the utility before the date on the notice and ask for a payment plan — get the agreement in writing, with the amount and dates. If anyone in the home is seriously ill or depends on medical equipment, say so on that call and ask about a medical certificate or medical hold. Dialing 211 connects you to local help (charities, churches, and emergency funds) in most of the country.

What to do next:

  1. Find the shutoff date on your notice — everything else is scheduled backwards from that date.
  2. Call the utility and set up a payment plan; in several states the plan itself is your legal protection. Write down the name of the person you spoke with.
  3. Apply for energy assistance above right away — approval can pause or prevent the shutoff.
  4. If the utility will not budge, file the complaint above before the shutoff date.
  5. Never ignore a notice because you cannot pay in full — every protection on this page starts with you contacting someone.

Financial scams in Texas

Scammers are professional liars, and they are good at it — Americans lose billions of dollars to fraud every year, and most of it is never reported. Falling for one is not a character flaw; these crews run scripts tested on thousands of people. The good news: almost every financial scam shares the same handful of tells, and once you know them, the spell breaks.

The golden rule: anyone who pressures you to pay right now with gift cards, a wire transfer, cryptocurrency, a cash app, or cash is running a scam — full stop. No real agency, bank, utility, or business demands payment that way. The second someone insists on secrecy (“don’t tell your family or your bank”), hang up. Urgency plus secrecy plus a strange payment method is the whole game.

Know the signs

Government impostors

A call, text, or email claims to be the IRS, Social Security, Medicare, or the sheriff: you owe money, your benefits will stop, or there is a warrant for your arrest. Real agencies almost always contact you by mail first, never threaten arrest over the phone, and never ask for gift cards or wire transfers. Hang up and call the agency yourself at the number on its official website or your paperwork.

Know the signs

The family emergency — now with cloned voices

“Grandma, I’m in jail, I need bail money — please don’t tell Mom.” Scammers can now clone a loved one’s voice from a few seconds of online audio, so it can sound exactly like them. Defenses that work: agree on a family code word in advance, and always hang up and call your loved one back on the number you already have. Real trouble survives a callback; a scam can’t.

Know the signs

Romance and investment scams

An online sweetheart who can never meet — and eventually needs money — is a script, not a relationship. So is the friendly stranger pitching can’t-miss crypto or investment returns, sometimes showing you a fake account that looks like it is growing (“pig butchering”). Guaranteed high returns with no risk do not exist. If you have to pay fees or taxes before you can withdraw your own money, it was never real.

Know the signs

Fake checks, phishing, and tech support

A check that overpays you — for a car, a job, a prize — with a request to send part back is counterfeit; it bounces days later and you owe the bank. Texts and emails about a “locked account” or a “missed delivery” are phishing for your logins — never use their link; type the company’s address yourself. And a pop-up warning that your computer has a virus, with a number to call, is fake: real companies never do that.

Take action

Report a scam in Texas

File a complaint with Texas’s consumer protection office. They track scam patterns, investigate, and sometimes get money back — and your report warns the next person.

File a complaint in Texas →
Official resource

Texas’s consumer protection office

Scam alerts, fraud guides, and your rights as a buyer — straight from the office that enforces Texas’s consumer laws.

Visit Texas consumer protection →
Federal

Report it nationally

Report any scam to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov → — it feeds the national database investigators actually use. If the scam came through the internet (email, social media, a website), also file with the FBI’s Internet Crime Complaint Center at IC3.gov → — reporting fast can sometimes help freeze money before it disappears.

By phone

Free help lines

Age 60 or older? The Justice Department’s National Elder Fraud Hotline: 1-833-372-8311 (Mon–Fri, 10 a.m.–6 p.m. ET) gives you a case manager who helps you report and find local help. Anyone, any age, can call the AARP Fraud Watch Network Helpline: 1-877-908-3360 (Mon–Fri, 8 a.m.–8 p.m. ET) — free, member or not, for advice on a suspicious call or support after a loss.

Robocalls and unwanted calls: what actually helps

Start with the free step that works on legitimate telemarketers: put your number on the National Do Not Call Registry at donotcall.gov (registration is free and does not expire). Know its limit: scammers and illegal robocallers ignore the registry — it stops honest companies, and a call that comes anyway is a warning sign, not a sales pitch. The law behind it is the Telephone Consumer Protection Act (TCPA), which generally bans autodialed and prerecorded sales calls and texts to your cell phone without your consent; you can take away consent you once gave — reply STOP to texts and tell callers to stop calling. The TCPA lets you sue for $500 per illegal call or text, and up to $1,500 per violation if the caller acted willfully — one reason some lawyers take these cases for a share of the recovery. Day to day: let unknown numbers go to voicemail (a real caller leaves a message), never press buttons or say "yes" to a robocall, never call an unknown number back, and use your carrier's free spam-blocking tools and your phone's silence-unknown-callers setting. Report illegal calls at donotcall.gov and to the FCC — the reports drive the enforcement sweeps that shut down call operations, and reporting a scam attempt at ReportFraud.ftc.gov takes two minutes.

Already paid or shared information? Act in this order:

  1. Call your bank or card issuer right now — use the number on your card or statement, not one the scammer gave you. Ask them to stop or reverse the payment. Wires and transfers have short rescue windows; hours matter.
  2. Paid by gift card? Call the company on the back of the card immediately and say it was a scam — if the money has not been drained yet, they can sometimes freeze it. Keep the cards and receipts.
  3. Shared personal information (Social Security number, passwords, account logins)? Go to IdentityTheft.gov for a step-by-step recovery plan, and place a free credit freeze with Equifax, Experian, and TransUnion so no one can open accounts in your name.
  4. Change the passwords on any account you touched or mentioned, starting with email and banking, and turn on two-factor authentication.
  5. Report it — to Texas’s consumer office and the FTC (links above) — and tell your family. Silence is the scammer’s best protection; a report is how the next victim gets warned.

What to bring to court in Texas

Walking in prepared makes the day go smoother and helps the judge see your case clearly. Use this as a packing list — a starting point, not legal advice. Your court or your lawyer may ask for more.

  1. Your photo ID and your case number. Courts run on case numbers — bring the one from your court papers.
  2. Copies of every document you filed, plus two extra sets. One set for the judge, one for the other side, one for yourself.
  3. Evidence that backs your claims, in order. Dated messages, receipts, photos, statements — a paper trail beats memory. Bring originals where you have them.
  4. A one-page timeline. What happened, in date order, and who was there. Judges love timelines.
  5. Financial records if money is at issue. Pay stubs, bank statements, bills, tax returns — bring more than you think you’ll need.
  6. A pen and paper. To write down the judge’s orders, next dates, and anything you’re told to do.

Before the day: call the courthouse (or check its website) to confirm the courtroom, the time, and anything you’re not sure about. Arrive 30 minutes early — you’ll go through security and need time to find the room. Leave anything distracting at home and silence your phone before you walk in.

Official court site

Office of Court Administration page for self-represented litigants, pointing to the court-created TexasCourtHelp.org for forms, filing a case, and getting ready for court, plus a pro se appellate guide.

TXcourts.gov →
If you feel unsafe: call the National Domestic Violence Hotline at 1-800-799-7233 (free, confidential, 24/7), or the Texas Council on Family Violence at 1-888-450-8238.

Small claims court in Texas

Suing someone without a lawyer, for smaller amounts, in a simpler court. Here's how it works in Texas.

Limits

Maximum claim

$20,000

Cost

Filing fee

varies by county, typically $35–$60 filing (service extra)

Lawyers

Can you bring one?

yes, allowed but not required

After you win: collecting your judgment

Winning is only half the job — the court does not collect the money for you. If the other side does not pay voluntarily, you become a judgment creditor, and Texas law gives you tools to collect, plus a deadline after which the judgment expires. Act while the trail is fresh: people move, change jobs, and close accounts.

How long you have

How long a Texas judgment lasts — and renewal

A Texas judgment awarded to a non-government creditor is generally valid for 10 years but can be renewed for longer; if it is not renewed, it becomes dormant. A creditor can ask to revive a dormant judgment, but generally has only two years in which to try to revive it.

Collection tools

Ways to collect in Texas

The Texas State Law Library points creditors to judgment liens, writs, and orders, and to post-judgment discovery to find out what assets the debtor owns and where they are. A Texas justice-court collecting guide describes filing an Abstract of Judgment with the county clerk to create a lien on the debtor's real estate in that county, and, 30 days after judgment, getting a Writ of Execution to try to seize the debtor's non-exempt property.

Know the limits

The rules that protect the other side apply to you too

Wage garnishment is often the most effective tool — and it has strict limits. The wage garnishment block in this page’s Credit Card & Debt Help section explains the caps and the income that can never be taken (such as Social Security); those protections bind everyone collecting, banks and individuals alike. Expect the court to enforce the debtor’s exemptions if they claim them.

Official source

Texas — Collecting a Money Judgment (Texas State Law Library guide)

The official Texas source for collecting a judgment, with the court’s forms and steps.

Official judgment-collection information →

Step by step: (1) Wait until any appeal or pay-in-full window in your judgment has passed. (2) Ask for payment in writing, and keep a record of every payment and every cost you add. (3) If they do not pay, use the court’s debtor’s-examination or disclosure process (where the card above lists one) to learn where they work, bank, and what they own. (4) Pick the tool that fits what you found — garnishment for wages, levy for a bank account, lien for real estate — and file the court’s forms; the sheriff or marshal usually delivers them. (5) Calendar your judgment’s expiration date and renew before it if Texas allows. When you are paid in full, file a satisfaction of judgment with the court — that is required, and it closes the case cleanly.

Bitten by a dog? What to do, and who pays

First: treatment, photos, and the report

Get medical care first. Then photograph the injuries, the dog, and the place where it happened, and write down what occurred while it is fresh. Get the owner's name and address, and ask about the dog's rabies vaccination. Report the bite to your local animal control office or the police non-emergency line — the report creates the official record your claim needs and starts the rabies-control check on the dog.

Who usually pays

Most dog-bite claims are paid by the dog owner's homeowners or renters insurance — not out of the owner's own pocket. Ask the owner for the insurer's name and policy number, and keep every medical bill, pharmacy receipt, and record of missed work. If the owner will not share insurance information, small claims court (this section) is the usual route for smaller bills, and many injury lawyers offer a free first consultation on larger ones.

The two kinds of state law

States split into two systems. In strict-liability states, the owner owes you even if the dog had never bitten or shown aggression before. In the rest, you generally must show the owner knew the dog was dangerous or was careless — the old “one free bite” idea. Where Texas's own law states its rule, it is in the card below, worded the way the statute states it.

Do this now: get treatment, take photos, and file the animal-control report today. Then get the owner's name, address, and insurance information in writing — those three things decide how the claim gets paid.

How a court case actually moves: filing, service, answer, hearing

Step 1 — Filing starts the case, but filing alone changes nothing yet

The person suing (the plaintiff) files a complaint or petition at the courthouse and pays the filing fee, and the court issues a summons. But the case cannot move against the other side until they are formally told about it — that telling is called service of process, and it is the step everything else hangs on. If you are suing, the court or clerk can tell you the approved ways to serve in your court; budget for a sheriff's or process server's fee on top of the filing fee.

Step 2 — Service of process: the formal delivery that starts the clock

Service usually means hand-delivery of the summons and complaint to the defendant by a sheriff's deputy or a licensed process server — someone who is not a party to the case. Leaving papers with another adult at the home, or mailing them, counts only where the rules allow it (sometimes after personal delivery has been tried, sometimes when the defendant signs a waiver of service). The server files a proof or affidavit of service with the court stating when and how delivery happened. That date starts the defendant's deadline to respond, and service is what gives the court power over the defendant: a judgment entered without valid service can be attacked and undone, as the default-judgment block in the Credit Card & Debt section explains.

Step 3 — The answer: the defendant's response, on a deadline printed in the papers

Once served, the defendant has a set number of days to file a written answer responding to the claims — the exact deadline is stated in the summons or the court's rules, so read the papers you were handed, not a website. Miss it and the plaintiff can win by default without proving anything. The answer is also where defenses and counterclaims are raised. If you were served with papers that look wrong — wrong person, wrong address, papers left with someone else — do not ignore them: improper service can be challenged, but usually only if you raise it promptly, so get advice or call the clerk right away.

Step 4 — Hearing, then judgment

After the answer, the court sets the hearing or trial date (some courts first order mediation or a settlement conference). Each side presents documents and witnesses — the "What to bring to court" list on this page is your packing checklist — and the judge decides. The loser can owe the amount plus court costs, and a judgment does not collect itself: the collecting-a-judgment block in the Credit Card & Debt section covers what happens next, and the appeals block below covers challenging the result.

Do this now: if papers were handed to you, find the deadline in the summons and calendar it today — that date, not the hearing, is the first one that can sink you. If you are the one suing, ask the clerk at filing exactly how service works in that court and what it costs.

If you lost: appealing a civil judgment

An appeal is not a new trial — with one big exception

In a regular civil case, an appeal does not redo the trial. The higher court reads the record of what already happened and looks for legal errors — a wrong rule applied, evidence admitted that should not have been, a decision no reasonable judge could reach on that record. New evidence and new arguments you never made below are generally not allowed. The exception: appeals from small claims and the lowest courts are, in many states, a genuine fresh start — a trial de novo in the next court up, where the case is heard all over again as if the first one never happened. Your state's line below tells you which kind your appeal is, where the official source says.

The deadline is short, and missing it usually ends the matter

You start an appeal by filing a notice of appeal — and the window is measured in days, not months, in most states, counted from entry of the judgment or from notice of it. These deadlines are treated as jurisdictional: file late and the appeal is typically dismissed without anyone reaching the merits, however strong your argument is. The periods in the state lines are what the official rules publish; if your state has no line, or your case type is different, confirm your exact deadline with the clerk of the court that decided your case — immediately, not after you have written the brief.

The appeal deadline in Texas

In a civil case, the notice of appeal must be filed within 30 days after the judgment is signed, under Texas Rule of Appellate Procedure 26.1, extended to 90 days if a timely motion for new trial or other listed post-judgment filing is made. An appeal from justice court to county court is a trial de novo, a new trial as if the justice court case had not happened.

Texas Judicial Branch — Texas Rules of Appellate Procedure

Filing the appeal does not automatically stop collection

Winning below means the judgment can usually be enforced while you appeal — wages garnished, accounts levied — unless you get a stay. A stay often requires posting an appeal bond (supersedeas bond) for the judgment amount, which guarantees the winner gets paid if you lose again. Ask the clerk about a stay the same day you file the notice of appeal; every week of delay is a week the judgment is working. In some courts and case types a bond or a deposit is also required just for the appeal itself to proceed — another clerk question on day one.

Costs, fees, and honest odds

Appeals carry a filing fee and usually the cost of preparing the trial transcript, which can exceed the fee by a lot. If you cannot afford them, the court fee-waiver block in the Free and Low-Cost Legal Help section covers asking the court to waive appeal costs too. Be clear-eyed about the standard: the appeals court is not asking "who should have won?" but "did the judge make a legal mistake that mattered?" Disagreeing with how the judge weighed the witnesses is rarely enough. A one-hour consultation with an appellate lawyer before the deadline — many legal-aid and lawyer-referral programs in this section offer them — is the cheapest reality check in law.

Do this now: find your notice-of-appeal deadline — in your state's line below or from the clerk today — and calendar it with days to spare. The appeal can be thought through later; the deadline cannot.

If you receive a subpoena, do not ignore it

A subpoena is a court order — not an invitation. It commands you to appear and testify, to hand over documents or records, or both, in someone else's case or your own. Ignoring one can end in a contempt finding, fines, or a warrant; "I was never part of this lawsuit" is not a defense, because witnesses and record-holders are exactly who subpoenas are for. Start by reading it closely: who issued it (a court or an attorney acting for one), what it demands, and the date by which you must appear or produce — that deadline drives everything. If the demand is overbroad, reaches private records, was served improperly, or gives too little time, the routes are a written objection and a motion to quash or modify, filed with the issuing court before the deadline — in general terms these exist in every court system, and the clerk can point you to the local form. Do not just skip it and explain later. If the subpoena touches your own legal exposure — your records could incriminate you, or it is really aimed at you — talk to a lawyer before you produce anything; witnesses are usually entitled to a small attendance fee and mileage, which the subpoena or clerk can confirm.

What to do next:

  1. Send the other side a written demand first — many disputes settle here.
  2. File your claim in the right county and pay the filing fee.
  3. Serve the other side properly — the court can’t hear your case without it.
  4. Bring your evidence organized: see what to bring to court.

Learn more about small claims court in Texas →

Clearing your criminal record in Texas

Expungement or sealing can hide eligible arrests and convictions from background checks. Here's where Texas stands.

Availability

Can records be cleared?

yes — expunction (deletion) and nondisclosure (sealing)

Eligibility

What qualifies

acquitted or dismissed charges, pardons; deferred-adjudication cases get nondisclosure (sealing)

Timing

Waiting period

none for acquittals; 180 days–3 years by offense level for dismissals; 5 years after discharge for most felony nondisclosures

Pardons and clemency: forgiveness when expungement is not an option

A pardon is forgiveness from the state — it is not the same as expungement

Expungement or sealing (covered above in this section) asks a court to hide or erase a record, and it only reaches convictions the statute covers. A pardon is different: the governor, or a pardons board, formally forgives the conviction. A pardon can restore rights a conviction took away — voting, serving on a jury, holding office, sometimes firearm rights — and it can matter for jobs, licenses, and immigration even when nothing can be erased. What it usually does NOT do is delete the record: in many states the conviction still shows, marked as pardoned. If both routes exist for your conviction, compare them — and many people pursue expungement first because it follows rules instead of discretion.

How the process works everywhere

You apply — you are not automatically considered. In most states the application goes to a board of pardons or parole board, which investigates, may hold a hearing, and recommends; the governor decides. In a few states the board itself grants pardons. Expect to document your whole sentence completed (including probation, fines, and restitution), your life since — work, family, community — and why the pardon should be granted. Waiting periods after the sentence are common. There is no deadline pressure the way a court filing has, but there is also no right to a pardon: it is an act of grace, and most applications are denied. A complete, honest application is the whole game.

Pardons in Texas

Applications are sent to the Texas Board of Pardons and Paroles Clemency Section in Austin; if the Board recommends clemency, the application is sent to the Governor, who makes the final decision for all clemency applications. A pardon restores certain citizenship rights forfeited by the conviction, such as the right to serve on a jury, hold public office, and serve as executor or administrator of an estate. The arrest is not automatically expunged upon a grant of full pardon, but a convicted person who receives a full pardon is entitled under Article 55A.004(2) to an expunction of all arrest records relating to the conviction.

Texas Board of Pardons and Paroles Clemency Process Page

Federal convictions are a separate track

A governor cannot pardon a federal conviction — only the President can, through the U.S. Department of Justice's Office of the Pardon Attorney. And a pardon in one state does nothing for a conviction from another state: apply where you were convicted.

Do this now: first check whether your conviction qualifies for expungement or sealing above — that route is rule-based. If it does not, or a pardon would restore more, start your state's application from the official page, and assemble your completion documents (discharge papers, paid-fine receipts, restitution proof) before you write a word.

What to do next:

  1. Get a copy of your criminal history so you know exactly what’s on it.
  2. Check the eligibility rules and waiting periods listed above.
  3. File the petition with the court — or get free help doing it: start with free legal help.

Learn more about clearing your criminal record in Texas →

Lemon law in Texas

Bought a new car that's constantly in the shop? Texas's lemon law may get you a refund or a replacement.

Coverage

Which vehicles qualify

new and leased vehicles within 2 years/24,000 miles (also used vehicles still under warranty)

Threshold

When it's legally a lemon

4+ failed repairs of the same defect (2 for serious safety hazards) or 30+ days out of service within 24 months/24,000 miles

Remedy

What you get

replacement vehicle, refund, or cash settlement

What to do next:

  1. Keep every repair order, receipt, and record of days your car was in the shop.
  2. Notify the manufacturer in writing about the defect.
  3. Give them the final repair opportunity the law requires, then file your claim with your documentation in hand.
