Tenant rights in Texas

Put it in writing: our free security-deposit demand letter and landlord repair request letter are copy-and-fill-in templates — no account, nothing collected.

Not legal advice This section is general information about renting and eviction in Texas, not legal advice and not a substitute for a lawyer. Eviction deadlines are measured in days and the rules change — confirm yours with the court or a local legal-aid office before you act.

Renting comes with rights most people never hear about until something goes wrong. Here are the key ones in Texas.

Before you sign a lease

A lease is a contract. These checks take a few minutes before you sign and can protect your money, your home, and your security deposit later. Lease rules vary by state and sometimes by city, so use the Texas tenant resources and official link in this section to check the exact rule.

  1. Read it before you sign. Read every page, including addendums and rules. Never sign a lease with blank spaces: cross out a blank that does not apply or write “N/A,” and have both sides initial any handwritten change.
  2. Get every promise in writing. A promise about repairs, appliances, parking, storage, or the move-in date is hard to enforce if it is only spoken. If it matters, put it in the lease or in a signed addendum.
  3. Question clauses that take away basic protections. In many states, clauses like these are illegal or unenforceable: giving up your right to an itemized security-deposit statement; letting the landlord enter at any time without notice; giving up notice or your right to go to court before an eviction; renewing the lease automatically with a large penalty that is not spelled out clearly up front; or making you pay for all repairs, including major systems. Check Texas’s rules with the tenant resource in this section, and ask for a problem clause to be removed or changed before you sign.
  4. Know the money up front. Add up the full move-in cost: first month’s rent, the security deposit, and every fee. The lease should say which charges are a refundable deposit and which are nonrefundable fees, when rent is due, the exact late fee, and how rent must be paid.
  5. Document the home on move-in day. Photograph or video every room and every flaw, including walls, floors, windows, and appliances. Write existing damage on a move-in checklist, date it, and keep a copy; if the landlord gives you a form, return it on time and keep your dated copy. That record helps protect your deposit when you move out.
  6. Get your complete copy. Leave with a signed copy of the lease and every addendum, plus the landlord’s legal name and a mailing address for repair requests and legal notices. Do not rely on a promise to send your copy later.
  7. Spell out who pays for what. Check who is responsible for utilities, lawn care, snow removal, and supplying and repairing appliances. If the lease does not say, ask before you sign and get the answer written in.
Eviction

Notice before eviction

3-day notice to vacate for nonpayment; 30 days to end a month-to-month tenancy

Security deposit

Deposit limits & return

No statutory cap; return within 30 days; written itemized list required

Repairs

When the landlord won't fix things

May repair and deduct up to 1 month's rent or $500 after written notice

How an eviction usually moves, step by step, in Texas

  1. 1. Notice3-day notice to vacate for nonpayment; 30 days to end a month-to-month tenancy
  2. 2. Court filingIf the problem is not fixed, the landlord files an eviction case in court.
  3. 3. HearingA hearing is held in court. Go to it, and bring your lease, notices, and payment records.
  4. 4. Lockout — only after a court orderOnly a court can order an eviction. A lockout happens only after a court order, not on the landlord’s say-so alone.

Steps and deadlines vary by case and by city. Confirm the exact deadlines with your local court. This is general information, not legal advice.

Eviction records and rental screening: clean up your record

An eviction case can follow you for years — even one you won, and even one that was dismissed — because tenant-screening companies scrape court filings and sell them to landlords. Federal law gives you rights against bad screening reports, and a growing number of states let you seal, expunge, or limit the court record itself. Here is the federal picture, then exactly where Texas stands.

Every state

A filing is not a judgment

When a landlord files an eviction case, that filing exists as a public court record from day one — before any judge hears anything, and even if the case is later dismissed, settled, or decided in your favor. Screening companies collect those filings in bulk, so a case that went nowhere can still show up as an “eviction” on a report and cost you an apartment. That is why cleaning up the report (below) and the court record itself (the Texas card) are two separate jobs — most people need both.

Every state

Your federal rights when a landlord screens you

A tenant-screening report is a consumer report under the federal Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA). If a landlord turns you down — or charges you more — based on one, the landlord must give you an adverse-action notice naming the screening company that made the report. You then have the right to a free copy of that report from the company if you ask within 60 days, and to dispute anything wrong or incomplete: the company generally must investigate within 30 days, fix what it cannot verify, and tell you the result. If a dispute does not resolve it, you may add a short written statement of dispute that must go out with future reports.

Every state

The 7-year rule — and what it does not do

Under the FCRA, screening companies must stop reporting most negative information after 7 years — civil judgments included. But the rule only limits what a report may contain. The underlying court record does not disappear, and a record a landlord finds by searching court files directly is not the screening company’s report. Closing the court file itself — sealing, expungement, or limited access — is a matter of state law, and states differ sharply, as the Texas card below shows.

Every state

The cleanup, step by step

(1) Get the report. Use the company named in your adverse-action notice first (free within 60 days); before you apply anywhere else, also order your file from the big tenant-screening companies — you have FCRA rights against each of them. (2) Dispute every error in writing, with a copy of the court’s final disposition (dismissal, judgment in your favor, satisfaction) attached, and keep proof of mailing. (3) Send court orders. If your case was sealed or expunged, send the order to every screening company listing the case — a sealed record must not be reported. (4) Explain what remains. For anything accurate that is still reportable, attach a short written explanation and bring your disposition paperwork when you apply, so a landlord sees the outcome, not just the filing.

In Texas

Eviction records in Texas: sealing and access

Texas does not have a process to remove or seal an eviction from your record, and that is mostly true even if the eviction case was dismissed or you won your case. Eviction court records are public under Rule 76a of the Texas Rules of Civil Procedure; the only exceptions the State Law Library lists are certain Rule 76a circumstances and cases dismissed under the Texas Eviction Diversion Program, which no longer exists and whose records the court must keep sealed.

In Texas

Applying in Texas: screening and fee rules

When an applicant is given a rental application, the landlord must make available printed notice of the landlord's tenant selection criteria and the grounds for denial, including criminal history, previous rental history, current income, credit history, or failure to provide accurate or complete information. If the landlord rejects an applicant without making that notice available, the landlord must return the application fee and any application deposit. Tex. Prop. Code section 92.3515.

Official source

Texas State Law Library — Can I remove an eviction from my record?

The official Texas source for eviction records and court access — confirm the current rule there before you file anything, and ask the court clerk where your case was heard about the exact form to use.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Find the adverse-action notice from your most recent denial — it names the screening company. Order that report today; it is free if you ask within 60 days of the notice. (2) Circle every error — wrong outcome, wrong person, a case that was sealed, anything older than 7 years — and dispute each one in writing with the court disposition attached. (3) Work the Texas route in the card above to seal, expunge, or limit the court record itself; where Texas has no such route, the FCRA steps are your cleanup — they reach every screening report even when the court file stays public. (4) Keep the final disposition and any sealing order with your rental papers, and bring copies when you apply.

Manufactured-home parks: your rights on the lot you rent

If you own a manufactured or mobile home but rent the lot under it, you live under two rulebooks at once — and the park rulebook is the one that decides whether you can stay. Most states give park tenancies their own law, with stronger protections than an apartment lease: longer notice before lot rent goes up, a short list of legal eviction grounds, long warning before a park closes, and rules for selling your home in place. Here is the pattern, then exactly where Texas stands.

Every state

Own the home, rent the lot

Your home is expensive to move — often $5,000 to $15,000 or more, and some homes cannot survive a move at all — so losing the lot can mean losing the home. That is why your lot lease and the park rules are the most important papers you own after the title. Read them before you buy a home in a park, get every promise about rent and rules in writing, and keep copies of every notice the park sends you. The protections in the Texas cards below come from state law; where your lease promises more, the lease promise counts too.

Every state

The four fights that matter

(1) Lot-rent increases — most park laws require long written notice, and a few states now cap the amount. (2) Eviction — in most park acts the owner may evict only for a short list of reasons (“good cause”), not for any reason or no reason. (3) Closure or conversion — closing a park usually requires many months of notice, and some states make the owner help pay to move your home. (4) Selling — you generally may sell your home in place, and in a growing number of states residents get a chance to buy the park itself when it is sold. Each fight has its own Texas card below.

Every state

If the park is sold or closing: the clock is short

Resident purchase windows are measured in days, not months — commonly 45 to 120 days from the owner’s notice — and relocation help usually has to be claimed, not waited for. If a sale or closure notice arrives: (1) note the date you received it and keep the envelope; (2) contact your homeowners’ association the same week — purchase rights usually run through an association, and if none exists, the notice itself often explains how to form one; (3) call the official source below and your state Attorney General’s office and ask what deadlines apply to you; (4) do not sign a buyout, waiver, or new lease at the door without advice — legal aid (see the Free Legal Help section) reviews these free.

In Texas

The law that governs your lot in Texas

Manufactured Home Tenancies, Texas Property Code Chapter 94. Under section 94.002 it applies only to a landlord who leases property in a manufactured home community (a parcel on which four or more lots are offered for lease for manufactured homes, section 94.001) to a tenant for situating a manufactured home on it; it does not apply where the tenant rents the manufactured home itself from the landlord.

In Texas

Lot-rent increases in Texas

Chapter 94 sets no standalone rent-increase notice period; a rent change comes with renewal. Under section 94.055 the landlord must give a notice to vacate or an offer of renewal, stating the proposed rent, not later than the 60th day before the lease expires (or, for a month-to-month lease, the 60th day before the intended termination), and the tenant must reject the new terms not later than the 30th day before expiration or the lease renews on the modified terms. A lease provision raising rent or fees during the term is void unless the tenant initialed it, under section 94.053(d).

In Texas

If the park closes or changes use in Texas

A landlord may choose not to renew a lease to change the community's land use only on notice given not later than the 180th day before the date the land use will change, under section 94.204; the disclosure required by section 94.051 likewise states at least 180 days' notice for a land-use change, versus 60 days for ordinary nonrenewal. Chapter 94 states no general relocation-assistance payment for a closure, although a landlord who asks a tenant to leave before the section 94.055 notice period ends must compensate the tenant in advance for relocation expenses, including moving and installing the home.

In Texas

Eviction from a park in Texas

Chapter 94 sets its own eviction grounds in Subchapter E. A landlord may terminate and evict for a violation of a lease provision, including a community rule incorporated in the lease (section 94.205), and for nonpayment where the amounts due equal at least one month's rent, the landlord gives written notice the payment is delinquent, and the tenant has not paid in full before the 10th day after receiving the notice (section 94.206).

In Texas

Selling your home — and a chance to buy the park — in Texas

The owner of a manufactured home may sell a home located on the leased premises if the purchaser is approved in writing by the landlord and the purchaser signs a lease agreement, under section 94.252. Unless the owner has agreed in writing, the landlord may not require the owner to contract with the landlord as agent or broker in selling the home, or require the owner to pay a commission or fee from the sale.

Official source

Texas Property Code Chapter 94, Manufactured Home Tenancies - statute text hosted by Cooke County, Texas

The official Texas source for manufactured-home park law — confirm the current rule there before you act on a deadline, and ask the agency or court that handles park complaints where to file one.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Find your lot lease and park rules and check them against the Texas cards above — a rule that contradicts state law is usually unenforceable. (2) Join your park’s homeowners’ association, or keep its contact information with your lease; it is the vehicle for purchase rights and the fastest warning system for sales and closures. (3) Calendar every deadline in any notice you receive the day it arrives — in park law, missing a short window (a purchase offer, a relocation claim, an objection) usually cannot be undone. (4) If the park breaks the rules above, complain in writing to the official source on this page and keep a copy; a paper trail is what enforcement runs on.

Rent going up? What the law says about increases

During a lease, the rent is locked

If you have a lease for a set term, the landlord generally cannot raise the rent until the term ends, unless the lease itself says otherwise. Increases land at renewal or in month-to-month tenancies — which is where the rules below matter most.

Notice is the rule almost everywhere

Most states require written notice before an increase takes effect — commonly 30 days, and 45, 60, or even 90 days in some states. Your state's rule, where an official source publishes one, is in the state card below. If the notice was short or only verbal, say so in writing and keep a copy.

Caps exist in only a few places

Statewide caps on how much rent can rise are rare — a few states set one, and a few cities have their own rules. Many states actually prohibit their cities from capping rents at all (the state card notes it where the statute says so). Where no cap and no state notice rule applies, the amount is set by your lease and the local market — which makes the notice and your renewal paperwork the things to watch.

If the increase follows a complaint, read the next block too

An increase that arrives right after you reported code violations, asked for repairs, or exercised a legal right may be illegal retaliation, not a normal increase. The landlord-retaliation block below covers the protections and the paper trail that proves timing.

What Texas law says

Under Texas Property Code § 91.001, either party may terminate a month-to-month tenancy by giving notice of termination. If rent is paid in monthly intervals, the tenancy terminates on the later of the date specified in the notice or one month after the day the notice is given, unless the parties agreed in writing to a different notice period.

Official sources

Texas Property Code Chapter 91 — Texas Constitution and Statutes (statutes.capitol.texas.gov)

Do this now: when an increase notice arrives, write down the date you received it and count the days against your state's notice rule. If the notice is short, tell the landlord in writing (email counts — save it) that the increase cannot take effect until proper notice is given, and keep paying the old rent until it is.

Evicted or hit with an increase right after you complained? That may be illegal retaliation

What counts as retaliation

In most states a landlord may not punish you for exercising a legal right — reporting health or code violations to an inspector, asking for repairs the law requires, complaining to a government agency, joining a tenant association, or withholding rent through the legal process where that is allowed. Punishment means things like filing for eviction, raising the rent, cutting services, or refusing to renew, done because you exercised the right.

Timing is the evidence

Many statutes presume retaliation when the landlord acts within a set window after the protected activity — commonly six months or a year (your state's rule, where an official source publishes one, is in the state card below). That is why dates matter more than arguments: a complaint filed in March and an eviction notice in April tells the story by itself.

Build the paper trail before you need it

Complain in writing and keep copies — email is fine, save the thread. Keep the inspector's report, photos with dates, rent receipts, and every notice the landlord sends. Write down dates of phone calls and what was said, the same day. If the landlord later claims a different reason, your dated paper is what a judge compares it against.

How it is used

Retaliation is usually raised as a defense in the eviction case itself — tell the judge, with your documents, what you did and when the landlord acted — and in many states it also supports your own claim for damages. Do not ignore an eviction filing because you believe it is retaliatory: the defense only works if you show up and raise it.

What Texas law says

Under Texas Property Code 92.331, a landlord may not retaliate because the tenant in good faith exercises a right or remedy granted by lease, ordinance, or statute, gives the landlord a notice to repair or exercises a remedy under the chapter, complains in good faith to a governmental entity, public utility, or civic or nonprofit agency about a building or housing code violation or utility problem, or establishes or participates in a tenant organization. Within six months after the tenant's action, the landlord may not retaliate by filing an eviction proceeding, depriving the tenant of use of the premises, decreasing services, increasing rent or terminating the lease, or engaging in bad faith in conduct that materially interferes with the tenant's rights under the lease.

Official sources

Texas Property Code 92.331 — Retaliation by Landlord (Texas Constitution and Statutes)

Do this now: make one dated list: when you complained or exercised the right, and when the landlord acted. Put every paper in date order in one folder. That folder is your defense — bring it to any hearing, and to the legal-aid office if you want help first.

What to do next:

  1. Put repair requests and complaints to your landlord in writing, and keep copies.
  2. If you get an eviction notice, don’t just move out — only a court can order an eviction.
  3. Get help fast if you’re facing eviction or unsafe conditions: free legal help in Texas.

Learn more about tenant rights in Texas →

Traffic Tickets & Court Fines in Texas

A traffic ticket is not just a bill. On most tickets you have three choices — pay it, fight it, or ask about a middle option like traffic school or a deferral — and the choice closes doors: paying is usually a guilty plea that puts a conviction on your driving record. This section lays out the fork, then Texas’s rules on points, courses, and what happens if a ticket is ignored.

Start here

Your three choices on almost every ticket

Pay it — in nearly every state, paying the fine is a guilty plea. The conviction goes on your driving record, points are added where the state uses them, and your insurance can go up. Fight it — plead not guilty by the date on the ticket and the court or tribunal named on it hears your case. Ask about the middle options first — traffic school, a deferral, or a reduced charge can keep a ticket off your record in many places, but they are usually gone once you pay. The one choice that always backfires is ignoring the ticket: expect a default conviction, added fees, and in most states a suspended license or a warrant.

In Texas

Points and your license in Texas

Texas has no point system anymore. DPS repealed the Driver Responsibility Program (points, surcharges, and the moving-violation surcharge) effective September 1, 2019, and DPS states the Department does not assign points for moving violations. DPS still records convictions on your driving record, and DPS can suspend for causes such as habitual violator findings based on repeated convictions (four or more moving violations in 12 months, or seven in 24 months), but there is no point total to watch.

Traffic school

Courses that can help in Texas

Texas keeps ticket dismissal by course: under Transportation Code Chapter 45A (recodified from Code of Criminal Procedure art. 45.0511), a judge can require a Texas Commission on Licensing and Regulation-approved Driving Safety Course and dismiss one charge if you are eligible — generally you hold a Texas license, the offense is a covered moving violation in a passenger vehicle, you have not taken a course for dismissal in the past 12 months, and you were not speeding 25 mph or more over the limit. You must ask the court by the appearance date on the citation; full fine/court costs usually still apply.

Your options

Fighting, deferring, or paying over time in Texas

To contest, appear or plead not guilty by the date on the citation in the justice court or municipal court named on it. Texas judges must offer payment alternatives when you cannot pay in full — Transportation Code Chapter 45A now requires an ability-to-pay inquiry and options such as a payment plan, community service, or waiver in appropriate cases (the Recodification of 2023 moved these rules to Transportation Code, Subchapter H). Miss the deadline or ignore it and you risk a separate failure-to-appear charge, a warrant, and a hold on renewing your license through the DPS OmniBase program until the court clears you.

Official source

Texas DPS – Driver Responsibility Program & moving violations FAQ

The official Texas page for points, suspensions, and ticket rules — the same source this section is drawn from.

Official ticket & points information →

What to do:

  1. Read the ticket closely. It names the court, the charge, and your deadline. That date controls everything — missing it is how a small ticket becomes a suspension.
  2. Decide before you pay. Paying is a guilty plea almost everywhere. Check the points, traffic school, and deferral options on this page first — most of them disappear once you pay.
  3. If you will fight it, plead not guilty by the deadline — in person, by mail, or online, as the ticket allows — and write down your hearing date.
  4. Ask about traffic school or a deferral before you enter any plea if Texas’s cards above list one. Ask the court clerk how to apply and what the deadline is.
  5. Can’t pay in full? Ask the clerk about a payment plan or ability-to-pay options before the due date. Courts help people who ask early; a missed deadline usually adds fees instead.
  6. Keep proof of everything — payment receipts, course certificates (send yours in wherever the state requires it), and court papers — then check your driving record a few weeks later to be sure the case closed the way you expected.

Fine and fee amounts are set by the court that issued your ticket, not by a statewide price list, so this section does not quote them — the amount on your ticket and the court’s own page are the figures that count. Commercial (CDL) licenses follow stricter federal rules on top of state ones: a ticket that is minor on a regular license can cost a professional driver their job. This is general information, not legal advice.

DUI / DWI in Texas

Driving under the influence carries some of the stiffest penalties in everyday law. Here's how Texas handles it.

Legal limit

Blood alcohol (BAC) limit

0.08; enhanced charge at BAC 0.15 or higher (Class A misdemeanor)

First offense

Typical penalties

72 hours to 180 days in jail, up to $2,000 fine, license suspension up to 1 year

Prior offenses

How far back DUIs count

Lifetime

Getting your license back in Texas after a DUI suspension

  • The fee: Fees stack by enforcement action: $125 for an Administrative License Revocation (ALR) suspension for failing or refusing a breath/blood test, and $100 each for a DWI conviction suspension, a Safety Responsibility (SR-22) suspension, and an Education Program suspension, so a driver with both an ALR and a conviction suspension pays $225.
  • Proof of insurance: Yes, after a DWI conviction. An SR-22 Financial Responsibility Insurance Certificate from an authorized insurer must be filed with DPS and maintained for two years from the date of conviction; an ALR suspension by itself is reinstated by paying the $125 fee.
  • Classes & interlock: Yes. DPS imposes a separate $100 Education Program suspension if proof that a required DWI or drug education program was completed is not provided within the time required by statute, so proof of completion must be submitted to DPS. Not a universal DPS condition of full reinstatement; interlock is court-ordered in some DWI cases. An occupational license issued after an ALR suspension or DWI conviction carries an interlock restriction whether or not the court order requires one.
  • Driving sooner: Yes, an occupational (essential need) driver license.
  • Where to apply: Reinstatement runs through Texas Department of Public Safety (DPS), Driver License Division. Get your personal requirements list first — it shows exactly what is still missing on your record — then finish each item and apply through that agency.
  • Do not drive yet: Driving on a suspended or revoked license is a separate crime — it can bring jail, more fines, and a longer suspension stacked on top of the DUI.

Official source: Texas license reinstatement

What to do next:

  1. Act fast — license suspension deadlines after a DUI/DWI arrest are short and separate from the criminal case.
  2. Don’t miss your court dates; a missed date can mean a warrant.
  3. Talk to a DUI lawyer before your first hearing: find one through the referral section.

Learn more about DUI / DWI in Texas →

Motorcycle lane splitting in Texas

Lane splitting is a motorcycle riding between lanes of traffic. Lane filtering is the narrower version — moving between stopped or very slow cars, usually to the front at a light. Only six states allow either one, and every one of them puts strict speed limits on it. Two motorcycles riding side by side in one lane is different — that is lane sharing, and it is legal almost everywhere.

Not allowed

The rule in Texas

Lane splitting and lane filtering are not allowed in Texas. Texas spelled it out in 2023: House Bill 4122 added an explicit ban (Texas Transportation Code § 545.0605), effective September 1, 2023 — a rider may not operate a motorcycle between lanes of traffic moving the same direction, pass another vehicle in the same lane, or ride more than two abreast in one lane. A rider who does it can be ticketed for improper passing or lane use.

If you drive a car

Know the numbers — they protect you

Most drivers don’t know the numbers — and the numbers are what protect you. In most states, fault in a crash is shared out between everyone involved, so a rider who splits outside the legal limits can carry a share of the blame — and that matters for insurance claims and tickets. A few states — Alabama, Maryland, North Carolina, and Virginia — use a stricter rule where even a small share of fault can block a claim entirely. And the strongest record of what happened is a dash cam: it shows the rider’s speed and position better than any argument afterward.

What to do:

  1. Riders: stay in your lane. Splitting or filtering can get you ticketed, and it can count against you if there is a crash.
  2. Drivers: before changing lanes, check your mirrors and your blind spot — a motorcycle is small and easy to miss, legal or not.
  3. Drivers: if a rider hits you while splitting illegally, save any dash cam footage and note the time and place — it is the clearest record of what the rider was doing.
  4. If there is a crash: get the police report and talk to a lawyer before giving a recorded statement to an insurer — fault is often shared, and the details matter.

This is general information, not legal advice. Traffic laws change, and the exact wording of your state’s statute controls.

Car accident injuries in Texas

A car crash can turn your life upside down in seconds. Here is the practical rundown for Texas: what to do first, how the insurance system works, and your deadline to act.

Right after the crash: call 911, get a police report, exchange information with the other driver, take photos, see a doctor even if you feel okay, don't admit fault, and notify your own insurer promptly.

Texas’s system: Texas is an at-fault state: the driver who caused the crash — and their insurance — is responsible for your injuries.

Deadline: In Texas you generally have 2 years from the crash to file an injury lawsuit. Miss it and you lose the right to sue.

Cost: Most car-accident lawyers work on contingency — free consultation, and they only get paid if you win, out of the settlement.

Official

Texas Department of Insurance

Help with auto insurance claims and complaints against insurers in Texas.

www.tdi.texas.gov →

Hit-and-run and uninsured drivers: who pays when they run, or carry nothing

The advice above assumes the other driver stopped and had insurance. When the driver runs — or stops with no insurance — the claim usually turns around and runs through your own policy, and some of the deadlines are short. Here is how the money paths work in Texas.

First — at the scene

The first hour decides the claim

Get safe and get medical help first. Then gather what you can before it disappears: the plate — even a partial one — make, model, color, and which way the car went; photos or video of your car, the scene, and any debris or paint left behind; names and numbers of witnesses; and nearby cameras (doorbells, dash cams, businesses) that may have caught it — that footage is often overwritten within days, so ask fast. Call the police and get a report made: after a hit-and-run, the report is not paperwork, it is evidence, and insurers usually require one. Staying matters just as much: leaving the scene of a crash is a crime in every state, and it gets far more serious when someone is hurt or killed.

Every state

The money paths, in the order to try them

(1) Your uninsured/underinsured motorist (UM/UIM) coverage — the coverage that stands in for a driver who ran or who carries nothing. Many policies require you to notify the insurer and the police very promptly after a hit-and-run — some windows are as short as 24 hours — and some states require proof the other car actually made contact, or independent evidence if it did not. Read the notice rules in your policy today, not next week. (2) Your collision coverage, which pays to repair your car no matter who was at fault, minus your deductible. (3) MedPay, no-fault PIP, or your health coverage for the medical bills. (4) The driver themselves, if the police identify them later — keep every bill and record in case that day comes.

In Texas

Uninsured motorist coverage in Texas

Texas insurance companies must offer you uninsured/underinsured motorist coverage, which pays if you are hit by a driver who has no insurance or not enough insurance, and it also pays if you are in a hit-and-run accident. If you don't want it, you must tell the company in writing. The Texas Department of Insurance says the same offer-and-written-rejection rule applies to personal injury protection (PIP) coverage.

Another path — crime-victim compensation

Texas’s victim compensation program

Texas's Crime Victims' Compensation Program treats hit-and-run crashes as covered vehicular crimes — the Attorney General's program materials list 'failure to stop and render aid' among them — so victims can apply for help with crash-related costs.

Official source

Texas Department of Insurance — Auto insurance guide

The official Texas source behind the coverage rules above — check your own policy against it.

Official uninsured-motorist information →

Do this now: (1) File the police report and write down the report number. (2) Call your own insurer and open the UM claim — ask exactly what your policy requires after a hit-and-run (report deadline, proof of contact) and get the answer in writing. (3) Photograph everything and save every estimate, bill, and pay stub. (4) Ask nearby homes and businesses for camera footage this week, before it is overwritten. (5) If the driver is identified later, tell your insurer — it changes who owes, and your company may go after the driver for what it paid you.

Slip and fall: hurt on someone else's property

What the owner owed you depends on why you were there

Premises liability is the other half of injury law: instead of a driver, the careless party is the store, landlord, or homeowner whose property hurt you. In general terms, owners owe the most care to people invited in — customers, guests, delivery drivers: they must fix or warn about dangers they know of, or should have found by looking. People allowed on the property for their own purposes get warnings about known hidden dangers. Even trespassers are owed a basic duty not to be harmed by traps, and children get extra protection around things like unfenced pools. The fight in almost every case is the same: was the danger there long enough that the owner should have known, and were you paying reasonable attention?

Document the condition before it disappears

A spill gets mopped, ice melts, a broken step gets fixed — your evidence has a shelf life of hours. Photograph the exact spot and what caused the fall, from several angles, before you leave if you can. Report it to the manager or owner and get a copy of the incident report (or write down who took it). Keep the shoes and clothes you wore, unwashed. Get names of anyone who saw it. Then get medical care the same day: the records connect the injury to the fall, and "I felt fine at first" is the insurer's favorite sentence.

The fault rule and the filing deadline in Texas

Texas law states that a claimant may not recover damages if his percentage of responsibility is greater than 50 percent. A person must bring suit for personal injury not later than two years after the day the cause of action accrues.

Texas Civil Practice and Remedies Code Chapter 33 — Proportionate Responsibility

The fault rule that can shrink — or erase — your claim

Most states use comparative fault: your recovery is reduced by your share of blame, and in many states you recover nothing once your share passes half. A few states still use the old contributory-negligence rule, where being even slightly at fault can bar recovery entirely — Maryland's, North Carolina's, and Virginia's official sources below confirm their courts still apply it, which is why your state's line matters so much. Either way, expect the property's insurer to argue you weren't watching where you were going; your photos and the incident report are your answer.

Fell on government property? The clock is much shorter

Falls in a public building, on a public sidewalk, or in a park usually require a formal written notice of claim to the government body within a short window — often a few months — before any lawsuit is possible, on top of the deadline in your state's line. If the property was public, treat the notice as the first task, not the last.

Who pays, and settling smart

The claim normally runs against the property owner's liability insurance (a homeowner's policy for a house, a commercial policy for a business). Do not sign a release or accept a quick settlement before your treatment is finished and you know whether the injury will linger — a release ends the claim forever, including problems that show up later. Slip-and-fall claims within your state's small-claims limit (see the Small Claims section) can be filed without a lawyer; bigger injuries are usually handled on contingency, where the lawyer is paid from the recovery.

Do this now: photograph the spot and the hazard today, make the incident report happen, and get checked by a doctor. Then read your state's fault rule and deadline above — and if the property was government-owned, find the notice-of-claim form this week.

Whistleblower protection in Texas

Fired, demoted, or punished for reporting something illegal or unsafe at work? The law may protect you — but deadlines can be as short as 30 days, so don’t wait.

Workplace safety: Federal law protects workers who report safety or health hazards. Complaints go to OSHA.

Financial fraud: The SEC runs a whistleblower program for securities fraud, with possible awards. The IRS has one for major tax fraud too.

Texas: Texas workers have the right to complain about job discrimination without punishment — the TWC Civil Rights Division says an employer can't fire, punish, or harass you for reporting discrimination or helping someone else report it.

Official

Bail & bail bondsmen in Texas

Someone you love is sitting in jail and you need them out. Here's how bail works in Texas.

Two ways to post bail: pay the full amount to the court yourself (you get it back when the case ends), or hire a bail bondsman — they post it for a fee, usually around 10% of the bail amount, which you do not get back.

Texas specifics: Texas licenses bail bond agents county by county through local Bail Bond Boards, not the state — check with the board in the county where the bond will be posted.

Protect yourself: get every fee in writing before paying, and never hand over cash without a receipt and paperwork.

Gun laws in Texas

Gun laws vary a lot from state to state — what's perfectly legal in one state can be a crime in another. Here's the big picture for this state. Laws change often, so double-check anything before you act.

Concealed carry

Carrying a concealed handgun

Permitless carry at 21+ (HB 1927, effective Sept 2021); shall-issue License to Carry still available

Open carry

Carrying openly

Generally allowed — handguns must be carried in a holster

Buying a gun

Background checks & waiting periods

Private sales: Not required on private sales No waiting period.

Red flag law

Extreme risk protection

No red flag law on the books here.

Renewing your License to Carry a Handgun (LTC)

  • How long it lasts: 5 years for a renewed license (the original license runs about 4 years — it expires on your birthday after the fourth anniversary of issuance).
  • When to renew: DPS guidance is to apply about 6 months before expiration; by statute you may still renew up to the first anniversary (1 year) after the expiration date (Gov't Code § 411.185, as amended by HB 668 in 2025). DPS must mail you a renewal notice at least 60 days before expiration.
  • How to renew: Renew online through the DPS licensing portal (RSD Connect, the new License to Carry platform) or by mail with the renewal application. DPS reuses your fingerprints and photo on file and will notify you if new fingerprints are needed.
  • Training to renew: No — Texas requires no continuing education or refresher training to renew an LTC.
  • Fee: $40 standard renewal (nonrefundable). Discounts: seniors 60+ pay $35, veterans $25, active-duty military $0.
  • If it expires: NONE for carrying. DPS states there is no grace period or extension on an expired LTC, even if your renewal application has already been submitted — you must wait for the new license to receive LTC benefits. If the license has been expired more than 1 year, you must start over as a new applicant (training, fingerprints, full process).

Don't miss: Texas has permitless carry for most adults 21+, so the LTC mainly matters for reciprocity, purchase conveniences, and places open only to license holders. Changing your address on your driver license does NOT update your LTC — report LTC address changes to DPS separately.

Official source: Texas's renewal page →

Selling or giving a gun in Texas — private transfers

  • Background check: No. The Texas State Law Library states plainly: 'Private sellers are not required by federal law or Texas law to do a background check before selling a firearm.' A private seller may voluntarily ask a licensed dealer to facilitate the sale and run a check, but dealers are not required to offer that service.
  • Buyer permit: No. Texas requires no permit, license, or registration to buy or own a firearm, and none is needed for a private transfer.
  • What it covers: Private-sale rules are the same for all firearms — handguns, rifles, and shotguns alike. (The federal rule that dealers may not sell handguns to anyone under 21 applies only to dealer sales, not private ones.).
  • Family transfers: No family exemption exists or is needed: no background check, permit, or paperwork requirement applies to any private transfer, family or not.
  • Paperwork & records: None. Per the Texas State Law Library, neither federal law nor Texas law requires a private seller to keep a record of the sale, and Texas maintains no firearm registry, so there is no registration to transfer.
  • Buyer age: 18 for any firearm in a private sale. Texas Penal Code 46.06 makes it an offense to knowingly sell a firearm to a child younger than 18; the statute gives an affirmative defense where a parent or legal guardian gave written permission for the sale (or effective consent to a gift).

Don't miss: Traps in Texas Penal Code 46.06: it is also an offense to knowingly sell a firearm to a person convicted of a felony before the fifth anniversary of the later of release from confinement or from supervision/parole, to an intoxicated person…

Across state lines: If the buyer or recipient lives in another state, federal law requires the transfer to go through a licensed dealer in their state — there is no family or friend exception for a living transfer, and handing the gun over directly across state lines is a federal crime. The one exception is a genuine inheritance. Special NFA items (suppressors, short-barreled rifles and the like) need ATF approval before any transfer, even inside one state.

Official source: Texas's transfer law →

What to do next:

  1. Before you buy or carry, confirm the permit rules above against the official source — laws change often.
  2. If you’re worried about someone with access to a firearm, call 911 in an emergency, or ask the court about an extreme-risk protection order.

Learn more about gun laws in Texas →

Filming the police: your rights in Texas

Federal appeals courts across the country have recognized a First Amendment right to photograph and record police officers performing their official duties in public. The U.S. Supreme Court has not ruled on it directly. The right does not protect interfering with officers, trespassing, or breaking other laws while you record.

Audio recording

One-party consent

If you are part of a conversation, you may record it — including with police. (Tex. Penal Code § 16.02)

Distance rules

Buffer-zone law — in effect

none in effect (a 2015 proposal, HB 2918, to criminalize filming police within 25 feet was dropped and never enacted)

Turner v. Driver, 848 F.3d 678 (5th Cir. 2017): the First Amendment protects recording police in public, subject to reasonable time, place, and manner limits. Officers can still order bystanders back for safety under the interference-with-public-duties law (Tex. Penal Code § 38.15).

What to do next:

  1. Keep a safe distance and don’t interfere — that’s the line the law draws.
  2. Know your state’s audio-recording rule stated above before you hit record.
  3. If you believe your rights were violated, write down exactly what happened and report it: how to report a civil rights violation.

Learn more about filming the police in Texas →

If a police officer, employer, landlord, or other authority treats you worse because of your race, sex, disability, religion, or another protected trait, that’s a civil rights violation — and you can report it. Deadlines are short: many complaints must be filed within 180 days to a year of what happened, so don’t wait. Read the full Constitution here.

Defamation in Texas

Someone posted or said something false about you? In Texas, defamation means a false statement of fact, shared with others, that harmed your reputation. Truth is a complete defense, and pure opinions (“I think he’s a jerk”) generally don’t count.

Deadline to sue: 1 year from when the statement was made (single 1-year limit for libel and slander). Miss it and you lose the right to file.

Anti-SLAPP: Texas has an anti-SLAPP law — it lets people get baseless lawsuits over speech thrown out quickly, and the person who filed can be ordered to pay the other side’s legal costs.

What to do next:

  1. Save everything. Screenshots with dates, links to the posts, and names of anyone who saw them. Don’t rely on the post staying up.
  2. Don’t fire back publicly. Responding online can hurt your case later.
  3. Talk to a lawyer who handles defamation. Many offer a free first consultation or take strong cases on contingency — you don’t pay unless you win.

Find a lawyer through the referral section on this page →

Defamation cases are hard to win and often expensive — most lawyers in this area work on contingency or offer a free first consult, so talk to one before filing.

Nonconsensual intimate images & AI deepfakes in Texas

If someone shares, or threatens to share, an intimate image of you without your consent — a real photo or video, or an AI-generated fake that looks real — that is image-based sexual abuse, sometimes called “revenge porn.” It is not your fault, and you do not have to handle it alone. The law in this area is moving fast: most states now have a law against nonconsensual sharing, and a federal law now requires covered platforms to act quickly on removal requests. Work the steps below in order — preserve evidence first, then report and request removal, then consider criminal and civil routes — and get support early, because the process can take weeks.

The law now

State laws and the federal TAKE IT DOWN Act

Most states criminalize knowingly sharing an intimate image without consent, and many also allow the person depicted to sue for damages and a court order; some states also specifically cover AI-generated sexual deepfakes, and others reach them through the general nonconsensual-image law. The exact elements, penalties, and deadlines vary sharply by state, and this area changes fast, so this page does not print a single Texas statute or penalty — the Cyber Civil Rights Initiative (CCRI) Safety Center below maintains a list of state laws, and a lawyer or legal-aid office can confirm the current Texas law for your situation. Federally, the TAKE IT DOWN Act (signed May 19, 2025) criminalizes knowingly publishing nonconsensual intimate images, including AI “digital forgeries,” and threats to publish them, and requires covered platforms to remove a qualifying image as soon as possible and within 48 hours of a valid removal request, plus make reasonable efforts to remove known identical copies. FTC enforcement of the platform-removal duty began May 19, 2026; if a platform ignores a valid request, you can report it at TakeItDown.ftc.gov.

Cyber Civil Rights Initiative (CCRI) Safety Center — state laws, removal guides → TakeItDown.ftc.gov — report a platform that ignored a removal request →
Evidence

Preserve evidence before you report — without spreading the image

Do not delete the messages, posts, or threats, and do not forward the image to friends to “prove” anything — every extra copy makes removal harder and can create legal risk of its own. Instead, take screenshots showing the URL, the username, the date and time, and the threatening or posting messages; save the original messages and emails; note where else the image appears; and keep a dated log of everything you report and every response. If you are being blackmailed for money or more images (sextortion), do not pay and do not send more images — payment rarely stops the threats, and the evidence you save is what police and platforms need. If the image involves someone who was under 18 when it was taken, report it to NCMEC’s CyberTipline (below) rather than handling it yourself: sharing or possessing such an image is itself illegal, even if your intent is to report it.

NCMEC CyberTipline — report sexual exploitation of minors →
Official resource

Getting images taken down: platforms, hash tools, and search engines

Work all three levels. Platform: report the post through the platform’s own reporting tool and, for a covered platform, send a written TAKE IT DOWN removal request (your signature, information identifying the image and where to find it, a brief good-faith statement that it is nonconsensual, and your contact information) — the 48-hour clock runs from a valid request. Hash tools: for images of you taken when you were under 18, NCMEC’s Take It Down creates a digital fingerprint (hash) on your device — the image itself never leaves your device — and participating platforms use the hash to detect and remove copies. For adults (18+), StopNCII.org works the same way: it hashes the image on your device and shares only the hash with participating platforms. Neither tool covers AI-generated fakes you do not possess, edited copies, or platforms that do not participate, so keep reporting directly too. Search engines: use Google’s and Bing’s removal forms for nonconsensual explicit content to remove links from search results — that does not erase the original page, so still pursue the platform itself.

Take It Down (NCMEC) — for images taken when you were under 18 → StopNCII.org — for adults (18+) →
Criminal vs civil

Criminal report, civil lawsuit, and court orders — different tools, different jobs

A criminal report asks the state to prosecute the person who shared or threatened to share the image; bring your preserved evidence to local police or the sheriff, ask for a written report number, and keep a copy. A civil lawsuit is yours to bring: it can seek money damages and, importantly, a court order (injunction) requiring removal and forbidding further sharing, which is sometimes the fastest way to reach a site that ignores reports. A protective order may also be available if the person is a current or former partner or is harassing you — see the Domestic abuse section on this page. Criminal and civil routes can run at the same time, but they have different deadlines and proof standards, so talk to a lawyer or legal-aid office (see the Free legal help section on this page) before choosing, and before signing any settlement that would let the image stay up. CCRI’s attorney directory can help you find a lawyer experienced in these cases.

Official resource

Where to get help right now

You do not have to do this alone. The CCRI Image Abuse Helpline, 1-844-878-2274, offers free help to adults dealing with image-based sexual abuse, including safety planning, takedown guidance, and referrals to attorneys. For images involving a minor, NCMEC’s helpline is 1-800-843-5678. If you are in immediate danger, call 911; if the abuse is part of domestic violence, the National Domestic Violence Hotline is 1-800-799-7233 (see the Domestic abuse section on this page). Beware of paid “reputation” or “removal” companies that charge large fees for what the free tools above do — check any service against CCRI’s guidance before paying anything.

CCRI Safety Center & Image Abuse Helpline (1-844-878-2274) →

What to do:

  1. Preserve it. Screenshot URLs, usernames, dates, and threats; save originals; keep a dated log. Do not forward the image.
  2. Being blackmailed? Do not pay, do not send more images, and save the threats for police and the platforms.
  3. Report and request removal. Use the platform’s tool plus a written TAKE IT DOWN request; covered platforms must act within 48 hours of a valid request.
  4. Use a hash tool. Take It Down (NCMEC) for images taken under 18; StopNCII.org for adults 18+ — the image never leaves your device.
  5. Also remove search links. File with Google/Bing removal forms; that helps even while the original page is pursued.
  6. Consider police and a lawyer. File a criminal report (keep the report number) and ask legal aid or CCRI’s attorney directory about a civil order for removal.
  7. Get support. Call the CCRI Image Abuse Helpline at 1-844-878-2274, or NCMEC at 1-800-843-5678 for a minor.

This page does not print a Texas statute, penalty, or filing deadline for nonconsensual-image or deepfake cases because those vary by state and change fast — the CCRI Safety Center’s state-law list and a Texas lawyer or legal-aid office are the exact sources for your case. If you are in danger, call 911. This is general information, not legal advice.

Domestic abuse & protection orders in Texas

If someone is hurting you or your kids, or threatening to, Texas courts can order them to stay away. It’s called a Protective Order.

How to get one: go to your county courthouse and ask for the protection-order paperwork — filing is free. A judge reviews it, usually the same day, and can grant a temporary order immediately. A hearing is scheduled within a couple of weeks where both sides can speak.

If you are in danger right now, call 911. For free confidential help anytime, call the National Domestic Violence Hotline at 1-800-799-7233 or text START to 88788.

If someone may be watching what you do on this phone or computer, use a safer device if you can — a friend’s phone, a work or library computer. The Quick exit button at the top of this page leaves this site fast, but it cannot erase your browsing history.

State coalition

Texas domestic violence coalition

Local shelters, legal help, and safety planning across Texas.

tcfv.org →

Harassed or stalked by someone who is not family? There is an order for that too

Not family? You are not out of options

The orders described above in this section mostly cover abuse by a partner or family member. For a neighbor, coworker, acquaintance, or stranger, most states have a second kind of order — called a civil harassment order, stalking protective order, antiharassment order, or something similar (your state's name is in the state card below). It can order the person to stop contacting you and stay away from you, your home, and your workplace.

What you usually have to show

A pattern, not one bad day: repeated unwanted contact, following, watching, threats, or showing up where you are — typically two or more incidents — that would make a reasonable person afraid or seriously distressed. A judge can often issue a temporary order the same day you file, with a hearing within a few weeks where both sides are heard before a longer order is granted.

Your evidence is your phone and your notebook

Start a log now: dates, times, places, what happened, and any witnesses. Save texts, messages, voicemails, and photos — do not delete anything, and do not reply in anger. Screenshots with visible dates help; so does a police report, even if no arrest was made. Bring copies of everything to the hearing.

Filing, and what it costs

You file at the courthouse your state's source points to — clerks have the forms and in many courts a victim advocate or self-help center will help you complete them. Many states charge no filing fee for stalking or sexual-assault-based orders; where a fee applies, ask the clerk about a fee waiver (the fee-waiver block in the Free Legal Help section explains those). After the judge signs, law enforcement serves the order on the other person — you do not deliver it yourself.

In Texas

The Texas State Law Library says Texas law allows a protective order in stalking cases under Chapter 7B of the Code of Criminal Procedure, separate from family violence cases under the Family Code. A victim of stalking may apply for a protective order for themselves or a child, a person can also apply if there is a threat of violence, and there are no court fees or costs to apply.

Official sources

Texas State Law Library — Protective Orders: General Information

Do this now: if you are in immediate danger, call 911. Otherwise, start the dated log today, save every message, and take your notes to the clerk your state card points to — ask for the order named there and whether a temporary order can be issued the same day.

Victim of a violent crime? Your state may help pay the costs

A fund exists in every state

Every state runs a crime victim compensation program, supported by federal VOCA money, that reimburses victims of violent crime for out-of-pocket costs no one else pays: medical and dental care, mental-health counseling, lost wages, and funeral and burial costs, sometimes also crime-scene cleanup or relocation. It is not a lawsuit, and it does not require a conviction — or even an arrest.

Two deadlines, not one

Most programs have two clocks: the crime must be reported to police quickly — often within 48 or 72 hours, in some states within days or months — and the claim itself must be filed within a window that ranges from a few months to several years depending on the state. Your state's two numbers are in the state card below. Both clocks often have good-cause exceptions, so a late report is a reason to explain, not a reason to give up.

What it does not pay

Property loss and damage are generally not covered, and neither is pain and suffering. The program is also the payer of last resort: insurance and other benefits pay first, and the program covers what is left, up to a state cap. Keep every bill, receipt, and pay stub — the program reimburses documented costs.

Cooperation is required; a conviction is not

You must reasonably cooperate with law enforcement and prosecution, and the crime must be a qualifying violent crime in the state where it happened (you apply to that state, even if you live elsewhere). Victim advocates — usually at the prosecutor's office or through the program itself — will help with the application for free.

In Texas: deadlines and coverage

The application should be received within three years of the crime, with special exceptions made based on age, circumstances, or good cause, and the crime must be reported to law enforcement. The Crime Victims' Compensation Program covers crime-related costs such as medical expenses, mental health care, lost wages, relocation, and funeral expenses, and it is the last source of payment by law.

Official sources

Texas Attorney General — Crime Victim Services General Information

Do this now: check the two deadlines in your state card. If the crime has not been reported, report it. Then file the application even if you are not sure you qualify — filing preserves your rights, and the program or a victim advocate will tell you what documentation to add.

Abortion laws in Texas

Abortion is banned in Texas at all stages of pregnancy, with an exception when a licensed physician determines in reasonable medical judgment that the pregnant person has a life-threatening physical condition placing them at risk of death or serious risk of substantial impairment of a major bodily function — a 2025 law (SB 31, the Life of the Mother Act, effective June 20, 2025) clarified that the risk does not have to be imminent.

These laws change fast — court rulings and new legislation can shift the rules with little notice. Always confirm the current law through the official source below before making decisions.

Questions? The National Abortion Federation hotline: 1-800-772-9100.

Juvenile law & school-to-court issues in Texas

Juvenile court is not adult court with smaller defendants. It was built around rehabilitation rather than punishment, it uses different words, different hearings, and usually no jury, and a young person’s record is handled very differently from an adult’s. That difference is good news, but it is not automatic: records are not always sealed on their own, school discipline can still lead to court, and some serious cases can be moved to adult court. This section covers the map; the Child safety and Special education sections on this page cover abuse reporting and school disability rights, and this section does not repeat them.

How it differs

How juvenile court differs from adult court

In juvenile court, a young person is usually “taken into custody” rather than arrested, charged by petition rather than indictment, and “adjudicated delinquent” rather than convicted — the judge, not a jury, usually decides the case, in a hearing that is often closed to the public. The court’s dispositions lean toward supervision, counseling, community service, restitution, and placement in a juvenile facility rather than adult prison, and parents or guardians are usually required to attend and can be ordered to participate. Young people still have core rights — to notice of the charges, to a lawyer, to confront witnesses, and against self-incrimination — so if your child is questioned by police or school resource officers, the safest first move is to ask for a lawyer before answering questions, and to say so calmly and clearly. Age limits, the right to a jury, and the rules for detention all vary by state, so confirm the Texas rules with the juvenile court or a juvenile-defense lawyer rather than assuming another state’s rules apply.

Office of Juvenile Justice and Delinquency Prevention (OJJDP) — juvenile justice information →
Common matters

Common juvenile matters: delinquency, truancy, and status offenses

Delinquency means an act that would be a crime if an adult did it — theft, assault, drug offenses, vandalism. Status offenses are acts that are only offenses because of age: running away, truancy (unexcused school absence), curfew violations, and underage drinking in some states. Truancy and other status offenses usually start with school and family interventions, not jail, but repeated truancy can reach court, where the judge can order counseling, supervision, or services for the family — so answer school letters early, keep records of illness and appointments, and ask the school in writing what support it has offered before the case reaches a judge. Emancipation is a separate court process by which some minors can be declared legally independent adults before 18; it generally requires showing the young person can support themselves and manage their own affairs, it is not available in every state, and it ends parental support obligations in ways that should never be rushed into without legal advice.

School to court

When school discipline leads to court involvement

School discipline and court are connected in both directions: a suspension or expulsion hearing is a school process with its own notice and hearing rights (see the Special education section on this page for students with disabilities), while fights, threats, drug possession, or property damage at school can also be referred to police and juvenile court. If your child faces both, handle both tracks: respond to the school in writing by its deadline, keep every notice, and do not let the school process substitute for legal advice about the court case — statements made to a principal can be used later. Ask the school what alternatives it considered (restorative practices, counseling, behavior plans) and whether the referral to police was required or discretionary; that record matters to a juvenile judge deciding whether the case belongs in court at all. If your child has a disability, tell the school and the court in writing: federal disability law can change what discipline is lawful.

Records

Juvenile records: sealing and expungement are not automatic

A juvenile record is not one file: police reports, court files, probation records, and school records may each be held separately, and sealing one does not always seal the others. Sealing generally hides the record from the public and most employers, while law enforcement and courts may still see it; expungement generally erases or destroys it, where the state offers it. In many states neither happens automatically at 18 — someone must apply to the juvenile court after the case closes and any waiting period passes, and serious offenses or later adult convictions can block eligibility. The practical steps: ask the juvenile court clerk in the county where the case was heard for the sealing/expungement forms and the waiting period, confirm which agencies hold records, and get the order in writing; then check that employers’ background checks actually come back clean. The Clearing your criminal record section on this page covers adult records; juvenile sealing uses a different process and different forms, so do not file adult forms for a juvenile case.

Transferred cases

When a juvenile case can move to adult court

For the most serious offenses, and for older teens, Texas law may allow a prosecutor to ask a judge to transfer (waive) the case to adult court, or may require it for certain charges. Transfer is the highest-stakes decision in juvenile law: an adult conviction carries adult penalties and an adult record that is far harder to clear. If transfer is raised, get a juvenile-defense lawyer immediately, attend every hearing, and gather school records, counseling records, and evidence of rehabilitation — judges deciding transfer weigh the seriousness of the offense, the young person’s history, and whether juvenile services can still help. Do not discuss the facts of the case with police, school staff, or on social media while transfer is possible.

Emancipation in Texas — becoming a legal adult before 18

  • The process: Yes. Texas Family Code Chapter 31 (sections 31.001-31.008) provides for removal of the disabilities of minority, for limited or general purposes.
  • Age & where to file: 17 years of age, or at least 16 years of age and living separate and apart from the minor's parents, managing conservator, or guardian, under Family Code 31.001(a). The petitioner files the petition in the county in which the petitioner resides, under Family Code 31.003. The minor may file suit in his or her own name and need not be represented by next friend, under 31.001(b).
  • What the judge weighs: The minor must be a Texas resident, meet the age rule in 31.001(a), and be self-supporting and managing his or her own financial affairs. Under 31.005 the court may remove the disabilities only if it finds removal is in the best interest of the petitioner, and the order must state the limited or general purposes.
  • What changes: Under Family Code 31.006, a minor whose disabilities are removed for general purposes has the capacity of an adult, including the capacity to contract, except for specific constitutional and statutory age requirements. Educational rights accorded to a parent, including education decisions, transfer to the minor except as provided by federal law.

Official source: Texas emancipation law

What to do:

  1. Questioned or taken into custody? Ask for a lawyer before answering questions; parents should attend every hearing.
  2. Truancy or status offense? Answer school letters early, keep illness/appointment records, and ask in writing what support the school offered.
  3. School discipline plus a court referral? Handle both tracks in writing; do not let school statements substitute for legal advice.
  4. Emancipation? Get legal advice first — it is not available everywhere and it ends parental support duties.
  5. Record still showing? Ask the juvenile court clerk for sealing/expungement forms and waiting periods; sealing one file may not seal them all.
  6. Transfer to adult court raised? Get a juvenile-defense lawyer at once and gather school and counseling records.

This page does not print a single Texas age limit, detention rule, truancy threshold, emancipation rule, or sealing waiting period because all of them vary by state — the juvenile court in the county where the case is heard, the Free legal help section on this page, and OJJDP’s information above are the exact sources for your case. This is general information, not legal advice.

Special education & student rights in Texas

If your child has a disability that affects learning, two federal laws protect them at school. The Individuals with Disabilities Education Act (IDEA) gives eligible children an Individualized Education Program (IEP) — specially designed instruction, written down, with goals the school must work toward. Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act covers children who have a disability that substantially limits a major life activity but do not need specially designed instruction — they get a 504 plan of accommodations instead, like extra time, preferential seating, or a quiet room for tests. You do not have to know which one fits before you ask: ask for an evaluation in writing, and the school must consider it.

IEP

How an IEP works — and the federal clocks

Ask for an evaluation in writing, addressed to the principal or the district’s special education director, and keep a copy with the date. The school must get your written consent before it evaluates. Under the federal default, the evaluation is completed within 60 days of your consent — your state may set its own timeline, so ask what Texas’s is — and once your child is found eligible, the school must hold the IEP meeting within 30 days. The IEP is reviewed at least once a year, and your child must be reevaluated at least every 3 years. You are a member of the IEP team: you can bring someone with you, you can disagree, and you can ask for another meeting at any time.

504 plan

If an IEP is not the fit: a Section 504 plan

A 504 plan does not come with specially designed instruction, but it does require the school to remove barriers: accommodations in the regular classroom, changes to how work or tests are given, and access to school activities. Ask the school who its Section 504 coordinator is and put your request in writing, with a note from your child’s doctor if you have one. If the school refuses an evaluation or a plan, ask for that refusal in writing — a written refusal (called prior written notice) must explain why, and it starts your right to challenge it.

Official resource

Texas’s Parent Training and Information Center (PTI) — free help

Every state has at least one federally funded Parent Training and Information Center, staffed by trained parents of children with disabilities. It is free, and it is the best first call in Texas: it explains your rights in plain language, helps you prepare for IEP meetings, walks you through what to do when you disagree with the school, and gives you Texas’s current state-complaint form and address. Find the center that serves your area through the national Parent Center Hub finder — click your state.

Find Texas’s Parent Center (PTI) →
Discipline

School discipline: extra protections apply

A student with an IEP or 504 plan cannot simply be suspended or expelled like any other student. If the school wants a disciplinary change of placement lasting more than 10 school days in a row, the team — including you — must hold a manifestation determination review: was the behavior caused by, or directly related to, the disability, or did the school fail to carry out the IEP? If the answer to either is yes, the student generally must return to the placement, unless the case involves weapons, drugs, or serious bodily injury, which have their own rules. If your child is suspended, ask in writing on day one whether a manifestation review is required, and do not let the days run while you wait.

Disagreements

If you and the school disagree

Work up the ladder in this order, and keep everything in writing: first, ask the IEP team to meet again and say plainly what you want changed. Second, ask about mediation — a free, voluntary meeting with a neutral mediator that your state provides. Third, file a state complaint with Texas’s department of education (your PTI, linked above, gives you the current form and address) if the school broke a rule or missed a deadline. Fourth, a due process hearing, where a hearing officer decides — this is the formal route, and deadlines apply, so ask your PTI or free legal help right away rather than waiting. You can also file a complaint with the U.S. Department of Education’s Office for Civil Rights if you believe the school discriminated because of disability.

What to do:

  1. Put every request in writing — evaluation, meeting, change to the plan — and keep a dated copy. Spoken requests are easy for a school to lose.
  2. Call your PTI before the meeting, not after. It is free, and a short prep call changes how the meeting goes.
  3. Do not sign an IEP you disagree with on the spot. You can take it home, ask the PTI to review it, and reply in writing about what you accept and what you dispute.
  4. Track the clocks: 60 days (federal default) for the evaluation, 30 days to the IEP meeting after eligibility, a review every year, a reevaluation every 3 years — and ask what Texas’s own timelines are, since states may differ.
  5. If discipline starts, act on day one: ask in writing whether a manifestation determination review is required, and keep a log of every school day missed.

This page does not list one PTI phone number or one state-complaint form for Texas because centers, numbers, and forms change — the national finder above always points to the current one. This is general information, not legal advice. IDEA timelines and state rules change, and your state’s current rules and your child’s written plan control.

Bullying in Texas

Every state has an anti-bullying law, and every school must have a plan. If your child is being bullied, document what happens and report it in writing to the school — and know what your state’s law requires.
