Section 8 & Public Housing in Texas

If rent eats most of your income, two federal programs can help: Housing Choice Vouchers — still widely called Section 8 — and public housing. Both are run by a local agency called a public housing agency (PHA), not by a national office, and both have waiting lists. This section explains how the programs work, how Texas runs them, and the exact steps to get on a list — and keep your place on it.

Federal program — all 50 states

How Section 8 and public housing work

With a voucher, you find a home from a private landlord; you generally pay about 30% of your adjusted income toward rent, and the housing agency pays the rest, up to a local limit it sets. Public housing is different: the home itself is owned by the housing agency, and rent is also based on your income. Eligibility for both turns on income — generally at or below 50% of the median income for your area, with most new vouchers reserved for households at or below 30%. A voucher can also move with you if you relocate to another housing agency’s area — ask about portability before you move. And if an agency denies you assistance, federal rules give you the right to an informal hearing to challenge the decision. General questions? HUD’s housing agency information line is (800) 955-2232.

In Texas

Finding your housing agency in Texas

Texas has no single statewide application. The Texas Department of Housing and Community Affairs (TDHCA) itself acts as a public housing authority and runs the Section 8 Housing Choice Voucher program in its 34-county service area, and many other local public housing authorities each run the program in their own specific area. Find the authority that covers the county or city where you want to live — through TDHCA or HUD's PHA list — and apply to that authority when its wait list is open; TDHCA does not oversee the other PHAs.

TDHCA also runs special-purpose vouchers, including Project Access and Mainstream vouchers that help low-income people with disabilities move from institutions into housing in the community. TDHCA's PHA Portal lets its applicants and tenants check their wait list status and assistance information online after registering.

Official source

Texas Dept. of Housing & Community Affairs — Housing Choice Voucher / Section 8 Housing

The official Texas starting point for vouchers, public housing, and waiting lists.

Official housing information →

What to do:

  1. Find your housing agency. Use the Texas card and official link above. Many areas have more than one agency — a city and a county agency, for example — and you may apply to each one whose list is open.
  2. Check whether its waiting list is open. Lists open and close, sometimes for years, often with little warning. Applying is always free — any website or person charging you to apply or to join a waiting list is running a scam.
  3. Apply, and keep proof. Save the confirmation or receipt, and write down how the agency says it will contact you — most contact is by mail or through an online portal.
  4. Keep your contact information current. Agencies remove people whose mail comes back or who miss a check-in letter. Tell the agency every time your address, phone, or email changes, and answer every letter it sends.
  5. If you are denied, ask for an informal hearing in writing, right away. The denial letter must explain how; deadlines are short. Bring documents that answer the reason given — a wrong record or a missing paper is often fixable.
  6. Already have a voucher and need to move? Ask your current agency about portability before you give notice or sign anything — the move has its own paperwork and deadlines.

Waiting lists, income limits, and program names change often and are set locally, so details that were true last year may have moved — the housing agency handling your application has the final word. This is general information, not legal advice.

Homelessness help in Texas

If you are homeless or at risk of losing your housing, help is close by. Shelters, food, health care, and long-term housing programs exist in every community.

Disaster & insurance recovery in Texas

Denied or underpaid? Our free insurance-claim dispute letter is a copy-and-fill-in template — fill your appeal deadline in from your policy and the insurer’s letters.

After a storm, fire, or flood, the order of operations matters more than speed: stay safe, document everything before you clean up, file the insurance claim promptly, and only then argue about the amount. Insurers, FEMA, and contractors each have their own deadlines and paperwork, and missing a deadline or throwing away damaged property too soon can cost you money you were owed. Work this section in order, keep copies of everything, and never sign anything under pressure in the first days after a disaster.

First 24 hours

Safety and documentation first — before you clean up

Do not re-enter a damaged home until authorities say it is safe, and do not touch downed power lines, gas leaks, or floodwater, which can hide hazards and contamination. Once it is safe, photograph and video every room and every damaged item before you move, clean, or throw anything away — insurers and FEMA decide from photos, not from memory. Make a room-by-room list with approximate purchase dates and values, save receipts for emergency repairs and temporary housing, and keep a log of every call (date, name, what was promised). If you must make emergency repairs to prevent further damage — tarping a roof, shutting off water — do so, keep the receipts, and photograph the damage first: your policy usually requires you to mitigate further damage, but it does not require you to repair everything before the adjuster sees it.

Official resource

Filing the insurance claim: deadlines and proof

Call your insurer as soon as you safely can and get a claim number in writing; claim-filing deadlines are set by your policy and by Texas law, so do not assume you have months — ask the insurer for the deadline in writing and keep that letter. Be ready with your policy number, the date and cause of loss, your photo/video inventory, and your emergency-repair receipts. Do not accept a verbal settlement on the spot, do not sign a release or an assignment of benefits you do not understand, and keep every adjuster visit, estimate, and letter. If the adjuster’s estimate looks low, get your own written estimate from a licensed local contractor and send it with your photos; that comparison is the core of any later dispute.

National Association of Insurance Commissioners (NAIC) — find Texas’s insurance department →
Denied or underpaid

If the claim is denied or underpaid: appeal, complain, appraise

A denial is not the last word. First, get the denial or low offer in writing with the exact policy language and reason cited, then appeal in writing to the insurer with the evidence that answers that reason — your photos, contractor estimate, and receipts. Second, file a complaint with Texas’s insurance department (find it through the NAIC link above): regulators can press an insurer to explain itself, and the complaint record matters. Third, check your policy for an appraisal clause: appraisal lets each side name an appraiser, and the two appraisers pick an umpire, to settle the amount of loss without a lawsuit — it costs money, so compare that cost to the amount in dispute, and get legal advice before invoking it if the policy also has a lawsuit deadline. Keep the lawsuit deadline in view from day one: many policies and state laws set a time limit for suing over a claim, and appealing does not always pause it.

Official resource

FEMA assistance — and appealing a FEMA decision within 60 days

FEMA help is separate from insurance: it can cover temporary housing and other needs insurance does not, but only after a federal disaster declaration for your area, and it does not duplicate what insurance pays. Apply at DisasterAssistance.gov or by calling the FEMA Helpline at 1-800-621-3362, and keep your FEMA application number and disaster number on every page you send. If FEMA denies you or awards less than you need, you can appeal — and the deadline is firm: appeals must be submitted within 60 days of the date on the FEMA decision letter (not the date you received it), with documents that answer the specific reason in the letter, such as repair estimates, receipts, bills, or proof of ownership or occupancy. A denial for missing paperwork is the most common kind, and it is often fixable on appeal.

DisasterAssistance.gov — apply for FEMA assistance → FEMA: How to appeal a FEMA decision →
Scams

Contractor and repair scams after a disaster

Disasters bring out door-to-door contractors, some legitimate and many not. Never pay a large amount up front, never sign a blank contract, never let a contractor pressure you into signing over your insurance check, and never hire someone who appears unsolicited the day after the storm. Check the contractor’s license with Texas’s licensing authority, get at least two written estimates, insist on a written contract with scope, price, start and finish dates, and a cancellation clause, and pay in stages as work is completed. Be especially wary of anyone who offers to “handle your claim” for a percentage, or who asks you to sign an assignment of benefits — get independent advice before signing either. Report suspected disaster fraud to the FTC at ReportFraud.ftc.gov and to Texas’s insurance department.

ReportFraud.ftc.gov — report disaster fraud →

What to do:

  1. Stay safe first. Do not re-enter until cleared; avoid downed lines, gas leaks, and floodwater.
  2. Document before you clean. Photo and video every room and item, then list values and keep emergency-repair receipts.
  3. File the insurance claim promptly. Get a claim number and the filing deadline in writing; never accept a verbal settlement.
  4. Denied or low? Get the reason in writing, appeal with your own contractor estimate, and complain to Texas’s insurance department via the NAIC link above.
  5. FEMA decision wrong? Appeal within 60 days of the date on the decision letter, with documents answering its specific reason.
  6. Hiring help? Verify the license, get two written estimates, pay in stages — and never sign a blank contract or sign over your insurance check.

This page does not print a single Texas claim-filing deadline, lawsuit deadline, or insurance-department address because those vary by policy and by state — your policy, the insurer’s written letters, and Texas’s insurance department (via the NAIC link above) are the exact sources for your claim. The 60-day FEMA appeal deadline is a federal rule stated in FEMA’s own guidance. This is general information, not legal advice.

Neighbor & HOA disputes in Texas

Most neighbor fights are not about the law — they are about a fence, a tree, a dog, or a dues bill, and about two people who stopped talking. The law still matters, because it decides who pays and who has to stop, but the cheapest wins in this section come before any courtroom: a survey, a dated letter, and a paper trail. Work the ladder in order — talk, letter, mediation, small claims — and do not skip steps, because a judge will ask what you tried first. The Deed fraud section on this page covers title and ownership scams; this section covers the everyday disputes between people who live next to each other.

Property lines

Property lines and surveys: never argue from a fence line

Fences, hedges, and driveways are often not on the real boundary — they were put up by guesswork years ago. Before you argue, or build, get the facts: your deed and plat map from the county recorder’s office, and, if the line is genuinely disputed, a licensed land surveyor’s survey. A survey costs money, but it ends arguments that a fence never will, and courts treat a surveyor’s markers as strong evidence. Do not move, remove, or destroy survey markers — tampering with them is illegal in many states. If the survey shows your neighbor’s fence, shed, or driveway is on your land, talk first and put any agreement in writing; if they refuse to move it, that written record and the survey are what a court or mediator will want to see.

Fences

Fences: who pays for a boundary fence depends on your state

Some states require neighbors to share the cost of a boundary fence equally; others put the cost on the person who wants it, or have special fence laws that apply mainly to farmland and livestock; and many cities add their own height, setback, and permit rules on top. There is no single national rule, so do not quote one to your neighbor — check Texas law and your city or county fence ordinance before you demand half the cost or refuse to pay it. Practical rules that hold almost everywhere: get any cost-sharing agreement in writing before work starts, keep the contractor’s invoice and your payment proof, and do not build on a disputed line without a survey. If a fence is dangerous — leaning, broken, electrified without warning — report it to code enforcement as well as writing to the owner.

Trees

Trees: overhanging branches, fallen trees, and who pays

The general principles courts commonly apply are these, with state-by-state variations: you may usually trim branches and roots that cross onto your property, up to the property line, but you may not trespass onto your neighbor’s land to do it, and you must not cut so much that you kill or destabilize the tree — killing a neighbor’s tree can make you liable for its full value. If a healthy tree falls in a storm, the damage usually falls where it lands, and each owner’s insurance handles their own property; but if the tree was dead, diseased, or visibly hazardous and the owner knew or should have known, the owner may be liable for the damage. The move that protects you in both cases is a dated written notice: if a neighbor’s tree looks dead or dangerous, write to them (keep a copy) and to your city’s tree or code office before it falls, so “nobody told me” is no longer available.

Noise & nuisance

Noise and nuisance: document it, then report it

Noise limits, quiet hours, barking-dog rules, and junk or odor complaints are almost always set by city or county ordinance, not state law — so the first step is finding your local rule, usually through your city or county code-enforcement office or animal control. A complaint without a record goes nowhere: keep a dated log (date, time, duration, what happened), save any recordings or photos, and keep copies of every message you sent the neighbor. Report in writing, not just by phone, and keep the complaint number. If the problem is a rental, write to the landlord or property manager too — landlords can often fix a tenant problem faster than a city can. Repeated, documented violations are what get fines issued and what win in mediation or court; a single angry call is not.

HOA

HOA fines: your rights are in the documents — read them before you pay or fight

An HOA or condo association can fine you only for rules it actually has the power to enforce, and that power comes from three places: the declaration and bylaws (the CC&Rs), the association’s rules, and Texas’s HOA or condo statute, which you can find through the state legislature’s website. In many states you have a right to a hearing before a fine becomes final, and a right to see the association’s records — budgets, meeting minutes, and the violation file on your home. If you get a fine: ask in writing for the exact rule you allegedly broke, the evidence, and the hearing procedure; attend the hearing and bring your evidence; and do not stop paying regular dues to protest a fine unless a lawyer tells you to — unpaid dues can lead to late fees and, in many states, a lien on your home, which is a far bigger problem than the fine. If the hearing fails, mediation or small claims court (below) is usually the next step, not ignoring the letters.

Escalation

The escalation ladder: talk, letter, mediation, small claims

Work it in order. Talk: calmly, once, with a specific ask and a deadline (“Can we split the fence repair by the end of the month?”). Letter: if talking fails, send a dated letter — by email plus certified mail for anything expensive — stating the facts, the rule or survey, what you want, and a deadline; keep a copy. Mediation: many courts and communities offer low-cost mediation, where a neutral mediator helps you reach an agreement you both sign; it is faster and cheaper than court and it preserves the relationship you still have to live next to. Small claims: if the other side will not talk or mediate, file in small claims court for the money you can prove. Going straight to court without the earlier steps does not help your case — judges notice who tried to settle.

Official resource

In Texas: small claims court, the last rung of the ladder

If talking, a written request, and mediation have all failed, small claims court in Texas is where most neighbor disputes end up — fence costs, tree damage, and unpaid shared bills are classic small-claims cases. The court’s own small-claims pages, already listed in the Small claims section above, have this state’s dollar limit, filing fee, and forms. File where the property or the other party is, bring your photos, survey, letters, and receipts, and sue for a dollar amount you can prove, not for an apology.

Small claims court in Texas →

What to do:

  1. Disputed line? Get a licensed survey before you argue, build, or pay — and never move survey markers.
  2. Fence cost: check Texas law and your local fence ordinance first; get any cost-sharing deal in writing before work starts.
  3. Dangerous tree: send a dated written notice to the owner (keep a copy) and to your city’s tree or code office before it falls.
  4. Noise or nuisance: keep a dated log, report in writing to code enforcement or animal control, and keep the complaint number.
  5. HOA fine: demand the rule, the evidence, and a hearing in writing; attend the hearing; do not stop paying regular dues to protest.
  6. Still stuck? Try mediation, then small claims court with your survey, letters, log, photos, and receipts.

This page does not print one national fence, tree, or noise rule or one HOA statute link because all of them vary by state and by city or county — the small-claims link above, your local code-enforcement office, and your association’s governing documents plus Texas’s HOA/condo statute are the exact sources for this dispute. This is general information, not legal advice. Property and HOA law change, and Texas and local law control.

Foreclosure Defense in Texas

Falling behind on a mortgage is frightening, but a foreclosure is a process with steps, deadlines, and decision points — not a single event. Most homeowners who act early have more options than they expect: federal law gives you time and a right to be reviewed for help, and Texas adds its own rules on top. This section explains how foreclosure works in Texas and what to do at each step.

Not legal advice This section is general information about foreclosure in Texas, not legal advice and not a substitute for a lawyer. Deadlines in a foreclosure are strict and the rules change — confirm yours with the court, your servicer, or a local legal-aid office before you act.

Two paths: court and out-of-court

How foreclosure works in Texas

Most Texas home foreclosures are non-judicial: the lender sends a notice of default giving you 20 days to pay the amount past due and bring the loan current, then must give a notice of sale at least 21 days before the sale, which is posted at the courthouse and filed with the county clerk. A judicial foreclosure through a court petition is also possible, and some loans, such as home equity loans, need a court order first.

Federal rules — all 50 states

The 120-day rule and your right to ask for help

Under federal rules (Regulation X), a mortgage servicer generally cannot start a foreclosure until you are more than 120 days behind on payments. Once you send a complete application for help — called a loss mitigation application — the servicer generally must stop moving the foreclosure forward while it reviews you, and it must review a complete application that arrives at least 37 days before a scheduled sale. Help can include a loan modification, a repayment plan, forbearance, a short sale, or a deed in lieu of foreclosure.

Find a free HUD-approved housing counselor →

Or call HUD at (800) 569-4287. Counseling from a HUD-approved agency is free.

After a sale

Redemption and deficiency in Texas

If the home sells for less than you owe, the lender may sue you for the difference, but the lawsuit must be filed within two years of the sale and you may ask the court to base the amount on the home's fair market value instead of the sale price.

Official source

Texas — foreclosure guide, before the sale (official)

The Texas State Law Library publishes a plain-language foreclosure guide for homeowners covering the steps before and after a sale and where to get legal help.

Official foreclosure information →

Think your property tax assessment is too high? You can appeal it

Your assessment is not final

The value on your assessment notice decides your tax bill, and you have the right to challenge it. Appeals are local: most start with the assessor or a local board of review or equalization, with a further appeal to a state board or tax court if you still disagree. Your state's route is in the state card below.

The deadline is the whole game

Appeal windows are short — often 30 to 45 days from the notice, or one fixed date each spring — and they are enforced. Miss the window and you usually wait until next year, paying the higher bill in the meantime. Read the date on your notice the day it arrives and calendar it.

What actually wins appeals

Two things: the assessor's own record is wrong (wrong square footage, bedrooms, lot size, or condition), or similar homes near you are assessed or recently sold for less. Get your property record card from the assessor and check every line, gather recent sales of comparable homes, and photograph anything that hurts value. Bring copies to the hearing and keep your originals.

Also check your exemptions

Homestead, senior, veteran, and disability exemptions cut the bill without any fight — but most are not automatic. You usually have to apply with the assessor, sometimes by a deadline. Ask what your home qualifies for and whether you are actually receiving it; it is printed on some notices and missing from others.

The route in Texas

Protest to the appraisal review board (ARB) if you disagree with the appraisal district's value, in most cases by May 15 or 30 days from the appraisal district notice's delivery date — whichever is later — with the deadline counted as 30 days from the date the appraisal district mails the notice. If dissatisfied with the ARB, you may appeal to the state district court in the county where the property is located, and depending on the facts and property type you may be able to appeal to the State Office of Administrative Hearings (SOAH) or to regular binding arbitration; the page also references residence homestead property.

Official sources

Texas Comptroller — Appraisal Protests and Appeals

Do this now: find the appeal deadline on your assessment notice and calendar it today. Then pull your property record card, check it line by line for errors, and file the review or appeal your state card describes — in writing, and keep proof you filed on time.

If the government wants your land: eminent domain and just compensation

Eminent domain is the government's power to take private property for a public use — a road, a school, a utility line. The Fifth Amendment and every state constitution attach two strings: the use must be public, and the government must pay just compensation, which means the fair market value of what is taken, and in many states something for damage to the part you keep when only a strip is taken. You cannot simply refuse a lawful taking, but you almost never have to accept the first number: the government's offer rests on its appraisal, and owners who get their own appraisal reviewed regularly find value the first one missed. In general terms, you can also challenge whether the taking is truly for a public use in some situations, and challenges to the amount are common and expected — the process typically runs notice, written offer, negotiation, and only then a court condemnation case if you cannot agree. Two traps: signing the initial offer or a "voluntary" agreement without advice waives the chance to contest the price, and the notices carry deadlines that matter as much as in any lawsuit. If the government (or a contractor) occupies or damages your property without ever filing — a flooded field after a drainage change, a road project that takes your access — that is called inverse condemnation, and the claim is yours to bring. Condemnation lawyers commonly review a case free and are often paid from the amount they win above the offer, so get that review before you sign anything.

What to do:

  1. Open every letter and write down every date. Notices contain deadlines that control your rights. Keep everything in one folder, and note the sale date if one has been set.
  2. Call your servicer and start a loss mitigation application now. Ask what options you qualify for, send the complete application, and keep copies plus a log of every call — a complete application generally pauses the foreclosure while it is reviewed.
  3. Get a free HUD-approved housing counselor. Use the HUD link in the federal-rules card above or call (800) 569-4287. Never pay an upfront fee for foreclosure help — upfront-fee “rescue” offers are the classic foreclosure scam.
  4. If Texas offers mediation or a settlement conference (see the cards above), sign up before its deadline. In several states you must ask within days of being served, and missing the window closes the door.
  5. If you were served with court papers, file your answer by the deadline. Ignoring a foreclosure lawsuit lets the lender win by default. Legal aid offices (see the Free or Low-Cost Legal Help section on this page) handle foreclosure cases.
  6. Calendar the sale date — and your last day to act. If a sale is scheduled, work backward from it: loss mitigation deadlines, mediation dates, and any redemption deadline after a sale.

Foreclosure timelines are short, and details differ by county and by loan type — FHA, VA, and USDA loans have their own rules and extra loss-mitigation options. When the sources above and your paperwork disagree, the court or agency handling your case has the final word. This is general information, not legal advice.

Deed fraud & squatters in Texas

Thieves can forge a deed to steal your home’s title — many counties offer free recording alerts that notify you if anything is filed against your property. And if someone is living in your property without permission, your state has specific rules for removing them: you generally cannot just change the locks.

National alert

FBI: protect your property from deed fraud

FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center alert on parcel-owner impersonation →

Deed fraud protection

Texas deed-fraud resources

Official Texas resources →

Removing squatters

Texas squatter-removal rules

Official Texas rules →

What to do next:

  1. Sign up for your county’s free property-recording alerts so you’re notified of any filing against your home.
  2. Suspect deed fraud? Contact the county recorder’s office and file a police report, then talk to a lawyer.
  3. For unwanted occupants, start the formal legal process — don’t change the locks or shut off utilities yourself.

Public benefits & appeals in Texas

A letter that cuts, denies, or reduces your benefits is not the final word. SNAP (food assistance), Medicaid, SSI, and SSDI all give you a right to appeal — usually called a fair hearing for SNAP and Medicaid, and a reconsideration and hearing for Social Security. The deadlines are the whole game: miss one and you may have to start over. Read every notice the day it arrives, circle the deadline and the date the change takes effect, and file before that effective date if you can — that is often what keeps your benefits flowing while you appeal.

SNAP

SNAP: ask for a fair hearing within 90 days

If Texas denies your SNAP application, cuts your benefit, or says you were overpaid, you can ask for a fair hearing within 90 days of the agency’s action. At the hearing you can bring evidence, bring witnesses, and have someone speak for you, including free legal help. If you ask before the cut takes effect, your benefits usually stay at the old level while the appeal is pending — but if the agency wins, you may owe back the extra amount, so ask about that risk when you file.

Medicaid

Medicaid: fair hearing — the notice controls the deadline

Federal rules give you up to 90 days from the date the Medicaid notice is mailed to ask for a fair hearing, but some states set shorter deadlines — as few as 30 days. The deadline printed on your notice is the one that counts. If you are already getting a service and you ask before the effective date on the notice, your coverage or service usually continues while the appeal is pending. If you are in a Medicaid managed care plan, you usually appeal to the plan first, and if the plan says no, you can then ask for a state fair hearing.

Social Security

SSI / SSDI: 60 days at every stage

Social Security gives you 60 days to appeal at each stage, and it assumes you got the notice 5 days after the date printed on it. The stages, in order, are: reconsideration (a fresh reviewer looks at your file), a hearing before an administrative law judge, Appeals Council review, and then federal court. Appeal online or with the paper forms — ask for reconsideration with Form SSA-561 and for a hearing with Form HA-501. If Social Security says your disability has ended, ask in writing within 10 days of that notice if you want your SSI payments to continue during the appeal.

How Social Security appeals work →
Overpayments

Got an overpayment letter? Appeal it too

An overpayment notice — “you owe us back” — can be appealed just like a denial: you can dispute the amount, dispute that you were overpaid at all, and ask the agency about a waiver or a smaller repayment plan. Do not ignore it and do not just stop cashing benefits to pay it back without asking first: an unchallenged overpayment can be taken out of future benefits. Put your request in writing, keep a copy, and ask for the agency’s answer in writing.

Official resource

Find Texas’s benefits agency and hearing form

SNAP and Medicaid are run by each state, so the office, form, and portal are Texas’s own. Find Texas’s SNAP agency through the U.S. Department of Agriculture’s state directory, and check what else you may qualify for at Benefits.gov. Your notice letter itself names the office and address to send your hearing request to — use that address, not a general office.

USDA SNAP state directory → Benefits.gov — check other benefits →
Free help

Free legal help with a benefits appeal

You do not need a lawyer for a fair hearing, but free legal aid offices handle these appeals every day and often take them at no cost — they know the hearing officers, the forms, and the evidence that wins. See the “Free or low-cost legal help” section on this page and call as soon as the notice arrives, because the deadline does not wait for a callback.

Medicare: signing up on time, and where to get free help

The four parts, in plain words

Part A is hospital insurance — inpatient hospital stays, skilled nursing care, hospice, and some home health care; most people pay no premium for it. Part B is medical insurance — doctors, outpatient care, medical equipment, and many preventive services — and it has a monthly premium. Part C is Medicare Advantage: a private plan Medicare approves that bundles Parts A and B, and usually drug coverage too. Part D is prescription drug coverage, sold by private companies Medicare approves. Original Medicare means Parts A and B together.

Your first sign-up window — do not miss it

Your first chance to sign up is a 7-month window: the 3 months before the month you turn 65, that month, and the 3 months after. If you are already getting Social Security benefits at least 4 months before you turn 65, Parts A and B start automatically and your card comes in the mail. Everyone else signs up through Social Security — online at SSA.gov or at a local Social Security office. Miss the window and you may have to wait for the January 1 to March 31 sign-up period, and pay penalties on top.

Late sign-up penalties last

Sign up late and the penalty sticks. For Part B, your premium goes up an extra 10% for each full 12-month period you could have had Part B but did not — and you usually pay that higher premium for as long as you have Part B. For Part D, going without drug coverage that counts as creditable coverage adds 1% for each month you were late, also for as long as you have drug coverage. Most people pay nothing for Part A.

Still working at 65? The 8-month rule

If you (or your spouse) are still working at 65 with health coverage from that job, you can usually sign up later without a penalty — but the clock is strict: your special sign-up period ends 8 months after the job coverage or the job ends, whichever comes first. Two traps catch people: COBRA is NOT counted as job coverage and does not extend that 8-month window, and retiree coverage ending does not start a new one. And if the employer has fewer than 20 workers, Medicare is expected to pay first — ask your state's free counselors below before you delay Part B.

Original Medicare + Medigap, or a Medicare Advantage plan?

Path one: Original Medicare plus a Medigap policy. Medigap is extra private insurance that helps pay your share of Original Medicare costs, and you can see any doctor who takes Medicare. Your best time to buy is the 6-month window that starts the month you have Part B and are 65 or older — during it, insurers cannot turn you down for health problems; later, they usually can. Path two: a Medicare Advantage plan — one private plan instead of Original Medicare, usually with a doctor network, approvals for some care, a yearly cap on out-of-pocket costs, and extras like dental, vision, or hearing. You cannot use a Medigap policy with an Advantage plan. Neither path is right for everyone — that is what the free counselors below are for.

Higher income costs more; lower income can get help

Higher income? If your tax return from 2 years ago shows income above a set amount, you pay an extra amount (called IRMAA) on top of your Part B and Part D premiums. Lower income? Medicare Savings Programs, run through your state Medicaid office, can help pay Part A and B costs like premiums — and getting one also gives you Extra Help with drug costs automatically. Extra Help also pays your Part D late penalty away while you have it.

Free Medicare counseling in Texas

Texas's free Medicare counseling program is Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP). Call 800-252-9240. HICAP counseling is reached through the statewide Area Agency on Aging line — ask for HICAP when you call.

Official sources

Texas Health Information, Counseling and Advocacy Program (HICAP) (Texas)

Medicare.gov — the official Medicare site (or call 1-800-MEDICARE, 1-800-633-4227)

Medicare is not Medicaid. Medicaid is the separate low-income health program covered in this same section — some people qualify for both, and Medicaid can help pay Medicare's costs.

Do this now: Turning 65 in the next few months? Mark your 7-month window on a calendar today, and before you pick any plan, call your state's free counseling program below — SHIP counselors do not sell insurance and do not get paid by any plan, so their only job is to help you choose.

What to do:

  1. Read the notice the day it arrives. Circle the effective date and the appeal deadline. If the notice and anything else disagree, trust the notice and call the agency the same day.
  2. File before the effective date if benefits you already get are being cut. That is usually what keeps them flowing while the appeal is pending.
  3. File the way the notice says — online portal, mail, fax, or in person — and keep a copy of everything plus proof you sent it (a confirmation number, a receipt, or a photo of the envelope).
  4. Gather your proof: pay stubs, bank statements, medical records, and every letter the agency sent you. Write down what changed and when.
  5. If the deadline has already passed, still ask in writing for a late filing and explain why you were late. Do not assume it is over until the agency answers.

This page does not list one Texas appeal deadline because the deadline depends on the program and on the date printed on your notice — the notice controls. This is general information, not legal advice. Benefit rules change, and the agency’s written notice and your state’s current rules control.

Wage garnishment in Texas — what a creditor can take, and how to fight back

  • The limit in Texas: Texas BARS wage garnishment for ordinary consumer debt. Tex. Const. art. XVI, sec. 28: 'No current wages for personal service shall ever be subject to garnishment, except for the enforcement of court-ordered: (1) child support payments; or (2) spousal maintenance.' Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem.
  • Extra protection: The bar is total for current wages - it is not a percentage cap. The only state-law exceptions in the constitutional text are court-ordered child support and court-ordered spousal maintenance.
  • If your pay is garnished: For wages, there is nothing to object to - an employer served with a garnishment writ for ordinary debt must not withhold current wages, and sec. 63.004 discharges the garnishee as to current wages.
  • Money they can't touch: Current wages for personal services (Tex. Const. art. XVI, sec. 28; Prop. Code 42.001(b)(1)); alimony, support, or separate maintenance received or to be received by the debtor for the support of the debtor or a dependent (Prop.

Official source: Texas garnishment law

If you’re sued over a debt: answer the lawsuit

A summons and complaint means a creditor or debt buyer has started a court case against you. This is the one envelope you must not ignore: if you do nothing, the court can enter a default judgment against you for the full amount claimed — and a judgment is what leads to the wage garnishment and bank levies described in the garnishment block above. Answering is usually a short form, it costs little or nothing in many courts, and it forces the other side to prove its case.

Your deadline

How long you have to answer in Texas

Your deadline depends on the court, and the citation (the first page of your papers) states it: in Justice Court your answer is due 14 days from when you are served; in county or district court your answer is due on the Monday following 20 days from when you are served. If you do not file an answer, or file it late, the creditor wins by default.

Filing your answer

How to file an answer in Texas

File a written answer with the court named in the citation before the answer date. Your answer can even be a handwritten letter to the court saying you do not agree with the lawsuit — include your case (cause) number, your mailing address, and any defenses you have.

Defenses

Defenses and things worth checking

Texas Law Help gives examples to put in your answer: the amount they claim you owe is incorrect, the account is not yours, or the debt is older than 4 years. It states the creditor must file the lawsuit within 4 years from the date of your last minimum payment or promise to pay, and must send you a written demand letter at least 30 days before suing.

Official source

Texas — sued for a debt (official court guide via Texas Law Help)

The official Texas source for responding to a lawsuit, with the court’s forms and instructions.

Official answer & self-help information →

Step by step: (1) Read the summons for the court’s name and your deadline, and calendar that date today. (2) Get the court’s answer form (the official source above links it) and respond to each numbered claim. (3) File the answer with the court and send a copy to the plaintiff’s lawyer before the deadline — keep proof. (4) Show up for every hearing; an answer on paper does not excuse a missed court date. (5) If a filing fee applies and you cannot afford it, the fee-waiver block in the Free or Low-Cost Legal Help section of this page explains how to ask the court to waive it. Never pay the collector or sign a payment agreement just because papers arrived — check first whether the debt is yours, the amount is right, and the time limit to sue has passed, and consider a free legal-aid review before your deadline.

A default judgment against you? How to ask the court to undo it

A default judgment means you lost a lawsuit without a hearing — because the court treated you as never having answered it. Many people first learn about one when wages are garnished or a bank account is frozen. In every state there is a way to ask the court that entered the judgment to undo it — called setting aside, vacating, or opening the judgment, depending on the state. The “Sued Over a Debt” block above is about answering a lawsuit in time; this block is for when the judgment is already entered.

Every state

You file in the same court that entered it

The motion goes to the court that entered the judgment — it is not an appeal and not a new lawsuit somewhere else. If the court grants it, the judgment is undone and the case reopens: the claim against you does not disappear, but you get the chance to defend it that you missed the first time.

Every state

Bring your defense with you

Courts rarely reopen a case just because you ask. In most states the motion must do two things: explain why you did not answer in time (you were never served, the papers went to an old address, a genuine mistake or emergency), and state a real defense to the claim itself — you already paid it, the debt is not yours, the amount is wrong, the claim is too old. Attach the answer you want to file and any proof you have; several states require it, and everywhere it makes the motion stronger.

Every state

The clock is the whole ballgame

Every state sets a deadline, and they differ enormously — as short as 10 to 30 days in some situations, up to a year for the common grounds, and sometimes longer for a judgment that is void or was entered without serving you at all. Most rules also say “within a reasonable time,” judged by how fast you moved once you learned of the judgment. The Texas card below gives your rule and your deadline. Count from the trigger the rule names — and if the date might be close, file now and confirm with the clerk, not the other way around.

In Texas

Undoing a default judgment in Texas

Texas has no Rule 60(b) equivalent. The tools are a motion for new trial / to set aside under Rule of Civil Procedure 329b, and after the court’s power over the judgment expires, an equitable bill of review under Rule 329b(f). Justice Court uses Rule 505.3.

Your deadline: A motion for new trial or to set aside must be filed within 30 days after the judgment is signed (14 days in Justice Court). If you first learned of the judgment more than 20 days after it was signed, Rule 306a can restart the 30 days — but never beyond 90 days after signing. After that, only a bill of review remains; the rule says it must be filed “within the time allowed by law,” and Texas courts generally apply the four-year residual limitations period (Civil Practice and Remedies Code section 16.051). A judgment rendered on citation by publication can be attacked by a new-trial motion within two years (Rule 329).

For a motion to set aside, TexasLawHelp states you must show the failure to answer was due to accident or mistake — not intentional and not the result of conscious indifference — that you have a good (meritorious) defense, and that setting the judgment aside will not cause delay or harm to the other side. A bill of review requires a meritorious defense, that you were prevented from presenting it by official mistake or the other side’s fraud, accident, or wrongful act, and that this was unmixed with any fault of your own.

Official source

TexasLawHelp (state court self-help guide) — I want to set aside a default judgment

The official Texas source for the rule above — read the current rule text there, and look for the court’s own forms and filing information.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Get the case file from the clerk of the court that entered the judgment: the case number, the date the judgment was entered, and how the papers say you were served — that service record is often your strongest ground. (2) Find your deadline in the Texas card above and count it today; if it is close, file first and polish later. (3) File the motion in that same court, attach the answer you want to give, serve a copy on the other side, and — where the card above says filing does not stop collection — ask the court in the motion itself to pause (stay) enforcement while it decides. (4) Go to the hearing, and bring proof of both halves: why you missed the case, and why the claim is wrong. (5) Can’t afford the filing fee? The court fee waiver block in the Free/Low-Cost Legal Help section of this page explains how to ask the court to waive it.

Payday loans, title loans, and the safer ways out

A payday loan is a small loan due in full on your next payday — usually a few hundred dollars, at a fee that looks small and isn’t. A title loan borrows against your car. The rules are completely different from state to state: some states cap the cost at 36% APR or ban these loans outright, and others let lenders charge what they want. Here is the federal picture, then exactly where Texas stands.

Every state

What a payday loan really costs

The standard pitch is about $15 for every $100 borrowed, due in about two weeks. Stated the way other loans are priced, that is roughly 391% APR. The real damage comes from repeat borrowing: if you cannot pay the whole amount plus the fee on payday, a new loan (or a rollover, where allowed) starts the fee clock again, and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau has found most payday loans go to borrowers who take out loan after loan. Price any payday or title loan by its APR and its total payback amount — never by the fee alone.

Every state

Your bank account: the federal two-strike rule

Since March 30, 2025, a federal CFPB rule has limited how payday, title, and similar high-cost lenders can pull payments from your bank account: after two consecutive failed withdrawal attempts, the lender must get your new written authorization before trying again, and it must send you payment notices. Know the limits of that rule, too: the CFPB announced in March 2025 that it is not prioritizing enforcement of these payment provisions, and the rule never capped prices — a planned federal requirement that lenders check you can afford the loan was repealed in 2020. The Texas law below is your main protection.

Every state

In the military? Federal law caps the cost at 36%

The federal Military Lending Act caps active-duty servicemembers and their dependents at a 36% military annual percentage rate on payday loans, title loans, and most other consumer credit — and a loan made over that cap to a covered borrower is void. Lenders must check military status before lending. If a lender charged you more, complain to the CFPB and your installation legal assistance office.

Every state

Title loans: your car is the collateral

A title loan is not like a payday loan with a different name — you hand the lender a claim on your car’s title, and if you miss payments the lender can repossess and sell the car, often without going to court first. Losing the car usually means losing the ride to work, which makes the debt harder, not easier. If a lender has taken or threatens to take your vehicle, read the Car Repossession section of this page for your rights and the steps to get the car back.

Every state

Safer exits to try first

(1) A credit union Payday Alternative Loan (PAL). Many federal credit unions offer PALs of $200 to $2,000 with APR capped at 28%, an application fee of no more than $20, and no rollovers — ask any federal credit union if it offers them. (2) Negotiate the original bill. The utility, landlord, or medical office you actually owe will often take a payment plan for far less than a payday loan costs — ask before you borrow. (3) Ask about a payment plan on the loan itself — in some states the law gives you that right (see the Texas cards below). (4) Be wary of “debt settlement” pitches that charge fees to negotiate your debts down; nonprofit credit counselors (look for NFCC members) review your budget for free or nearly free.

In Texas

Payday loans in Texas: the law

Payday lending in Texas operates through the Credit Services Organization (CSO) model rather than a direct payday-loan license: the Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner (OCCC) states that payday businesses register as credit services organizations under Texas Finance Code ch. 393 and arrange loans made by separate third-party lenders under the Finance Code's authorized interest frameworks, and Texas law does not cap the CSO fee or the rate on these transactions in the way a payday statute would. OCCC registers CSOs and licenses the underlying lenders; municipal ordinances in many Texas cities add amount and repayment limits locally. Characterized as OCCC describes the model.

In Texas

Title loans in Texas

Texas title loans run through the same CSO model and, for direct lenders, the pawn and Finance Code frameworks: title loans arranged by CSOs are registered under ch. 393, and motor vehicle title loans made directly are governed by Texas Finance Code ch. 342's provisions as OCCC summarizes them — the car is the collateral either way.

Official source

Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner - Credit Access Businesses

The official Texas source for payday and title lending — check whether a lender is licensed, see the current rules, and file a complaint there if a lender breaks them.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) If you are thinking about one of these loans, price the PAL and the original creditor’s payment plan first — both are almost always cheaper. (2) If you already have a payday loan, do not roll it over or take a second one to pay the first; ask the lender, in writing, for the payment plan described above if Texas gives you one, and keep the reply. (3) If the lender keeps debiting your account, you can revoke the debit authorization: tell the lender in writing, tell your bank, and keep copies — the two-strike rule above also limits repeat failed attempts. (4) If a lender here is unlicensed or broke the caps above, complain to the official source on this page — in several states an illegal loan is void and uncollectible. (5) If your car is on the line, act before the due date, not after: repossession is faster and cheaper for the lender than it is for you to undo.

Student loans: repayment, default, and the way out

Student loans split into two worlds. Federal loans come with repayment plans based on your income, official ways out of default, and free help from the U.S. Department of Education. Private loans are just a contract with a lender — the federal options do not apply. The federal rules changed on July 1, 2026 under the 2025 law, and this block follows what the Department of Education itself publishes now. This area is still moving, so confirm your own status in your StudentAid.gov account before you act.

Every state

First: is your loan federal or private?

Everything below about repayment plans, default, and getting out of default applies to federal loans. The Department does not cover private student loans on StudentAid.gov: for a private loan, your rights are the contract you signed, plus Texas law (see the Texas card below if there is one) — private lenders generally do not have to offer income-based payments, deferment, or forgiveness. Not sure which kind you have? Log in at StudentAid.gov: every federal loan you owe is listed there. A loan that is not listed is private — contact that lender directly about your options.

Every state

Can’t afford the payment? Income-driven plans

Ask about an income-driven plan before you miss payments. The Department’s current plans are Income-Based Repayment (IBR) and the new Repayment Assistance Plan (RAP), which started July 1, 2026. The older PAYE and ICR plans are being phased out under the 2025 law; borrowers in them with loans from before July 1, 2026 have until July 1, 2028 to choose RAP, the new Tiered Standard plan, or IBR. Under RAP, payments run between 1% and 10% of your income depending on what you earn, reduced by $50 a month for each dependent; on-time payments get leftover monthly interest waived plus a matching principal payment of up to $50, and a remaining balance can be discharged after 360 on-time payments. Loans first taken out on or after July 1, 2026 use RAP or the Tiered Standard plan (fixed terms of 10, 15, 20, or 25 years depending on the amount borrowed) — and if you never choose, the servicer will place a new loan on Tiered Standard. Applying takes about 10 minutes in your StudentAid.gov account. The SAVE plan is not among the plans the Department currently lists; if you were in SAVE, check your account and the Department’s announcements for your loans’ current status.

Every state

What default means — and why to avoid it

A federal Direct or FFEL loan goes into default after 270 days without a scheduled payment. The consequences the Department lists: the entire unpaid balance becomes due at once; tax refunds and federal benefit payments can be taken through Treasury offset; wages can be garnished; you lose deferment, forbearance, and your choice of repayment plan; you lose eligibility for more federal student aid; and the default is reported to the credit bureaus. Even before default, a delinquency of 90 days or more is reported. One warning: “Fresh Start,” the temporary program you may have heard about, ended on October 2, 2024 — anyone offering to enroll you in it now is offering a program that no longer exists.

Every state

In default? The two ways out

The Department names two main exits (paying in full is a third, impractical for most people). (1) Rehabilitation — usually the better deal. You agree in writing to make nine voluntary, reasonable, and affordable payments — they can be as low as $5 — during a period of 10 consecutive months. Finish, and the default status is removed, garnishment and offset stop, and the record of default is removed from your credit history (late payments from before the default stay). Rehabilitation is a one-time opportunity per loan today; starting July 1, 2027, you will be able to rehabilitate the same loan twice. (2) Consolidation — the faster exit. Roll the defaulted loan into a new Direct Consolidation Loan after three consecutive, voluntary, on-time, full payments, or by applying with an income-driven plan. The catches: unpaid interest is added to your principal, and the record of default stays on your credit history. Compare both at StudentAid.gov/default-support, or call the Default Resolution Group at 1-800-621-3115.

Every state

Wage garnishment: no court order needed — and a current pause

On a defaulted federal loan, the Department can order your employer to withhold up to 15% of your disposable pay — this is called administrative wage garnishment, and it does not require taking you to court. You have rights first: request a hearing in writing, postmarked within 30 days of the garnishment notice, and the garnishment pauses until after the hearing; Treasury offset likewise comes with a written notice and a 65-day window to dispute it. Current status: in January 2026 the Department announced it was delaying involuntary collections — wage garnishment and Treasury offset — while the new repayment plans roll out, and no restart date has been announced on the Department’s pages; defaults are still reported to the credit bureaus during the delay. Check StudentAid.gov for the current status, and do not count on the pause lasting.

Every state

Work for government or a nonprofit? Public Service Loan Forgiveness

PSLF cancels the remaining balance on your Direct Loans after 120 qualifying monthly payments made under a qualifying repayment plan while you work full-time for a government or not-for-profit employer. An income-driven plan (or the 10-year standard plan) is the usual route. Use the PSLF Help Tool on StudentAid.gov to check your employer and submit the form — do not rely on your servicer’s say-so alone.

Every state

Servicer problems — and the fee scam

Your servicer is the company that bills you. Find yours by logging in at StudentAid.gov, or call the Federal Student Aid Information Center at 1-800-433-3243. If the servicer will not fix a problem, submit it through the FSA Feedback Center on StudentAid.gov — and if the answer looks wrong or incomplete, you can add information to your case and ask for escalation to the Office of the Ombudsman. Finally, the Department’s own warning: help with your federal loans is free. A company charging “enrollment,” “subscription,” or “maintenance” fees to get you out of default is running a scam — walk away.

Official source

Texas Attorney General — Beware of Student Loan Scams

The official Texas source for student loans — start there to check a servicer, learn the state rules, or ask for help. For federal loans, the official source is StudentAid.gov.

Go to the official Texas page →

Do this now: (1) Log in at StudentAid.gov and confirm what you owe, who services it, and whether any loan is in default — a red warning box shows on the dashboard for defaulted loans. (2) Behind but not yet in default? Call the servicer now and ask about IBR or RAP — getting onto a plan beats every cure for default. (3) Already in default? Start at StudentAid.gov/default-support or call the Default Resolution Group (1-800-621-3115), and choose rehabilitation unless you need consolidation’s faster exit. (4) Never pay a company to do any of this — every step above is free. (5) If your loan is private, read your contract, ask the lender in writing what hardship options exist, and use the Texas source on this page if the lender breaks state rules.

Debt settlement and credit repair companies: the rules that protect you

The advance-fee ban — the single most important rule

If a debt relief company sells by phone — including when you call them after seeing an ad — the FTC's Telemarketing Sales Rule makes it illegal for them to collect any fee before they have actually settled or renegotiated at least one of your debts, you have agreed to the written settlement, and you have made at least one payment under it. A demand for an upfront enrollment, setup, or document fee in that setting breaks federal law. Before you sign, the company must also disclose how long results will take, the total cost, and the negative consequences — and it may not misrepresent what it can do.

Credit repair has its own federal law

The Credit Repair Organizations Act bars a credit repair company from taking any payment before the promised service has been fully performed. You must get a written contract and a statement of your rights, and you have three business days to cancel. No company can legally remove accurate negative information from your report, and one that tells you to dispute true information, lie on an application, or use a new "credit profile number" is telling you to break the law.

How debt settlement really works — the part ads skip

Settlement programs usually tell you to stop paying your creditors and save into a separate account instead. While you save, late fees and interest pile up, your credit score falls, and creditors can sue you — and many debts never settle at all. Debt that is forgiven can also be taxed as income (watch for a Form 1099-C). None of this means settlement never works; it means the risks are real and the fee should only be paid for results actually delivered.

Try the free route first

Nonprofit credit counselors — the kind listed earlier in this section — typically review your whole budget in a first session that is free or low-cost, and can set up a debt management plan that pays creditors in full at reduced interest, without the stop-paying gamble. You can also negotiate with a creditor yourself: many will settle or reduce rates directly, and every dollar of fee you skip is a dollar off the debt.

What Texas requires

The Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner lists Debt Management and Settlement Providers among its registered industries, and applications for that registration are submitted through its ALECS system. The OCCC describes these providers as consumer credit counseling services that help consumers get out of debt by dealing with creditors.

Official sources

FTC — Debt Relief Services & the Telemarketing Sales Rule (the advance-fee ban)

Regulated Industries - Texas Office of Consumer Credit Commissioner

Do this now: before paying any company, verify its license or registration with your state regulator (state card above), demand the total cost and timeline in writing, and take one free session with a nonprofit credit counselor. If a company demands money before it has settled anything, report it at ReportFraud.ftc.gov and to your state attorney general.

What to do next:

  1. If a collector contacts you, ask for written validation of the debt before you pay anything.
  2. Collectors can’t harass you — keep a log of every call with dates and times.
  3. Call the CFPB number above, or find a nonprofit credit counselor for a free review of your options.

Small business basics in Texas

This section is general information for people starting a small business — a different audience from most of this site, but the same plain-language, verified-links approach. Starting a business is mostly paperwork done in the right order: pick a structure, register it, get your tax IDs, check licenses, and put agreements in writing before money changes hands. The details, fees, and forms are set by Texas and by your city or county, so this page gives the national steps and the official places to check, and does not print a single Texas fee or form number that it has not verified.

Official resource

Pick a structure first: sole proprietorship, LLC, partnership, corporation

The structure decides your personal liability, your taxes, and your paperwork. A sole proprietorship is the simplest — you and the business are the same legal person, so you are personally liable for its debts. An LLC (limited liability company) separates your personal assets from the business’s debts in most cases, with simpler paperwork than a corporation. A partnership splits ownership between two or more people and needs a written partnership agreement even when the law does not require one. A corporation offers the strongest separation but the most formalities (directors, bylaws, annual filings). There is no universally best choice: liability, taxes, and how many owners you have should drive it, and a tax adviser or small-business counselor can help you compare them for your situation before you file anything.

U.S. Small Business Administration — Launch your business guide →
LLC formation

Forming an LLC in Texas: the general steps

The usual sequence is: (1) choose and check a business name against Texas’s business registry so it is not already taken; (2) name a registered agent with a physical address in Texas to receive legal papers; (3) file articles of organization (the name varies by state) with Texas’s secretary of state or equivalent business-filing office, usually online; (4) pay the state filing fee, which varies widely by state and is not printed here because it changes and was not verified for all 50 states in this section; (5) write an operating agreement, even if Texas does not require filing it — it settles who owns what, who decides what, and what happens if an owner leaves; and (6) keep the business’s money separate from your own from day one. File through the secretary of state’s own site, not a paid middleman site that charges extra for the same filing: search “Texas secretary of state business filings” and check that the site is the official .gov address before paying.

Official resource

Tax IDs: get an EIN free from the IRS

An Employer Identification Number (EIN) is a nine-digit tax ID the IRS assigns to a business; you need one if you hire employees, operate as a corporation or partnership, file certain tax returns, or open a business bank account (many banks require one). Apply online directly from the IRS — it is free, and you will usually get the number immediately on completion. Any site charging a fee for an EIN is selling you a free government service. Keep the IRS confirmation letter: banks, licensing offices, and tax filings will ask for it. Separately, check whether Texas or your city requires a state tax ID or sales-tax registration — that is a different registration from the federal EIN, handled by Texas’s tax or revenue department.

IRS — Employer Identification Number (apply free online) →
Contracts

Simple contracts: what a solid written agreement needs

Handshake deals fail exactly when they matter, so put it in writing before work starts. A solid simple agreement names the parties, describes the work or goods precisely, states the price and payment schedule, sets start and finish dates, says who owns the work product, covers changes (written change orders, not verbal), and states how disputes are handled and how either side can end the deal. Both sides sign and date it, and each keeps a copy. For partnerships and multi-owner LLCs, the operating or partnership agreement is the contract that matters most: it should cover ownership percentages, contributions, decision rules, what happens if an owner dies or leaves, and how the business is valued. Template contracts from the internet are a starting point, not a substitute for advice on a high-value or long-term deal — have a lawyer review anything that could sink the business if it goes wrong.

Licenses

Business licenses and permits: where to check

There is no single national business license. Check three levels: (1) Texas — the secretary of state’s business office and the state tax/revenue department for state registration and tax permits; (2) your city or county — the clerk’s or licensing office for a general business license and any home-occupation permit if you work from home; and (3) your industry — health, food, construction, childcare, and professional services are licensed by separate state boards with their own exams and requirements. Also check zoning before signing a lease: a business that is legal in one part of town may not be permitted in another, and a home-based business may need a home-occupation permit even if no customers visit. The SBA’s guide above and your local Small Business Development Center (free counseling, found through the SBA site) can walk you through the licenses for your specific business.

Traps

Common first-timer traps

Mixing personal and business money (open a business account and pay yourself properly); signing a commercial lease or a personal guarantee without reading the term, renewal, and exit clauses; hiring a first worker as a “contractor” when the law treats them as an employee, which creates payroll-tax liability; skipping insurance (general liability at minimum, plus professional liability or workers’ compensation where required); ignoring sales tax on what you sell; and paying a middleman for a free government filing (EIN, and in many states the basic registration). Each of these is cheaper to fix before it happens than after, and each is a reason to spend one session with a small-business counselor or accountant before launch.

What to do:

  1. Pick a structure. Compare liability and taxes (sole proprietorship vs. LLC vs. partnership vs. corporation) before you file.
  2. Form it properly. Check the name, name a registered agent, file articles of organization with Texas’s secretary of state, and write an operating agreement.
  3. Get an EIN free from the IRS. Apply on irs.gov directly; never pay a middleman for it.
  4. Check licenses at three levels: state, city/county, and your industry board — plus zoning before you sign a lease.
  5. Put agreements in writing. Scope, price, dates, ownership, changes, and exit terms, signed by both sides.
  6. Separate the money and get insured. Business bank account from day one; liability insurance and workers’ compensation where required.

This page does not print a single Texas LLC filing fee, form number, or license list because those vary by state and by city, county, and industry — Texas’s secretary of state’s business office, Texas’s tax/revenue department, and your city or county clerk are the exact sources for your business. Scope note: this section is general information for starting a business, not tax or legal advice for a specific venture. This is general information, not legal advice.

Owe back taxes, facing an audit, or disputing an IRS bill? Low Income Taxpayer Clinics give free legal help with federal tax disputes — and the Taxpayer Advocate can cut through IRS red tape.

If the IRS is not resolving your problem, call the Taxpayer Advocate at 1-877-777-4778 — free, independent help inside the IRS.

Free help with IRS disputes for qualifying taxpayers →

Got an IRS notice? Read the number first, then act by the date on it

Start with the notice number

Every IRS notice prints a number at the top (like CP14 or LT11). Look it up free on the IRS site or the Taxpayer Advocate site before you do anything else. Common ones: CP14 is your first bill for unpaid taxes. CP501 and CP503 are reminders. CP2000 means the IRS thinks your return does not match its income records — it is a proposal, not a bill, and you can disagree in writing. CP504 warns the IRS intends to take your state tax refund. LT11 or Letter 1058 is a final notice that the IRS intends to levy — take money from wages or bank accounts — and it carries the right to ask for a hearing.

Answer by the date on the notice — even if you can't pay

Ignoring a notice never makes it smaller; interest and penalties keep growing. Respond by the deadline printed on your notice: pay, ask for a payment plan, or explain in writing why you disagree. For a final levy notice (LT11 or Letter 1058), you generally have 30 days from the letter's date to request a Collection Due Process hearing (Form 12153), which stops the levy while your case is heard. Keep copies of everything and proof of mailing.

Can't pay? The IRS's own payment plans

A short-term plan lets you pay in full within 180 days with no setup fee (individuals owing under $100,000). A long-term installment agreement can be set up online at irs.gov if you owe $50,000 or less: the setup fee is $29 with automatic bank payments, $69 without ($107 or $178 if you apply by phone or mail), and low-income taxpayers pay less or nothing. While your request is being considered — and while a plan is in effect — the IRS generally cannot levy you. If even a small payment is impossible right now, ask the IRS about a temporary delay of collection for financial hardship.

Settling for less: Offer in Compromise

If you genuinely cannot pay in full, an Offer in Compromise (Form 656) asks the IRS to settle for less, based on your income, expenses, and assets. It costs a $205 application fee plus a first payment (20% down on a lump-sum offer); low-income applicants owe no fee or payments during review. Use the IRS's free Offer in Compromise Pre-Qualifier tool first — the IRS itself says to explore a payment plan before offering.

Free help that is actually free

The Taxpayer Advocate Service (1-877-777-4778) is an independent organization inside the IRS that helps when IRS procedures are causing you financial harm or your problem is stuck; ask for help with Form 911. Low Income Taxpayer Clinics give free legal help with IRS disputes to taxpayers under income limits — the IRS lists them on irs.gov. The Low Income Taxpayer Clinic in this section is another free route.

Skip the “tax relief” companies

Companies advertising on TV and radio charge thousands of dollars up front to file the same forms you can file free above — and the FTC has shut many down for taking fees and doing nothing. Never pay a tax-relief company before you have used the IRS's free pre-qualifier tool and talked to the Taxpayer Advocate Service or a Low Income Taxpayer Clinic.

Owing Texas taxes too?

If you disagree with a Texas Comptroller audit, examination, or refund denial, the Comptroller explains options including a reconciliation conference, an Independent Audit Review Conference, and a redetermination or refund hearing.

Official sources

IRS — look up your notice or letter number

Taxpayer Advocate Service — free help inside the IRS

Contesting a Comptroller Audit, Examination or Refund Denial (Publication 96-1253) (Texas)

Do this now: find the notice number at the top of your letter, look it up on irs.gov, and write down the response date printed on the notice — then pay, request a payment plan, or respond in writing before that date. If the problem is stuck or causing hardship, call the Taxpayer Advocate Service at 1-877-777-4778.
